# Softanza · The Software Crafters Platform of the AI Age

Built 2026-10-09 04:27 UTC+01:00. Library commit 93d7a39bdd040f82eae21d949460d69c32a75c70. Every page below is a draft unless it says otherwise.

# Preface

Source: Preface. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

This is the second book of the AYOUNI imprint. The first, *Refinement-Oriented Programming*, told one discipline through one bank. This one tells the platform beneath it: Softanza, a library, an engine and a language, built to one idea. Declare a thing, and then govern it. A person says what a language is, what a world is, what a solution is, and a court that was born with the declaration judges whoever speaks it, person or model.

I wrote it as a running document. Every block of code in it was run inside the library, at a commit that the page names, and judged against the lines that begin with `#-->`, which say what the block printed. If a block does not print what it promises, the build fails, and so does the page. This is why the book is not finished the day it is written: it is finished each time it runs.

**How to read it.** The book has seven parts and thirty-three chapters. Nine of them, the spine, carry the whole argument and can be read alone: the four of the first part, the three that each state a law (chapters 5, 11 and 17), and the last two. The reader has a button that shows the spine only. Read it first, and come back for the rest when a door of it interests you.

Every chapter has the same nine elements, in the same order. The concept as the field holds it today; the first principle; the form it takes in Softanza, in code; the decision, and what I refused; the court, which is the list of guards that prove it; the field, with what it has that Softanza lacks; the limit; six doors, one for the programmer, the analyst, the designer, the decision-maker, the educator and the agent; and an exercise that you answer before you run. I put the limit in every chapter on purpose. A book that says only what works is an advertisement.

**The three stage words.** Every limit is stated in three words and no others. *Built*: the code exists, a guard exists, and a block ran. *Designed*: a document says so, and nothing runs. *Absent*: I looked, and I say how far. The words are the same on the site, where they have five names, and chapter 3 prints the mapping once.

**What the page says about itself.** Under each title a line says when the blocks ran, at which commit of the library, and how many kept their promise. A second line says what ran them: the runtime of the former host language, version 1.27. Haro, the language this book writes in, does not run the library yet, and each page says so. The engine's binaries are copied from a build made at the same commit, and the page says how recent that build is.

**A draft is a draft.** Each chapter carries a banner that says it has not been read by the author. The marks that begin with `[AUTHOR:` are the questions that the record could not answer and that I could not settle by myself. They are in the pages on purpose: you can see where I stopped.

**The three media.** The site holds the record, with its proof lines. The course, in the library's education program, teaches the same ground in four languages, and by exercises that are judged by running the learner's program. This book holds the argument. Where they differ, the page says so.

**What is owed.** The last chapter is about a line that the record does not yet carry: a solution that ran on Softanza's own engine, and what a named user said of it. Three projects, a restaurant, a bank and an organisation, have taught the library, and none yet runs on it. I wrote the chapter before the line exists, and I will write it again when it does.

The French edition exists as a translation of the English drafts. Each French page says so, and it is read again once I have marked the English page as read.

[AUTHOR: This preface was written last, by the session that drafted the book, from the book that exists. It says nothing about why you wrote it, since the record does not. Keep it as a working page, or replace it with the preface you drafted.]

# Change arrives faster than trust

Source: Chapter 1. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the court that admits or refuses a change, for a person and for a machine; absent: a customer platform running on the Softanza engine

Run: 1 of 1 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:24 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

Ask the industry what changed in programming these last years and you will get one answer, said with excitement or with dread: *machines write the code now.*

The picture is familiar. A model proposes a function, a file, a whole application. A person reviews it, or does not. Tools are added around the model to make it safer: linters that read what it wrote, tests it generates for itself, guardrails that catch the worst of it, a second model that judges the first. The model is at the centre. Everything else is a fence built around it after the fact.

I do not think that picture is wrong about the facts. It is wrong about where to stand.

## 2. The first principle

Here is the sentence I keep coming back to, and it is the oldest sentence in this book: **change arrives faster than trust.**

Code has become cheap. A thousand lines cost a sentence. What has not become cheap, what has in fact become the scarcest thing in software, is the trust that those lines do what their author, human or machine, says they do. Every team I have met in the last two years has more code than it can review and less confidence than it had before. The bottleneck of our age is not writing. It is admission: deciding what may enter a system and stay there.

So the question Softanza asks is not *how do we generate better?* It is *how do we admit?* Who judges a change, by what law, before it runs, and what is written down about the verdict so that someone can read it later and be answerable for it.

When you put admission at the centre, the picture turns around. The court is in the middle. The model is one proposer among others, and it comes last, after the person, after the data, after the formula, after the plain sentence. Nothing it says enters the world until the court has read it. That is not a fence built around a generator. It is a different building.

## 3. The Softanza form

Let me show you rather than tell you, by example, which is how I have always preferred to teach.

A solution declares that it depends on a mail service. While I program, I bind that dependency to a sandbox: an object that captures every message and sends nothing, so my tests can read what *would* have gone out. Free, offline, deterministic. Then I move the solution to production, with the sandbox still bound, the way people do at two in the morning before a demo.

The registry does not call it sound. The first line it prints is a zero, and the second says why: a fake must never ship. The fake is refused by name, and a service that was declared and never bound raises the moment it is asked for, instead of silently doing nothing. I did not remember to check this. The phase decided what comes back, and the check is enforced, not remembered.

That is the whole philosophy in one small scene. Expression is free: bind what you like, while you like. Admission is governed: the gate reads the phase and the binding, and refuses.

Now a second scene, because the first one has no model in it and you are waiting for the model.

An agent that knows a restaurant's world proposes to place an order. In Softanza the agent does not place it. It proposes a plan. A workbench rehearses the plan on a twin of the system, a copy that holds no reference to the real one, and records every file it would write and every message it would send. A court reads the rehearsal against the rules the restaurant declared: who may order, what may be ordered, what a supplier must confirm. Only then does a governed actor commit, and the commit carries its reasons. The guard that proves this sequence, `safeworld_narrated`, holds seventy-six assertions as I write, and you can watch it run on the site.

Notice what the model did and did not do. It proposed. It did not decide, did not write, did not send. It was one register of answer among five, and the other four, an option chosen, a data structure given, a formula written, a sentence spoken, are all available to the person before the model is called at all. I call this *programmatic by default, the model last*. It is the sentence under every chapter of Part IV.

And a third scene, the smallest, because it shows the same law at the level of a single line. The platform is judged on what its code looks like and what it answers:

```haro
o1 = new stzList([ "tea", "rice", "tea", "fish", "rice", "tea" ])
? @@( o1.FindAll("tea") )
#--> [ 1, 3, 6 ]
? @@( o1.DuplicatesRemoved() )
#--> [ "tea", "rice", "fish" ]
```

> run: kept

The question is spoken as it is thought: find all, remove duplicates. And the two lines under the two questions are not my commentary. They are promises the library kept on the night this chapter was built. If it stops keeping them, this page fails. The same law, admission before trust, applied to a paragraph.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

Every chapter of this book carries this section, and I promised in the preface that it is the reason the book exists. So let me be concrete about the decisions behind this first chapter, because they were not comfortable.

**I refused to put the model at the centre.** When the models arrived, that was the obvious move, and I had the pieces to make it: a natural layer, a knowledge graph, an agent class. I could have wired a model into each and shipped a Softanza that "talks". I did not, and I wrote the refusal into the architecture as a law: the mind is programmatic by default, and a model may only propose. I gave up some fashion by that choice. I think I kept the platform.

**I retired the language I was born on.** Softanza was written for years on a small, warm scripting language that I love and that I pleaded with, long before the models, to make executable natural language its first concern. It did not, and that is its right. In 2026 I ruled that the language would not be carried forward: the runtime beneath Softanza is now one we own, written in Zig, wearing the old surface so that every line I wrote still runs, under a new name, **Haro**. The old surface stays because the code is the asset; the old runtime goes because sovereignty over the runtime is the first of five commitments I made that summer. That decision was not free. It is in Chapter 27 with its numbers, including the one I did not want: our runtime is slower than the one we measured against, by a factor between one and two, on every row. I published the loss the day I measured it. A book that hides that is not worth your trust, and neither is a platform.

**I refused to let anything into BASE without a court, and I failed.** The three layers of the library, CORE, BASE and MAX, were meant to keep the foundation small and the innovations above it. Under the pressure of time I let everything go into BASE: ninety-three percent of the code sits there today, and the dependency rule is held at the engine and broken at the inheritance. I say this on the first chapter, not the last, because the discipline I am about to preach for thirty-two chapters is one I have broken, and the court caught me. That is what a court is for.

**I refused a second embedding.** When someone asks "what about Python, what about JavaScript, what about my language", the answer is one door, governed, and never another host runtime sewn into the platform. Seven languages were chosen for seven layers of a solution, and that is Chapter 16. The door is the answer to "what about language X", and it is one answer, not twenty.

What was refused is as much a part of Softanza as what was built. You will not find the refusals on the site, because the site shows what is. You will find them here, in every chapter, under this heading.

## 5. The court

What proves this chapter? Three things, and I give them to you by name so that you can check them rather than believe me.

The service registry's refusal of a fake in production is a guard, `service_registry_narrated`, in the system plane. The agent's propose, rehearse, judge and commit is `safeworld_narrated`, seventy-six assertions, in the agentic plane, beside `governance_crossing_narrated` for the crossing itself. The code block above was run by the book's own runner inside the library on the night this page was built, and its output is shown as it came.

Three guards for a chapter of philosophy may seem few. They are the right three, because they are the three places where philosophy meets a verdict.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

I will not pretend the field has nothing. It has, in the last three years, built the most capable code generators in history, and every day I use them. The industry's answer to the trust problem is real work: generated tests, review bots, policy engines, sandboxes for agents, structured outputs, a second model to grade the first. Where the field leads, and it leads clearly, is in the generators themselves. Softanza does not build the large generators and never will compete with them; where it trains, it trains small models bound to one domain, whose every answer is checked by a grammar. The field also leads in scale: its review tools read volumes of generated code that nothing in Softanza has been run against.

Where I believe Softanza stands alone is in the order of things. The field adds admission to a generator. Softanza adds a generator to a court. In the field, a model writes in your language and tools check it afterwards; here, the model may only write in a language you declared, whose grammar is closed and whose court was born with it, so that an invalid sentence cannot be spoken at all. In the field, the dialogue with a system is a prompt; here, the system asks, and the answer may be an example, a formula or a sentence before it is ever a model. In the field, the record of a decision is a log; here, it is a transcript with a speaker and a certainty, answerable forever.

Those are claims, and each has its chapter and its guard. I list them here so that you know where the argument is going, and so that you can hold me to it.

## 7. The limit

The honest limit of this chapter is also the honest limit of the whole platform, and I put it on the first page on purpose.

Softanza has been used. A bank in Niamey runs an assessment platform built with it, under licence, attested by the installation guide we delivered. A restaurant in Lyon runs a platform for neighbourhood commerce whose first demo its owner made himself, on his own phone, over a messaging app. An organisation in Niger has its hub designed. Their lessons are in the code, by file name, in Part VI.

But the customer platforms run today on ordinary stacks. The bank's is a Python application; the restaurant's is a set of single-file web worlds; the organisation's hub is not built. Their declarations are being written as Softanza's own, so that moving them onto the Softanza engine is a change of engine and not of plan. That move has not been made. The court is built; the crowd has spoken; the crowd has not yet run on the engine. That is the one line this book owes, and Chapter 33 is about nothing else.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** You will keep your editor and your language. What changes is that every claim you make about your code can carry a promise under it, and a court will read it.

**The analyst.** Your rules become data a person can change, and every change is judged before it lands and recorded after.

**The designer.** The interface is judged too: twenty-two verbs, one hundred and twenty-two rules, and a generator that cannot drift from them.

**The decision-maker.** The question you ask every vendor, "who decides, and what is written down", has a mechanical answer here, and it is the same answer for a person and for a machine.

**The educator.** The tutor asks and never answers first. That is a law of the platform, not a feature of a product.

**The agent.** You are welcome here, and you will be judged. Appendix G is yours.

## 9. Exercise

Take one function a model wrote for you this week. Under it, write the line it must print for one input you care about, in the form you have just met:

```haro exercise
? YourFunction(the_input)
#--> the line it must print
```

> run: exercise

Run it. If the line matches, you have your first promise. If it does not, you have your first finding, and you have learned more about that function in a minute than the model's explanation told you. Do not fix it yet. Chapter 2 is about who you are when you do.

# The software crafter

Source: Chapter 2. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the agent file and its court; the registry that refuses a fake in production; rules as data on a model; a regulator's rules judging an organisation chart; the natural layer in Hausa and Arabic; designed: a journey page for each kind of crafter, with each step run; the door through which a coding agent asks the library to judge its work; absent: the three crafters' platforms running on the Softanza engine; a crafter's own medium, the messaging app on their phone, as a target

Run: 9 of 9 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:24 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

The field already has a word for programmers who care about their work: craftsmanship. A book of 2001 gave the idea its name, a manifesto of 2009 gave it its slogan, and since then "software craftsman" has meant a professional who holds their code to a standard higher than the one the job requires. (General knowledge, not a study of the record.) It is a good movement. It is also a movement of programmers, about programmers' standards.

The field's two other answers to "who makes software, if not programmers" define the person by a tool. The first is low-code: business people building applications on a vendor's visual platform, the "citizen developer" of the analysts. The second is the coding agent: a model that reads files, edits them and runs commands, steered in plain words, with "vibe coding" as the name for keeping whatever it writes. Both answers share a shape. The person is defined by a tool, and the work lives where the tool lives. When the vendor changes its prices or its model, the person's work changes with it.

## 2. The first principle

The site gives the definition in two sentences, with the word "maker" in them, and this book takes them with the word changed: **a crafter turns what they know about a world into something that runs, without waiting for the software industry; they are not defined by programming skill but by ownership: the artefact is theirs, in plain text, and it does not expire.** [AUTHOR: These sentences were drafted for the site and have not been ruled by you in the record. And "craft" suggests skill, where the sentence says the crafter is not defined by it. Do you keep ownership alone, or would you word the definition around the care a crafter takes? And in the paragraph that refuses "maker" below, say in your own words what the cover image carries, and I will put your sentence there.]

## 3. The Softanza form

I was the first crafter of Softanza, and not for a reason I would have chosen. My wife runs a music club where people learn to sing classical Arabic songs. Every Sunday morning she sends me the list of songs to prepare, and I search for their lyrics. The lyrics found online repeat every phrase as the singer performed it, and she reminds me, every time, to remove the duplicates. I wrote `RemoveDuplicates()` to stop doing it by hand, and its family is still in the library:

```haro
o1 = new stzList([ "tea", "rice", "tea", "fish", "rice", "tea" ])
? o1.ContainsDuplicates()
#--> 1
? o1.NumberOfOccurrence("tea")
#--> 3
```

> run: kept

I write about this in an article on what the library was built on: I introduced its features when a real problem asked for them. The crafter comes first, and the feature after.

Since 2022, I have presented Softanza not by its anatomy, its modules and classes, but by who you are and what you are trying to do. The 2022 deck had five audiences, each with a recipe of three to five steps. The site today has six doors, one per kind of crafter, and each door says what you declare, what you get, and where to start. Here is what each one declares, in a line or two that run.

**The programmer** declares intent in a library that reads like a sentence. You have just seen it.

**The analyst** declares the data of a world, and the engine computes the view. Here, three regions of Niger with the population the 2012 census counted:

```haro
o = new stzTable([ [ :region, :population ],
    [ "Agadez", 487620 ], [ "Maradi", 3402094 ], [ "Zinder", 3539764 ] ])
o.Show()
#--> ╭────────┬────────────╮
#    │ Region │ Population │
#    ├────────┼────────────┤
#    │ Agadez │     487620 │
#    │ Maradi │    3402094 │
#    │ Zinder │    3539764 │
#    ╰────────┴────────────╯
```

> run: kept

**The designer** declares a picture as a program, and gets the same picture every time:

```haro
? Q("Softanza").BoxedXT([ :AllCorners = :Round ])
#--> ╭──────────╮
#    │ Softanza │
#    ╰──────────╯
```

> run: kept

**The decision-maker** declares what each actor in the world may do, a person or an agent, and the court enforces it. An agent is a small text file; one that does not say what it covers is refused when it is loaded, not when it first acts:

```haro
cPia = "pia: 1
name: stock-watcher
kind: pi
reversibility: compensable
schedule:
  timer: 20
skills:
  - name: restock
    does: learn stock level ordered
"
oD = StzAgentDeclarationQ(cPia)
? oD.IsValid()
#--> 0
? oD.CiteFindings()
#--> [pia-coverage @ coverage] an agent must say WHAT IT COVERS. The engine loop refuses to schedule an agent with no coverage statement (law 18), so a file without one could never run and is refused here instead of at the first tick.
```

> run: kept

**The educator** declares a course, and the learner speaks to the machine in their own language. A sentence in Hausa runs; the engine counts the letters of an Arabic word, not its bytes, and knows its script:

```haro
? NaturallyIn("ha", "Yi rubutu dauke 'hello' Maida shi").Result()
#--> HELLO
? Q("سلام").NumberOfChars()
#--> 4
? Q("مرحبا بالعالم").Script()
#--> arabic
```

> run: kept

**The agent** is the sixth crafter, the one 2020 could not draw. It declares itself in the same kind of file, and it is welcome, with one thing withheld. An agent whose mind is a language model may propose; it may not hold an effect, and a file that gives it one is refused at construction:

```haro
cLlm = "pia: 1
name: rogue
kind: llm
coverage: proposes a summary
reversibility: reversible
schedule:
  timer: 20
proposes:
  prompt: Summarize {input}
  structure:
    - field: topic
      type: string
skills:
  - name: send
    when: always
    does: propose
    effect: send-email
"
oBad = StzAgentDeclarationQ(cLlm)
? oBad.IsValid()
#--> 0
? oBad.CiteFindings()
#--> [no-llm-effectful @ skills.send.effect] REFUSED: granting 'effectful' to llm actor 'rogue' -- an LLM proposes, only a pi-gate commits (no-llm-effectful, enforced at CONSTRUCTION, not merely audited).
```

> run: kept

Now the three crafters this book is for. They are not personas. They are people with documents, and Part VI tells each of them in full. Here, a page each.

**A restaurateur in Lyon.** He owns a couscous restaurant, and with a few neighbours he wanted a platform for neighbourhood commerce. The project's notes say it plainly: he is not a programmer. The project began with an offer in May 2024 and then two years of rich specification that produced nothing he could use. What came out of that failure is the rule I have worked by since: deliver small, fast and useful, and let the foundations mature in parallel without ever blocking a delivery. On 4 July 2026 he answered a scoping report in his own words and reversed the order of the work: customer first, restaurateur next, supplier last. The project's architecture was redrawn as a constellation of worlds, using the library's design, and the first amendment closed on 4 August 2026. The project counts eleven iterations and a campaign of twenty-two simulated tests behind its interface rules, and in his own report the speed of ordering is his first, non-negotiable priority. And on 19 August, after a day of tests in the field with two phones and a messaging app, the notes call him the project's co-architect. [AUTHOR: Chapter 1 says the first demonstration was made by the owner himself, on his own phone, over a messaging app. The record's checklist for the 15 August demonstration plans it for the owner alone, with his own phone and a messaging app as the support line, and the measurements he took over a messaging app are those of 19 August. May the sentence in chapter 1 stay as written?]

One of his needs is carried by a law of the library: try payments before paying anyone for an account. A fake payments service is welcome while you rehearse, and refused when you go live:

```haro
oReg = new stzServiceRegistry("restaurant")
oReg.Bind(:payments, new stzPaymentsSandbox())
oReg.SetPhase(:emulated)
? oReg.IsSound()
#--> 1
oReg.SetPhase(:production)
? oReg.IsSound()
#--> 0
? oReg.Findings()[1][:message]
#--> still bound to a SANDBOX in a production phase -- a fake must never ship
```

> run: kept

**A bank in Niamey.** A commercial bank under the rules of the central bank of the West African monetary union runs an organisation-assessment product I built, under licence, on its own internal network; the installation guide delivered to the bank is the document that attests it. The lesson it left in the library is that an organisation can be judged by its own chart, under a regulator's rules. Declare a chart with a chief executive and an internal audit that reports to him, and the regulator's rules answer:

```haro
oOrg = new stzOrgChart("bank")
oOrg.AddPositionXTT("ceo", "Chief Executive", [ :department = "executive" ])
oOrg.AddPositionXTT("audit", "Internal Audit", [ :department = "audit", :reportsTo = "ceo" ])
? @@( oOrg.ValidateBCEAOGovernance() )
#--> [ [ "status", "fail" ], [ "domain", "BCEAO_governance" ], [ "issuecount", 3 ], [ "issues", [ "BCEAO-001: No Board of Directors found", "BCEAO-002: Audit reports to non-board position", "BCEAO-003: No dedicated Risk Management function" ] ] ]
```

> run: kept

Three findings, each a rule with a number an auditor can read. [AUTHOR: Who at the bank is the crafter in the sense of this chapter, and what did they declare? The record shows the bank as the licensee of a product you built; it is silent on what the bank's own people declared.]

**An organisation in the field.** An organisation working in Niger, with a head office in Niamey and bases in the field, asked for a hub. Its own specification preferred a low-code platform built on a large vendor's office suite. My offer of 5 August 2026 was chosen instead, for one reason above the others: ownership. The organisation owns its vocabulary and its rules, changes them without programming, pays no licence per seat, and can hand everything to a developer of its own country. The design study was built with its teams between 22 September and 8 October 2026. Two of its principles are the book's in small: the default is to work offline, and the agent proposes while a person decides. That rule is data the organisation attaches to its own model, and the model judges itself:

```haro
oRule = new stzGraphRule("no-llm-effectful")
oRule.SetDomainQ("agentic")
oRule.WhenQ("kind", "equals", "llm_actor")
oRule.WhenQ("capabilities", "contains", "effectful")
oRule.ThenViolationQ("an LLM proposes, only a pi-gate commits")
g = new stzGraph("agents")
g.AddNode("writer")
g.SetNodeProperty("writer", "kind", "llm_actor")
g.SetNodeProperty("writer", "capabilities", [ "inference", "effectful" ])
g.AddRule(oRule)
? g.RulesAreSound()
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

Three crafters: a restaurateur who is not a programmer, a bank under a regulator, an organisation that chose ownership over a vendor. None of them waited for the software industry. Each of them owns, or will own, what was built.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I kept the programmer first.** The vision of the platform, which I ratified, says it in one line: the first audience stays the programmer in a small team; the agentic era did not replace that person, it multiplied them. I refused the reading that agents make programmers obsolete.

**I added the agent as a crafter, and withheld one power.** The agent is the sixth reader of every Softanza language: both a builder and a user. It gets a workbench and a court. It does not get the capability to commit.

**I refused "maker", and I refused "builder".** "Maker" names the workshop movement, the soldering iron and the 3D printer, and the site's headline used it for a year. "Builder" names someone who assembles what a plan says. Neither says what I think programming is. It is handwork, done with care, on something that is yours: it is craft. I call the person who does it a crafter, and the word carries the culture of this book's cover image as much as my own view of the work.

**I refused to count a customer without a document.** The site counts a project as a reference only when a document proves it, quotes no number of countries and no number of developers, and names a customer only on my word. A lesson is listed only when a file of the library carries it.

**I refused to present the library by its anatomy.** Since 2022, the front door is who you are and what you want to do, and the anatomy is placed under each step. The site keeps six doors; the journeys behind them, one page per crafter with every step run, are designed and not built.

## 5. The court

`agentfile_narrated` proves that an agent is a file judged when it is loaded: one refusal per gate, the missing coverage among them, and the model agent holding an effect refused by the same rule the graph uses. `service_registry_narrated` proves that a sandbox in production is a violation. `graphrule_object_narrated` proves the rule as an object attached to a model. `semantic_resolver_narrated` proves the Hausa path. It does not pass at this commit: I ran it on 9 October 2026 and it stops with an error raised from a string-splitting function, before it ends, so the page counts its assertions and does not claim its verdict. `00_orgchart_narrated` proves the organisation chart and its validators.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

Coding agents lead in hands. They already read, search, edit and run, on any project, in any language. What they lack, by the site's own account, is a way to know an unfamiliar interface without guessing, to judge their work against rules and promises, and to present a change as an intention a person can review. Softanza's judges are built; the door between them and a coding agent is decided and not built.

Low-code leads in installed base and in everything around it. The organisation in the field keeps its vendor's suite for its accounts, its documents and its notifications; the crafter's platform sits beside the field's tools, not in place of them.

The messaging apps lead where the crafters already are. The restaurateur's tools before the project were his head, a paper notebook and a spreadsheet; his own report of 4 July 2026 gives the spreadsheet, and the line that names messaging apps is the label of an option in the scoping form, and not his answer. No page of the site names those apps, and no conversational medium is a target of the platform yet. Chapter 25 is about that gap.

And the educator's door is the least proven of the six. The course's editions in French, Arabic and Hausa are drafts that no reviewer has read, and no institution has adopted it.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the agent file and its court; the registry that refuses a fake in production; rules as data on a model; a regulator's rules judging an organisation chart; the natural layer in Hausa and Arabic.

What is designed: a journey page for each kind of crafter, with each step run in order and a guard that fails when a step breaks; the door through which a coding agent asks the library to judge its work.

What is absent: the three crafters' platforms running on the Softanza engine. Today the restaurant's platform is a set of web worlds, each a single file; the bank's is a Python application; the organisation's hub is a design. Their declarations are being written as Softanza's own. And the crafter's own medium, the messaging app on their phone, is not yet something a world can be declared to.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** You declare intent in a library that reads like a sentence, and an engine computes under every call. You stay the first audience.

**The analyst.** You declare your organisation's entities, rules and flows in plain text, and the rules judge the model.

**The designer.** You declare a picture, a map or a screen as a program, and the first rule of an interface can come from a real trade: for a restaurateur, the speed of ordering.

**The decision-maker.** You declare your world, your governance, and what each actor may commit, and you own all of it, in plain text.

**The educator.** You declare a course in your learners' language, and every exercise is checked by running.

**The agent.** You declare yourself in a file: what you cover, how reversible your acts are. You get a workbench and a court. You do not get the capability to commit.

## 9. Exercise

Declare one agent for a world you know: a shop, a class, an office. Write its file: what it covers, how reversible its acts are, when it runs, one skill. Before you run it, write the promise you expect under the question below. Then run it.

```haro exercise
? StzAgentDeclarationQ(cYourAgent).IsValid()
#--> 1
```

> run: exercise

If the court refuses, read the rule it cites and change your file, not the court. Then make your agent a language model and give it an effect, and read why it is refused. You will not find the corrected file in this book.


=====

# The method, from first principles

Source: Chapter 3. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the court behind every block of this chapter, and the stage words the site prints on every product; designed: the register of rethought concepts as data, with a guard that fails when a concept has no proof; absent: a page that shows every rethought concept with its proof; a test file for the paradigm engine of section merging

Run: 5 of 5 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:24 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

In our industry, "from first principles" is a slogan. It means: reason from the bottom, not by analogy. You will hear it in talks and read it in launch posts, and you will rarely be shown what it produced.

The field does have a method for the decisions behind software, and it is a good one. A team writes a short record for each decision it takes: the context, the decision, the consequences. Many teams call these architecture decision records, from a blog post Michael Nygard wrote in 2011. The field also has a method for proving what a function does: an example written under the function, run by the tools every time the project is built. Python has done this with its doctest module since 2001, and Rust runs the examples in its documentation as tests. And when the field compares one tool with another, it draws a grid: rows of features, one column per tool, ticks and crosses.

## 2. The first principle

Here is the sentence the whole method returns to: **you declare a world in a language made for it, and you govern how that world changes.**

Two acts. Everything else in this book is one of them, or the two together.

## 3. The Softanza form

"From first principles" can be checked. In Softanza it has a narrow meaning: take a concept everyone uses, write down how the field holds it, redesign it, and leave code that carries the redesign and a court that judges it. The site's history page is titled *From first principles*, and the library has been on GitHub since 12 March 2022. The phrase is not decoration there. It is a work order.

Let me show you the smallest rethink I know. Ask Python how many times the empty string occurs in the word "text", and it answers five: one before each letter and one at the end. Ask it whether "text" contains the empty string, and it says yes; JavaScript says the same. (Python 3.13 and Node 22, run on 8 October 2026.) That is a convention of implementers, not a fact anyone would say aloud. Softanza states a rule instead, and the rule has a name in the narration that argues it: emptiness is uncountable, and emptiness is uncontainable.

```haro
? Q("text").Count("")
#--> 0
? Q("text").Contains("")
#--> FALSE
```

> run: kept

Two lines, and a decision you can disagree with. That is what a rethink looks like when it is honest: small enough to read, explicit enough to refuse.

A reading of the record, made in October 2026, counted forty-six such concepts, in seven families: the paradigms, such as the mental model, knowledge programming and reactive programming redesigned; the paradigm engines, such as truth, quantifiers and time; patterns and reaction; expression and perception; data, time and reasoning; system, delivery and operations; and the library that knows itself. Forty-six is a reading and not a census. Each concept on it has an article or a design document of its own, and that was the test for entering the list.

Now the two acts, each in a few lines.

**Declare.** A kind of value is declared in a sentence, and every verdict against it is computed and explains itself:

```haro
ConstrainQ("score").ToBeQ(:Number).AndQ().ToBeQ(:Positive).AndQ().ToBeQ(:Even)
? CanQ(42).BeA("score")
#--> TRUE
? CanQ(-4).BeA("score")
#--> FALSE
? Why()
#--> no: -4 is not positive (constraint 2 of 'score')
```

> run: kept

Notice the last line. The verdict names the rule it applied, by its number. An answer that cannot say why is not admitted in this house.

**Govern.** A declared constraint, once enforced, refuses a change before it lands:

```haro
oName = new stzString("hello")
oName.EnforceConstraint("keep-lowercase", :Lowercase)
bRefused = FALSE
try
	oName.UpdateWith("HELLO")
catch
	bRefused = TRUE
done
? bRefused
#--> TRUE
? oName.Content()
#--> hello
```

> run: kept

The update was refused, and the object stands as it was. The error the library raises says what was tried, why it was refused and what to do: fix the new value, or relax the constraint. Declaring a constraint and enforcing it are two acts, and the guard behind this block checks that a constraint you only declared stays passive.

Governing also has a side that faces the person. The system asks, and it does not let you close a world that is not complete:

```haro
oKB = new stzKnowledgeGraph("restaurant")
oKB.Know("margherita", "dish").Know("tiramisu", "dish")
oKB.AddConversationQ("setup").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "contains"))
aQ = oKB.AskInXT("setup")
? aQ[:why]
#--> every dish needs 'contains'
try
	oKB.ConcludeIn("setup", "world")
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Can't conclude: 2 gap(s) remain -- the wise-coding loop is not done. Ask the next question.
```

> run: kept

The goal was declared: every dish must say what it contains. The gap between the goal and what is known generates the question, and the question carries its reason. Concluding while two gaps remain is refused, and the refusal tells you what to do next. Chapter 18 is about this conversation. Here it is enough to see that governing is not only refusing; it is asking.

One more rethink, because the paradigm engines are part of the method too. Truth, in the language beneath Softanza, is a matter of zeros and non-zeros, and a list of words counts as true. Softanza keeps that truth by default and lets you extend it by declaration:

```haro
SetItemsMakingAListFalse([ "false", "wrong", "dangerous" ])
? IsTrue([ "hello", "this", "is", "wrong" ])
#--> TRUE
? IsTrueXT([ "hello", "this", "is", "wrong" ])
#--> FALSE
```

> run: kept

The host's answer is unchanged. The extended answer reads your declaration. "Truth becomes a matter of policy", the narration says, and the policy is written where you can read it.

So this is the method, in the form you will meet in every chapter after this one. A concept as the field holds it. The one principle the record returns to. The redesign, in running code, with its promise under each block. The decision, and what was refused. The court that proves it, by name. The field, and where it leads. The limit, in three stage words: **built** means the code exists and its guards pass; **designed** covers what the site calls in construction, specification and ratified proposal; **absent** covers a name with nothing behind it yet, and what nobody has named. Six doors, one for each kind of reader. One exercise.

And the book is judged the way the library is. Every block in it ran inside the library on the night the page was built, at the commit printed at the top of the page, and a page whose promises are not kept does not build.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

There is no single record of a decision called "the method". The method was decided in pieces, and the pieces are in the record.

**I refused to add a feature without a need.** I wrote it in an article long before this book: "I introduce new features only when they solve a tangible problem or when I anticipate they'll meet developers' needs." First principles without that rule produce a museum. With it, a rethink has to earn its place: someone had a problem the field's form did not answer.

**I refused free text as the meeting ground between people and machines.** The vision of the platform says it in one line, which I ratified: humans and AIs meet in declared languages, small, closed and judged, and not in general-purpose code. Declaring is the first act because it makes the second possible. A court can only judge a sentence of a language it knows.

**I refused a title that looked back.** When this book was first proposed, its title was *Programming by Heart*, the slogan of the 2020 presentation. I turned it down as outdated: it named a stance, and the book had to name what was rethought. The next proposal was *Declare, Then Govern*. I kept the site's own sentence for the cover instead, and the two verbs moved to the back cover, where you will find them. They organise every chapter; they did not need to be its name.

**I refused the grid alone.** The library's documents hold dozens of comparison grids, and in them Softanza's column wins almost every row. A reader who sees only those grids is right to discount them. The honesty in the record is elsewhere: in the sections of the field studies titled "what the field has that Softanza lacks, honestly", and in measured numbers. When the runtime beneath Softanza was measured again in September 2026 against the one it was compared with, the earlier headline did not reproduce, and the loss was published the same day. That is the rule this book follows: a loss is printed beside the win, in the same section.

**I refused to call a reading a census.** The forty-six concepts are a reading of the record by one session, not a list I ruled row by row. [AUTHOR: Do you endorse the forty-six-row register as the book's Appendix A as it stands, or will you rule it row by row first?]

## 5. The court

The four blocks of the two acts stand on two guards. `modality_narrated` proves that a declared kind can be inspected, that a verdict names the constraint that failed, that an unknown kind never silently allows, and that an enforced constraint refuses a bad update before it lands while a declared one stays passive. `wisecoding_narrated` proves the conversation: gaps computed from the declared goal, a question that says why it is asked, answers admitted, a law refusing through the conversation and reaching a person, and concluding with open gaps refused. Its sister, `wisecoding_rich_narrated`, carries the richer cases of the same loop.

The rethinks of emptiness and truth stand on the library's classic tests, the numbered blocks with their answers written under them, which the house kept as its first test form.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in adoption. Decision records are a light standard that many teams use, with templates and tools around them; the decision element of this book exists in this book and in one study of the record, and not yet as data in the library. Examples in documentation are run by the standard tools of Python and Rust in every project that writes them; the promises of Softanza are judged by the house's own runners, which no outside tool reads. And the field's habit of comparing by grid is no worse than the record's, which shows the same weakness on its own pages.

Where I think Softanza stands apart is in one place only: the rethink is carried all the way down. The decision is written, the redesign runs, the promise is judged at build, and the stage word says how far each piece has gone. A decision record does not run. An example does not say what was refused. Here they are one object.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the court behind every block of this chapter, and the five stage words the site prints on each product.

What is designed: the register of rethought concepts as data, one row per concept with its article, its code, its guard and its stage, and a guard that fails when a new concept arrives without a row. It is a specification today.

What is absent: a page that shows the whole register with its proofs; a test file for one of the paradigm engines, section merging, which has none of its own; and a few rethinks that do not run as their article wrote them. The article on quantifiers asks whether all your marks `AreGreaterThan` the average, and that method is spelled differently in the library today; its friendlier quantifiers, some, most and few, draw at random, so they cannot carry a promise. The register is a reading, and a reading can miss a concept that lives in only one article.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Every claim you make can carry its promise, and a declared constraint refuses a bad update at the one place updates happen.

**The analyst.** Your rule is a declaration, and its verdict names the rule that failed, by number.

**The designer.** The stage word is part of the interface. A product that does not exist yet says so, in a word you can check.

**The decision-maker.** Ask every vendor for the loss beside the win. Here it is printed, with its date.

**The educator.** Each rethink has an article that argues it: the concept, the field's form, the redesign. That is a curriculum, ordered by family.

**The agent.** You learn this platform from the platform, and you are corrected by it. A verdict that says why is written for you as much as for a person.

## 9. Exercise

Choose one default of your own language that you never questioned: what the empty string counts to, what counts as true, what "most" means. Before you run anything, write in one sentence the rule you think is right. Then write two promises in the form you have met: the line your language prints today, and the line your rule must print.

```haro exercise
? YourDefault(the_input)
#--> what your language prints today
? YourRule(the_input)
#--> what your declared rule must print
```

> run: exercise

Run both. I will not tell you which rule is right. If your rule and your language disagree, you have found a decision that someone took for you, and you can now take it yourself.

# The platform in one picture

Source: Chapter 4. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the engine and its libraries; the base layer; worlds, constellations and platforms with their guards; the engine subset a part ships; designed: the max layer loaded by its entry file; the base layer inheriting the core one; a loader for one domain of base alone; a court that checks the layers against their contract; absent: a whole program and its engine packed into one file; a customer platform on the engine

Run: 8 of 8 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:24 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

The field draws a platform as a stack of boxes. The kernel at the bottom, a runtime and its libraries in the middle, applications on top, and sometimes a column at the side for security or tools. Inside one codebase the same picture becomes a rule about dependencies: an inner layer knows nothing of the outer ones, and the arrows only point inward. Rust's three standard crates, one for bare metal, one for memory, one for a full operating system, are the cleanest version I know: three depths of the same language, chosen by the target and not by a flag.

For applications, the field's picture is a host. A large application embeds a runtime and runs small applications inside it, which share its services and its store. In both pictures the judge is outside: a test suite beside the code, a review beside the store. Neither draws the court as a column standing at every floor.

## 2. The first principle

The stack of the platform is summed up, in the vision I ratified, in one idea at every altitude: **nothing is believed, everything is judged.**

## 3. The Softanza form

Here is the whole platform in one picture, read from the bottom up. Each floor stands on the one beneath it, and each floor is judged by a court beside it. The numbers in it are not typed by me: the build reads them, the night this page is made, from the library and from the site's map of its areas.

{{fig:platform-one-picture|The platform in one picture. Each floor stands on the one beneath it; each is judged by the court beside it; the architect works across all of them. The numbers are read at build from the library's engine, its test tree and the site's Atlas.}}

Let me walk it, floor by floor, with one block at each floor that you can run.

**The engine.** At the bottom is an engine written in Zig: a set of libraries behind a C interface, one per domain, grouped by the layer that loads them. The engine does the computation; the library above it gives it a vocabulary. A small example makes the division visible. The language beneath Softanza, asked for the length of the Arabic word for peace, counts bytes; the engine counts letters, and knows the script:

```haro
? len("سلام")
#--> 8
? Q("سلام").NumberOfChars()
#--> 4
? Q("مرحبا بالعالم").Script()
#--> arabic
```

> run: kept

The engine also has its own door, beneath every class. Numbers too large for the machine's arithmetic are added exactly, by the engine itself:

```haro
a = StzEngineBigIntFromString("99999999999999999999999999999999")
? StzEngineBigIntToString( StzEngineBigIntAdd(a, StzEngineBigIntFromString("1")) )
#--> 100000000000000000000000000000000
```

> run: kept

**The layers.** Above the engine, the library is cut in three layers, by depth of capability. Core, with the prefix `stk`, is lean and talks to the engine directly, for small devices and small binaries. Base, `stz`, is the rich layer, where the vocabulary and its forms live, enough for most solutions. Max, `stx`, is meant for the strongest innovations. A layer is chosen by the file a program loads, and the same verb means the same act at each depth:

```haro
o1 = new stkList([ "one", "and", "one", "makes", "two", "ones" ])
? o1.FindFirst("one")
#--> 1
o2 = new stzList([ "one", "and", "one", "makes", "two", "ones" ])
? o2.FindFirst("one")
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

**The areas.** Across the layers, the library is mapped by the site's Atlas into areas grouped in bands, and each area into lanes, each lane rated from emerging to strong with the line that proves it. The Atlas is the platform's anatomy: what exists, and how well. One rule holds across all of it: a verb means the same act on every structure that admits it. On a string it answers character positions; on a list, item positions:

```haro
? @@( Q("tea rice tea").FindAll("tea") )
#--> [ 1, 10 ]
? @@( Q([ "tea", "rice", "tea" ]).FindAll("tea") )
#--> [ 1, 3 ]
```

> run: kept

**The worlds.** Above the library, the unit is not a module but a world: an application is a living world of meaning, with its things, its relations and its rules. A world is declared, and it can explain itself:

```haro
oApp = new stzApp("FieldVisits")
oApp {
    AddThingQ(:Account) { Has([ :number, :chapter, :balance ])  IsTrue(:balance, "@ >= 0") }
    AddThingQ(:Client)  { Has([ :code, :name, :city ])  Owns(:Account) }
    AddRelation(:Account, :belongsTo, :Client)
}
oApp.Explain()
#--> WORLD FieldVisits   lives in: memory (not persisted)
#      BEING
#        account (number, chapter, balance)
#            true when balance @ >= 0
#        client (code, name, city)
#      RELATIONS
#        client owns account
#        account belongsto client
oApp.Live()
#--> [FieldVisits] is live -- 2 thing(s), 0 flow(s), 0 reaction(s), 0 goal(s); 0 proposal(s)
```

> run: kept

Worlds compose into a constellation. Here a restaurant and its produce supplier are two worlds; a bond between them declares the one crossing that is allowed, and every other crossing is refused with its reason:

```haro
oResto = StzAppQ("resto")
oResto.AddThingQ(:dish) { Has([ :name, :price ]) }
oSupplier = StzAppQ("supplier")
oSupplier.AddThingQ(:crate) { Has([ :produce, :qty ]) }
oCon = new stzSuperApp("acme-group")
oCon.AddWorld("resto", oResto)
oCon.AddWorld("supplier", oSupplier)
oCon.Bond("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
oCon.GovDeclareRisk("order-produce", 2)
oCon.GovGrant("resto", "order-produce")
oCon.GovSetAuthority("resto", :Delegated)
? oCon.CallAcross("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
#--> 1
? oCon.CallAcross("resto", "supplier", "raid-kitchen")
#--> 0
? oCon.Why()
#--> no bond declares 'raid-kitchen' from 'resto' to 'supplier'.
```

> run: kept

And a platform is the envelope around a solution: its parts, the systems they run on, and the order in which it is built, rehearsed and deployed. You will see it refuse to deploy before it is built in chapter 33. Here is what it does with the layers: a part of a solution ships only the engine groups it needs, and only those that are Softanza's own:

```haro
oDelivery = new stzDelivery("my-shop")
oDelivery.AddSuperApp(:phone, :Android)
oDelivery.AddBackend(:api, :LinuxServer)
oDelivery.NeedsIn(:phone, [ :Unicode, :PivotTable, :ConstraintSolver, :Regex ])
oPlan = oDelivery.Plan()
? @@( oPlan.EngineCapsFor(:phone) )
#--> [ "pivottable", "constraintsolver" ]
? @@( StzWasmGroupsFor( oPlan.EngineCapsFor(:phone) ) )
#--> [ "aggregation", "solver" ]
```

> run: kept

The phone asked for four capabilities and ships two engine groups, because the phone's own system already handles the other two.

**The crafters.** At the top are the six doors of chapter 2: the programmer, the analyst, the designer, the decision-maker, the educator and the agent. And one person works across every floor, from the machine to the organisation: the platform architect. The record names that person in three unrelated places, and no other role crosses every band of the Atlas.

**The court.** Beside every floor stands its judge. The engine has its tests and the contracts between the floors. The layers have the narrated guards. The areas have a proof line for every lane. The worlds have their bonds and norms, the order of build and deploy, and the refusal of a fake in production. The crafters' interfaces have a constitution of verbs and rules that every element must trace to. And above all of it, the platform's threat model lists its guarantees, each tied to the guard that proves it, and its own guard fails when a row names a guard that no longer exists.

Parts II to IV of this book are rethinks, and each of them is a box of this picture. Part V assembles the picture in use.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused computation in the faces.** The engine is the product. The language above it adds beauty, not computation: thousands of methods that read like sentences, and the algorithms beneath them in one place. Every feature is implemented once, in the engine.

**I removed the old framework, with no fallback.** For years the library sat on a large C++ framework for its text, its dates and its files. When the engine replaced it, every path through the framework was removed: no fallback, no feature flag kept in case.

**I chose sovereignty as a verdict on each dependency, not as "every line ours".** The engine vendors and pins a set of third-party libraries, the figure counts them, and each one is a decision. The record defines the word in one line: nothing anyone else can withdraw. That is the test. A library whose source is pinned in the tree passes it; a binary blob that cannot be pinned does not, and was not taken.

**I refused a flag to choose a layer.** The first way of choosing a layer was a flag. It could never work, because the language beneath loads every file it sees at compile time, so all three layers loaded on every call and the failure of one was the failure of all. The flag was removed rather than kept as a switch that silently does nothing, and the reason was written where the next reader would stand: a layer is chosen by the file you load.

**I let everything go into base, and I say so.** Under the pressure of time, the domains landed in base while the engine, the planes and the customers moved forward. That was the agile choice, and it is defensible only while the debt is visible. So the figure draws base widest, and chapter 27 tells the debt with its numbers.

**I refused mini-applications.** The field's super-app is a host that runs small applications. Softanza's is a governed graph of worlds: worlds, not mini-apps; bonds and norms, not a container.

## 5. The court

`00_stzapp_narrated` proves a world declared and made visible. `06_stzsuperapp_narrated` proves the constellation: worlds joining, the commons as world zero, crossings gated by norms with the bond named as the reason, a retired world breaking its bonds. `full_scope_model_narrated`, the architect's common ground, proves the solution modelled first, the scopes that refuse what a target forbids, and the order of build and deploy. `01_platform_narrated` proves the platform's duties. `threat_model_narrated` reads the platform's threat model and fails when a guarantee names a guard that no longer exists.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

Rust's three depths are enforced by its compiler and used every day on bare metal. Softanza's rule is held at the engine and at the entry files, and broken at the inheritance, as section 7 shows.

The field's text libraries lead on locale: Softanza's own map says that the clear gap of its strings is the international components' locale layer, and that its locale area is a deliberate bet against those components, with no collation, no plural rules and no time zones.

The field's super-app hosts run real users at a scale this platform has never met. The record names them as the model it refused and measures nothing against them, so I will not either.

And the Atlas itself prints where Softanza is weak: the binary area is a plan with no shipped code, extensibility is mostly planned, the system area has few strong lanes. A map that prints its weak areas is the only kind worth reading.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the engine and its libraries, the base layer, worlds, constellations and platforms with their guards, and the engine subset a part ships.

What is designed, or built and not reachable. The max layer's own classes are written and loaded by no entry file:

```haro
try
	o3 = new stxString("one and one")
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Error (R11) : Error in class name, class not found: stxstring
```

> run: kept

The base layer does not inherit the core one, as the layer contract says it should; the two share the engine and implement their common domains twice. A loader that brings one domain of base without the others is not built. A court that reads the layer contract against the tree and fails on drift is a specification. [AUTHOR: Is the base-inherits-core rule to be realised, or the contract amended to "two implementations sharing one engine"?]

What is absent: a whole program and its engine packed into one file with no dependency, and a customer platform running on the engine.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** You choose your depth by the file you load, and one verb means the same act on a string and on a list.

**The analyst.** The Atlas tells you, lane by lane, where the platform is strong and where it is emerging. Build on the strong lanes.

**The designer.** Your interface has a court of its own, the constitution's verbs and rules, in the same column as the code's guards.

**The decision-maker.** Ask two questions of every floor: what stands beneath it, and who judges it. The engine's answer to the first is a written list of pinned libraries, each a verdict.

**The educator.** The picture is the book's map. Each rethink in Parts II to IV is one box of it.

**The agent.** The picture exists as data you can read, the Atlas as a file, and every floor you write to has a court that will read you.

## 9. Exercise

Draw your own solution the way this chapter draws Softanza: floors, and a court beside each. For each box, write the file that declares it and the guard that judges it, and put a stage word on it: built, designed or absent.

Then declare two parts of it with `stzPlatformProfile`, and before you run anything, write the line you expect when you ask it to deploy before you build it. Run it. A box with no court beside it is your first finding.

# Law: the code adheres to the way a person thinks, and the court judges it whoever wrote it

Source: Chapter 5. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the forms of the vocabulary in thousands of method names; the house rules as a court over the library's own code; the promise read by a machine in the house's own form; designed: a court of style for the code a programmer or an agent writes; the writing grammar declared in Haro, so a model can only write in it; absent: any machine that judges today the style of a program written with the library

Run: 7 of 7 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:24 UTC+01:00.

## What the AI age changes here

For years, most of the code written with Softanza was written by one hand, and the hand was mine. The style of the library was simply the way I wrote. Then the machines arrived, and today most code, here as everywhere, is written by models. The design of the intelligence plane says it in one sentence: most programming today is done by agents and language models, not humans.

That changes what a style is. A style that lives in one person's habits dies when that person stops writing every line. It has to become something a machine can be held to, and something a machine can check, or it thins away. In this house it did thin, and I will show you where. So this movement of the book, Programming rethought, opens on a law that has two halves, and the second half exists because of the age: the code adheres to the way a person thinks, and the court judges it whoever wrote it.

There is a dependency to say aloud. This book follows the order of its cover, from the familiar to the new: programming, then languages, then intelligence. The record's own logic runs the other way. The first act is to declare a language; programming happens inside a declared language; intelligence is governed by both. The vocabulary's forms are already a declaration in Haro, and the usage that this chapter describes, the scene, the chain, the named parameter, the promise under each line, is meant to become one too. So read this law knowing that Part III holds it up.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

In our industry, "coding style" means two things, and neither is about how a person thinks. The first is layout and naming, enforced by machines: Python's style guide since 2001, Go's formatter since 2009, and the formatters that every modern project runs before a commit. They settle arguments about spaces and capital letters, and they are very good at it. The second is the fluent interface: methods chained so that a line reads more or less like a sentence.

The age of generated code added a third practice: check after. A model writes, the tests and the linters read what it wrote, and the model tries again if they complain. The line itself is judged for its shape and its result. Whether it follows the order in which the writer reasoned is a question nobody asks, because there is nothing that could answer it.

## 2. The first principle

My motto for this has not changed since 2020: **what you think is what you write.**

## 3. The Softanza form

The founding slide of 2022 put the whole idea on one page. On the left, a person's four steps of thought: I take the word "softanza"; I turn it to uppercase; I get its characters; I join them with a space. On the right, the same four steps as one line of code, as if the text on the left had been pasted and run.

That exact line no longer runs. Its third link, a method that turned the characters into a list of strings in one step, is not in the library today; it survives only in an archived file. I tell you this first because a law chapter that hides its own founding example would not deserve your trust. Here is the nearest form that runs, with the four steps still visible, and then the form the library offers as one dedicated verb:

```haro
? StzListOfStringsQ( StzStringQ("softanza").UppercaseQ().Chars() ).ConcatenatedUsing(" ")
#--> S O F T A N Z A
? StzStringQ("softanza").UppercaseQ().Spacified()
#--> S O F T A N Z A
```

> run: kept

The idea is intact; the fluent link was lost. A request goes to the library to restore it.

The mental model is the same idea at the level of a problem. You say the problem in plain words. You select the object that holds the data. Then you ask, in order: does it contain what I care about, how many, where. Then you act on those positions, or you call the verb that does it for you:

```haro
o1 = new stzList([ "A", "", "B", "", "", "C" ])
? o1.ContainsEmptyStrings()
#--> TRUE
? o1.CountEmptyStrings()
#--> 3
? @@( o1.FindEmptyStrings() )
#--> [ 2, 4, 5 ]
o1.ReplaceEmptyStrings(:With = "~")
? @@( o1.Content() )
#--> [ "A", "~", "B", "~", "~", "C" ]
```

> run: kept

Contain, count, find, act. Each line is one step of thought, and the names are the words you would use to say it.

The verb's form says what happens to the object. The past participle gives you a changed copy and leaves the object alone; the plain verb acts on the object; the verb with Q acts and hands the object back, so the next step can follow:

```haro
o1 = new stzString("RIxxNxG")
? o1.Removed("x")
#--> RING
? o1.Content()
#--> RIxxNxG
o1.Remove("x")
? o1.Content()
#--> RING
? Q("rixxnxg").RemoveQ("x").UppercaseQ().Spacified()
#--> R I N G
```

> run: kept (touches files)

A parameter is named, and the name reads as English. You do not need to remember which argument comes second:

```haro
? Q("I love Haro").Replaced("love", :With = "♥")
#--> I ♥ Haro
o2 = new stzList([ "tea", "rice", "fish" ])
o2.SwapItems(:AtPositions = 1, :And = 3)
? @@( o2.Content() )
#--> [ "fish", "rice", "tea" ]
```

> run: kept

When several questions are asked of one thing, they are asked inside its scene, a block of braces around the object, so that the object is named once and the questions read as a list:

```haro
Q("haro php HARO ruby Haro") {
	? NumberOfOccurrence(:Of = "haro")
	#--> 1
	? NumberOfOccurrenceCS(:Of = "haro", :CS = FALSE)
	#--> 3
	? @@( FindAllCS("haro", FALSE) )
	#--> [ 1, 10, 20 ]
}
```

> run: kept

And the steps of thought can be read back. An object lifted with history keeps the result of each step of its chain:

```haro
? @@( Qh(12500).AddQ(500).RetrieveQ(1500).DivideByQ(500).MultiplyByQ(2).History() )
#--> [ 12500, 13000, 11500, 23, 46 ]
```

> run: kept

The article that first told this promised four values, without the first, and a sum one unit short. The guard behind the block states today's rule: the initial value, then each step's result. The book takes the run's answer, not the article's.

Notice the line under every question. It is not a comment. It is a promise, what the line must print, and in this house it has been the form of an example from the first day: a short story, a few questions, and the answers written under them. That is the first half of the law, made visible: the code follows the order of thought, and each step says what it expects.

Now the second half. A rule a person must remember is folklore. The library turns its house rules into validators that run over the graph of the code, so that a person or an agent can check a class before it lands. Here a class breaks the house's convention that a verb ending in Q returns the object:

```haro
cCode = "class Basket" + nl + "def Add(x)" + nl + "	return x" + nl + "def AddQ(x)" + nl + "	return 42" + nl
? StzCodeIsClean(cCode)
#--> FALSE
? StzCheckCode(cCode)[1][:rule]
#--> q-returns-object
```

> run: kept

The rule does not care who wrote the class. That is the whole of the second half. And the promise under each question is read by a machine too: the library's runner judges a promise as a claim about what the line printed, so the house's oldest form of example is also its newest form of test.

The chapters of this part prove the law, each from one side. Chapter 6 tells the mental model and the four metaphors that replace loops: walk, check, yield, perform. Chapter 7 tells the writing style as fourteen rules, and the seven design goals as its architectural half. Chapter 8 tells the forms a function's name can take, and scope named in the verb. Chapter 9 tells patterns of thought: the machine that reacts to what it recognises, reactive programming in plain words, and the engines of truth, quantity and time. Chapter 10 tells how programming becomes refinement.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I let the library's implementation go.** The code the models wrote inside the library is accepted as good. It does not look like mine. Its local variables carry underscores on both sides, hundreds of times per thousand lines, because the language beneath Softanza lets a library's local variable overwrite a caller's global, and the convention prevents it. I did not ask for the old style there, and I would refuse a campaign to impose it.

**I refused to let the style of the code written with Softanza go.** Every line a programmer, a person or an agent, writes with the library must take the historic style: the scene, the chain one step per line, the named parameter, the verb's form, the promise under each question. That is the line I drew when I saw what had happened.

What had happened is measurable. A study of the record counted the promise under each line, per thousand lines. In the short recipes the house keeps, there are nearly a hundred. In my articles, seventy-five. In the classic tests, fifty-two. In the guards the sessions wrote, under one: they assert in another form, a call that names the expectation, which they adopted because the runner of the time could not tell a block that failed from a block that never ran. That gap is closed now; the runner reads the promise as a claim. [AUTHOR: Is the classic form, a title, the questions and the promises under them, now the one form for new test blocks, with the converted blocks left as they are?]

**I refused to rewrite code when the language changed its name.** When the language beneath became Haro, the code in my articles was renamed and nothing else. The forms are the asset; the name is a label.

**The forms of the vocabulary were made data, and three old features were refused as grammar.** In Haro, the forms a verb can take are declared once and generated, and the library's shipped names are judged against the declaration. The design of that work refuses three things the 2022 deck counted as features: names that tolerate a misspelling, parameters in any order, and method names in other human languages. They stay possible in the library; they are not part of the grammar a machine is held to. [AUTHOR: Is that refusal yours, and may the book present it as a decision of this movement?]

**I chose goals before laws.** The 2022 design held seven goals, expressiveness, flexibility, reliability, consistency, human metaphors, practical abstractions and manageability, and every feature served one. The operating rules of today are laws paid for by incidents, which say how and not why. Chapter 7 puts the goals back beside the laws.

## 5. The court

`coderule_narrated` proves that a house rule is a rule over the code graph, that the findings come out on planted code, that clean code passes, and that the rules are fourteen. `testoor_promises_narrated` proves that the promise is judged by a machine in the house's own form, each rule planted both ways: the lie it must catch, and the truth it must not accuse. `05_containsemptystrings` is the mental model's steps on the same list, a classic block kept as a guard. `466_keeping_the_history_of_updates_of_a_softanza_objec` proves the history of a chain.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in enforcement. Every mainstream language has a formatter and a linter that run on every commit by default. The court that would judge the style of a program written with Softanza is a specification; the one that exists judges the library's own classes.

The field leads in reach and in people. There is a labour market for its tools and none yet for this one, and the field's generators are the largest that exist.

The field also leads, today, over Softanza's own pages. The study that measured the record found that the site's code blocks carry no named parameter and no scene: a reduced dialect of the library, shown to every visitor and every agent that learns from the site. A style that is not shown is not learned.

Where I think Softanza stands apart is in what the style is tied to. A formatter judges the shape of a line; a fluent interface makes a line pleasant. Here the line is tied to a step of thought, the verb's form is a contract about the object, and the promise under the line is the example, the test and the documentation at once.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the forms of the vocabulary, in thousands of method names; the house rules as a court over the library's own code; the promise judged by a machine.

What is designed: a court of style for the code a programmer or an agent writes with the library, which would read a positional argument where a named one exists, a question with no promise, a chain on one line past two steps; and the writing grammar declared in Haro, so that a model's output is held to it before it is written.

What is absent: any machine that judges, today, the style of a program written with the library. The founding slide's chain does not run as written. And the discipline's own name is not settled. [AUTHOR: Which name does the discipline carry in this book: "Programming by heart", "What you think is what you write", or "The Softanza way"?]

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Your lines follow your reasoning: select, contain, count, find, act. Each question carries its promise, and a court reads it whether you wrote it or an agent did.

**The analyst.** The method starts from your problem in plain words. The keywords you use become the names of the steps.

**The designer.** The verb's form is an interface contract: the past participle never touches the object.

**The decision-maker.** A style a machine can be held to can be reviewed goal by goal, not line by line, which is the only reading one person can give to hundreds of thousands of lines.

**The educator.** The course teaches the discipline whole, and in order: formulate, then express, then be judged.

**The agent.** You will be corrected by the library, not by folklore. Write the question, then the promise under it.

## 9. Exercise

Take the list `[ "tea", "", "rice", "", "fish", "" ]`. Write the questions of the mental model in order, one line each: does it contain empty strings, how many, where. Then one act, with a named parameter. Under every question, write the line it must print, before you run anything.

Run it. Then ask an agent to solve the same problem, and judge its answer with two questions only: does each line follow a step of thought, and does each question carry its promise.
