# The mental model, and the metaphors

Source: Chapter 6. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the one-dimensional walker, the checker, the yielder and the performer on lists and strings; the condition language compiled by the engine, with its host-code twin; designed: the four metaphors as native engine modules (only the yielder is one); a walker over any indexed structure; absent: runnable code for the three models of the founding article; the edge table of the walker narration as written

Run: 7 of 7 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:24 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

The field has one tool for repeating a thing, the loop, and a family of cousins that hide it: the iterator, the comprehension, the stream with its map, filter and reduce, the lambda that carries a condition. They are good tools, and they all share one habit. They are written in the language of the machine's motion: an index that starts at zero, a counter that is incremented, a variable that accumulates. A person who reads the loop has to recover the question it asks.

The field also has a vocabulary of metaphors for programming, but it keeps them in the documentation. The code stays a loop.

## 2. The first principle

**Say the problem in plain words, ask the same questions in the same order of any object, and when you would write a loop, name the move you are making: walk, check, yield or perform.** [AUTHOR: The site's Craft page says "a loop hides which move it is making; Softanza names the move", in a session's words of 2 October 2026. May the book give you that sentence as your principle for the metaphors?]

## 3. The Softanza form

The mental model is seven steps, and they are the order in which a person asks. Define the problem in natural terms and take its keywords. Select the object that holds the data. Ask whether it contains what you care about. Ask how many. Ask where. Act on those positions, or call the verb that does it. And celebrate, and repeat. In chapter 5 you ran those steps on a list with empty strings. They do not belong to the list. Here they are on a string, in the same order:

```haro
o1 = new stzString("You and you")
? o1.ContainsCS("YOU", FALSE)
#--> TRUE
? o1.NumberOfOccurrenceCS("YOU", FALSE)
#--> 2
? o1.FindNthCS(2, "YOU", FALSE)
#--> 9
o1.ReplaceSection(9, 11, :With = "Me")
? o1.Content()
#--> You and Me
```

> run: kept

Contain, count, find, act, and the same suffix that says whether case matters. The three questions you ask of a string are the three you asked of a list.

When the steps repeat, the loop appears, and this is where the library gives you four metaphors instead. Each one names a move.

**The Walker moves along a route.** A walker is a map of its whole journey, built when it is created: the positions it will visit, the positions it will skip, where it stands, what remains.

```haro
oWalker = Wk(1, 10, 2)
? @@( oWalker.Walkables() )
#--> [ 1, 3, 5, 7, 9 ]
? @@( oWalker.Unwalkables() )
#--> [ 2, 4, 6, 8, 10 ]
? @@( oWalker.Walk() )
#--> [ 1, 3 ]
? oWalker.CurrentPosition()
#--> 3
? @@( oWalker.RemainingWalkables() )
#--> [ 5, 7, 9 ]
```

> run: kept

A loop cannot tell you which positions it will skip until it has skipped them. A walker can, before it moves. And it has a rule for the edge of the road. Beyond the end, it stops in silence. Before the start, it refuses:

```haro
oWalker = Wk(1, 10, 3)
? @@( oWalker.WalkNSteps(10) )
#--> [ 1, 4, 7, 10 ]
? @@( oWalker.Walk() )
#--> [ 10 ]
oWalker = Wk(1, 10, 3)
try
	oWalker.WalkBackward()
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Can't walk backward! Already at the beginning of walkable positions.
```

> run: kept

Ask for ten steps from a walk that has four and you get the four. The article argues that this mirrors how we walk in the physical world: a path may end, and a step behind the first is not a step.

**The Checker asks yes or no.** All of them, any of them, or where:

```haro
o1 = new stzList([ "A", 5, "B", "C" ])
? o1.CheckW('{ isString(@item) and IsUppercase(@item) }')
#--> FALSE
? o1.ContainsW('{ isNumber(@item) }')
#--> TRUE
? @@( o1.FindW('{ isNumber(@item) }') )
#--> [ 2 ]
```

> run: kept

**The Yielder produces new data from old, and leaves the old alone.** **The Performer is its opposite number: it changes the items where the condition holds.** Put side by side on the same list, the contrast is the point:

```haro
o1 = new stzYielder([ 1, -2, 3, -4, 5 ])
? @@( o1.Filter(:IsPositive) )
#--> [ 1, 3, 5 ]
? @@( o1.Map(:Abs) )
#--> [ 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 ]
? o1.Reduce(:Sum)
#--> 3
o2 = new stzList([ 1, "haro", 3, "python", 5, "ruby" ])
? @@( o2.YieldW('isString(@item)', 'upper(@item)') )
#--> [ "HARO", "PYTHON", "RUBY" ]
? @@( o2.Content() )
#--> [ 1, "haro", 3, "python", 5, "ruby" ]
o2.PerformW('isString(@item)', 'upper(@item)')
? @@( o2.Content() )
#--> [ 1, "HARO", 3, "PYTHON", 5, "RUBY" ]
```

> run: kept

Look at the third line from the end and the last one. The yield returned the uppercase words and the list was untouched. The perform changed the list. The verb tells you which, so a design can say "this step only reads" and mean it.

Now the thing that all four share, which is the last piece of the title: a condition travels inside an argument. It is a small string, in the same seat of find, of items, of contains, kept in a variable if you want; and it has a twin that takes a real function:

```haro
o1 = new stzList([ 3, 8, 5, 12, 1, 9 ])
cCond = '{ @item > 5 }'
? @@( o1.FindW(cCond) )
#--> [ 2, 4, 6 ]
? @@( o1.ItemsW(cCond) )
#--> [ 8, 12, 9 ]
? o1.ContainsW(cCond)
#--> TRUE
? @@( o1.FindWF( func x { return x > 5 } ) )
#--> [ 2, 4, 6 ]
```

> run: kept

The string form is the W condition, W for where; the function form is WF. They look alike and differ in a way that matters. The engine reads a W condition as data, in a small language of its own: literals, `@item`, arithmetic, comparisons, and a short list of functions. It cannot call your code. So a condition that tries to is not run:

```haro
o1 = new stzList([ 3, 8, 5, 12, 1, 9 ])
? @@( o1.FindW('{ @item > 5 and print("hacked") }') )
#--> [ ]
? @@( W('{ @item > 5 }') )
#--> [ "where", "{ @item > 5 }" ]
```

> run: kept

Nothing was printed. A W condition can be stored, shown to a person, and approved before it runs, because it cannot escape. The WF twin runs host code, and the library's own table says it is not safe with input you do not trust.

The three models of the founding article, the semantic, the mental and the expression, are one idea in three places. The semantic model is the world of the program as knowledge: an apple is a fruit. The mental model is what you just did: create an object, store data in it, find things inside it, act on the positions. The expression model is the code that says it: select the object, type the name of the action, continue the flow of actions, convert the result into another object, and keep doing so until you have the final answer. I tell them in words because the article's code does not run at this commit, as section 7 says.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I made the metaphors a design goal.** The 2022 presentation lists "human-centric metaphors" as one of seven design goals, and its four features are these four. A goal is what makes a feature justifiable or refusable, so I did not leave the metaphors as a nicety of the documentation. They are one of the reasons the library has the shape it has.

**I replaced `eval` with a language the engine owns.** The condition seat used to be compiled by evaluating the string as code, with a faster extended form on the side for speed. I removed both. The engine now reads the condition in its own small language, and the library keeps the host-code form as a separate verb, WF, with its warning. I refused arbitrary code from text, and I refused to split speed from expressiveness: the extended form is retired because the plain one does everything it did.

**I kept the seven steps as written.** When the course was designed, a reading of the technical core found five steps: select, contains, count, find, apply. The course teaches the seven as the source narration has them. Nobody renumbers the source.

**I let the edge rule be a design principle.** Before the start is an error, and beyond the end is ignored. The narration gives its reason in a sentence: it mirrors how we walk. The record does not date it as a ruling, so I tell it as the library's design.

## 5. The court

The four metaphors have no single guard. The yielder has a narrated one, `00_yielder_narrated`: four map operations, three filter operations and a sum. The walker has `597_walker_basics`, named walkers and what they walked, and `495_walking_until_and_until_before`, a walk that stops on a condition, including the item that stops it, or not. The checker has `611_checkw`, which says a check is false when one item fails. The performer has `621_perform`, which says it changes the list in place. `483_w_and_wf_forms_in_conditional_code` proves that W with cursor words and with raw index arithmetic give the same positions.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in laziness. A generator produces the next item on demand and can be endless. The walker builds a map of its whole journey the moment it is made. That is a win for foresight, you can ask which positions it will skip before it moves, and a loss for a very large or endless traversal.

The field leads in openness. A lambda carries any logic. The W language is bounded on purpose, and the price of the bound is the second path, WF, which has full host power and no safety against untrusted input. There is also a quieter cost I measured once, on one case: a mistyped condition and a condition that matches nothing look the same. Both give an empty list and no error.

The field leads in reach and in tooling. Iterators and comprehensions are in every mainstream language, with editor support, type checking and a literature. These four metaphors exist in one library.

And I will not print the library's own comparison of walkers with loops and iterators. Its table has thirteen rows, all of them in the walker's favour, none of them run. A table with no loss on it is the shape of a brochure.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the one-dimensional walker, in the max layer, and the walks on lists; the checker as `CheckW`, `ContainsW` and `FindW`; the yielder as a class backed by the engine, and as `YieldW`; the performer as `PerformW`; and the W condition language compiled by the engine.

What is designed: the metaphors as native engine modules. The engine's design marks the walker, the checker and the performer as planned; only the yielder has a file there. And a walker that is not about lists at all but about any indexed structure.

What is absent or broken at this commit, from my runs:

- The walking-at-the-edge article's table does not hold. A single step larger than the range raises where the table promises silence, and a mixed-direction path raises where it promises acceptance. The two principles hold in the cases shown above; the table is a draft. [AUTHOR: Is the principle the law, and the table a draft, so the book may state the principle and print only the cases that run?]
- The "Beyond Loops" article's code differs in places. The history of a walker is a list of walks; a backward walk takes no argument now; the direction prints as "backward" where the article says "reverse". The site's own run of the article kept ten of eighteen blocks.
- The founding article's three models: none of its code runs, twelve blocks raised in the site's run. So I told them in words.
- The mental-model article's dedicated verb for empty strings does not exist; the verb that does is `RemoveEmptyStrings`.
- The dedicated checker class raises when asked `ContainsW`, and the string walker lacks the backward-with-condition walk.
- And "no eval" has not reached everything. The near-natural resolver, the other direction of chapter 13, still evaluates host code.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Ask the same four questions of any object, and when you reach for a loop, name the move: walk, check, yield or perform.

**The analyst.** Say the problem in plain words, and the verbs you used are the steps: "are all of them", "which ones", "give me the big ones", "make them negative".

**The designer.** The yielder never changes the list and the performer does, and the verb tells you which.

**The decision-maker.** A W condition is a string the engine reads and cannot run as code, so it can be stored, shown and approved. The price is a small language and a mistake that stays quiet.

**The educator.** The self-study book already has the order: find, then apply, then walk, ask, produce and act.

**The agent.** Write the condition as the string, not as code. When the answer is empty, check the condition's words before you trust the emptiness.

## 9. Exercise

Take the list `[ 4, 9, 12, 7, 20, 3 ]`. Four sentences are given. One: are all of them above 2? Two: give me, as a new list, those above 8, doubled. Three: make the ones above 8 negative, in the list itself. Four: which positions are 2, 4 and 6?

For each, say first which move it is, walk, check, yield or perform, and write under the line you will write what you expect it to print. Then write the line, with the condition as a string wherever there is a condition, and run it. After the second and the third, print the list again. Say, before you look, in which of the two the list has changed.

# The writing style, and the seven goals

Source: Chapter 7. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the library's fourteen house rules as a court over its own code; the promise judged as a claim; the small languages of ranges, lists and conditions in strings; the code seat; the viz prefix; alternatives and named parameters in any order, in part; designed: a court that judges the style of a program written with the library; a court that reads the goals; the writing grammar declared in Haro; the classic form of a test block as the one form; absent: any machine that judges the style of the code you write today; the writing in any human language of a function's name; the near-natural sentence as the deck wrote it; the eval-based code on a parameter's name

Run: 10 of 10 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:24 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

The word *style* means two things in the field, and I have to say which one I mean.

The first is style as layout, enforced by a tool. Python's style guide dates from 2001, Go's formatter from 2009, and every project runs a linter before a commit. They judge the shape of a line: spaces, capitals, length, unused names. They are very good at it, and they run by default. They say nothing about whether a line follows the order in which its writer reasoned.

The second is the small language inside the language. Programmers already write small languages in strings, and nobody calls that a style: a regular expression, a SQL text, a format string such as `"%5.2f"`. Each is a separate notation, with its own tool, learned apart.

For goals, the field says *quality attributes*. The ISO standard for software quality, of 2011, lists eight of them, and architecture methods rank them before a system is drawn. (General knowledge, not a study of the record.) The seven goals of this chapter are a different list, chosen for a different purpose. They do not say what a product promises its users. They say what a feature must serve to be admitted into one library.

## 2. The first principle

**What you think is what you write.** It is chapter 5's sentence, from the diagram of 2020, and I do not argue it again. Chapter 5 took five rules of the style. This chapter takes the rest, as a grammar, and then the goals under which the grammar was designed.

For the goals, the point is the author's, as the study reports it, and not a dated quotation: a software architecture that is truly goal-oriented is the only way one architect masters a project of this size.

## 3. The Softanza form

The study of the record wrote the style down as fourteen rules, in its ruling 12.16, from reading the code. They are readings of the code, stated as rules, and not a standard anyone signed. Here they are with the state each is in at the commit this book runs. I put the table first because it shows which rules carry a block and which carry a limit.

| # | Rule | State at this commit |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | A value is lifted to its object by `Q()` or a typed lifter | runs; chapter 5 |
| 2 | A scene is a brace block on one object | runs; chapter 5 |
| 3 | Every printed line carries its answer under `#-->` | judged by a machine; chapter 5 |
| 4 | A parameter is named, and the name reads as English | runs; chapter 5, and here in any order |
| 5 | A chain mirrors the order of thought, one step per line | runs; chapter 5 |
| 6 | The verb's form says what happens | runs; chapters 5 and 8 |
| 7 | Suffixes and prefixes are small and mean one thing each | in part: `CS`, `XT`, `viz` run; the deck's `FF`, `dft` and `inf` families raise |
| 8 | A function has alternatives for the way a person says it | in part |
| 9 | Precision over generality, when it costs nothing | in part |
| 10 | Code travels inside an argument as a small string | the seat runs; the `@` on a parameter's name is retired |
| 11 | Small literal languages live in strings | runs in one form; the braced form raises |
| 12 | A sentence can be near-natural | not this chapter's; none of the deck's three forms ran as written |
| 13 | A function may be named in any human language | does not run |
| 14 | A comment speaks before the code, in the teacher's voice | runs |

Rule 11 first, because it is the strangest. The host language counts in integers and cuts the decimals. Softanza reads a small range language inside a string, and keeps them. It counts numbered words and letters of any script. The comments below speak before the code, in the teacher's voice, which is rule 14:

```haro
# The host's own range counts in integers: the decimals are cut. Softanza reads
# a small language inside a string, and keeps the decimals, the numbered words,
# and the letters of any script:
? @@( 1.02 : 3.08 )
#--> [ 1, 2, 3 ]
? @@( L("1.02:1.05") )
#--> [ 1.02, 1.03, 1.04, 1.05 ]
? @@( L('"♥1" : "♥3"') )
#--> [ "♥1", "♥2", "♥3" ]
? @@( L(' "ا" : "ج" ') )
#--> [ "ا", "ب", "ة", "ت", "ث", "ج" ]
```

> run: kept

The range language is an argument like any other. It gives the replacements for each occurrence, in order, and the extended form wraps the palette when the occurrences outnumber it:

```haro
o1 = new stzString("haro php ruby haro python haro")
o1.ReplaceByMany("haro", L('"♥1" : "♥3"'))
? o1.Content()
#--> ♥1 php ruby ♥2 python ♥3
o2 = new stzString("haro php ruby haro python haro ruby haro")
o2.ReplaceByManyXT("haro", L('"♥1" : "♥3"'))
? o2.Content()
#--> ♥1 php ruby ♥2 python ♥3 ruby ♥1
```

> run: kept

A list can also be carried as a string in its short form, recognised, converted between forms and evaluated back:

```haro
? StzStringQ('1:3').IsListInString()
#--> TRUE
? @@( StzStringQ('[1,2,3]').ToListInShortForm() )
#--> "1 : 3"
? @@( StzStringQ(' "A":"C" ').ToList() )
#--> [ "A", "B", "C" ]
```

> run: kept

Rule 10: code travels inside an argument as a small string with a placeholder, `@char` for the characters of a string and `@item` for the items of a list. Chapter 6 showed the seat on a list. Here it is on a string, where it finds and removes by a rule and not by a loop:

```haro
o1 = new stzString("SOooooFTAaaannnNZA")
? @@( o1.FindW('{ Q(@char).IsUppercase() }') )
#--> [ 1, 2, 7, 8, 9, 16, 17, 18 ]
o1.RemoveW('{ Q(@char).IsLowercase() }')
? o1.Content()
#--> SOFTANZA
```

> run: kept

The deck of 2022 writes the same call with a named seat, `:Where = '...'`, and that form raises on a string in this commit. I do not show it. The same deck puts an `@` at the end of a parameter's name, `:With@`, to mark that its value is code to be evaluated. That form is retired. A guard's header says so: the eval-based forms are retired. The deck itself marks the form "TODO". Code now travels in the seat as a bounded string that the engine evaluates, or as a function.

Rule 8: the same act may be said in two orders of words, with the container's name, or with the side spelled four ways. Each pair is one method. The library's own narration calls the first with two arguments and it raises; the guards pass the starting position, and so does the block:

```haro
o1 = new stzList([ 1, 2, "♥", 4, 5, "♥", "♥" ])
? o1.FindNthNext(2, "♥", :StartingAt = 1)
#--> 6
? o1.FindNextNth(2, "♥", :StartingAt = 1)
#--> 6
? Q("---haro").NumberOfOccurrenceOfCharLeftSide("-")
#--> 3
? Q("---haro").HowManyOccurrenceOfCharStartSide("-")
#--> 3
o2 = new stzList([ "C", "B", "A" ])
o2.SwapItems(:AtPositions = 1, :And = 3)
? @@( o2.Content() )
#--> [ "A", "B", "C" ]
```

> run: kept

The deck promised more. A family of five names for removing characters at the two ends, `LeadingAndTrailingCharsRemoved` among them, is not defined. Only `LeadingCharsRemoved`, `TrailingCharsRemoved` and `RemoveLeadingAndTrailingChars` are.

Rule 9: precision over generality. The precise name says what is counted. A string's characters are counted by `NumberOfChars`, and `Size()` is the host's byte length, so the two answers differ on an Arabic word:

```haro
? Q("سلام").NumberOfChars()
#--> 4
? Q("سلام").NumberOfBytes()
#--> 8
? Q("سلام").Size()
#--> 8
o1 = new stzList([ "tea", "rice", "fish", "salt" ])
? o1.ContainsBoth("tea", "fish")
#--> TRUE
? o1.ContainsAll([ "tea", "oil" ])
#--> FALSE
```

> run: kept

The library's own documentation line for `NumberOfChars` lists *size* among its alternative words. The run shows that `Size()` does not mean it. I list it among the requests.

Rule 7: a prefix `viz` shows the answer under the word, and the suffix `XT` adds the positions as numbers:

```haro
o1 = new stzString("fjharoljharodjharog")
? o1.vizFind("haro")
#--> fjharoljharodjharog
#--> --^-----^-----^----
? o1.vizFindXT("haro", [ :Numbered = TRUE ])
#--> fjharoljharodjharog
#--> --^-----^-----^----
#-->   3     9     15
```

> run: kept

The narration and the deck write `vizFindAll`. It does not exist at this commit, and `vizFind` does.

### The goals

Under the grammar there are seven goals, written in the 2022 deck as a matrix of thirty-three features. The study's ruling 12.14 reads them as the library's architectural skeleton: a feature that serves none is refused, and a goal with no feature is a gap. The study's headline is a session's, and no document in the library states the rule in these words. The nearest is the engine's design document: the engine must enable all seven.

I will not list the seven as a table of slogans. Here is what runs of three of them.

**Flexibility: the order of named parameters is free.** Two calls, with the parameters in two orders, are the same call:

```haro
o1 = new stzString("----@@--@@-------@@----@@---")
o1.ReplaceNextNthOccurrence(2, :Of = "@@", :StartingAt = 12, :With = "##")
? o1.Content()
#--> ----@@--@@-------@@----##---
o2 = new stzString("----@@--@@-------@@----@@---")
o2.ReplaceNextNthOccurrence(2, :With = "##", :StartingAt = 12, :Of = "@@")
? o2.Content()
#--> ----@@--@@-------@@----##---
```

> run: kept

A tension must be told with it. The design of Haro refuses free-order parameters as grammar, and the library ships them. Whether they are "kept in the library and left out of the grammar a machine is held to", or retired, is the author's. [AUTHOR: The deck counted free-order parameters, alternatives under any wording and names in other languages as features. The Haro principle refuses them as grammar, and the library still ships the first two. Are they features kept in the library and left out of the grammar a machine is held to, or retired?]

**Consistency: one vocabulary across a string and a list.** The same verb answers on both:

```haro
? @@( StzStringQ("hello").FindAllCS("L", :CaseSensitive = 0) )
#--> [ 3, 4 ]
? @@( StzListOfStringsQ([ "h", "e", "l", "l", "o" ]).FindAllCS("L", :CaseSensitive = 0) )
#--> [ 3, 4 ]
? Q("").IsEmpty()
#--> TRUE
? Q([]).IsEmpty()
#--> TRUE
```

> run: kept

The loss beside it, from the same slide group: the deck's "item for any enumerable" does not hold. `NumberOfItems()` on a string raises, and on a list it answers.

**Manageability: a failure that says where, what, why, and what to do.** The library raises an error in four fields. I do not print its message, because its first line carries a line number of the library, and a line number is not a promise.

The other four goals (reliability, expressiveness, universality and the one the deck calls extensibility) I leave to the tables of the study. I have not run them for this chapter, and I will not describe from the deck what I have not run.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused a style that was changed when AI became the main writing tool.** The study reports the author's disappointment in these terms, and chapter 5 tells it. The implementation code that the AI wrote is accepted as good. What is demanded is that every line a programmer, human or agent, is asked to write with Softanza embraces the historic style.

**I refused a goal that lives only in a document.** The seven goals are written in one design document and in tags that no court reads. The study's finding is that there is no court over either.

**I refused to hide the cost of the style.** The study measured markers per thousand lines. The narrated guards, the test files written to be read as articles, carry the promise `#-->` in under one line per thousand, and a call to the assertion helper in eighty-five. The older numbered tests carry the promise in fifty-two. The library's code carries the promise in less than two. The style survived where the author wrote for a reader, and thinned where the AI wrote for others. A marker is a reading of the text, not a judgement of the code; the study says so itself.

**I refused to recount the tags as mine.** The goals are marked in the code by info-tags, in comments that begin with `#<` and an `@`, applied by hand. The study counted about six thousand. I counted again on 8 October 2026, with the command in the research file, and found 6,126, with 89 spellings. But 1,360 of them sit outside the archive folders, and 4,766 sit in the archived monolithic files. So the sentence "applied by hand at six thousand points" is, at this commit, mostly a sentence about the archive. No guard reads them: the reflection layer reads a method's name, with a grammar, and not its tag.

**I refused the tally as a count of passes.** The study counts thirty-three features: twenty-four built, four transformed with the project, two that changed form, one partial, one archived, one a name only. The arithmetic holds. But it is a count of names found, and a definition found is not a passing test. Several of the features have names in the code and raise when called: the families `FF` and `FX` for sections, the families `dft` and `inf` for defaults and infinities, `WalkFF` and `AddWalkerFF`, the `Check` on a list, and the deck's `Yield` and `Perform` forms. I print the tally, and I print its losses with it. [AUTHOR: The deck says seven goals and thirty-three features and "7 root causes of programming complexity". No slide lists the seven causes. Do you want the chapter to name them, one per goal, and which are they?]

## 5. The court

`coderule_narrated` proves that a house rule is a rule over the code graph; that planted code is found by name; that clean code passes; and that the library lists fourteen. `testoor_promises_narrated` proves that the promise `#-->` is judged as a claim of a stop: kept, diverged, unreached or unjudged. `602_l_function` and `59_use_of_the_l_small_function` prove the range language, in Arabic too. `527_identifying_lists_inside_a_string` proves the lists hosted in strings. `48_replacebymany` proves that the palette cycles. `483_w_and_wf_forms_in_conditional_code` proves the condition in a string and in a function. `28_numberofoccurrenceofcharleftside` proves the alternative names in Arabic as well as Latin, and carries a note that the archive's promise was wrong and the implementation is the authority. `646_content` proves the named parameters. `972_narration_viz-finding_a_recurring_substring` proves the viz tour.

Here is the house court itself, the fourteen rules of the library and not the fourteen of the style. The numerical coincidence is a coincidence. A planted misspelling is found by name and the code stays clean, because it is a warning. A planted write to a constant is found by name, and the code is refused, because it is an error:

```haro
? @@( StzCodeRuleNames() )
#--> [ "no-len-method", "q-returns-object", "no-aggressive-verbs", "engine-first", "q-has-plain-twin", "no-case-collision", "dead-knob", "setter-resets-on-reject", "setter-only-moves-one-way", "misspelled-name", "library-prints", "empty-method-body", "writes-a-mutable-constant", "value-called-as-function" ]
cOne = "class Inbox" + nl + "def Recieve(x)" + nl + "	return x" + nl
? StzCheckCode(cOne)[1][:rule]
#--> misspelled_name
? StzCodeIsClean(cOne)
#--> TRUE
cTwo = "class Inbox" + nl + "def Count()" + nl + "	nl = 3" + nl + "	return nl" + nl
? StzCheckCode(cTwo)[1][:rule]
#--> writes_a_mutable_constant
? StzCodeIsClean(cTwo)
#--> FALSE
```

> run: kept

A detail of the spelling, for Appendix B: the names list uses hyphens, and the finding of eight of the fourteen comes back with underscores. The block shows what the run printed.

What the court does not read is the code of a program written with the library. None of the fourteen rules of the house is a rule of writing.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in enforcement by default. A formatter runs on every commit. For the code a programmer writes with Softanza, the enforcement today is none, and chapter 5 says so.

The field leads in having its goals somewhere a reader looks first. The seven goals are in one design document and in tags that no court reads, and the operating notes of the library's own repository name none of them. An AI reader's account of the library says that sixty paradigm documents could easily be sixty disconnected ideas. The study reads it as a sign that the discipline is in the code and written nowhere a reader would look first. It is a session's document, and not an independent witness.

The site once showed a reduced dialect: its blocks had no named parameter and no scene. It has since put the fourteen rules on its Craft page and the rules as data. I did not re-measure the site's blocks after that.

Where I think the style stands apart is that every rule serves a step of thought, and every step has a written form (study 12.17). The verb's form is a contract on the object. And the promise under the line is the example, the test and the documentation at once. The 2022 matrix served as a skeleton for one architect over about three hundred thousand hand-written lines.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the fourteen house rules of the library as a court over its own code, run in the block above; the promise judged by a machine; the small languages, the seat, the prefix. And the features that run (`CS`, `XT`, `ZZ`, `viz`, named parameters, the same verb on a string and a list).

What is designed:

- **A court that judges the style of a program written with the library.** It is a specification in the study: the fourteen rules as house rules over a snippet's graph, such as a positional argument where a named one exists, or a printed line with no promise. I searched the library for it and found nothing.
- **A court that reads the goals.** It would harvest the tags. Not built.
- **The classic form of the test block as the one form for new blocks.** A decision that awaits the author, and chapter 5 asks it. [AUTHOR: Is the classic block, with its narrative header, its questions with `#-->` and its closing time, now the one form for new test blocks, with the converted blocks left as they are?]
- **The writing grammar declared in Haro.** The vocabulary half exists: the forms of the library's names are data in Haro, and the session's record is 584 of 595 shipped spellings agreeing. The usage half, the scene, the chain, the code seat and the promise, is not declared.
- **The discipline declared once.** Partly done on the site, as data, which is the page's source and not a court.

What is absent:

- Any machine that judges, today, the style of a program written with the library.
- Rules 12 and 13 as running forms. The deck's near-natural samples did not run as written, and neither did a function named in Chinese or in French.
- The `@` on a parameter's name as a working form.
- The braced form of the range language, `L('{ "♥1" : "♥6" }')`, which raises, and ranges that change width, such as `day8 : day11`, which also raise, with an uninitialised-variable error from the host. The list constructor from a string raises too.
- The visualised call stack, and the cache and log decorators, which exist as names in comments.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Fourteen rules you can hold your own line against. Lift the value, ask in a scene, put the answer under `#-->`, name the parameter, chain one step per line, read the verb's form; and put a range, a condition or a palette in a string.

**The analyst.** The words of your problem become the names of the steps. A rule written in a string is a rule you can store and read, and not code you must trust.

**The designer.** The viz prefix draws the answer where the eye expects it. The goals are the headings under which a design is judged, one feature at a time.

**The decision-maker.** Seven goals and thirty-three features are a reading grid. A feature that serves none is refused, and a goal with no feature is a gap. The tally is printed with its losses.

**The educator.** The discipline is taught whole and in order. The converted guard for lists in strings keeps the old promise's wrong spacing in a comment, which is a lesson on the cost of hand-typed answers.

**The agent.** The rules are given to you as data on the site. For the code you write, the court is still a specification. Write the question, then the promise under it, and put your condition in the seat, not in an evaluation of a string.

## 9. Exercise

Write, before you run anything, the line each task needs and the promise under it:

1. From one string, the list `[ "♥7", "♥8", "♥9" ]`.
2. The string `"haro php haro"` with the two `haro` numbered `♥1` and `♥2`, in one call that uses the small language of task 1.
3. The list `[ "C", "B", "A" ]` ordered by swapping the first and the third items, with the positions named.
4. The count of characters of `"سلام"`, with the name that says what is counted.
5. A guess: what does `L("day8 : day11")` print?

```haro exercise
# 1.
# 2.
# 3.
# 4.
# 5.
```

> run: exercise

Run it. For the fifth, you were allowed to be wrong, and you were probably wrong: the range changes width, so the host reads `day8` as a variable, and it raises. That is the limit of the small language, learned by being surprised once. Do not change your promise to match the output. Explain the output first.


=====

# Functions as linguistic expressions, and scope

Source: Chapter 8. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: fourteen of the twenty-two forms as the article shows them, and three more in another shape; the library reading a name as a sentence; scope in the regex verbs, in the system and in the placement of a deployment; designed: the forms declared once as data and generated, in Haro; the scope paradigm for time, units, memory and style; absent: five forms: partial, future, free, default and multilingual; concurrency as a scope: a plan with no code

Run: 13 of 13 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:24 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

In the field, a function's name is a label. It follows a convention, camel case or snake case, and it tells you roughly what the function does; the details are in the signature and the documentation. A name does not carry grammar. The related tools are all about structure: the keyword argument that Python and Swift offer so that a call can say which parameter is which, the fluent interface that chains calls so a line reads like a sentence, the overload that gives one name several signatures.

And when the field wants a function to behave differently in a different context, it changes its meaning by a flag set somewhere else: a mode, a locale, an environment variable. The function's name does not tell you which frame it will run in.

## 2. The first principle

**A function's name is a sentence, and its prefixes, suffixes and parameters are a grammar that the library can read.** The paper that first made the claim says it was achieved without any model or any parser of natural language, only by naming conventions that encode meaning, parameter order that mirrors speech, flexible forms and intelligent defaults.

## 3. The Softanza form

Look at a name the way the library looks at it. `FindNextSTCS`: find next, from a start position, with a case flag. `RemoveManyCS`: remove, many things, a case flag. Each suffix is small and means one thing, and the order of the suffixes is the order of the parameters. Here the first name finds, and the second removes every occurrence of three letters, whatever their case:

```haro
o1 = new stzString("123456789HARO")
? o1.FindNextSTCS("haro", 5, FALSE)
#--> 10
o2 = new stzString("SOOooooFFfffTANNnnnZA")
o2.RemoveManyCS([ "o", "f", "n" ], FALSE)
? o2.Content()
#--> STAZA
```

> run: kept

The article promised `SOFTANZA` on the second line. The code is right and the article is wrong: a removal that does not care about case removes the capitals too. I print the run.

The library does not only follow this grammar. It reads it. Give it a name, and it parses it into the sentence it is, and it can explain a method to you in those terms:

```haro
? @@( _StzParseName("FindNextSTCS") )
#--> [ "find next", [ "case-sensitive", "from-position", "active" ] ]
? @@( _StzParseName("RemoveManyCS") )
#--> [ "remove", [ "case-sensitive", "plural", "active" ] ]
? StzSelfDocQ("stzString").ExplainMethod("RemoveMany")
#--> RemoveMany -- Remove every occurrence of each given substring (mutating).
#-->   (plural form -- applies the operation to many items at once)
#-->   reads as: remove -- over many values at once
```

> run: kept

That is the claim, made mechanical: the grammar is in the code and not only in the article. The parser decodes the prefixes and the suffixes, the words "except" and "is not", and the voice: a name that ends in `ed` is passive.

I will walk the forms now. Chapter 5 showed three of them: the active, the passive and the fluent, and the named parameter. Here are the others that run.

**Z and ZZ** say whether a position comes as a number or as a section. A suffix, and the answer changes shape:

```haro
o1 = new stzString("haro php haro ruby haro")
? @@( o1.FindZ("haro") )
#--> [ 1, 10, 20 ]
? @@( o1.FindZZ("haro") )
#--> [ [ 1, 4 ], [ 10, 13 ], [ 20, 23 ] ]
o2 = new stzString("SOFTANZA")
? @@( o2.FirstHalfZ() )
#--> [ "SOFT", 1 ]
? @@( o2.FirstHalfZZ() )
#--> [ "SOFT", [ 1, 4 ] ]
```

> run: kept

**The prefix viz** changes the kind of answer: a drawing. The position is shown under the text, and an option can number it:

```haro
o1 = new stzString("fjharoljharodjharog")
? o1.vizFind("haro")
#--> fjharoljharodjharog
#--> --^-----^-----^----
? o1.vizFindXT("haro", [ :Numbered = TRUE ])
#--> fjharoljharodjharog
#--> --^-----^-----^----
#-->   3     9     15
```

> run: kept

**The plural** extends a verb to many things without a loop, and **the exceptional form** keeps only what you name. The article's example for the second, `RemoveNonLettersExcept`, does not exist, but the form does, in other names:

```haro
o1 = new stzString("__H~~~AR***O__")
? @@( o1.FindMany([ "~", "*" ]) )
#--> [ 4, 5, 6, 9, 10, 11 ]
o1.RemoveMany([ "_", "~", "*" ])
? o1.Content()
#--> HARO
o2 = new stzString("--Haro--&__Softanza__")
o2.RemoveAllExcept([ "Haro", "&", "Softanza" ])
? o2.Content()
#--> Haro&Softanza
```

> run: kept

**The negative** is a word in the name, not a NOT around the call:

```haro
? Q("*").IsNotLetter()
#--> TRUE
o3 = new stzList([ "R", "I", "N", "G" ])
? o3.IsNotAString()
#--> TRUE
? o3.DoesNotContain("♥")
#--> TRUE
```

> run: kept

**The deep form** answers a path, a list of indices that goes as deep as the data does, and the same family replaces and removes at that depth:

```haro
o1 = new stzList([ "you", "other", [ "other", "you", [ "you" ], "other" ], "other", "you" ])
? @@( o1.DeepFind("you") )
#--> [ [ 1 ], [ 3, 2 ], [ 3, 3, 1 ], [ 5 ] ]
o1.DeepReplace("you", :By = "♥")
? @@( o1.Content() )
#--> [ "♥", "other", [ "other", "♥", [ "♥" ], "other" ], "other", "♥" ]
```

> run: kept

**The immutable form**, QC, runs the whole chain on a copy and leaves the original alone:

```haro
o1 = new stzString("haxxrxo")
? o1.RemoveQC("x").UppercaseQ().Spacified()
#--> H A R O
? o1.Content()
#--> haxxrxo
```

> run: kept

**The misspelled form** is accepted, with a note I owe you. A misspelling of a name works:

```haro
? Q("   Haro ").WithoutSapces()
#--> Haro
```

> run: kept

The article says the library recognises and corrects minor typos, and that the intelligence resides in the library itself. The code shows something plainer: `WithoutSapces` works because that spelling was written by hand as an alias. Another misspelling, `WithoutSpces`, fails. So the form is real and it is a list of declared aliases, not a correction engine.

Now the scope half of the chapter. The same disease shows up everywhere in programming: behaviour with an invisible governing frame. A function does one thing here and another there, and nothing in its name says which frame it is in. The cure, in four moves, is the paradigm I call scope-oriented programming. Name the hidden frame. Turn it into a small, closed set of named scopes. Choose the scope at the call site, in the verb, and not in a flag set earlier. And give the library the scope to reason with, a semantic handle and not a comment.

It was born in regular expressions, where the frame is how much of the text a pattern must cover. The same pattern, the same text, three verbs, three answers:

```haro
cText = "First: Line1" + nl + "Second: Line2" + nl + "Third: Line3"
o1 = new stzRegex("^Second:.*$")
? o1.Match(cText)
#--> FALSE
? o1.MatchFirst(cText)
#--> FALSE
? o1.MatchLine(cText)
#--> TRUE
```

> run: kept

`Match` asks whether the pattern covers the whole value. `MatchFirst` asks whether it occurs anywhere, with the line anchors off. `MatchLine` makes `^` and `$` bind at each line. The frame is in the verb. There is a word scope too, which supplies the word boundaries so that you do not write them:

```haro
o2 = new stzRegex("pre\w+")
? o2.MatchFirst("compress")
#--> TRUE
o3 = new stzRegex("pre\w+")
? o3.MatchWord("compress")
#--> FALSE
```

> run: kept

The second instance is the system. Feature code is written in a named scope, and the same verb gets a different verdict in each: the scope can make a laptop behave like a device it does not have, or refuse what the target forbids. Here, one solution with a backend on a Linux server and a firmware on a small board:

```haro
oSol = new stzPlatformProfile("my-iot-product")
oSol.DevelopedOn(:Windows)
oSol.WithServer(:backend, :LinuxServer)
oSol.WithApp(:firmware, :ESP32)
oBack = oSol.App(:backend).System()
oFirm = oSol.App(:firmware).System()
? oBack.Spawn("worker")
#--> native
? oFirm.ReadPin(4)
#--> rehearsed
try
	oFirm.Spawn("worker")
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> scope 'esp32' (espidf) forbids 'process' -- spawn 'worker'. This target has no such capability.
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

The same verb, `Spawn`, has two verdicts. The scope decides, not the operation. And the laptop has no pin to read, so the scope says the call is rehearsed. This is the fifth move: when a scope's capabilities differ from the host's, the library bridges the difference, in both directions. Two declared systems can be compared as data, with a closed set of capabilities:

```haro
oEsp = DeclareSystem("esp32-firmware")
oEsp.SetOSName(:rtos)
oEsp.SetCapabilityList([ :gpio, :network, :clock, :threads ])
oBox = DeclareSystem("laptop")
oBox.SetOSName(:windows)
oBox.SetCapabilityList([ :filesystem, :process, :network, :clock, :threads ])
? @@( oBox.Forbids(oEsp) )
#--> [ "filesystem", "process" ]
? @@( oBox.Requires(oEsp) )
#--> [ "gpio" ]
```

> run: kept

What the laptop can do that the board cannot, and what the board needs that the laptop lacks. That is the second instance. The third is deployment: the target is the frame, and the scope decides not to ship what the target already does well; chapter 4 showed the plan. The fourth is concurrency, and it is a plan.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I made forms a grammar that the language owns.** In Haro, the forms are declared once and the compiler generates and checks each family: the passive form may not mutate; a verb with Q returns the object; suffix order is parameter order; a W takes a literal parsed at compile time. The first measure of that work is the library's string class: of its shipped spellings of the verbs find, replace and remove, 584 agree with the declaration and 11 do not. I told in chapter 5 what Haro refused as grammar: the misspelled form, the free order of parameters and per-language method names. Here is what it means for the table below: five forms do not run.

**I trimmed the future form on purpose.** On 10 July 2026, I removed the deferred-execution suffixes and their global queue from the natural code, for a reason written in the design: natural language demands building the whole computational model before executing, and the engine's token plan now does that. The idea moved home. It is a plan of the natural engine and no longer a suffix.

**I changed the mechanism of the conditional form.** It used to compile through evaluation of a string. It now goes through the expression language of the engine, and the old extended form is retired.

**For scope, I refused the floating current.** Feature code is never written against an ambient "current system". It names its scope. And for concurrency I refused two things before there was any code: a method per operation that smears the frame across a dozen verbs, such as `DoubleEachConcurrently`, and a mode set earlier, the very antipattern this paradigm was founded against. I also refused a scope that no host can perform. My first guess for concurrency was thread vocabulary, and the language beneath has no threads.

**I refused to ship the whole engine to the edge.** The placement rule is: ship only what is critical and either unique to Softanza or weak on the target. A target that already excels at something gets nothing shipped.

## 5. The court

`00_regex_narrated` proves the scopes of the regex verbs: `Match` anchors, `MatchFirst` searches, Z hands back positions and does not print them. `972_narration_viz-finding_a_recurring_substring` proves the drawn answer. `full_scope_model_narrated` proves the system scope, with its three verdicts. `system_scope_model_narrated` proves the closed set of capabilities; one of its assertions is a timing limit, and it failed under load on one of my runs, which I report and do not hide. `delivery_narrated` proves the placement plan.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in convention. A name that is a label is easier to teach and easier to type; the grammar of this chapter has to be learned, and some of its forms are rarely used.

The field leads in the guarantee. Python's keyword arguments and Swift's labels are checked by the compiler, for every function, from the first day. The forms here are checked in Haro and in the library's own parser, and only in the names that follow them.

On scope, the field leads in maturity. The closest field practice is the explicit context: the Go habit of passing a context to every call, a locale object that carries a frame. They are older and more widely used. What this paradigm adds is that the frame is one of a small, closed set, chosen in the verb, and the library can reason with it.

## 7. The limit

What is built: fourteen of the twenty-two forms of a name run as the article shows them; three more run in another shape; the library reads a name as a sentence; and scope runs in the regex verbs, in the system, and in the placement of a deployment. [AUTHOR: The article has twenty-three sections, the twenty-two forms and "small functions". Do I keep twenty-two as the book's number?]

What is designed: Haro's forms as data, with generated families; and the scope paradigm for time, units, memory and style, as rows of a table.

What is absent, and the page should say each with its failure:

- **Partial**, `@Removed`, is in the archive only: the call is not defined.
- **Future**, `BeforeQ`, was trimmed on purpose, as I said.
- **Free**, the suffix that lets a call give its parameters in any order and any subset: there is no definition of it outside the archive, and the library's own parser knows the name `FX` and not `FF`. The two narrations that tell it use two names, and the code has neither.
- **Default**, `dftRange` and `infRange`: not defined; the library's comment calls the informing form not yet built.
- **Multilingual**, a method named in French or Arabic: not in the live library. The names exist in the archive. Their heirs are the language packs of chapter 11.
- The three that run in another shape. The exceptional form exists, but the article's example does not. Free order works for the first order only: the swapped call ends the program with a status of one and prints nothing. And the statement form is half right: the "All" half works, and the "No" half returns the wrong truth value; the library's own classic test promises true, true, true and the run prints them wrong.

On the scope side: the regex verbs differ from the design table in two places. `MatchLine` lets a dot cross a line, where the table says it stays within one, and `MatchOneSegment`, which the table offers for the lazy case, is not defined. The system scope is built, and the design document still calls it designed: the record contradicts itself. [AUTHOR: Is the scope design document your own text, and do you rule the system scope built?] And concurrency is a plan of record with no code: the word `Concurrently` has no definition.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Read a name as a sentence: its suffixes are its parameters, in order.

**The analyst.** A position can come as a number or as a section; a name says which.

**The designer.** The prefix changes the kind of answer: viz draws it.

**The decision-maker.** A scope is chosen in the verb, so the frame is visible where the call is made, and a review can see it.

**The educator.** The library parses a name into its sentence, so a learner can ask it what a method says.

**The agent.** Write the scope in the verb. A call that names a scope cannot be moved to another frame by a flag set elsewhere.

## 9. Exercise

Take one verb from a library you use that behaves differently in two frames: a mode, a locale, a target. Say in one sentence what the hidden frame is. Then write, as you would like it to be, two names for the same act in the two frames, with the frame in the verb. Before you run anything in Softanza, take `Match` and `MatchLine` on a text of three lines and write what each prints for a pattern that anchors at the start.

Run it. If your two names differ only by a flag, you have not yet moved the frame into the verb.


=====

# Patterns of thought: the Softanzuter, Reaxis and the engines

Source: Chapter 9. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the Regexuter and the Listexuter, reacting with a state that remembers; Reaxis, reactive programming in plain words, on the engine's event loop; the timeline, the grid and deep operations; the agent whose skills cascade to a fixpoint; designed: the Softanzuter proper, many faculties sharing one state and cascading until it settles; the universal engine behind it; its three siblings, genetic, linguistic and quantic; absent: a dependency chain that links one trigger to another; a section-merging verb the article presents as its point

Run: 8 of 8 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:24 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

The field has three tools for making a program react. Regular expressions recognise a shape in text, and nothing more: they say "this matches", and they stop. Reactive programming, the family of observables, subscribers and schedulers that Rx made popular, lets data flow through a program over time; its vocabulary comes from plumbing, telephony and publishing: pipes, backpressure, hot and cold sources. And rule engines, the production systems of the field with their forward chaining, fire rules when facts appear, and keep firing while new facts appear. (General knowledge; the rule engines of the field are older and more mature than anything in this chapter.)

Each tool is separate. A regular expression does not trigger a computation, a reactive stream does not recognise a pattern, and a rule engine does not have a vocabulary that a person would use to say a thought.

## 2. The first principle

The definition I gave this paradigm is a single sentence, and everything below is an attempt to make it mechanical: **a Softanzuter is a computational representation of a thinking machine, based on one or many Regexuters, enabling it to identify and react to patterns of thought.**

## 3. The Softanza form

I think of it as a ladder with four rungs, each adding one thing.

The first rung is the pattern. You have seen it in chapter 11: a shape in a medium, a string, a list, a number, that can say "this matches, and here is how", and do nothing more. The second rung gives a pattern a reaction: the Regexuter, a reflex arc. You bind a pattern to code, process a text, and the code fires on every match. A Regexuter reacts, and it remembers what it did. Here it redacts the secrets in a sentence, and the state it keeps says what it matched, what it computed, where, and which trigger fired:

```haro
rxu() {
	Trigger(:SensitiveData = "(password|secret|key):\s*(\w+)")
	@C(:When = :SensitiveData, :Do = '{ @value = "REDACTED-" + len(@value) }')
	Process("User credentials: password: abc123 secret: def456")
	? @@( Results() )
	#--> [ "REDACTED-16", "REDACTED-14" ]
	? @@( Positions() )
	#--> [ 19, 36 ]
	? State()[1][:matchedvalue]
	#--> password: abc123
	? State()[1][:computedvalue]
	#--> REDACTED-16
	? @@( State()[2][:dependson] )
	#--> [ "sensitivedata" ]
}
```

> run: kept

The state is provenance: every reaction keeps its why. The last line is also an honest limit, and I will come back to it: what the Regexuter records as a dependency is today only its own trigger.

Now the third rung, the one the definition is about: many Regexuters, over different media, sharing one state, so that what one faculty writes is a medium another faculty can match over. A thought is a state entry with its provenance, and a settled thought is a state in which nothing more fires. That rung is not built, and the block shows how I know. The second trigger below is written for numbers of three digits or more. The first trigger turns the 7 into 700, which is one. If the machine thought in cascades, the second would fire on the first's result:

```haro
rxu() {
	Trigger(:Number = "\d+")
	Trigger(:Big = "\d{3,}")
	@C(:When = :Number, :Do = '{ @value = @number(@value) * 100 }')
	@C(:When = :Big, :Do = '{ @value = "BIG" }')
	Process("Found 7 items")
	? @@( Matches() )
	#--> [ "7" ]
	? @@( Results() )
	#--> [ 700 ]
	? @@( Triggers() )
	#--> [ "number" ]
}
```

> run: kept

It did not. Every trigger is matched once against the original text, and what one trigger writes is not offered to the others. That is the whole difference between the second rung and the third.

The cascade runs one rung above, in the agent: a mind made of skills, each with a condition and an action, which chain. The second skill fires on the fact the first one wrote. `Pursue()` returns how many skills fired and goes on until the world stops changing; asked again, it answers zero. That is a fixpoint:

```haro
oAgent = new stzPIAgent("cook")
oAgent.MemoryQ().Learn("order", "state", "placed")
s1 = new stzAgentSkill("cook")
s1.SetWhen(func oM { return oM.Fact("order", "state", "placed") })
s1.SetDoes(func oM { oM.Forget("order","state","placed") oM.Learn("order","state","cooking") return 1 })
s2 = new stzAgentSkill("serve")
s2.SetWhen(func oM { return oM.Fact("order", "state", "cooking") })
s2.SetDoes(func oM { oM.Forget("order","state","cooking") oM.Learn("order","state","served") return 1 })
oAgent.AddSkill(s1)
oAgent.AddSkill(s2)
? oAgent.Pursue()
#--> 2
? oAgent.MemoryQ().Fact("order", "state", "served")
#--> TRUE
? oAgent.Pursue()
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

The guard that proves it labels the scene in its own words: the cascade to fixpoint, the Softanzuter at agent level. In chapter 17 you saw this same agent refused at a gate. Here is the other face of it: the agent that chains, because its mind is programmatic.

So the word has three meanings in the record, and the reader deserves to be told which is which. In this chapter, "Softanzuter" is the third rung, the thinking machine of the definition: designed, not built. The engine has a module with the same name, which is the body of the agent: slots with a state and a bounded mailbox, where a message into a full queue is refused, counted and never dropped quietly. And the design of a universal engine behind all of it, a medium where any pattern language becomes a computation, is planned.

**Reaxis** is the other half of the chapter's title, and it is a different idea. It is reactive programming with a vocabulary redesigned from the ground. A reactive thing is a Container, a Stream and an Rfunction: a function in waiting, which is not a callback. Data flows through a stream in plain words, a transformation, a filter, what to do with what passes:

```haro
aSeen = []
Rs = new stzReactiveSystem()
Rs {
	oStream = CreateStream("prices")
	oStream {
		Transform(func price { return price * 1.20 })
		Filter(func price { return price >= 100 })
		OnPassed(func item { aSeen + item })
		FeedMany([ 80, 90, 100, 120 ])
	}
	RunLoop()
}
? @@( aSeen )
#--> [ 108, 120, 144 ]
```

> run: kept

The 80 becomes 96 and is dropped by the filter; the 90 becomes 108 and passes. Three functions are in waiting, and nothing subscribes to anything.

Reaxis replaced backpressure with overflow, a word you do not need a diagram to understand. And it was audited. A reading of the four strategies the article documented found that three of them lost data and reported none: the default discarded everything past the capacity while the counter read zero. That is fixed, and proved for all four strategies. Here, four items into a buffer of two, and the stream says what happened:

```haro
oRs = new stzReactiveSystem()
oS = oRs.CreateStream("probe")
oS.SetOverflowStrategy(BUFFER_REJECT_NEWEST, 2)
for i = 1 to 4
	oS.Receive(i)
next
? oS.OverflowStats()[:currentBuffer]
#--> 2
? oS.OverflowStats()[:droppedCount]
#--> 2
```

> run: kept

And for what the field calls debounce, Reaxis says what it means: wait for the attribute to settle. Three changes in quick succession, one reaction, after the value has stopped changing for fifty milliseconds:

```haro
nSettled = 0
oRs4 = new stzReactive()
oX = oRs4.ReactiveObject()
oX.WaitForAttributeToSettle("query", 50, func(attr, oldV, newV) { nSettled++ })
oX.SetAttribute("query", "s")
oX.SetAttribute("query", "so")
oX.SetAttribute("query", "sof")
oX { ProcessPendingReactions() }
oRs4.RunLoop()
? nSettled
#--> 1
oRs4.Stop()
```

> run: kept

The third part is the engines, the small paradigms the library rethought one by one. Chapter 3 gave you truth and emptiness. Two more, run here. Time is a workspace, not a number: a timeline holds spans, computes the gaps and the overlaps, and a blocked region refuses a point that would fall into it, with the reason, at insertion:

```haro
oTl = new stzTimeLine("2024-01-01 00:00:00", "2024-12-31 23:59:59")
oTl.AddSpan("PHASE1", "2024-01-01 00:00:00", "2024-02-15 23:59:59")
oTl.AddSpan("PHASE2", "2024-03-01 00:00:00", "2024-04-15 23:59:59")
? len(oTl.Gaps())
#--> 1
? oTl.Gaps()[1][:after]
#--> PHASE1
? oTl.Gaps()[1][:before]
#--> PHASE2
? oTl.HasOverlaps()
#--> FALSE
oTl.AddSpan("PHASE2B", "2024-04-01 00:00:00", "2024-05-15 23:59:59")
? oTl.HasOverlaps()
#--> TRUE
oB = new stzTimeLine("2024-01-01", "2024-12-31")
oB.AddBlockedSpan("MAINTENANCE", "2024-06-15 09:00:00", "2024-06-15 17:00:00")
oB.AddPoint("EVENT1", "2024-06-15 08:00:00")
try
	oB.AddPoint("EVENT2", "2024-06-15 12:00:00")
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Point 'EVENT2' falls within a blocked span or blocked point
```

> run: kept

And a grid has a cursor with the intelligence of a place: position is column and row, and moving past an edge stays at the edge.

```haro
o = new stzGrid([ 5, 5 ])
o.MoveDown()
o.MoveDown()
o.MoveRight()
o.MoveRight()
o.MoveRight()
? @@( o.CurrentPosition() )
#--> [ 4, 3 ]
o.MoveRight()
o.MoveRight()
o.MoveRight()
? @@( o.CurrentPosition() )
#--> [ 5, 3 ]
```

> run: kept

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused to leave regex as a way to read text.** I made it a computational medium: a trigger fires a computation, the computation writes a state, and the state records its why. The Regexuter is a faculty and not a mind, and it holds no goal. I added the goal at the rung above it, in the agent, on purpose.

**I refused the field's vocabulary for reactive programming.** Observables, subscribers, backpressure, hot and cold sources, schedulers: borrowed from plumbing, telephony and publishing, and not from the problem. A function in waiting is an Rfunction. Data flows without a theatre of producers and consumers. Overflow replaces backpressure. Waiting for an attribute to settle replaces debounce. The old spellings still work as aliases: feed, emit, send and receive all call one method, so my change was one of naming and not of removal.

**I refused to build the siblings.** The article that tells the Regexuter describes four more: an analyser, a genetic one where patterns evolve against a fitness, a linguistic one where triggers are grammar rules, and a quantic one where a pattern may be in several states at once. I wrote them in prose and not in code, and the design later found their purpose: answering by example demands pattern induction, and several acceptable answers demand superposed candidates, collapsed by rules or by choice. Chapter 18 tells that. They remain a vision, with their purpose written.

**I refused to claim the cascade.** The library's architecture says it itself: the scaffolding anticipates cascading but does not yet re-fire dependent triggers to a fixpoint. I would rather show you the block that fails to cascade than a block that does.

## 5. The court

The Regexuter and the Listexuter have classic tests, and no narrated guard. The Regexuter's tests that I ran keep their promises; the Listexuter's first five do not. The agent's cascade is proved by `piagent_narrated`, scene five. Reaxis has many guards: `00_overflow_narrated` proves the four overflow strategies and the case that loses nothing; `60_reaxis_on_reactor_narrated` proves the repeating timer, the settling attribute and the stream on the engine's loop. The timeline has `test_timeline_regression`, the grid `00_grid_narrated`, and deep operations `01_deeplist_narrated`.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field's rule engines lead. They are older and more mature than the Regexuter, they chain to a fixpoint, and the algorithms behind them are decades old. What I claim is not a rule engine but the condition side: rules that are sentences of the pattern languages over six media, and a state in which every firing keeps its why. The library's own architecture calls the Regexuter a nascent forward-chaining rule engine, and I will not call it more.

The field's reactive libraries lead in reach. Rx exists in every mainstream language, with operators by the hundred. Reaxis is one library on one event loop. The engine's own module for it is a small table of substring rules: the Container, Stream and Rfunction model lives in the library, on the event loop of the engine.

I will not print the article's comparison with the field's star-rating example. It is undated and unsourced.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the Regexuter and the Listexuter; Reaxis on the engine's event loop; the timeline, the grid and deep operations; the agent whose skills chain.

What is designed: the third rung, many faculties on one shared state, cascading until it settles. The universal engine for it is planned. The three siblings are vision. The macroplan marks the engine module as done and the architecture table marks the planned medium as planned: the same name for two things.

What is absent or broken at this commit:

- The dependency chain is not one. Each state entry depends only on its own trigger, so the chain records which trigger was active and never a link between two. Asking for the chain overflows the stack. No test calls it.
- The Listexuter's classic tests keep none of their promises. Its first block no longer raises the error its log remembers, but it still prints the last section's result three times, and its server pattern never fires.
- A nested list that matches as a whole is reported twice.
- The article's repeating timer takes three arguments and the library takes two; the form with units is `RunEveryXT`.
- The deep-lists article's path verbs live on a subclass, `stzDeepList`, and not on a plain list.
- The section-merging article presents a comprehensive verb as its point, and that verb does not exist. The two that do exist, merging what overlaps and merging what is included, run. Section merging has no test file of its own.
- And the dependency of the model on the engine: the Container, Stream and Rfunction model runs in the library, not in a native module.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Bind a pattern to code, and process; or declare a stream and say in words what happens to the data that flows through it.

**The analyst.** Time is a workspace: gaps, overlaps and blocked regions are computed, and a point that would fall in a blocked region is refused with the reason.

**The designer.** The state of a reaction keeps its why: the match, the computed value, the position, the trigger.

**The decision-maker.** Overflow is a number you can read: kept, dropped. A strategy that lost data and said nothing was found by an audit, and fixed.

**The educator.** The ladder is a curriculum: a pattern, a reaction, a state, a goal.

**The agent.** Your mind can be skills that chain on what they perceive, until the world stops changing.

## 9. Exercise

Write a Regexuter with two triggers, one for numbers and one for numbers of three digits or more, with code that turns a number into its triple. Before you run it, write under each question what you expect: what the matches are, what the results are, and whether the second trigger fires on the first one's result. Run it. If the second trigger fires, you have found something I did not.

Then say in one sentence what would have to change in the machine for it to fire, and which rung of the ladder that change belongs to.
