# Programming becomes refinement

Source: Chapter 10. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the refinement point declared in the source; the gate with five checks; the cascade of a point and of a class; reversal by step, by checkpoint and to zero; the trust floor and governed refining; designed: the experience the article describes: asking a point to explain itself, applying a plan, a one-line refinement; the cascade of the code graph joined to the gate; the refinement seat of Haro; absent: a saved history: the file keeps the source and nothing of the timeline

Run: 9 of 9 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:24 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

The field has a name for changing infrastructure with a rehearsal: plan, then apply. A tool computes what would change, shows it, and applies it only when you say so; Terraform made the pattern familiar, and the field's admission controllers apply the same idea to a request before a cluster accepts it. More recently, the field has a name for a way of working with code-writing agents: specifications first, and the agent fills in. (General knowledge, not a study of the record.)

What these share is where they stand. The plan and the admission control are about infrastructure and requests. For the code itself, the field's answer to a change is still a diff, a patch of text, and a person's review of it. That worked while every change had a person behind it who could be asked. It is a weak instrument when most changes arrive from agents, in volume.

## 2. The first principle

I wrote the sentence on the back cover of my first book, and I have not found a better one: **stop trying to govern the intelligence, and govern the change.** The library's header says the same thing in the form of a type: a change is a typed proposal, not a diff.

## 3. The Softanza form

The practice has changed names in my life. I used to say I programmed. I now say that, more and more, I refine, and I mean something exact by it. A refinement is a change to code that was declared to be changeable, proposed in a typed form, judged by a gate, recorded as a step, and reversible. The first book of this imprint, *Refinement-Oriented Programming*, tells the discipline through the story of a bank facing a regulator's letter. This chapter tells what the library carries of it.

It begins by declaring where the code may change. A refinement point is a named knob written in the source as a tag, with its kind, its value and its bounds:

```haro
cSrc = 'vat_rate = <R:PARAM name="vat" value="0.20" min="0" max="0.25">' + nl +
       'sort_by  = <R:ALGO name="sort" value="quick" options="quick|merge|heap">'
o = new stzRefinableCode(cSrc)
? o.NumberOfPoints()
#--> 2
? @@( o.RefinementPoints() )
#--> [ [ "vat", "param", "0.20" ], [ "sort", "algo", "quick" ] ]
? o.KindOf("vat")
#--> param
```

> run: kept

A change is then a proposal. It names a point and a value; if it clears the gate it takes effect and is recorded as one step:

```haro
r = o.Refine("vat").To("0.22")
? r[:admitted]
#--> 1
? o.ValueOf("vat")
#--> 0.22
? o.NumberOfSteps()
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

And a proposal that does not clear the gate changes nothing, and says at which stage it was refused and why:

```haro
r2 = o.Refine("vat").To("0.99")
? r2[:admitted]
#--> 0
? r2[:why]
#--> constraint: 0.99 above max 0.25
? o.ValueOf("vat")
#--> 0.22
? o.Refine("sort").To("bubble")[:why]
#--> constraint: 'bubble' not in options quick|merge|heap
? o.Refine("ghost").To("1")[:why]
#--> structural: no refinement point 'ghost'
? o.NumberOfSteps()
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

Three refusals, and the timeline did not grow by a step. That is the third law of the house applied to code: a rejected proposal never mutates. Each refusal names its stage, so that a person, or an agent, can correct the proposal without reading a diff.

**The cascade** is what you read instead of the diff. Before the change, ask what it reaches. For a point, it is the line it sits on and the other points on that same line:

```haro
cPrice = 'price = <R:PARAM name="vat" value="0.20" min="0" max="0.30"> * <R:PARAM name="margin" value="1.1" min="1" max="2">' + nl +
         'floor = <R:PARAM name="floor" value="0.10" min="0" max="0.30">'
oc = new stzRefinableCode(cPrice)
aC = oc.Cascade("vat")
? aC[:lines][1]
#--> 1
? @@( aC[:alsoTouches] )
#--> [ "margin" ]
? oc.Cascade("floor")[:lines][1]
#--> 2
? len(oc.Cascade("floor")[:alsoTouches])
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

For a class, the cascade is a property of the structure of the code: who descends from it and who inherits a method. The library computes it from the code graph, and it can scan a text without touching a file:

```haro
cCls = 'class Tariff' + nl +
       '	def Rate()' + nl +
       '		return 0.20' + nl +
       'class Invoice from Tariff' + nl +
       '	def Total()' + nl +
       '		return 1' + nl +
       'class Receipt from Invoice' + nl +
       '	def Print()' + nl +
       '		return 1' + nl +
       'class Quote from Tariff' + nl +
       '	def Rate()' + nl +
       '		return 0.18' + nl
g0 = new stzRingCodeGraph("")
g0.ScanSource(cCls, "world.ring")
g0.BuildGraph()
? @@( g0.Cascade("Tariff")[:descendants] )
#--> [ "Invoice", "Quote", "Receipt" ]
? g0.Cascade("Tariff")[:blastRadius]
#--> 3
? @@( g0.ImpactOf("Rate")[:inheritedBy] )
#--> [ "Invoice", "Receipt" ]
```

> run: kept

Change the rate of the tariff and three classes are in the blast radius; but two of them inherit the method and the third redefines it, so it owns the method and does not merely inherit it. That is the difference `ImpactOf` draws, and a diff cannot.

Inside the gate, a change reaches its dependents by a rule that spans several points, judged on the state the change would produce. If the rule fails, the value is put back:

```haro
d = new stzRefinableCode(cPrice)
d.DeclareDerivation("vat-above-floor",
	func oCode { return ring_number(oCode.ValueOf("vat")) >= ring_number(oCode.ValueOf("floor")) },
	"vat must not drop below floor")
? d.Refine("vat").To("0.30")[:admitted]
#--> 1
r3 = d.Refine("vat").To("0.05")
? r3[:admitted]
#--> 0
? r3[:why]
#--> derivation: rule 'vat-above-floor' violated -- vat must not drop below floor
? d.ValueOf("vat")
#--> 0.30
```

> run: kept

Now the question of this age: who proposed it? Every proposal carries its origin, and a point can set a floor of trust that refuses a lower origin, without refusing a person. A proposal that comes from a language model is below a floor that a trusted one clears:

```haro
d.TrustFloor("vat", :trusted)
r4 = d.Refine("vat").As(:llm).To("0.25")
? r4[:admitted]
#--> 0
? r4[:why]
#--> trust: posture :llm below the required floor for 'vat'
? d.ValueOf("vat")
#--> 0.30
? d.Refine("vat").As(:trusted).To("0.25")[:admitted]
#--> 1
? d.PostureOf(d.NumberOfSteps())
#--> trusted
```

> run: kept

And who may turn which knob is declared, too: a permission, and an authority level against a risk tier. A delegated actor cannot turn a knob of the third tier. Escalated, it can, and the decision is recorded:

```haro
gv = new stzRefinableCode(cPrice)
gv.SetGovernedBy(new stzGovernance("release-ops")).SetActor("release-bot")
gv.SetRiskFor("vat", 3).SetAllowRefine("vat").SetAuthorityLevel(:Delegated)
r5 = gv.Refine("vat").To("0.22")
? r5[:admitted]
#--> 0
? r5[:why]
#--> governance: refused: 'release-bot' holds 'delegated' authority (level 2) but 'refine-vat' is risk tier 3 (SHOULD does not cover it)
? gv.ValueOf("vat")
#--> 0.20
gv.SetAuthorityLevel(:Autonomous)
? gv.Refine("vat").To("0.22")[:admitted]
#--> 1
? gv.GovernanceQ().NumberOfDecisions()
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

Last, reversibility is part of the data model. Every admitted step keeps its prior value, so a person can step back one, rewind to a named checkpoint, rewind to zero, and go forward again:

```haro
t = new stzRefinableCode(cPrice)
t.Refine("vat").To("0.21")
t.Refine("vat").To("0.22")
t.Checkpoint("reviewed")
t.Refine("vat").To("0.28")
? t.ValueOf("vat")
#--> 0.28
t.RevertToCheckpoint("reviewed")
? t.ValueOf("vat")
#--> 0.22
? t.NumberOfSteps()
#--> 2
t.RevertTo(0)
? t.ValueOf("vat")
#--> 0.20
? t.CanRevert()
#--> 0
t.Redo()
? t.ValueOf("vat")
#--> 0.21
```

> run: kept

That is the gate in the form the library has it. Five checks, in the order the code runs them: the structure of the proposal, then the constraints of the point, then the governance, then the trust of the origin, and last the derivation across points. The header of the file calls this a four-stage gate in one line and five in another; the code numbers its checks 1, 2, 3, 4 and 4b, because the trust check was added to the four on 16 July 2026 and the design's count was not changed. I report what runs: five checks.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused to govern the intelligence.** The field's approach to agents is to make the model safer: alignment, guardrails, a prompt that asks for confirmation. I refused that frame. A model's output is whatever it is, from whichever model, and the intelligence of the next generation will not hold still for me to govern it. The change is the thing that stays, and it is the thing that reaches the world. So the gate stands where the change arrives, and it asks one thing: what is proposed, from where, and does the code say this point may change that way.

**I refused the diff as the unit.** A diff is a text change. A refinement is a typed proposal about a declared point, with a kind, bounds and an origin. I kept the diff as an artefact, and I refused it as the thing to review.

**I made the library carry the verb, and left the environment elsewhere.** The refinement as a library verb, the point, the gate, the cascade and the reversal, belongs to the open foundation. The environment in which a team lives with it day to day, the screens, the live mode, the book, is a product, and does not leak into the library.

**I printed the gate as it is.** I wrote the design with four stages, and the code has five checks. A page that quotes my design and not my code would be wrong, and it is the code that runs.

## 5. The court

`refinablecode_narrated` proves the point declared, the proposal that passes, the three refusals that leave the source whole, the cascade, the reversal, and the saved file. `refine_deepening_narrated` proves the timeline with its checkpoints and its redo, and the trust floor. `gate_deepening_narrated` proves the derivation across points with its rollback, and the governed refusal with its lineage.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in infrastructure, where plan and apply are the daily practice of thousands of teams and the apply talks to real providers. Softanza's gate is about the change to code, which the field handles with a review.

The field's tools lead in breadth: they are not tied to one library, one language, or one declared knob. Softanza's gate judges only what the code declared as a point. A change to a place that was never declared is not a refinement here; it is outside the gate.

And on trust, the field's approach is the one that scales to any model. Mine rests on one declaration per point.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the refinement point, the gate with its five checks, the cascade of a point and of a class, the reversal, the trust floor, and governed refining; each with a guard that passes at this commit.

What is designed, and absent from the library by name: the experience the original article describes. The class that article names, `stzPolyCode`, never existed. The one-line refinement of the design document, a point that explains itself, a plan that is applied, are not in the library. The cascade of the code graph and the cascade of the gate are not joined. And Haro has a gate in a pure-Ring spike, with its own seat for refinement not built.

What is absent, and the page should say it: the limits of the gate as it stands.

- It checks the constraints of three kinds of point, a parameter, an algorithm and a library; a point of another kind, a block of code for instance, parses and passes unchecked.
- An unknown trust posture ranks as trusted. A proposal that says it comes from a martian was admitted in my run against a floor of trusted.
- A derivation is applied tentatively and restored on failure. So I say "never ends changed", not "never touched".
- The saved file holds the rendered source and nothing else: no history, no postures, no timeline, and no way to load it back. Reversibility does not survive the file. [AUTHOR: The library still writes the format `.zrfn`, and the Haro charter, ratified on 1 October 2026, retires it for `.rfn`. Do I print `.zrfn` as shipped, with one line on the charter?]

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Declare the places that may change, and propose changes to them as typed proposals. A refusal names its stage.

**The analyst.** The rule that spans several points is yours to declare, and the gate judges it on the state the change would produce.

**The designer.** The cascade is what you read instead of the diff: which line, which points on it, which classes inherit.

**The decision-maker.** Who may turn which knob is a permission and an authority against a risk tier, and the decision is recorded with its authority at the time.

**The educator.** Step back one, rewind to a named checkpoint, rewind to zero, go forward again. A learner can try without fear because every step keeps its prior value.

**The agent.** You may propose. Your origin is on the proposal, and a point can set a floor your origin does not clear.

## 9. Exercise

Take two settings of a program of yours: a rate and a ceiling, say, with a rule that the rate must stay below the ceiling. Declare them as points, with bounds, and declare the rule across them. Before you run anything, write under each proposal whether it is admitted and, if not, at which stage it is refused. Propose a change of the rate above the ceiling, and one from an origin you declare as a model, against a floor you set. Then rewind to zero and go forward one step.

Run it. If you were wrong about the stage, you have found a place where your own gate disagrees with your own expectation.


=====

# Law: a language is declared and closed, and whatever speaks it, person or model, is judged by it

Source: Chapter 11. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the natural layer's vocabulary as data in five languages, with its refusals; the court of structured output, its grammar compiler and its sampler; the pattern languages; designed: the factory that produces a language's parser, court and grammar from its declaration alone; a grammar emitted for every declared language; the writing style as a declared grammar; absent: a natural layer closed all the way down: one form of list still reaches host code; the standard Hausa letter ɗ in the Hausa pack

Run: 11 of 11 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:25 UTC+01:00.

## What the AI age changes here

In Part II, the age changed who writes the code. Here it changes who speaks the sentence. A sentence of a language used to have a person behind it, someone who could be asked what they meant. Today the writer of a sentence may be a model, which cannot be asked, and which will write a fluent sentence whether or not it is true.

So the grammar must be closed, and the court must read it. A language whose sentences may contain anything cannot refuse anything, and a court cannot judge a sentence it cannot read. The vision of the platform puts the whole movement in one line, which I ratified: humans and machines meet in declared languages, small, closed and judged, not in general-purpose code.

This is also where the order of the book turns around. The cover goes from the familiar to the new: programming, languages, intelligence. The record's logic goes the other way. The first act is to declare a language. Programming, the discipline of Part II, happens inside declared languages: the forms of the vocabulary are already a declaration in Haro. Intelligence, Part IV, is governed by both: a model is held to a declared grammar before it speaks, and its sentence is judged after. If you read this book in the record's order, this chapter comes first.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

For a working programmer, a language is a general host, Python, JavaScript, Java, and a "domain language" is usually a library whose calls read like sentences while the file around them may contain any code of the host. The estate's own study of the field, written in August 2026, names what happened to those designs: internal languages died of openness, because a build file is declarative only until someone writes arbitrary code in it; language workbenches died of the weight of their tools. And it names the languages that won: SQL, regular expressions, Make, HTML and CSS, the configuration languages. They won because they are closed.

The model era added one practice: when a model writes, either check what it wrote, or constrain its output while it writes, with a grammar or a schema. The reference implementation of grammar-constrained sampling is an open project, and the record says so where it uses the same algorithm. And in the field's common language today, "programming in natural language" means one thing: prompting a model.

## 2. The first principle

**A language is declared and closed, and whatever speaks it, a person or a model, is judged by it.**

## 3. The Softanza form

Begin with a sentence that runs. Not because a model guessed what it means, but because every word of it is in a dictionary the library owns:

```haro
Naturally("
    Make a string with 'hello niger' inside
    Spacify it and uppercase it
    @Box it while the box@ is rounded
    Display it on the screen
")
#--> ╭───────────────────────╮
#    │ H E L L O   N I G E R │
#    ╰───────────────────────╯
```

> run: kept

The dictionary is data, keyed by meanings, not by English words. So the same meaning can be said in another language by adding a block of data, not code. Here is one instruction in English, in Hausa, in French and in Arabic:

```haro
? Naturally("Create a string with 'hello' and uppercase it").Result()
#--> HELLO
? NaturallyIn("hausa", "Yi rubutu dauke 'hello' Maida shi").Result()
#--> HELLO
? NaturallyIn("fr", "Crée une chaine avec 'hello' et mets la en majuscules").Result()
#--> HELLO
? NaturallyIn("ar", "أنشئ نصا بـ 'hello' ثم كبّره").Result()
#--> HELLO
```

> run: kept

And a program says back what it understood, in its own language. This matters more than it looks: a person reading Arabic can check the machine's reading of their sentence without reading a line of code.

```haro
o1 = NaturallyIn("ar", "أنشئ قائمة بـ [ 5, 3, 5 ] ثم أزل تكراراتها")
? @@( o1.Result() )
#--> [ 5, 3 ]
? o1.Understood()
#--> أنشئ قائمة بـ [ 5, 3, 5 ] -> أزل التكرارات
```

> run: kept

Now the part a model cannot do: say what it did not understand. A misspelled verb is not guessed. It is reported, with the word the library thinks you meant, and the program admits that it did not understand everything:

```haro
o1 = Naturally("Create a string with 'softanza' Uppercse it")
? o1.Result()
#--> softanza
? @@( o1.Unresolved() )
#--> [ [ "Uppercse", "uppercase" ] ]
? o1.UnderstoodAll()
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

That is the permissive mode, made for a person learning. An agent gets the strict mode, which refuses, and a world can declare the only operations its sentences may perform:

```haro
try
	NaturallyStrict("Create a string with 'x' Fooify it")
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Strict natural mode: not understood: 'Fooify' (did you mean 'boxify'?)
try
	NaturallyStrictIn("en", "Create a list with [ 3, 1 ] Reverse it", [ "METHOD_REMOVEDUPLICATES" ])
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Operation not permitted in this world: METHOD_REVERSE
```

> run: kept

In that world, reversing a list is not forbidden. It is not a sentence of the language at all.

A verdict says why. The other direction of natural code in Softanza is the near-natural chain, where the person is the parser and the chain reads as a sentence; when it answers no, it gives the reason:

```haro
? IsQ().TheQ().LengthQ().OfQ("softanza").LessThanQ().TheQ().LengthQ().Of("Haro")
#--> 0
? Why()
#--> no: 8 is not less than 4
```

> run: kept

Now the law at its sharpest: a model. The library declares the shape of an answer as a schema, and the schema is judged when it is declared, before any model is called:

```haro
try
	StzOutputSchemaQ([ [ :field = "x", :type = :colour ] ])
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> stzOutputSchema: unknown field type 'colour' (use string|number|boolean|oneof|list|structure).
```

> run: kept

A correct schema is a small closed language. The court judges any answer against it, whoever wrote the answer, and the schema emits its grammar, so that a model can be held to it while it writes:

```haro
oS = StzOutputSchemaQ([
	[ :field = "name", :type = :string ],
	[ :field = "age",  :type = :number, :must = [ [ ">=", 0 ], [ "<=", 130 ] ] ],
	[ :field = "mood", :type = :oneof,  :choices = [ "positive", "negative" ] ]
])
? oS.Accepts("name: Hadiza" + nl + "age: 41" + nl + "mood: positive")
#--> 1
? oS.Accepts("name: Hadiza" + nl + "age: 41" + nl + "mood: angry")
#--> 0
? oS.Accepts("name: Hadiza" + nl + "age: 900000" + nl + "mood: positive")
#--> 0
? oS.Accepts("name: Hadiza" + nl + "age: 62" + nl + "mood: positive")
#--> 1
? oS.ToGBNF()
#--> root ::= name-line age-line mood-line
#    name-line ::= "name: " text "\n"
#    age-line ::= "age: " number "\n"
#    mood-line ::= "mood: " ("positive" | "negative") "\n"
#    text ::= [^\n]+
#    number ::= "-"? [0-9]+ ("." [0-9]+)?
#    boolean ::= ("yes" | "no" | "true" | "false")
```

> run: kept

Look at the fourth answer. Hadiza is forty-one in the first answer and sixty-two in the fourth, and the court accepts both. A grammar kills malformed sentences, not false ones. The record says it in six words, structure kills malformedness, not falsehood, and so does this book: the truth of an answer is for the knowledge graph, the rules and a person. A grammar is the gate, not the judge of everything.

A template is a contract too. A sentence with a hole in it is refused before it runs:

```haro
try
	NaturallyWith("Create a string with {#1} and {#2} it", [ "x" ])
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Unfilled template hole! The narration still contains a {#n} slot with no value for it.
```

> run: kept

And patterns are small closed languages of their own. The shape of a list is declared, not written as a loop:

```haro
? Lx("[@N, @S]").Match([42, "hello"])
#--> 1
? Lx("[@N, @S]").Match(["hello", 42])
#--> 0
? Lx("[@N, [@N2], @N]").Match([1, [2, 3], 4])
#--> 1
? Lx("[@N, [@N2], @N]").Match([1, [2], 4])
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

The chapters of this part prove the law from five sides. Chapter 12 tells a language declared: the factory, the closed grammar with no escape into the host, the court born with the grammar, the grammar emitted so that a model can only speak valid sentences, and the surfaces a language may wear. Chapter 13 tells the two directions of natural code, the machine as the parser and the person as the parser, in one discipline. Chapter 14 tells knowledge programming, one file that is a system. Chapter 15 tells the pattern languages, regex lifted to lists, numbers, tables, matrices, time and graphs. Chapter 16 tells the seven languages Softanza reaches through one governed door, and why a door is not a surface.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I chose the act: declare a language.** Not "use our language". Declare yours: the language of your domain, in your words. That is the kernel of the platform, and it is the reason this part exists.

**I refused the open escape.** The meta-language that declares languages in the estate refuses, by its founding rule, to define any form that would let a sentence reach host code, call a function, evaluate a string or touch a file. Not forbidden: unexpressible. An internal language that cannot refuse arbitrary host code is, in the words of the estate's field study, an interface wearing a language's costume.

**I refused natural language as a way to declare.** Natural language asks; it never declares. A model may draft a declaration, and the draft enters the world only as a proposal: the model proposes, the court decides, a person ratifies.

**I kept the promise of 2020 and changed its mechanism.** My first slide promised executable natural language. The vision of 2026 judged that slogan fairly: it promised more than a court can defend, because a natural sentence can be ambiguous and a lie can be grammatical. So the naturalness stays at the door, where a sentence asks or drafts, and the execution belongs to closed languages a court can judge. I built the natural layer that way long before the vision named it: only words defined in the dictionary are interpreted, and every other word becomes a literal or a refusal, never a guess.

**I took governance out of the library and kept the refusals in it.** In July 2026, a kit for governing agents landed inside the natural layer. I removed it the same day: the strict mode, the refusals, the why and the understood belong to the library; a product for governing agents does not. And I removed a set of markers that made chains easier to parse and harder to read, because a language a person speaks never marks a reference that way. An ungrammatical chain that runs is a bug.

**I refused a second embedding.** When someone asks "what about Python, what about my language", the answer is one door, governed, and never another host sewn into the platform. A door brings a foreign runtime; a surface brings a familiar syntax over the same grammar. Chapter 16 tells the difference.

## 5. The court

`multilingual_pack_narrated` proves the languages as data: the packs, the programs in French and Arabic, a pack added at runtime, and the program paraphrasing back in its own language. `semantic_resolver_narrated` proves the unknown word with its suggestion, the strict mode, and the allow-list under which everything off the list is grammatically impossible. It does not pass at this commit: I ran it on 9 October 2026 and it stops with an error raised from a string-splitting function, before it ends, so the page counts its assertions and does not claim its verdict. `literate_templates_narrated` proves that a template is a contract. `question_narrated` proves that every answer records its why. `structuredoutput_narrated` proves the court of structured output, including the lie it lets through. `schema_grammar_narrated` proves the grammar compiler, and `constrained_decoding_narrated` the sampler that holds a model to it.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in tools. The language workbenches remain ahead on the axes the estate's design deliberately gave up: editors that understand a language as you type, integration with every environment, theoretical depth. The estate's study says it in its own words: that is a bet, and it is named as one.

The field built constrained decoding first. Softanza's sampler follows the reference implementation's algorithm and vendors nothing. Measured against one small model of a hundred and thirty-five million parameters, on ten prompts, the answers were valid at the first attempt two times in ten when checked afterwards, and ten times in ten when the model was held to the grammar. That is one small model and ten prompts, and nothing in the record measures a large one.

And the age cuts both ways. The same flood of generated code makes careless language-making cheap too. That is why the court is not an accessory of a declared language. It is the core.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the natural layer, its vocabulary as data in five languages, English, Hausa, French, Arabic and Turkish, and its refusals; the court of structured output with its grammar compiler and its sampler; the pattern languages.

What is designed: the factory that would produce, from a language's declaration alone, its parser, its court, its printer and its grammar for a model. A first sketch of it was built outside the library in October 2026; the library cannot run it yet. The vision's text says the factory has not yet forged; the sketch has since forged courts and grammars for several languages, judged by those languages' own fixtures, and the vision has not caught up. Chapter 12 tells it with its limits. A grammar for every declared language is promised and not shipped.

What is absent, and the law must say it. The natural layer is not closed all the way down. One form, a list written inside brackets, is still handed to the host to evaluate, so a host function inside the brackets runs:

```haro
? @@( Naturally("Create a list with [ upper('x'), 1 ] and remove its duplicates").Result() )
#--> [ "X", 1 ]
```

> run: kept

The function `upper` is not a word of the natural dictionary. It ran anyway. The record already knows this escape and has routed its repair; until the repair lands, "the natural layer is a closed language" is true of its words and not of its lists, and this book will say so.

And the Hausa pack does not yet read the letter ɗ, which standard Hausa spelling uses: the pack stored it in a broken encoding, so a sentence written correctly fails without a word of complaint. The Hausa in this chapter is written without the hook, the way the pack reads it today. [AUTHOR: Has a Hausa speaker read the pack's sentences? The book should not print Hausa that no speaker has read.]

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** You keep writing code. What you declare is a closed grammar, and the court judges your sentence and an agent's by the same rules.

**The analyst.** Your domain's words become a data pack, and the system understands them or refuses them with the reason.

**The designer.** The refusals are part of the language: what is not a sentence cannot be said, so it never needs a warning.

**The decision-maker.** A model held to a declared grammar answers in the right shape. It can still answer a falsehood in the right shape, and the record prints that limit with the result.

**The educator.** A sentence in Hausa, French or Arabic runs and says back what it understood, so a learner reads the machine's reading of their own words.

**The agent.** You may speak only the declared language. Strict mode is your posture, and your draft is a proposal the court judges.

## 9. Exercise

Declare, with `StzOutputSchemaQ`, a record of three fields from a domain you know: a loan request, for instance, with an amount inside a band, a currency from a closed list, and a purpose in free text. Before you run anything, write three answers a model might give: one the court must accept, one it must refuse because of its shape, and one it will accept although it is false. Under each, write your prediction as a promise.

Run it. The third answer is the lesson of this chapter. Write down, in one sentence, who in your domain must judge it, since the grammar cannot.


=====

# A language, declared

Source: Chapter 12. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: in the library: a declaration judged when it is declared; a court and a grammar from the one declaration, with the list of what the grammar dropped; a grammar machine that walks bytes and refuses by name; a description for a person and a prompt for a model from the same text. Outside the library: a forge that turns a declaration into a court and a grammar, run on the estate's own languages; designed: the forge inside the library; its full list of products from one declaration (printer, knowledge graph, natural door, narration, runtime, one static binary); a grammar that closes a menu in the forge's output; the projections of a language to an application; absent: a factory in the library; the forge's grammar for a menu word; a guard that asserts the refusal of a host function in a condition; the figure two of ten against ten of ten, which needs a model file the snapshot does not hold

Run: 8 of 8 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:25 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

Two families of tools let you declare a language in the field. The first is the language workbench and the parser generator: JetBrains MPS, Xtext, Spoofax, and Racket's `#lang` give you the means to define a language with its grammar, its editor and its checks. The estate's own field study of August 2026 says why they did not win. They died of ecosystem gravity, and Racket's languages live and die inside Racket.

The second is the internal DSL: a library whose calls read like sentences, while the file can still hold any host code. The same study has the sentence I keep: closedness is the product, and an internal DSL that cannot refuse arbitrary host code is an API wearing a language's costume. Its control group is the closed languages that won: SQL, regex, Make, HTML and CSS, HCL, GraphQL, Markdown, CUE.

For the grammar a model must obey, the record names one tool: the grammar sampler of a well-known local inference library, which the library's machine follows for its element encoding and its algorithm. Nothing of it is vendored. (General knowledge, not a study of the record: model vendors offer structured-output modes that take a JSON schema, and open libraries turn a schema or a regular expression into a mask on the model's tokens.)

Chapter 11 told what a closed language is, and what was lost when the field left it open. I take that as read. This chapter says what is new: what *produces* a closed language.

## 2. The first principle

**Declaring a language is as easy as declaring a variable.**

It is the heading of one rule of Haro, ruled on 1 October 2026, and the kernel of the vision says the same: the era makes declaring a language as easy as declaring a variable. The sentence of the other half of the chapter, from the alphabet's totality doctrine, ratified on 27 September: malformedness dies at the sampler, falsehood dies at the court, and non-termination is unexpressible.

And the one I must carry beside every win, from the library's contract for structured output: structure kills malformedness, and not falsehood.

## 3. The Softanza form

Take a declaration of what a restaurant's order must look like. It has three fields: a dish from a closed menu, a quantity between 1 and 12, and an optional note. One text is read by three readers. A person reads a description. A model reads a clause for its prompt. And the court reads the same text and judges an answer:

```haro
oO = StzOutputSchemaQ([
	[ :field = "dish",     :type = :oneof,  :choices = [ "margherita", "tiramisu" ] ],
	[ :field = "quantity", :type = :number, :must = [ [ ">=", 1 ], [ "<=", 12 ] ] ],
	[ :field = "note",     :type = :string, :optional = 1 ]
])
? oO.Describe()
#--> schema 'output' -- 3 field(s)
#      dish : oneof (margherita | tiramisu) [required]
#      quantity : number [required], must be at least '1', must be at most '12'
#      note : string [optional]
? oO.PromptClause()
#--> Answer with ONLY this structure, one field per line, nothing before it and nothing after it:
#
#    dish: <one of: margherita | tiramisu>
#    quantity: <number>
#    note: <text>
aV = oO.Verify([ :dish = "pizza", :quantity = 40 ])
? aV[:ok]
#--> 0
? aV[:findings][1][:message]
#--> field 'dish' is a closed enumeration (margherita | tiramisu) and the model answered 'pizza'.
? aV[:findings][2][:message]
#--> field 'quantity' must be at most '12', and the model answered '40'.
```

> run: kept

Now the same declaration produces a grammar. The court and the grammar are born of one text, and they agree on the shape. They part ways on a value. Forty portions has the right shape, so the grammar lets it through and the court refuses it. The library even lists what the grammar dropped:

```haro
cG = oO.ToGBNF()
cGood  = "dish: margherita" + nl + "quantity: 2" + nl + "note: no basil" + nl
cBand  = "dish: margherita" + nl + "quantity: 40" + nl + "note: no basil" + nl
cShape = "dish: margherita" + nl + "quantity: many" + nl + "note: no basil" + nl
? StzGrammarAccepts(cG, cGood)
#--> 1
? oO.Accepts(cGood)
#--> 1
? StzGrammarAccepts(cG, cBand)
#--> 1
? oO.Accepts(cBand)
#--> 0
? StzGrammarAccepts(cG, cShape)
#--> 0
? oO.Accepts(cShape)
#--> 0
? len(StzFind("lessequal", oO.UnenforcedByGrammar())) > 0
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

The fourth pair is the point of the chapter. I do not print the list of what the grammar dropped, because its text carries the former name of the court in one place. I test that it names the limit of the range.

A model is not asked to be careful. The sampler asks the grammar at every candidate byte whether it could continue. So a word that is not on the menu is not refused after it is said. It cannot begin:

```haro
? StzGrammarCouldContinue(cG, "dish: t")
#--> 1
? StzGrammarCouldContinue(cG, "dish: p")
#--> 0
? StzGrammarCouldContinue(cG, "dish: margherita" + nl + "quantity: 2")
#--> 1
? StzGrammarCouldContinue(cG, "dish: margherita" + nl + "quantity: <")
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

The last line is a failure the measurement found in a small model: the placeholder `<number>` echoed where a number belongs. And a token is not a byte. A piece may be valid as a prefix and invalid as a completion, and the machine is fed the whole piece:

```haro
cY = 'root ::= "yes" "\n"'
? StzGrammarCouldContinue(cY, "y")
#--> 1
? StzGrammarCouldContinue(cY, "ye")
#--> 1
? StzGrammarCouldContinue(cY, "yesterday")
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

What the machine will not hold, it refuses by name. A closed language refuses what it cannot carry, and never approximates. A left-recursive rule is refused by a code, and so is a rule referred to and never defined. A nested structure is refused, and the court still reads it:

```haro
? left(StzGrammarRefusal('root ::= tail' + nl + 'tail ::= tail "x" | "x"'), 7)
#--> GBNF-M7
? left(StzGrammarRefusal('root ::= greeting'), 7)
#--> GBNF-M4
? StzGrammarRefusal(cG) = ""
#--> 1
oNest = StzOutputSchemaQ([
	[ :field = "title",  :type = :string ],
	[ :field = "author", :type = :structure, :fields = [ [ :field = "name", :type = :string ] ] ]
])
? oNest.IsExpressibleAsGrammar()
#--> 0
try
	oNest.ToGBNF()
catch
	? len(StzFind("GBNF-F5", cCatchError)) > 0
done
#--> 1
? oNest.ParseOutput('{ "title":"T", "author":{"name":"Ada"} }')[:ok]
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

The refusal texts carry the former name of the court, so I print only the codes. The last line is the point: the court reads what the grammar refuses to write.

The declaration language is itself closed. A typo in the law is refused when the law is declared. The library's contract says why: a key such as `:requird = 0`, quietly leaving a field required, is exactly the class of typo a court must not tolerate in its own law:

```haro
try
	StzOutputSchemaQ([ [ :field = "x", :type = :string, :requird = 0 ] ])
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> stzOutputSchema: unknown key ':requird' in field #1 (use :field, :name, :type, :required, :optional, :choices, :oneof, :of, :fields, :must, :note, :description).
```

> run: kept

And a host function is not a word of the closed language of conditions. There are two ways to say a condition, the closed language and the door of a function. A square root is a word of the engine's expression language, and a sine is not. The textual form answers nothing, and the function form runs host code and answers everything:

```haro
o = new stzList([ 4, 7, 10, 3, 8 ])
? @@( o.FindW('{ sqrt(@item) < 3 }') )
#--> [ 1, 2, 4, 5 ]
? @@( o.FindW('{ sin(@item) < 2 }') )
#--> [ ]
? @@( o.FindWF( func x { return sin(x) < 2 } ) )
#--> [ 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 ]
```

> run: kept

What I saw, and the narration does not say: the refusal is silent and whole. A condition with the true left branch and a sine on the right printed `[ ]`. A condition that calls a function of mine never called it. A syntax error also printed `[ ]`. The language is closed, and it refuses without a word. Chapter 11's natural layer says why it refuses, and this one does not.

### The factory, outside the library

A language declared once should give its court, its mouth, its voice and its readers. That is the factory, and it is where the study of this book meets its own limit: the factory is not in the library.

I will say what the record holds, and what I observed on 8 October 2026 on the older runtime, the oracle. The record is a charter and a spike in another repository, in pure core code that runs on the oracle. A forge reads a declaration and writes two files, a court program and a grammar. The spike's rungs, between 1 and 4 October 2026, forged courts and grammars for the machine language of the estate, for a fleet language declared over it, for the expression language, and for four more letters of the alphabet. Each was judged by the pre-existing language's own fixtures, unadapted. 117 of 120 for the machine language, 32 of 32, 29 of 29, 16 of 16, 25 of 25, 26 of 26 and 35 of 35 for the others. This is chapter 3's method, declare then govern, applied to the factory itself.

I ran the forge on a small language I wrote for this book, a table's order in the words of the dining room. It is an example, written for the book and run once, outside the library:

```text
DEFINE LANGUAGE order AS ( VERSION "0.2", GRAMMAR "0.2", COURT "none", EXTENSION ".order" )
  RATIONALE "A table's order, in the words of the dining room"
DEFINE MENU dishes AS ( WORDS [margherita, tiramisu, chianti],
  MESSAGE "the kitchen cooks margherita, tiramisu or chianti" ) RATIONALE "The kitchen's closed menu"
DEFINE RANGE portions AS ( MIN 1, MAX 12,
  MESSAGE "{value} portions is not an order: one to twelve" ) RATIONALE "A table orders between one and twelve"
DEFINE DECLARATION line AS ( VERB "ORDER", SEATS [dish: dishes, quantity: portions, note: text?] )
  RATIONALE "One line of an order: what, how many, and a word for the kitchen"
DEFINE REFUSAL unknown_verb AS ( MESSAGE "The dining room speaks only of orders." )
  RATIONALE "The closure speaks when an alien verb arrives"
```

The forge printed that it had forged one kind and one refusal, in a quarter of a second of wall time, and wrote a court of about 2.6 kilobytes. The court refused three sentences in the declaration's own words:

```text
DISH pizza          ->  order (line 2): the kitchen cooks margherita, tiramisu or chianti, not 'pizza'
QUANTITY 40         ->  order (line 3): 40 portions is not an order: one to twelve
DROP TABLE orders   ->  order (line 1): Unknown verb 'DROP': The dining room speaks only of orders.
```

The refusals are the declaration's own words, written beside the grammar. [AUTHOR: May the chapter show this small `order` language, said plainly to be an example written for the book and run once, outside the library? Or do you prefer a declaration from the record, described and not shown?]

Three more observations, each a limit.

**The meta-court refuses the escape.** The declaration of the language of declarations lists the operators and operands it may use. A declaration that listed `eval` among its operators, in a fixture pinned on 29 August 2026, was refused: `eval` is not in the closed operator menu, an escape hatch is unexpressible, not forbidden. A host call among the operands was refused in the same way.

**The factory's grammar holds shape only.** A menu seat comes out as a general identifier, so the forged grammar lets `pizza` through. The library's own schema compiler, in the blocks above, closes the same menu into an alternation. On the estate's machine language, the forged grammar accepts a dangling reference that the court refuses. The record names a difference between mouth and court, the part that a context-free grammar cannot say: duplicate names, dangling references. I found no record that states the menu gap as a gap.

**The vision has not caught up.** The vision's text, dated between 27 September and 5 October 2026, says the forge has not forged. The charter's own first morning says that the forge does not exist. The same repository built the spike the same day and forged for several languages. So the true sentence has two sides: the factory has forged courts and grammars for several languages, outside the library; it has not forged the full list the principle promises; and the vision's text has not been updated. [AUTHOR: Shall chapter 12 say "built outside the library, designed for the library", and shall chapter 11's limit sentence be amended to match? I have amended it to say the sketch has since forged.]

### The facets a declared language wears

By facet I mean here a surface of a declared language. The charter lists what the forge produces from a declaration, and I give each a stage word:

| facet | what it is | stage |
|---|---|---|
| the text | the declaration, plain and closed, with a rationale required on every declaration | built outside; the library's analogue is the schema declaration |
| the court | the judge generated for the language, speaking in the declaration's own words | built outside; the library's analogue is `Verify` and `Accepts` |
| the mouth | the grammar for a constrained model | built in the library for schemas; built outside for declared languages, shape only |
| the prompt | the instruction a model reads, tuned by measurement | built in the library; not known for declared languages |
| the person's reading | a description for a human | built in the library (`Describe`) |
| the voice | the language narrating a run, a sentence, a refusal; never stored and never parsed back | built outside; the library's analogue is `Understood()` |
| the tongues | a human language as a data file | built outside for two tongues; built in the library for five packs (chapter 11) |
| the projections | the world rendered to an application, a server, a page, a device | designed |
| the faces | a second syntax of Haro | designed; chapter 16 tells it |

The word *facet* has other meanings in this book, and I define mine here: a surface of a declared language. The second sense is a syntax of Haro for a programmer of another language, which is chapter 16's. The third is a tool of the environment's ribbon, which is chapter 26's.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I decided that Haro is the language of languages.** The charter's header quotes me, from 1 October 2026: Haro is a language of languages, in which the crafters define a language for their domain and program in it, or let their agents. The charter records a reversal: its first decision, as the family's second language, was reversed. The rest of that ratification was my "go with your recommendations" on seven decisions, and those are rulings by delegation.

**I decided to declare, and not to write.** The act of the kernel: declare a language. It was extraction and not invention: the first languages were declared after they already ran and had consumers.

**I refused an open escape form.** No clause, operator or operand denotes host code, calls, evaluation or input and output. Unexpressible, not forbidden. Two fixtures pin the refusal, and I re-ran them.

**I refused logic in the declaring surface.** The declaration declares and does not compute. The alphabet is deliberately not Turing-complete, and that is its power and not its poverty.

**I refused natural language as the declaring surface.** Natural language asks, and it never declares. A draft may be proposed, and the court decides, and the human ratifies.

**I refused one text for declaring and for computing.** Programmability and closure never occupy the same surface. Computing code reaches a declared world only through the doors the forge generates, and chapter 16 is about the door.

**I refused to let the grammar half retire the court.** The contract says the court does not retire when constrained decoding lands. And what the grammar cannot express it refuses, and never flattens or approximates.

**I refused a second face as a second meaning.** A face spells the graph; it never gives it Python's or JavaScript's meaning. That is a session's memo, which proposes and rules nothing, and its question of how many faces before the announcement is mine, and unanswered in the record.

## 5. The court

`structuredoutput_narrated` proves that a declaration is judged when declared, with an unknown type, an unknown key and an empty menu refused; that two wire shapes are read and nothing is guessed; that there is no partial credit; that nesting works; that a retry fits in one budget; and, in its tenth scene, that a lie is let through on purpose. `schema_grammar_narrated` proves that the declaration compiles to a grammar, that a closed enumeration becomes a closed alternation, that optional and list arity are handled, that the unenforced constraints are listed by field and operator, and that a nested structure is refused with a named code. `constrained_decoding_narrated` proves that the machine walks a grammar byte by byte, that a prefix is not a whole, that `yesterday` is refused, that the placeholder the model wrote cannot be reached, that refusals are named, that structure is not falsehood, and that a grammar on a streaming session is refused. Its two scenes that need a generative model say so. `483_w_and_wf_forms_in_conditional_code` proves that the two forms of a condition give the same positions. It does not assert the closed language's refusal, and no guard does.

No guard in the library tests a factory, since there is none. The factory's guard is outside: the spike passes twenty-three stages in 367.5 seconds on 4 October 2026, by its own file. I did not run it. And the meta-court's own first run was twenty-eight of twenty-eight on 29 August 2026. The book's guard reader resolves only guards in the library's test tree, so these cannot be named in the front matter.

The figure that the first attempt is valid in two cases of ten when checked afterwards, and in ten of ten when constrained, is a number in a comment of these guards. It cannot be re-taken here: the live scenes need a generative model file, and the snapshot holds only a checksum file in its folder for models. Chapter 11 carries the figure, and I point to it.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The workbenches lead on tooling. The field study says it itself: they remain superior on the axes the design deliberately abandons, projectional tooling, editor integration and depth of macros, and the bet that those axes are the wrong ones is named as a bet.

The same flood of generated code cuts both ways: it makes undisciplined language-making cheap too, which is why the court is the core and not an accessory.

Constrained decoding: the field built it first, and the reference implementation is another library's. What the record adds is the pairing: a grammar and a court from one declaration, and the list of what the grammar dropped.

A grammar over-approximates a court. Duplicate names, dangling references and the coherence of a menu are not context-free. I observed it both ways above: a band the grammar cannot carry, and a reference it cannot carry.

The code a factory emits must be read by someone. The family style that every declared language inherits has a success test, that learning the second language should take an evening, because it is the first language wearing different verbs. It is a design requirement and not a measurement. No learner has been timed in the record I read.

## 7. The limit

The library has two closed formula languages, and they behave differently at the edge. A reader who learns "the library's formulas are closed" from chapter 11 and from the condition above will be wrong at the table. The calculated column of a table falls back, by its own header, to the classic evaluation path when the engine cannot compile a formula. The word `sin` that the condition refused runs here:

```haro
o1 = new stzTableAggregator([
	[ :A, [ 0, 1, 4 ] ],
	[ :B, [ 2, 4, 20 ] ]
])
o1.AddCalculatedCol(:SINE, 'sin(@(:A))')
? @@( o1.Col(:SINE) )
#--> [ 0, 0.84, -0.76 ]
```

> run: kept

I also saw, and do not show, that a formula `@(:A) + HostBonus(@(:B))` with a function of mine that multiplies by a thousand printed `[ 2001, 4003, 20010 ]`: a user-defined host function ran from inside a formula string. And a formula that names a function that does not exist raised the host's own error. So the second closed language is open at its edge. No guard asserts either behaviour.

What is built: in the library, a declaration judged when declared; a court and a grammar from it; the list of what the grammar dropped; a grammar machine that says it is constrained; a prompt and a description from the same text. 151 assertions in three narrated guards, as the build counts them. Two closed little languages of conditions, with limits. The natural layer's tongues as data.

What is built, but outside the library: a forge from a declaration to a court and a grammar, in the Haro charter's repository.

What is designed: the forge producing from a declaration alone the parser, court, printer, grammar, knowledge graph, query grammar, natural door, narration, runtime and one static binary. By my reading of the charter, the spike has shown the court, the grammar, a runtime that walks the declaration, a query grammar, a narration and the court of the natural letter. I did not find shown a printer for declared sentences, a knowledge graph forged from a declaration, or the one static binary per language. The library having a factory: nothing in it declares or forges a language. A grammar for every declared language shipping in the library: promised in the kernel, not shipped. And the projections: the world rendered to an application, a server, a page, a device.

What is absent: the figure of two of ten against ten of ten, re-taken; and any record that the factory runs on the Haro runtime. At the time of the vision's writing the language did not exist; the charter's runs are on the older runtime and on the oracle.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** You declare the shape in a few lines and get its court, its grammar and its prompt from them. What you do not get is a way to run a host function from the declaration: the language has no word for it.

**The analyst.** The declaration is plain text in the domain's words, and the court answers in those words.

**The designer.** The refusal is declared beside the grammar, in the domain's voice, and is not authored per runtime. A designer chooses what a closed menu says when it is broken.

**The decision-maker.** One declaration produces the mouth and the court, and the factory itself publishes where they disagree. The factory is outside the library today. Do not buy it as shipped.

**The educator.** The reader can predict, byte by byte, what the sampler will allow, and learns that a grammar kills malformedness and not falsehood.

**The agent.** You speak only the grammar you were handed. A sentence you draft is a proposal that the court judges. Your mouth is shape, and your falsehoods are the court's and a person's.

## 9. Exercise

Write, by hand, a grammar for one line of a lunch order: `dish: ` then one of two dishes of your choice, then a newline. Before you run anything, write your predictions as promises:

```haro exercise
cG = 'root ::= ...'     # your grammar here
? StzGrammarAccepts(cG, "dish: <your first dish>" + nl)
#--> ?
? StzGrammarAccepts(cG, "dish: <a dish not on your menu>" + nl)
#--> ?
? StzGrammarCouldContinue(cG, "dish: <the first letters of your first dish>")
#--> ?
? StzGrammarAccepts(cG, "dish: <the first letters of your first dish>")
#--> ?
? StzGrammarRefusal(cG) = ""
#--> ?
```

> run: exercise

Then add a number to the line, `quantity: ` and digits, and write a fifth prediction: does your grammar accept `quantity: 4000`? Run it. The lesson is the third and the fifth. The machine already knows that a prefix could continue and is not yet a sentence, and it cannot know that the number is too large. Write down, in one sentence, who in your restaurant must refuse forty portions, since the grammar cannot.

# Natural, and executable: two directions, one discipline

Source: Chapter 13. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: both directions: chains where the human is the parser, and sentences where the machine is; five language packs as data and a sixth added at runtime; refusals with reasons on both sides; question frames; a thin truth chain; natural code as data in documents and templates; designed: a grammar that holds a model to the lexicon; a guard that no socket opens during a natural run; the plan emitted as a chain; the article examples pinned as fixtures; absent: any machine that judges a chain's grammar; Ajami, Zarma and Fulfulde; a natural layer closed all the way down; a dictionary protected from the drift of the documentation it is read from; the old articles' promises, several of which do not run

Run: 17 of 17 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:25 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

In the field's common language, "programming in natural language" means one thing: asking a model for code, or asking it to do the task, in a prompt. The tools are chat assistants and code completers, in wide use since about 2021. What the sentence runs on is the model's guess. The same sentence may give different code on different days, the model needs a network or a local runtime, and a person reads the result to know what was understood. Chapter 11 said it, and I point back.

Older practices exist, and the record names them. Inform 7, a language whose programs are English sentences, compiled whole, with a world model and a way to say *it* across sentences. AppleScript, English-like and hard to write, because its naturalness was on the surface and not in a semantic model. Controlled natural languages, whose strongest device, the library's own comment says, is showing what can be said next. A session's reading of 30 August 2026 is that Inform 7 is a whole-text compiler with referential context, and not a command dispatcher. (General knowledge for the dates, not a study of the record.)

None of them is what the record claims. The claim is the opposite of prompting: a sentence executes because every word of it is in a closed lexicon, defined as data, mapped to a verb the library documents, and anything else is refused with the reason. And it has two directions, which the library's design review tabulates. In the first, the machine is the parser. In the second, the human is.

## 2. The first principle

**A sentence runs because every word of it is in a dictionary the library owns. What the library does not know, it refuses, with the reason.**

The study's thesis line for the page it asked for, and the lede of the site's page of 8 October 2026, say it. For the second direction, the design review's thesis: in near-natural language, the human is the parser. And the older motto, which chapter 5 carries: what you think is what you write.

## 3. The Softanza form

I start with the second direction, because it is the older.

### The human is the parser

A near-natural chain is a sentence in which every link is a real call. The sentence is "the word haro is a lowercase Latin word with a length of 4 letters and only 2 vowels". When it is false, the chain says why:

```haro
? TheWordQ("haro").IsAQ([ :Lowercase, :Latin, :Word ]).WithQ().ALengthQ().OfQ(4).UnitQ(:Letters).AndQ().OnlyQ(2).VowelNB()
#--> TRUE
? TheWordQ("haro").IsAQ([ :Lowercase, :Latin, :Word ]).WithQ().ALengthQ().OfQ(4).UnitQ(:Letters).AndQ().OnlyQ(1).VowelNB()
#--> FALSE
? Why()
#--> no: expected exactly 1, found 2
```

> run: kept

Degree words are devices, and the reason lives on the chain:

```haro
? Q("AnnIE").AtLeast(2).VowelNB()
#--> TRUE
? Q("AnnIE").AtMost(2).VowelNB()
#--> FALSE
? Why()
#--> no: expected atmost 2, found 3
```

> run: kept

The chain refuses what its subject cannot do. It speaks the same lexicon as the machine's direction: a French word works inside a chain. And a misspelled word is refused with the nearest word. This is "one lexicon, two directions" in three lines:

```haro
try
	Q(5).VowelNB()
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> NNL: a stznumber cannot count 'numberofvowels' -- and neither can the chain subject.
? Q("haro").IsAQ(:String).IfSo("majuscule").Content()
#--> HARO
try
	Q("haro").IsAQ(:String).IfSo("uppercse")
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> NNL: this stzstring does not understand 'uppercse'. Did you mean 'uppercase'?
```

> run: kept

A chain branches on its own truth, and an ordinal is a word:

```haro
? Q("haro").IsAQ(:String).IfSo(:Uppercase).Otherwise(:Reverse).Content()
#--> HARO
? Q("hello").IsAQ(:Number).IfSo(:Reverse).Otherwise(:Uppercase).Content()
#--> HELLO
? Q("hello brave world").TheNth(2, :Words)
#--> brave
```

> run: kept

Questions work the same way. A force word opens a frame, one word and one method. A tag question ends in "or not". The session keeps every answer and folds them, like a narration:

```haro
? @@( WhatQ().TheQ().VowelsQ().OfQ("AnnIE").Is() )
#--> [ "A", "I", "E" ]
? HowManyQ().WordsQ().Of("hello brave world")
#--> 3
ClearAnswers()
? IsQ().TheQ().LengthQ().OfQ("Haro").TheSameAsQ().TheQ().LengthQ().OfQ("Ruby").OrNot()
#--> 1
? Why()
#--> yes: 4 is the same as 4
? IsQ().TheQ().LengthQ().OfQ("Haro").TheSameAsQ().TheQ().LengthQ().OfQ("softanza").OrNot()
#--> 0
? Why()
#--> no: 4 is not the same as 8
? @@( AnswersSoFar() )
#--> [ 1, 0 ]
? AllYesSoFar()
#--> FALSE
? AnyYesSoFar()
#--> TRUE
```

> run: kept

### The machine is the parser

Now the other direction. The sentence is a string, and the library reads it. The same verdict can come from a narration of questions and from a chain of truth, and both say why and how sure:

```haro
oT = Naturally("Create a string with 'haro' Is it lowercase ? Does it contain 'r' ?")
? @@( oT.Answers() )
#--> [ 1, 1 ]
? oT.AllYes()
#--> TRUE
? oT.Understood()
#--> create a string with haro -> ask: is lowercase -> ask: contains
? TruthOf("haro").IsA(:Lowercase).AndIsA(:Latin).AndContaining("r").Holds()
#--> TRUE
? Why()
#--> yes: "haro" is lowercase and is latin and containing "r"
? Certainty()
#--> 1
? Evidentially()
#--> certainly
```

> run: kept

The whole paragraph is compiled into code before anything runs, and the code is readable. It is the library's own account of why the layer exists:

```haro
o1 = Naturally("Create a string with 'test.data' Replace '.' with '_' Uppercase it")
? o1.Result()
#--> TEST_DATA
? o1.Code()
#--> oStr = StzStringQ("test.data")
#    oStr.Replace(".", "_")
#    oStr.Uppercase()
#    @result = oStr.Content()
```

> run: kept

The same meaning in Hausa lands on the same code, and the program says back what it understood, in its own words. Hausa is written here without the hooked letter, as the pack reads it today, for a reason that chapter 11 gives:

```haro
oE = Naturally("Make a string with 'hello niger' inside Spacify it and uppercase it")
oH = NaturallyIn("hausa", "Yi rubutu da dauke 'hello niger' a ciki Raba shi kuma maida shi")
? oE.Code() = oH.Code()
#--> TRUE
? oH.Understood()
#--> yi rubutu dauke hello niger -> raba -> maida
? oE.Understood()
#--> create a string with hello niger -> spacify -> uppercase
```

> run: kept

The sentence-dictionary is not the only vocabulary. A word it does not hold is looked up in the library's own vocabulary, here the documentation tags of the uppercase verb. A value in quotes is data, and never a verb. An unknown word in a value position stays a literal:

```haro
? Naturally("Create a string with 'softanza' Capitals it").Result()
#--> SOFTANZA
? Naturally("Create a string with 'capitals' Uppercase it").Result()
#--> CAPITALS
? Naturally("Create a string with 'a-sep-b' Replace sep with '_'").Result()
#--> a-_-b
```

> run: kept

The third line is also a limit. The word `sep` was in no dictionary, and was used as the text to replace. A word it does not know is a literal.

**Four languages as data.** The dictionary is keyed by meaning, and each language's word lands on one id. French, Arabic and Turkish words land on the same one:

```haro
? StzResolveSemanticInLang("fr", "doublons")
#--> METHOD_REMOVEDUPLICATES
? StzResolveSemanticInLang("ar", "تكرارات")
#--> METHOD_REMOVEDUPLICATES
? StzResolveSemanticInLang("tr", "tekrarları")
#--> METHOD_REMOVEDUPLICATES
? StzResolveSemantic("removeduplicates")
#--> METHOD_REMOVEDUPLICATES
```

> run: kept

The languages follow their own grammar, and they are not word-for-word translations. Turkish puts the verb last, with the parameters before it. Arabic, with its vowel marks and a doubled letter, is stripped by the pack; the suffix on the verb is the masculine "it" for a text. French elides:

```haro
? NaturallyIn("tr", "'a.b' ile bir metin oluştur '.' yerine '_' koy").Result()
#--> a_b
? NaturallyIn("tr", "'haro' ile bir metin oluştur ve tersine çevir").Result()
#--> orah
? NaturallyIn("ar", "أنشئ نصا بـ 'haro' ثم اقلبه").Result()
#--> orah
? NaturallyIn("ar", "أنشئ نصًا بـ 'haro' ثمّ اقلِبْه").Result()
#--> orah
? NaturallyIn("ar", "أنشئ نصا بـ 'haro' ثم اقلبه").Understood()
#--> أنشئ سلسلة بـ haro -> اعكس
? NaturallyIn("fr", "Crée une chaine avec 'haro' et mets la à l'envers").Result()
#--> orah
```

> run: kept

No native reader has read these sentences. They are the guard's, and I do not know that a speaker has read them. [AUTHOR: Chapter 11 asked it of Hausa. Has a speaker read the Arabic, Turkish, French and Swahili sentences of the pack's guard?]

A language is a block of data. Here is Swahili, added at runtime, by the guard's own words:

```haro
StzAddNaturalLanguage([
	:code = "sw",
	:name = "swahili",
	:script = "latin",
	:ignored_words = [ "na", "ya", "kwa", "hii" ],
	:semantic_mappings = [
		[:natural = "unda",   :semantic = "CREATE_OBJECT"],
		[:natural = "orodha", :semantic = "OBJECT_LIST"],
		[:natural = "na",     :semantic = "VALUE_INDICATOR"],
		[:natural = "panga",  :semantic = "METHOD_SORT"]
	],

		[:semantic = "METHOD_REMOVEDUPLICATES", :words = "ondoa nakala, futa nakala"]
	]
])
? StzHasLanguagePack("sw")

osw = NaturallyIn("sw", "Unda orodha na [ 9, 4, 9 ] ondoa nakala")

#--> [ 9, 4 ]

#--> unda orodha na [ 9, 4, 9 ] -> ondoa nakala
```

Natural code is data too. A document's fenced blocks run, and a template is filled:

```haro
cDoc = "This tutorial explains deduplication." + nl +
	"#<" + nl +
	"Create a list with [ 3, 1, 3 ] and remove its duplicates" + nl +
	"#>" + nl +
	"The end."
? len( StzNaturalBlocks(cDoc) )
#--> 1
? @@( NaturallyFromDoc(cDoc)[1].Result() )
#--> [ 3, 1 ]
cT = "Create a list with {#1} and remove its duplicates"
? @@( NaturallyWith(cT, [ [ 5, 3, 5 ] ]).Result() )
#--> [ 5, 3 ]
? @@( NaturallyWith(cT, [ [ 9, 9, 4 ] ]).Result() )
#--> [ 9, 4 ]
```

> run: kept

**What the packs can say.** The packs are honest about how much they say. The library has a function for it, and no guard prints its figures. I count against twenty-five reference meanings, which are the structural ones and every method and modifier any shipped pack names. They are not the thousands of verbs the library grew:

```haro
for cLang in [ "en", "ha", "fr", "ar", "tr" ]
	aC = StzPackCoverage(cLang)
	? cLang + " " + aC[:covered] + " of " + aC[:total]
next

#    ha 9 of 25
#    fr 25 of 25

#    tr 10 of 25
? StzHasNeuralModel()
```
