> run: kept

The Hausa pack speaks nine of twenty-five meanings. And no model is loaded. The layer has an optional rescue by embedding, when nothing lexical was found and a model is loaded. At this commit none is, and none is needed. So I say "no model is loaded", and not "never touches a model".

### From the 2020 diagram to the three eras

The library names three eras, in a design document of 10 July 2026. The first is near-natural language, the first paradigm. The second is natural programming: lower the language toward code, with free text, and one object per block. The third, of 2026, is one lexicon shared by execution and documentation, multilingual packs with morphology, and `Understood()`. I call the third *the shared lexicon*. The vision's own heading for it uses a word this book does not use.

Here are the dates, as the record gives them. My strategic diagram carries the years 2020 to 2025, shared on 13 September 2026; its Narrations band reads "executable natural language". The deck of 2022 was exported on 20 September 2022, and its slide 2 says to express thinking in an executable natural language. The library's repository begins on 12 March 2022, and its history cannot date 2020 or 2021. The near-natural files and the engine at its present path were first committed between 16 and 19 September 2025. On 30 October 2025 languages became configuration. On 2 November 2025 string input replaced brace capture. On 9 July 2026 the lexicon was unified from the documentation tags. On 10 July 2026 the two design documents were written, and the audit of fifteen canonical examples found that four ran, and two of those answered correctly. The site's flagship page is of 8 October 2026. (The git dates come from the library's own repository, which the snapshot lacks, and all the commits cited are ancestors of the pin.) [AUTHOR: Which date does the chapter print for the first slide? The structure says "the 2020 slide". The record has the diagram of 2020 to 2025 and the deck of 2022; chapter 5 says "the founding slide of 2022".]

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I built it without a model, as a dictionary.** The design says everything in the natural folder attacks one wall: the semantic gap between a human intention and its execution. Its thesis: zero NLP, zero ML, native speed, full determinism, and the programmer in control. The layer quietly delivered a fourth promise that the old documents never dared to make: accountability.

**I decided on two directions, one discipline.** The review's table: who parses, a machine with a lexicon or the human; ambiguity, refused loudly or impossible by construction; vocabulary, bounded by packs or unbounded; understanding, verified by the machine or by a human reading; audience, domain experts and agents or programmers. Both end at the same place: a deterministic sequence of semantic operations.

**I refused markers on reference.** On 10 July 2026 I rejected the family of suffixes that marked a reference. The cipher was inverted by its own inventor from memory, it duplicated meanings, and language itself never marks reference this way. More than two hundred and sixty methods were removed. Chapter 11 tells it in two sentences.

**I refused the ungrammatical chain.** The chain is the sentence, and an ungrammatical chain that runs is a bug. The doctrine is held by reading aloud, not by a machine, and I show the proof below.

**I refused negation as a prefix.** Negation lives in negative forms, in antonym comparators and in the tag closer `OrNot()`. The tag question above is that ruling running.

**I refused silent tolerance.** The old typo tolerance was replaced by explaining everything: the refusal with the nearest word, above.

**I refused global context.** Two chains interleaved contaminated each other, and the fix put the context on the chain. The process-wide `Why()` remains as a convenience for the console and the tests.

**I refused the markup language.** Annotations that handed the machine what it could not infer governed by making the human annotate. The system governs by dialogue. Two seeds were kept as thin doors, and they are the document and the template above.

**I refused a command interpreter for narratives, and brace capture.** In my own words in the article: it is designed for commands, not narratives. And one object per block. The string form replaced the brace form, because natural code became a first-class data type, storable, versionable and searchable. The rule since relaxed: a program may name objects and switch between them.

**I refused governance inside the natural layer.** I ruled that scope out of the library on 12 July 2026, and the code was removed.

**I refused natural language as a way to declare.** It asks, and never declares.

## 5. The court

`nnl_narrated` proves the flagship sentence, the generated surface of nouns, the degree words, the conditional mood, the ordinal reference, the refusal that accounts for itself, the grammaticality doctrine, context on the chain, and the discourse tense. `multilingual_pack_narrated` proves packs as data, programs in French, Arabic and Turkish that run, verified affix stripping, a language added at runtime, and `Understood()` back in the program's language. `question_narrated` proves the question frames and that a frame with no aspect is refused. `literate_templates_narrated`, the documents and templates. `truthchain_narrated` and `interrogative_narrated`, the folded verdicts.

`semantic_resolver_narrated` is the guard that ought to protect the dictionary, and at this commit it fails. I ran it on 9 October 2026 and it stops with an error; a researcher's run on 8 October counted three assertions failed, and the run ended after the twenty-ninth. The word "caps", which the guard asserts, now resolves to a method for bounds, and "mirrored" resolves to nothing. The reason is the design's own: the vocabulary is read from the library's documentation tags, plus seventeen hand-written operations grown by reflection to about three thousand, so a documentation edit changes what a word means. The library refuses honestly in the second case: `Unresolved()` names the word and `UnderstoodAll()` prints 0. The court of the dictionary has failed on the dictionary, and chapter 11, chapter 22 and chapter 2 name this guard.

The other guards named above were run once each by a researcher on 8 October 2026, and passed all their assertions. The sum of the assertion calls in the eleven narrated guards of this form is 454.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in breadth of phrasing. A model accepts any phrasing in any language, and the dictionary accepts what the packs name. Measured above: the Hausa pack covers nine of twenty-five meanings, Turkish ten, Arabic sixteen, English twenty-three, French twenty-five. And the sentence layer acts on three classes only: strings, lists and numbers. A customer's domain objects are not among them; the domain goes through the knowledge graph.

The field leads in whole-program semantics. Inform 7 compiles a whole world. Here a program is a sequence of operations on a live object.

The field leads in an editor. The suggestion function answers nine continuations for a half-written sentence, and the code credits the controlled-language field for the idea. There is no editor here that shows them as you type.

And there is a loss on the library's own side. `Understood()` prints verbs and not their arguments: `Replace` prints as `replace`, and `Add 3 to it` prints as `add` while the result was 8. A reader who checks the machine's reading sees no 3. This qualifies chapter 11's claim that a person can check what the machine understood.

Where the win is, with the loss beside it: the same sentence gives the same answer, with a reason, offline, in the user's language. The loss is that the user's language must be in a pack, and a word outside is a literal or a refusal. The record says the winning case is regulated or sovereign work: an instruction that executes locally and can be audited to the word.

## 7. The limit

An ungrammatical chain runs. The second chain below reads "which a length of 4", which is not English, and nothing judges it:

```haro
? TheWordQ("haro").IsAQ(:Word).WhichQ().HasQ().ALengthQ().Of(4)
#--> TRUE
? TheWordQ("haro").IsAQ(:Word).WhichQ().ALengthQ().Of(4)
#--> TRUE
```

> run: kept

The doctrine is that reviewing a chain is reading it aloud as English. It is the human's job.

A chain's descriptor reaches host code. `IsAXT` builds a string and evaluates it, so a function defined by the program and named in the string ran. This is the residue that the study of the record and chapter 6 name:

```haro
? Q("haro").IsAXT([ "String('x') and HostRan() and @isString" ])
#--> host code ran
#    TRUE

func HostRan()
	? "host code ran"
	return 1
```

> run: kept

And a word of the old era is reported, and never guessed. Permissive mode still runs the rest, and strict mode refuses. The suggestion offered, `addn`, is a poor one:

```haro
o = Naturally("Create a string with 'softanza' Spacify it and_ Uppercase it")
? o.Result()
#--> S O F T A N Z A
? o.UnderstoodAll()
#--> FALSE
? @@( o.Unresolved() )
#--> [ [ "and_", "addn" ] ]
try
	NaturallyStrict("Create a string with 'softanza' Spacify it and_ Uppercase it")
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Strict natural mode: not understood: 'and_' (did you mean 'addn'?)
```

> run: kept

**The old articles have decayed.** The 2020 sentence needs a marker that I removed by my ruling of 10 July 2026. Several chains of the near-natural article do not run at this commit: `InLowercase()` raises for lack of an object; `LettersNB`, `BeforeQ`, `TheirQ().FirstCharQ()` and `ReplaceLowercaseCharsQ` are not defined; and the brace form `Naturally() { ... }` raises for too few parameters. Of the twelve chains of that article that I ran, eight work. The bridging article's paragraph still runs in permissive mode, and prints two nested boxes around "NOTHING SOFTANZA" instead of the one it promises. The machine said so, since `UnderstoodAll()` is 0 and the unresolved word is named, but a reader has to ask.

What is built: both directions; five packs as data and a sixth at runtime; refusals with reasons on both sides; the question frames, the thin truth chain, the evidential register; natural code as data. The folder holds thirteen files and about twelve thousand lines in twelve classes, by `wc` and `grep` on 8 October 2026.

What is designed: the plan of a narration emitted as an executable chain, so that chains gain `Understood()` for free. A grammar emitted for the natural lexicon, so that a model may only produce sentences the layer executes: I searched the folder, and no grammar emitter touches it. A guard that fails if a socket opens during a natural run, which does not exist. A coverage figure per pack printed by a guard. And the articles' examples pinned as fixtures.

What is absent: Hausa in the Ajami script, Zarma and Fulfulde. A natural layer closed all the way down: a plain list in a natural sentence reaches host code, which chapter 11 prints, and a descriptor reaches host code, above. Any machine that judges a chain's grammar. The standard Hausa letter, which the pack stores broken. A dictionary protected from drift. [AUTHOR: May the chapter tell your plea to the project of the former host language in your own words? The record has no sentence of yours in that voice, only a private memo of 30 August 2026 that quotes "seductive at first, unusable after".]

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Write the chain as the sentence and read it aloud. The library refuses what its subject cannot do, and says why. You are the parser, so the sentence is only as grammatical as you are.

**The analyst.** A requirement can be a paragraph that runs, with a paraphrase that comes back. Today it acts on strings, lists and numbers, so the analyst's domain words go in a knowledge file (chapter 14), not here.

**The designer.** A designer's tone is in the refusals: a message names the word and the nearest word. The record has no designer-specific evidence for this chapter, and I keep the line short.

**The decision-maker.** Ask any "natural language" claim what it does with a word it does not know. Here it reports it, refuses it in strict mode, or takes it as a literal, and says which. It runs offline with no key. The no-network claim is not yet measured.

**The educator.** A learner writes in Hausa, French, Arabic or Turkish, and the program says back what it understood in the same language. The Hausa pack speaks nine of twenty-five meanings, and no reviewer has read the course's other-language editions.

**The agent.** Speak the lexicon. Use strict mode and an allow-list (chapter 11). Expect refusal with a reason. Your sentence is a proposal. A grammar that would hold you to the lexicon is designed and not built.

## 9. Exercise

Take one sentence you say about a word in your own language: that it is lowercase, has a given length, and a given number of vowels. Write it twice before you run anything. First as a near-natural chain, where you are the parser. Second as a `Naturally` sentence, where the library is. Under each, write the line you expect it to print.

```haro exercise
# 1. as a chain, where you are the parser:
# 2. as a Naturally sentence, where the library is:
```

> run: exercise

Then run both. Then change one word of the chain, and one word of the sentence, to a word the dictionary does not hold. Which direction told you? Which did you have to tell yourself? In one direction the machine tells you, and in the other you must tell yourself. If a promise breaks, do not change it: explain the output first.

# Knowledge programming

Source: Chapter 14. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: a system as a plain text file of facts, with laws (unique, transitive, symmetric) and the source of each fact; Prove with its steps, a no as certain as a yes; strict mode; the file written, read back and shipped, from a course catalogue to an agent's memory; designed: rules in the file; derivation on any graph; a query that joins; several relations between two things; class inheritance; validation on load; absent: the 2022 founding dialog, which does not run; Explain on a knowledge graph; the rules section of the file, which the reader skips

Run: 13 of 13 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:25 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

Three practices of the field meet in this idea, and none of them is the same as it.

The first is the semantic stack: RDF, OWL and SPARQL. A fact is a triple of subject, predicate and object, written in a shared vocabulary. An ontology says what classes and properties exist and what follows from them, and a reasoner derives the consequences. These are W3C recommendations, of 2014, 2009 and 2013. What the field asks of the author of a knowledge base is an ontology language of their own, a store and a query language, usually beside the application and not in it.

The second is the property-graph database, with its query language. The record has one comparison that names it, and it is a model's text kept in the library: it credits the field with scale, persistence and transactions.

The third is rules over facts, in Prolog and in Datalog. Facts and rules in one program, a query by resolution. The record knows it as a language to reach, and not to rebuild.

A fourth practice stands behind the contrast, and the design document names it only to refuse it: retrieval, a pile of document fragments searched by similarity. (General knowledge, not a study of the record. Check the dates before print.)

So in the field the knowledge lives in a store and its logic in a reasoner, both beside the program. Here the knowledge is a plain text file that the program loads.

## 2. The first principle

**A system is a file. Adding a line to it makes the system know more, and nothing is coded or trained.**

Two sentences of the record carry it. The definition, in the design document written from my directive of 13 July 2026: intelligence is the computational ability to derive new knowledge from existing knowledge. And what the 2026 vision keeps from it: adding one fact augments the system's intelligence, with no code change and no training. Chapter 17 opened on the first, and I open here on the second. [AUTHOR: The record holds the sentence and not the story. Is there a moment, before July 2026, when you first wrote that a business could describe itself in one file, and that adding a fact would be enough?]

## 3. The Softanza form

Here is a system as a text. Two facts: flour is made from millet, and couscous from flour. Asked whether couscous is made from millet, the graph says it cannot derive it. Then one line is added to the file, a law saying that *made from* is transitive. The same two facts now prove the chain:

```haro
cFacts = 'knowledge "coop"' + nl + nl + "facts" + nl +
         "    flour | made-from | millet" + nl + "    couscous | made-from | flour" + nl
oA = new stzKnowledgeGraph("coop")
oA.ImportKnow(cFacts)
? oA.Prove([ "couscous", "made-from", "millet" ])[:narration]
#--> not derivable: no recorded fact or lawful chain gives 'couscous' made-from 'millet'
oB = new stzKnowledgeGraph("coop")
oB.ImportKnow(cFacts + nl + "ontology" + nl + "    made-from | transitive" + nl)
? oB.Prove([ "couscous", "made-from", "millet" ])[:narration]
#--> proved: couscous made-from flour made-from millet
```

> run: kept

The text is the program. The same two facts, one extra line, a different capability. The block does not touch the disk: `ImportKnow` takes a text, and takes a path when the string ends in the file's extension.

Now add a fact and nothing else. The chain gets longer, and the proof writes its steps out. The graph without the law, which also never received the fact, still says no, and I want you to notice that this no has two causes:

```haro
oB.KnowRelation("porridge", "made-from", "couscous")
? oB.Prove([ "porridge", "made-from", "millet" ])[:narration]
#--> proved: porridge made-from couscous made-from flour made-from millet
? oA.Prove([ "porridge", "made-from", "millet" ])[:narration]
#--> not derivable: no recorded fact or lawful chain gives 'porridge' made-from 'millet'
```

> run: kept

A refusal is as certain as a proof. The library's comment on `Prove` says it: a certain no is still certain.

The same growth reaches the domain model of chapter 19. Nothing is trained. But a model forged from a graph is a snapshot: the second answer below is the old model still refusing, and only a model forged again knows the new fact:

```haro
oKB = new stzKnowledgeGraph("coop")
oKB.Know("couscous", "dish").KnowRelation("couscous", "contains", "semolina")
oDLM = StzDlmQ(oKB)
? oDLM.Ask("what is millet?")
#--> That is outside the 'coop' domain (I know 1 entities and 1 relations).
oKB.Know("millet", "grain").KnowRelation("couscous", "contains", "millet")
? oDLM.Ask("what is millet?")
#--> That is outside the 'coop' domain (I know 1 entities and 1 relations).
oDLM = StzDlmQ(oKB)
? oDLM.Ask("what is millet?")
#--> Millet is a grain.
? oDLM.Ask("couscous contains what?")
#--> Couscous contains semolina and millet.
```

> run: kept

**The file the library writes.** A clinic. A relation declared unique, and a graph in strict mode, where every fact needs its provenance. The first fact is refused for lacking one. Then a fact with its source and confidence is admitted. A second, contradicting one from a rumour, is refused, and the refusal is kept:

```haro
oCl = new stzKnowledgeGraph("clinic")
oCl.ConstrainRelation("treated-by", :Unique)
oCl.EnableStrictMode()
try
	oCl.AddFact("fatou", "treated-by", "dr-diallo")
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Strict mode: every fact needs provenance -- use AddFactXT(s, p, o, [ :source = ..., :confidence = ... ]).
oCl.AddFactXT("fatou", "treated-by", "dr-diallo", [ :source = "registry", :confidence = 0.95 ])
? oCl.AddFactXT("fatou", "treated-by", "dr-sani", [ :source = "rumour", :confidence = 0.2 ])
#--> 0
oCl.WriteToKnowFile("clinic")
? read("clinic.zknw")
#--> knowledge "clinic"
#
#    facts
#        fatou | treated-by | dr-diallo
#
#    provenance
#        fatou | treated-by | dr-diallo | source | text | registry
#        fatou | treated-by | dr-diallo | confidence | number | 0.95
#
#    contradictions
#        fatou | treated-by | dr-diallo | dr-sani | rumour
#
#    ontology
#        treated-by | unique
```

> run: kept (touches files)

That is what the library writes: a header, then up to four sections in this order, the facts, the provenance when a fact carries any, the contradictions when one was refused, and the ontology when a law was declared. One line per record, fields separated by a bar. A bar or a line break inside a value is escaped. The refused rumour sits in the file, named, and never resolved. The code comment on the contradiction list says it: named, never silently resolved.

The file reads back into a strict graph. The law, the source of every fact and the refusal travel inside it:

```haro
oBack = new stzKnowledgeGraph("clinic")
oBack.EnableStrictMode()
aR = oBack.ImportKnow("clinic.zknw")
? aR[:merged]
#--> 1
? @@( oBack.MetaOfFact("fatou", "treated-by", "dr-diallo") )
#--> [ [ "source", "registry" ], [ "confidence", 0.95 ] ]
? len(oBack.Contradictions())
#--> 1
? oBack.RelationHasLaw("treated-by", "unique")
#--> 1
remove("clinic.zknw")
? fexists("clinic.zknw")
#--> 0
```

> run: kept (touches files)

A file of this kind was once written without its provenance. A session found it: the export wrote only the bare triples, so knowledge read back from the disk no longer said where it came from, and a strict graph could not load its own export. The fix is the two sections above. The guard that proves it is named in section 5.

**A law that fires when a fact is added.** On the library's default world, the one behind the natural-language sugar, a symmetric law derives the reverse fact as the first one is added, with no call to a derivation step. Nobody wrote that Ibrahim is married to Amina:

```haro
StzConstrainRelation("married-to", :Symmetric)
StzKnowRelation("amina", "married-to", "ibrahim")
? DefaultKnowledgeGraph().Query([ "ibrahim", "married-to", "amina" ])
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

This is the one place where "adding a fact fires a rule" runs at this commit, and it runs only on the default world. I show the same law on an instance graph in section 7, where it does not.

**A system at scale.** The education program of the library is a knowledge file. Its catalogue, `program.zknw`, says what the program is called and which courses and languages it has:

```haro
oP = StzProgramQ("../../education/program")
? oP.Id()
#--> softanza-education
? @@( oP.Languages() )
#--> [ "en", "fr", "ar", "ha" ]
```

> run: kept

Adding a course is adding a line there, and the guard checks that every declared course is a folder and every folder is declared. Under that one catalogue the snapshot holds ninety-nine knowledge files, counted with `find` on 8 October 2026: courses, exercises, worlds and skills, a project world for a learner, and the overlays an institution lays over the core. An agent's memory is also a knowledge graph, saved through the same format, with provenance.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I decided that a business describes itself in one file, and that the file is the program.** The design document records my directive: entities, relations, an ontology of domain-specific semantics, and rules, in a file that is readable, diffable and hand-editable, validated on load. The last two words I have not yet met, and section 7 says where.

**I decided on four-letter extensions.** `.zknw` for knowledge, and the others of the family; the old spellings still read. The snapshot writes `.zknw` and still reads `.stzknow`. The file of rules was not renamed: the graph still writes and reads `.stzrulz`, though the family names four letters for it. I report it as the record has it.

**I refused a parallel world.** The natural layer once held its entities in globals, and the design document called it the wrong home, the wrong shape and a parallel world. The resolution is one default graph standing behind the natural-language sugar. The law behind it is that naked domain globals are forbidden; a global form may exist only as thin sugar over one default instance. So the dialog of the symmetric law above is sugar over the same graph that the first blocks used.

**I refused to resolve a contradiction in silence.** Strict mode, where every fact needs its provenance, is opt-in knowledge hygiene.

**I refused retrieval.** The design document says: no code change, no training, no RAG machinery. It is the same refusal as chapter 17's.

## 5. The court

`knowledge_integration_narrated` proves that the world answers `WhatIs`, that the graph grew alongside it, and that the law lives in the ontology; that `Prove` narrates the law first and then the links of the chain, with certainty 1, and refuses what it cannot prove; that the file round-trips; that a symmetric law derives the reverse edge when a fact is added through the graph; and that strict mode admits a provenanced fact, refuses a naked one, and refuses and records a unique contradiction. `provenance_survives_save_narrated` proves that a fact's source and confidence survive a save; that a strict graph loads its own export; that a refused contradiction survives; that a bar or a line break in a value is data; that an old file with no provenance still loads; and that an agent's memory records who learned a fact, and when. `program_narrated` proves that the program counts its courses, that every course declared is a folder and every folder is declared, and that the texts read the same from a checkout with other line endings.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The record's own graph comparison says where the traditional solutions win: large-scale systems that need distributed storage and processing, and performance-critical applications. Its table puts Softanza at business scale, in the hundreds to thousands of nodes, in a single process. That is the fair summary of the loss: the field leads in scale, persistence, transactions and tooling. (The comparison's row for persistence predates the file; the knowledge graph now writes one.)

The field leads in standards and reach, in general knowledge. A knowledge base in RDF can be shared with any tool that reads the W3C formats, linked to public vocabularies and queried across stores. A knowledge file is read by this library and by nothing else. A reasoner for a rich ontology language does what three laws and a class declaration do not. The field leads in the expressiveness of rules: Prolog and Datalog derive new facts from arbitrary rules, and here one law kind fires, and only on the default world. The field leads in multi-step queries, since SPARQL and Cypher join patterns. And in many relations between two things.

Where I think this stands apart, with the measured limits beside it: the knowledge is a text file a person can read, edit, diff and ship with the program. Every answer is provable with its steps, and a no is as certain as a yes. What a fact claims travels with its source and its refusals. And the same file runs a course, a learner's project and an agent's memory. None of that needs a store, a server, a model or a training run.

## 7. The limit

The limit of this chapter is the one the structure line already carries: *entities, relations, an ontology, rules*. The file at this commit has no rules. Here is the file with a rules section, and what the reader makes of it:

```haro
cRules = 'knowledge "pairs"' + nl + nl + "facts" + nl + "    margherita | pairs-with | chianti" + nl + nl +
         "rules" + nl + "    pairs-with | symmetric" + nl
oRul = new stzKnowledgeGraph("pairs")
aM = oRul.ImportKnow(cRules)
? aM[:merged]
#--> 1
? len(oRul.Ontology())
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

The parser recognises the header `rules`, and has a handler for none of the lines under it. They are dropped, and no refusal comes back. The same law under the header `ontology` gives an ontology of length 1. Rules live elsewhere, as data in a rule attached to a graph, which are chapters 29 and 30, and in a separate file that names rule functions. In this file there are facts and laws. I print "facts and laws", and I send the rules to those two chapters. [AUTHOR: May the chapter say "facts and laws" for what the file holds, or must it promise the single file with rules and print the gap as its limit?]

The law is also not derived on an instance graph. The same symmetric law, the same fact as the default world, and the reverse is not there:

```haro
oSy = new stzKnowledgeGraph("pairs")
oSy.ConstrainRelation("pairs-with", :Symmetric)
oSy.KnowRelation("margherita", "pairs-with", "chianti")
? oSy.RelationHasLaw("pairs-with", "symmetric")
#--> 1
? oSy.Query([ "chianti", "pairs-with", "margherita" ])
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

The class header says it: only unique and transitive are acted on here; symmetric is only recorded. Two more limits, each with its run.

**One relation between two things.** The graph stores one edge per node pair. A second relation between the same two entities is refused with the library's own sentence:

```haro
oShop = new stzKnowledgeGraph("shop")
oShop.KnowRelation("amina", "sells", "bread")
aV = oShop.Admit("amina", "buys", "bread", [ :source = "till" ])
? aV[:admitted]
#--> 0
? aV[:why]
#--> Can't add fact: 'amina' and 'bread' already carry the edge 'sells' (stzGraph stores one edge per node pair).
```

> run: kept

A knowledge model that cannot hold *sells* and *buys* between the same two entities is a real limit. The code says it: a known limitation, to revisit with multi-edge support.

**A two-step question answers only its first step.** `QueryPath` ignores the rest of its patterns, so it is no multi-step query yet, and the predicate of a query cannot be a variable:

```haro
oQ = new stzKnowledgeGraph("coop2")
oQ.KnowRelation("amina", "sells", "couscous").KnowRelation("couscous", "made-from", "flour")
? @@( oQ.QueryPath([ [ "amina", "sells", "?p" ], [ "?p", "made-from", "?m" ] ]) )
#--> [ "couscous" ]
? @@( oQ.Query([ "amina", "?p", "?o" ]) )
#--> [ ]
```

> run: kept

And the reader is silent about a malformed line. Four lines, three of them not facts: two fields, four fields, and no bars. All three vanish and nothing is refused:

```haro
cBad = 'knowledge "t"' + nl + nl + "facts" + nl + "    a | likes | b" + nl + "    c | likes" + nl +
       "    d | likes | e | f" + nl + "    g likes h" + nl
oT = new stzKnowledgeGraph("t")
aBad = oT.ImportKnow(cBad)
? aBad[:merged]
#--> 1
? @@(aBad[:refused])
#--> [ ]
```

> run: kept

The design document promises that the file is "validated on load". At this commit the parser keeps only a line with exactly three fields.

**The 2022 founding dialog.** The narration tells knowledge programming through a dialog: an apple as a fruit and as a company, an owner, relations asked as questions. At this commit its first line does not run. The text, which I show and do not run, is `@Q("Apple").IsA(:Fruit)`, and it stops with "Calling Function without definition: @isfruit". The string class reads the predicate as natural language and looks for a function by that name. Two other forms stop elsewhere. The narration's promise lines are stale. The nearest form that runs keeps its point, one name with two meanings, both known:

```haro
StzKnow(:Apple, :Company)
StzKnow(:Apple, :Fruit)
? @@( WhatIs(:Apple) )
#--> [ "company", "fruit" ]
```

> run: kept

The study of the record already says the dialog was kept at the margin, and that the centre moved to the file. [AUTHOR: Should the chapter open on the 2022 dialog as history, with its failure shown beside the runnable form, or on the file? I opened on the file.]

What is designed: rules in the file; derivation on any graph; a multi-step query; several relations between two things; class inheritance. `DefineClass` writes a fact, and no inheritance follows. The nearest that runs is to declare `is-a` transitive and state the class as a fact of that relation. And the design's knowledge hygiene beyond provenance, bounded queries, scoped graphs and revision with rollback, has no method of that name.

What is absent or broken: `Explain()` on a knowledge graph, which calls a function defined nowhere; and `ValidateOntology()`, which returns 1 whatever the ontology holds, as its own comment says: a known defect.

One note on the design document's history. Its amendment of 22 August 2026 says that no knowledge file exists anywhere in the tree, and that this is hygiene and not a gap. At this commit that is no longer true: there are ninety-nine, all of them in the education program.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** One class and a text file: `Know`, `KnowRelation`, `ConstrainRelation`, `Prove`, `Query`, `WriteToKnowFile`, `ImportKnow`. The file is diffable and ships with the program.

**The analyst.** The offering page names it: what you declare is your organisation's entities, rules and flows, in a plain-text knowledge file. Read "rules" as the limit above. Where to start, in the course: the chapter called "Teach a world".

**The designer.** The record is silent. Neither the offering page nor the study ties the knowledge file to the designer's door, and I do not invent one.

**The decision-maker.** The file is plain text that a manager can read. Every fact can carry a source and a confidence. A refused claim is kept, named and never resolved. An answer comes with its proof, and a no is certain. It runs offline, with nothing to subscribe to.

**The educator.** The whole education program is knowledge files. A learner teaches a world, then questions it, and the project's guard checks the world file, the agent file and one printed answer. An institution adapts by overlay, never by fork.

**The agent.** Your memory is a knowledge graph with provenance, and your world is a file you load and query.

## 9. Exercise

Here is a world with two facts, and a question it cannot answer yet. Your promise is the line proving that class 3b is part of primary, with the chain written out. Change the world, and not the question.

```haro exercise
cWorld = 'knowledge "school"' + nl + nl + "facts" + nl +
         "    class-3b | part-of | grade-3" + nl +
         "    grade-3 | part-of | primary" + nl
oW = new stzKnowledgeGraph("school")
oW.ImportKnow(cWorld)
? oW.Prove([ "class-3b", "part-of", "primary" ])[:narration]
# this prints a refusal. Your promise: the line proving that class-3b is part of primary,
# with the chain written out. Change the world, not the question.
```

> run: exercise

Run it and see what prints. If you change the question to ask about grade 3, it will prove something, and it will not be the line you promised. Then ask the reverse question, whether primary is part of class 3b, once your world has its law. Does the law add a fact in the wrong direction? I do not tell you the line to add.

# Pattern languages

Source: Chapter 15. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: patterns for text, numbers and tables, each with a guard; the same small habit across the media (a constructor, a pattern in a string, Match, Tokens); designed: one pattern engine shared by every medium; the pattern as the condition side of the cascade; the pattern written from examples; absent: the refusal of a word the pattern language does not know, in three of the media; sequences over a timeline in time; a narration and a small function for graphs; the genetic, linguistic and quantic siblings

Run: 13 of 13 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:25 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

For a working programmer, a pattern is a regular expression: a compact notation for the shape of a string, handed to an engine that says whether the text fits, and where. The engines are old and well known. The one beneath this library is PCRE2, and the site measures it against two others.

For data that is not text, the field uses other tools, one per medium and none shared. Structural pattern matching in the languages of the ML family, in Rust, and since Python 3.10 in Python. Schema checks for lists and records. Data-quality libraries for tables. Row pattern recognition in SQL, and stream engines, for sequences over time. And graph query languages, whose core is a path pattern. (General knowledge, well established; the record holds no dated study of any of them, and I invent none.)

The estate's own field study of August 2026 gives the small languages that survived a profile: a closed grammar, one domain question, plain text, a tiny surface, a specification with a conformance suite, decades of stability. A pattern language is exactly that. One question, does this data fit, in a few characters. What the field does not offer is the same notation across the media.

## 2. The first principle

**A pattern is a rule about the shape of data, written as data. The same habit serves every kind of data.**

The sentence the library's articles return to, in each medium, is the one in the article on numbers: instead of instructing the machine how to test a condition, you state what you mean. The record also has the superlative, that this is done for the first time in the history of computer science. The study refused it, and so do I. The claim the study allows is the measured one: one regex-shaped grammar across seven kinds of data in one library, with the same small-function habit. And I will show you at once that the grammar is not quite one. [AUTHOR: "For the first time in computer science history" is a phrase I could not find in any file. Is it yours, from which text, and may the chapter print it with the study's caution or leave it out?]

## 3. The Softanza form

A pattern called by name is a closed catalogue. The library has a data file of named patterns, and asking for a name it does not hold is refused:

```haro
? rx(pat(:isoDate)).Match("2023-12-25")
#--> 1
? rx(pat(:isoDate)).Match("25/12/2023")
#--> 0
? rx(pat(:isoDate)).Explain()
#--> Matches ISO 8601 date format (YYYY-MM-DD)
try
	pat(:banana)
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> The pattern name you provided does not exist in stzRegexData file.
```

> run: kept

A pattern can also answer the half-written value, which is what a form wants as the person types:

```haro
o1 = new stzRegex("^\d{3}-\d{2}-\d{4}$")
? o1.MatchAsYouType("123-45")
#--> 1
? o1.MatchAsYouType("abc")
#--> 0
? o1.IsPartialMatch("123-45")
#--> 1
? o1.IsPartialMatch("123-45-6789")
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

Now the move that gives the chapter its title. Take a list. Its pattern is written in square brackets, and counts, nesting and negation are written, not looped. `@N` stands for a number, `@S` for a string, `+` for one or more, `@N1-3` for one to three, and `@!` for not:

```haro
? Lx("[@N+, @S]").Match([1, 2, 3, "end"])
#--> 1
? Lx("[@N1-3, @S]").Match([1, 2, 3, 4, "end"])
#--> 0
? Lx("[@S, [@N+]]").Match(["tea", [1, 2, 3]])
#--> 1
? Lx("[@S, [@N+]]").Match(["tea", ["x"]])
#--> 0
? Lx("[@!N, @N]").Match(["a", 1])
#--> 1
? Lx("[@!N, @N]").Match([1, 1])
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

Every call here uses a fresh object, for a reason that section 7 gives. Chapter 11 showed four calls of the same language, and I do not repeat them.

The numbers have properties, and a property is meaning, not procedure. The last line turns a pattern from a test into a search:

```haro
? Nx("{@Property(Even) & @Property(Prime)}").Match(2)
#--> 1
? Nx("{@Property(Even) & @Property(Prime)}").Match(4)
#--> 0
? Nx("{@Digit(:unique)+}").Match(1234)
#--> 1
? Nx("{@Digit(:unique)+}").Match(1223)
#--> 0
? Nx("{@Relation(Mod:5=0)}").Match(10)
#--> 1
? @@( Nx("{@Property(Perfect)}").MatchingNumbersBetween(1, 500) )
#--> [ 6, 28, 496 ]
```

> run: kept

Tables: a data-quality rule is a sentence of the language. The person who owns the data can read it. Niamey appears twice, so the cities are not unique, and the negation of that is true:

```haro
oT = new stzTable([
	[ :ID, :NAME, :AGE, :CITY ],
	[ 1, "Ali", 28, "Niamey" ],
	[ 2, "Sara", 32, "Paris" ],
	[ 3, "Omar", 41, "Niamey" ],
	[ 4, "Awa", 25, "Dakar" ]
])
? Tbx("{cols(4) & unique(id) & avgcol(age:>30)}").Match(oT)
#--> 1
? Tbx("{cols(4) & unique(city)}").Match(oT)
#--> 0
? Tbx("{@!unique(city)}").Match(oT)
#--> 1
? Tbx("{sorted(id) & numeric(age) & alphabetic(name)}").Match(oT)
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

A pattern is data, so patterns compose: one is built from patterns.

```haro
oA = new stzTablex("{cols(4)}")
oB = new stzTablex("{rows(4)}")
oAnd = oA.And_(oB)
? oAnd.Pattern()
#--> {cols(4) & rows(4)}
? oAnd.Match(oT)
#--> 1
? oA.Not_().Pattern()
#--> {@!cols(4)}
? oA.Not_().Match(oT)
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

A matrix: a concept, square and symmetric, is one line.

```haro
? Mx("{shape(square) & property(symmetric)}").Match([ [1,2,3], [2,4,5], [3,5,6] ])
#--> 1
? Mx("{shape(square) & property(symmetric)}").Match([ [1,2,3], [4,5,6], [7,8,9] ])
#--> 0
? Mx("{size(2x3)}").Match([ [1,2,3], [4,5,6] ])
#--> 1
? Mx("{property(identity)}").Match([ [1,0], [0,1] ])
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

Time, in the part of it that runs. A duration must fit a rule: ninety minutes is inside one to two hours and on a step of a quarter of an hour; eighty is inside and off the step; three hours is outside.

```haro
oTm = new stzTimex("{@Duration(1h..2h:15min)}")
? oTm.Match(new stzDuration("1 hour 30 minutes"))
#--> 1
? oTm.Match(new stzDuration("1 hour 20 minutes"))
#--> 0
? oTm.Match(new stzDuration("3 hours"))
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

A graph: a pattern reads like the path it looks for. The labels match without regard to case, and direction matters, as the second line shows:

```haro
g = new stzGraph("Order")
g.AddNodeXT(:a, "Placed")
g.AddNodeXT(:b, "Cooking")
g.AddNodeXT(:c, "Served")
g.AddEdgeXT(:a, :b, "starts")
g.AddEdgeXT(:b, :c, "finishes")
? (new stzGraphex("{@Node(placed) -> @Edge(starts) -> @Node(cooking)}", g)).Match(g)
#--> 1
? (new stzGraphex("{@Node(served) -> @Node(placed)}", g)).Match(g)
#--> 0
? (new stzGraphex("{@Node(placed) -> @!Node(refunded)}", g)).Match(g)
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

Last, the habit. The pattern is data in every language, and a language that exposes its tokens can be read back by a person and by an agent. Five of the six languages do:

```haro
? len( Lx("[@N, @S]").Tokens() )
#--> 2
? Lx("[@N, @S]").Tokens()[2][:type]
#--> string
? Nx("{@Property(Even) & @Property(Prime)}").Tokens()[1][:type]
#--> conjunction
? len( Mx("{shape(square) & property(symmetric)}").Tokens()[1][:conditions] )
#--> 2
```

> run: kept

Graphs have no `Tokens`, no `Pattern` and no working `Explain` at this commit, and no small function either. There is no `Gx`, and there is no `Tx` for time, although the study's table names both. The small functions are `rx`, `Lx`, `Nx`, `Tbx`, `Mx` and `Tmx`.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I decided that a pattern states a meaning and not a procedure.** That decision is in the articles, and the articles are mine. The same ones decided to carry the regex idea to non-text data with one habit: braces, an `@` for a token, `&`, `|`, `@!` for not, `@cs:` for case. Lists keep square brackets. The other five use braces. That is the first honest correction of the phrase "one grammar".

**I refused to reinvent the notation for text.** Simple matches are kept: a classic pattern string, named groups and captures work as written. Clearer names are put around it. The older work on naming is kept as history, for educational value, which is the right way to retire a mechanism.

**I refused, for now, to build the siblings.** The articles planned classes in which patterns are genetic, linguistic or quantic. The article says that they are in an early prototype and not in the library, and a search of the regex folder for "induce", "from example" and "infer" finds nothing. The purpose was found two years later, in the answer protocol: induction from examples, and superposed candidates. That is chapter 18's ground, and chapter 9's refusal.

**I refused the declarative builder as a headline.** Eighteen of the fifty-one files in the builder's test folder are marked retired: the parser of a declarative pattern is pending. The article's lead example, `CanContainAChar(:Between = ...)`, raises. What runs is the conditional builder, and the look-around object. I do not build the chapter on the builder's headline form.

**I have not yet refused a word the language does not know.** That is the tension of this chapter, and I keep it for section 7.

## 5. The court

`00_regex_narrated` proves the anchors and the search, the classes, the exact quantifiers, the groups, the four match types, partial matching as you type, and positions as data. `00_numbrex_narrated` proves prime, perfect, Fibonacci, even and odd, square and composite, each with a number that matches and one that does not; its header records a regression in which `Match()` once returned TRUE for every input. `tablex_narrated` proves the parser, counting, uniqueness, sorting, types, sums and averages, the three connectives, sequences, and building patterns from patterns. Its header names the fault it killed: the parser sliced with an end position through a function that counts, and about ninety patterns parsed into a token of the right type with no value. `00_matrex_narrated` proves exact size, any size and the square shape; its header records that `Match()` once returned FALSE for every input. `00_graphex_narrated` proves that a pattern compiles to a graph, present and absent labels, a sequence as a subsequence, the case rules, negation, alternation, numeric properties, and the cache by graph signature.

Note the placement: the guard for tables lives in the folder for regex, and its stem has no `00_` prefix.

There is no narrated guard for lists, time, or the regex builders. The courts of those are classic files without usable promises. The tests of lists sit after a function definition, so the host language reads them as the body of the function and runs none of them. I ran a copy with the function moved to the end: sixty checks, two of which disagree with the file's own expectation. The classic file of time says that a promise is TRUE and the run prints 0, and the central record filed this on 1 October 2026 as still open.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

**Strings.** The field leads on safety and speed, and Softanza leads on naming. The site's page for the area says it itself: it trails the modern engines on safety and throughput, with no JIT and no linear-time matcher, and the control against catastrophic backtracking is not reachable from Haro, although the code for both is already compiled into the binary. The engine's header confirms that the hardening exists underneath: a match limit, a recursion limit, length caps.

**Lists, numbers, time, graphs.** The field has older, stronger single tools. The study says it without ranking them: one tool has list patterns, uniqueness and stepped ranges; three languages have structural matching; graph query languages exist; event processing exists for time. What the field lacks, and what the record claims, is the one habit across the media.

**Tables.** Data-quality libraries lead in reach and ecosystem. Here a rule is a sentence the person who owns the data can read. The loss is that it runs only on `stzTable`.

**Where the family is weak in itself.** The class headers print their defects as warnings: for matrices, graphs and tables. That is rare, and I say it. Graphs: the quantifiers are parsed but not yet used by the match. Matrices: known gaps today, each carried as a warning on its method.

**A date for the family's one recurring bug.** The same root cause hit the family language by language: the parsers sliced with an end position through a function that counts. The numbers and matrices were repaired on 14 June 2026, the tables on 5 October, and the graphs in a fix whose date the file does not give. Time still carries it. This is evidence that the family shares one design and one parser's habits; it is also the reason time fails.

## 7. The limit

What is built: strings, numbers and tables, each with a guard and a small function. The shared habit.

What is built in part:

- **Lists**: types, counts, nesting, negation, alternation and sets run. Stepped ranges raise. The uniqueness flag is ignored, so `[@N2-3{1; 2; 3}U]` accepts `[1, 1]`. The wildcard of the article, `@A`, is not a token; the real wildcard is `@$`. And a reused object answers from a cache whose key is the list's length and the sum of its characters' codes, which does not depend on order. A fresh object answers 0 for `["hello", 1]`, and the same object after `[1, "hello"]` answers 1.
- **Matrices**: size, shape, element and three properties run. The class header lists the terms that accept every matrix: `row`, `col`, `pattern`, `determinant` and `sum`. A matrix of two by two is accepted by `{row(99)}`.
- **Time**: instants and durations with a step run. A range without a step raises. And sequences over a timeline do not run at all: here is a timeline with a meeting, a break and a lunch, and the pattern the article promises, which should match:

```haro
oTl = new stzTimeLine("2025-10-22", "2025-10-22")
oTl.AddInstant("Meeting", "2025-10-22 09:00:00")
oTl.AddSpan("Break", "2025-10-22 10:00:00", "2025-10-22 10:15:00")
oTl.AddInstant("Lunch", "2025-10-22 12:00:00")
o1 = new stzTimex("{@Event(Meeting) -> @Duration* -> @Event(Break)}")
? o1.MatchPartial(oTl)
#--> 0
o2 = new stzTimex("{@Event(Lunch)}")
? o2.MatchPartial(oTl)
#--> 0
? @@( o2.Tokens()[1][:label] )
#--> "Lunch)"
```

> run: kept

The article promises that the first is TRUE. The second shows that even a single event the timeline holds does not match, and the third shows why: the label was read with its closing parenthesis. That is the shared bug, still present in time.

- **Graphs**: labels in order along a path, negation, alternation and numeric properties run. The arrow means *later on one path*, and not *next*. The quantifiers are read and ignored. The graph of the block above has three nodes; here a pattern asks for a node ten between them, and it matches:

```haro
? (new stzGraphex("{@Node(placed) -> @Node(served)}", g)).Match(g)
#--> 1
? (new stzGraphex("{@Node(placed) -> @Node10 -> @Node(served)}", g)).Match(g)
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

Cooking lies between the first and the last, so the first answer is the arrow's meaning. The second is the quantifier being ignored. The cache of this class is keyed by the node and edge counts and the node ids, so a second graph with the same ids and other labels is answered from the cache, until `ClearCache()`.

**The refusal the family has not yet made.** Chapter 11's law is that a language is declared and closed, with refusals. Here is the family, asked a word it does not know:

```haro
? Nx("{@Banana(3)}").Match(7)
#--> 1
? Nx("{@Banana(3)}").Match(1000003)
#--> 1
? @@( Nx("{@Banana(3)}").Tokens() )
#--> [ ]
? Nx("{@Property(Banana)}").Match(7)
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

A pattern with a word the language does not know matches every number. The tokens are empty, and nothing is raised. The matrix language returns an ERROR token that nothing raises, and the other four answer 0. The Haro charter session filed this as a finding on 28 September 2026: the six pattern classes accept unknown tokens silently. I reproduced it on this commit, and the request is not closed. A closed language that cannot refuse is not yet the law of chapter 11. [AUTHOR: Do I print this as my debt, in the first person, or as a finding of the court?]

What is designed: one pattern engine shared by all the media. The engine design says the backtracking engine, the constraint solver and the pattern compiler are the same across all domains, and that only the vocabulary changes. Its status is planned. At this commit the vocabulary changes and so does the code. The engine's own file named *pattern* is another thing: palindromes, repeats and arithmetic and geometric sequences. The pattern as the condition side of the cascade is chapter 9's rung two, and designed.

What is absent: induction from examples, which is reserved. The genetic, linguistic and quantic siblings, which are vision only. The explanation of an unnamed pattern, since `rx("[a-z]+").Explain()` raises because the analyser class was never built. And any use of the pattern classes by the rest of the library: no class takes a pattern. `stzList`, `stzNumber`, `stzTable`, `stzMatrix` and `stzGraph` have no `Matches(pattern)`. Only the education course uses them, in a chapter and an exercise whose answer is checked by running the learner's program. [AUTHOR: Do I tell the family as it runs, with the defects printed beside the wins, or only the media that run and name the rest in one paragraph? I have done the first.]

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** One habit across seven kinds of data: a small constructor, a pattern in a string, `Match`. The pattern is data you can read and compose. Use the six verified ones, and build nothing on the cache of a reused list pattern.

**The analyst.** A data-quality rule is a sentence of the table language. The person who owns the data can read it.

**The designer.** A pattern states a shape the interface can show before the data arrives, and a half-typed value is answered on the way.

**The decision-maker.** A rule is a line and not a procedure. It can be reviewed, and its verdict is 1 or 0. Say beside it that a pattern with an unknown word is not refused, and that a graph arrow means *later*.

**The educator.** The course teaches patterns in two chapters, in English, French, Arabic and Hausa. The ladder is a curriculum: text, list, number, table.

**The agent.** A pattern is a small closed notation that an agent can be held to and can read back. Be told that three of the media cannot yet refuse a word they do not know, and that writing a pattern from examples is reserved.

## 9. Exercise

Three small tables are given. Write one pattern that accepts only the first. Before you run anything, write under each of the three printed lines what you expect and why.

```haro exercise
t1 = new stzTable([ [ :ID, :EMAIL ], [ 1, "ali@x.org" ], [ 2, "sara@x.org" ] ])
t2 = new stzTable([ [ :ID, :EMAIL ], [ 1, "ali@x.org" ], [ 1, "sara@x.org" ] ])
t3 = new stzTable([ [ :ID, :EMAIL ], [ 1, "ali@x.org" ], [ 2, "" ] ])
# write ONE pattern, then print whether it matches t1, t2, t3, on three lines
cPattern = "{ ... }"
? Tbx(cPattern).Match(t1)
? Tbx(cPattern).Match(t2)
? Tbx(cPattern).Match(t3)
#--> 1
#--> 0
#--> 0
```

> run: exercise

Run it. One clause rejects the second table and another rejects the third; which is which? And what would the pattern say if the email were a single space? I do not answer first. If a promise breaks, do not change it: explain the output.

# Seven languages, one door

Source: Chapter 16. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: a closed registry of six foreign runtimes and a process-based call that brings Python's and NodeJS's results home as lists; the trust postures; the twin's rehearsal of a spawn; a reader of Python and of JavaScript into one code graph; designed: the door governed, with a posture asked at the call; the engine's bridge and its three tiers; the domain library on top of the door; Haro wearing a Python syntax and a JavaScript one; absent: a door whose way back is closed; a door for R that runs; a passing run of a foreign runtime in the library's own court; the model as a door

Run: 10 of 10 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:25 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

For a working programmer, reaching another language from the one you write means one of two things: a foreign function interface, a call into a shared library, or a child process, a run of an interpreter whose output you parse. Notebooks and polyglot virtual machines make the same move with more comfort. (General knowledge. The record holds no dated study of these tools, and says so itself.)

The estate's own field study of August 2026 has two sentences on this, and they pull in opposite directions. On the way out: the host language is the escape hatch, and the escape hatch is always taken. It gives two internal languages that died of openness. On the way in: the differentiator is escape; a language you make must be consumable from any stack. It gives one language that stalled at the border of its own world.

So the lesson of the field is double. An escape that is open destroys a closed language. And a language with no way out dies at its own border. A door is where those two lessons meet.

There is a second habit, which looks like the first and is not. Tools that let you write in the syntax you know over another engine, a transpiler, a second front end for one compiler, I will call by the word of this book, *facets*. The estate's design memo for them is blunt about their cost. The honest promise of a second face is readability for a human who knows the other language, and not behavioural transfer.

## 2. The first principle

**A facet is a syntax of one grammar, and never a runtime. A door is a runtime, and never a syntax. The door is one, and it is the answer to "what about language X".**

The vision's own sentence, ratified in session on 28 August 2026, says it of the door: foreign code in the selected languages, governed, one door, with no further host embeddings. The study of the record reads the pair in a line I keep: the facet brings the programmer, and the door brings the ecosystem. Chapters 1 and 11 both promised that this chapter would tell the difference. This is it.

## 3. The Softanza form

The door's list is closed. The foreign languages are a registry, and the host is not on it. This block asks the door about six languages, then about the host, then about one that was never on the list:

```haro
oDoor = new stzExterCode(:python)
aNames = [ "c", "nodejs", "julia", "r", "prolog", "python", "ring", "cobol" ]
for cLang in aNames
	? cLang + ": " + oDoor.IsLanguageSupported(cLang)
next
#--> c: 1
#--> nodejs: 1
#--> julia: 1
#--> r: 1
#--> prolog: 1
#--> python: 1
#--> ring: 0
#--> cobol: 0
```

> run: kept

"What about language X" has one answer, and for a language not on the list it is a refusal by name:

```haro
try
	oNo = new stzExterCode(:cobol)
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Language 'cobol' is not supported
```

> run: kept

Now the door runs a real runtime. A short note on the machine first. The door writes a script, starts the runtime by it, and reads a result file back. This book was built on a machine where Python lives at its own path, so the path is given as a short path without spaces, and Node and R are at the library's default places. The blocks that start a runtime are marked, in the reader, as touching files and the system, and they show the output that was recorded on the date of the build.

The Python result comes home as the host's own structure, a list, and the call leaves a trace:

```haro
cPython = "C:/Users/ASUSV3~1/AppData/Local/Programs/Python/Python313/python.exe"
oPy = new stzExterCode(:python)
oPy.SetRuntimePath(cPython)
oPy.SetCode("
res = { 'numbers': [1, 2, 3, 4, 5], 'mean': sum([1, 2, 3, 4, 5]) / 5 }
")
oPy.Execute()
? @@( oPy.Result() )
#--> [ [ "numbers", [ 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 ] ], [ "mean", 3 ] ]
? oPy.CallTrace()[1][:language]
#--> python
? oPy.CallTrace()[1][:mode]
#--> interpreted
? isNumber(oPy.LastCallDuration())
#--> 1
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

The Python float `3.0` came back as `3`. The trace also holds a duration. The block prints only that it is a number, because a duration is a clock, and a clock cannot be promised.

That is the rationale of the whole plane, in one picture: each layer of a solution in the language best at it, and the host as the conductor that passes data in as text and reads data back. The library's own article lists the layers: system performance in C, event-driven systems in NodeJS, scientific computing in Julia, statistics in R, logical reasoning in Prolog, machine learning in Python, AI reasoning in a language model, and solution integration in the host. Here are two of them in a pipeline, Python computing the averages of two students and NodeJS deciding. The thresholds are mine; the data is the article's:

```haro
aStudents = [ [ "Alice", [ 85, 90, 78 ] ], [ "Bob", [ 72, 65, 70 ] ] ]
oPy2 = new stzExterCode(:python)
oPy2.SetRuntimePath(cPython)
oPy2.SetCode("
students = " + @@(aStudents) + "
res = [ [ s[0], round(sum(s[1]) / len(s[1]), 2) ] for s in students ]
")
oPy2.Execute()
aAverages = oPy2.Result()
? @@(aAverages)
#--> [ [ "Alice", 84.33 ], [ "Bob", 69 ] ]
oNode = new stzExterCode(:nodejs)
oNode.SetCode("
const avgs = " + @@(aAverages) + ";
const res = avgs.map(a => [ a[0], a[1] >= 80 ? 'Excellent' : 'Needs work' ]);
")
oNode.Execute()
? @@( oNode.Result() )
#--> [ [ "Alice", "Excellent" ], [ "Bob", "Needs work" ] ]
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

The data goes in by composing the code string with the host's own list printer, since the article's marshalling functions do not exist (section 7).

**Which seven?** The title commits to a number, and the record counts it four ways. The article on the plane and the article on performance count six runtimes and the host. The article on the domain library and the study count seven domains: the six and a language model, with the host as an eighth that orchestrates. The library's registry has six and no host. The vision says "the 7 selected languages" and never lists them. The engine design has a different enumeration still, five of them, with COBOL and without C, NodeJS and Prolog. I do not choose among them in a sentence the record does not hold. The registry above is the one that runs. [AUTHOR: Which seven does the chapter name, and is the language model one of them? Chapters 17 and 20 make the model a proposer that cannot commit, while the narrations run it as a first-class runtime through a function that does not exist.]

**A facet is not a door.** Reading a foreign language is also not running it. The two languages that the author names as Haro's planned facets are also the two that the library can already read, and not run or write, into one code graph. One set of questions answers over both:

```haro
cPy = "class Animal:" + nl +
      "    def speak(self):" + nl +
      "        return '...'" + nl +
      "" + nl +
      "class Dog(Animal):" + nl +
      "    def speak(self):" + nl +
      "        return 'woof'"
cJs = "class Animal { speak() { return '...' } }" + nl +
      "class Dog extends Animal { speak() { return 'woof' } }"
oPG = StzPyCodeGraphFromSourceTS(cPy)
oJG = StzJsCodeGraphFromSourceTS(cJs)
? oPG.Language()
#--> python
? @@( oPG.ParentsOf("Dog") )
#--> [ "Animal" ]
? @@( oPG.OwnersOf("speak") )
#--> [ "Animal", "Dog" ]
? oJG.Language()
#--> javascript
? @@( oJG.ParentsOf("Dog") )
#--> [ "Animal" ]
? @@( oJG.OwnersOf("speak") )
#--> [ "Animal", "Dog" ]
```

> run: kept

This is a parser into an index of classes, methods and inheritance, and not a program graph that can print a program. It is a coincidence of the order of work, and not yet a facet. What the record has of facets is my statement, in session on 8 October 2026: a link should be made with the future vision of Haro and its facet languages, where Ring is already there and Python and JavaScript are planned. The charter of Haro on this machine speaks of one grammar and one text, and its design memo for a second face, which proposes and rules nothing, calls them faces and recommends printers read by humans before any parser. Its own table lists the collisions: a Python reader expects zero-based indexing and Haro's is one-based. [AUTHOR: May the chapter name Python and JavaScript as Haro's planned facets on your word alone? The book says facet and the memo says face.]

**A door is governed.** The governance has the word for foreign code. An executor with no declared trust posture never proceeds, and the refusal says why. `external` is the posture the library defines as out-of-process:

```haro
oGov = new stzGovernance("shop")
? oGov.MayExecute("python")
#--> 0
? oGov.Why()
#--> refused: 'python' has NO declared trust posture
oGov.DeclarePosture("python", :External)
? oGov.MayExecute("python")
#--> 1
? oGov.Why()
#--> allowed: posture 'external' declared
```

> run: kept

And the twin can rehearse a spawn before it runs, flagged as a risk. Chapter 24 owns the twin, and one block here is enough to say what the door does not use:

```haro
oTwin = new stzVirtualEnvironment()
oTwin.Spawn("python analyse.py")
? oTwin.NumberOfPendingSpawns()
#--> 1
oPlan = oTwin.GenerateUpdatePlan()
? oPlan.Narration()
#--> Update plan (1 of 1 operations to commit):
#-->   * 1. spawn process: python analyse.py
? @@( oPlan.Risks() )
#--> [ [ 1, "RUNS an external command (side effects): spawn process: python analyse.py" ] ]
```

> run: kept

Nothing is run there. The script named does not exist.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I decided on one door.** Its vision name is the door of the foreign languages, and the letter of it is x: the family of the platform's letters has one that stands for external. The retired names are two older ones. The letter X was refused for algorithmics, because it would have sat beside the door, the clash the alphabet chapter named as its sharpest. The door took a letter and kept it.

**I refused further host embeddings.** The door is the answer to "what about language X". Chapter 1 promised this chapter on those words: seven languages were chosen for seven layers of a solution.

**I refused to surrender the domains to generated code.** The article on the domain library puts it as a stance: rather than forcing developers to master each domain, or to surrender to AI-generated code, provide carefully crafted abstractions. It is a position of 2025, and its code is absent.

**I refused a facet as a runtime.** The study's plan says it in a line: a facet is a syntax of one grammar and never a runtime, so the door stays the only way to foreign code. It is a plan, proposed. It is not ruled.

**I refused an approximating printer.** The design memo's rule for faces: a printer refuses before it approximates, and a face spells the graph and never gives it the meaning of Python or JavaScript.

## 5. The court

`51_extercode_narrated` proves that the registry knows Python, R, Julia, Prolog, C and NodeJS, without regard to case, and rejects an unknown name; that the code set is the code read back; and that the factory constructs and forwards. Its header says that it has no `Execute`: no real input or output in tests. `governance_narrated`, scene six, proves that an execution without a posture refuses, that a declared posture proceeds and names itself, and that postures travel through save and load. `virtual_environment_twin_narrated` proves that a queued spawn does not run until commit, by a marker file; that a scope refuses; and that the plan narrates and flags the spawn. `pycodegraph_narrated` and `jscodegraph_narrated` prove classes, methods, inheritance chains, owners, impact and cascade over Python and over JavaScript sources, offline and without the runtime; the second skips when the engine's tree-sitter is absent.

I want to be exact about the court of the door. No guard in the snapshot runs a foreign runtime and passes. The door's court is a registry and a construction test. The folder's own run record, under an older runtime version, lists thirty-seven failures; several promises cannot match by construction, because the file names carry a random suffix and the calls a duration.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The host loses the race the door exists for. The article on performance measures seven runtimes on three tasks, and the host comes last in two of them; I carry its caveats from chapter 22: it has no versions and no dates inside its text, and says that its times are relative to a specific computer environment. Sorting a million elements takes the host about thirty seconds and Python about four, in the article's table. I print no more of it.

The field leads in runtimes it can actually run. Of six doors, three ran on this machine: Node, Python, and R, which raised. The other three had no runtime to start. The study says its description of the field's polyglot tools was written from the genre and not re-read, and I claim no measured comparison.

The field leads in the ecosystem and in being used. The compare page speaks of Python only as a rival; to cover its thirty rows in Python you assemble numpy, pandas and others. The door is how a Softanza solution takes them without leaving.

The field's other loss is that the foreign process is outside the court. Chapter 21's threat model puts code already running in the process out of scope.

And the honest cost of a facet, in the memo's own table: a Python reader expects zero-based indexing, and Haro's is one-based; the string with a line break is a backslash and an `n`; two letters of different case are one variable. "Any model writes Haro in the shape it knows" is true only of the spellings that are the minority.

## 7. The limit

The word *governed* is a design. This is the block that shows it. The governance says no, and the door runs anyway:

```haro
oGov2 = new stzGovernance("shop")
? oGov2.MayExecute("python")
#--> 0
oPy3 = new stzExterCode(:python)
oPy3.SetRuntimePath(cPython)
oPy3.SetCode("res = 6 * 7")
oPy3.Execute()
? oPy3.Result()
#--> 42
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

Nothing in the door's folder mentions governance or posture. The door calls the system directly, and not the twin.

The way back is not closed. The result file is read by the host's own `eval`, and a string that comes out of the foreign runtime is wrapped in single quotes without escaping. A foreign string that carries a quote and a call reaches host code. The function used here is `upper`, harmless on purpose:

```haro
oPy4 = new stzExterCode(:python)
oPy4.SetRuntimePath(cPython)
oPy4.SetCode(`
res = "x' + upper('y') + 'z"
`)
oPy4.Execute()
? oPy4.Result()
#--> xYz
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

The result file held `'x' + upper('y') + 'z'`, and `Result()` evaluated it. A plain apostrophe in data, `it's fine`, writes a string the host cannot read, and the method prints the error and returns the raw text. It is the same defect class as chapter 11's list in a natural sentence. I did not test the other five languages' marshalling.

The R door raises on every call, because its generated script is not valid R: it holds an identifier that begins with an underscore, which R rejects. I confirmed it with R on a one-line file. And the trace records an exit code of 0 for a call that failed, because the code passes a literal 0 to the recorder, and failure is detected only by the absence of the result file:

```haro
oStat = new stzExterCode(:R)
oStat.SetCode("res <- c(1, 2, 3)")
bRaised = 0
try
	oStat.Execute()
catch
	bRaised = 1
done
? bRaised
#--> 1
? oStat.CallTrace()[1][:exitcode]
#--> 0
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

R's message was in French on this machine, so I do not promise it. [AUTHOR: At this commit the door is neither governed nor closed on the way back, and the R door raises. May the chapter print these three as its limit, with the runs, in the voice of chapter 11's open list? I advise yes, and the vision's word "governed" then stays a design.]

What else is designed: the engine's own polyglot bridge, which the engine design marks as planned (the macroplan carries a correction that says the module was once marked done with no source); the three tiers for Python, of which only the external subprocess exists; the domain library on top of the door, none of whose classes I found in code; and the facets, a memo that proposes and rules nothing.

What is absent: the article's functions that bring data in, `@@R`, `@@C`, `XC()` and `XAI()`, which are defined in no file. Factories exist for Python, R, Julia and Prolog. The C and NodeJS doors are reached only by the class. The model is not a door of this plane at all; the article's function that runs it inside the host is the opposite of chapter 17's law, and the book does not teach it.

And a door shape I did not run: Julia, C and Prolog, for which no runtime is installed here. And a third relation, named in the study beside the door, the shim that pastes a solution found on the internet in another language and changes it a little: `range0`, Python's range as a host function, runs; the Python slice form prints an empty list, and the guard's own record lists it as a failure.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** You write Haro and, for the layer another language does best, you hand it a string of code and a list of data and take a list back. In the plan, a Python or JavaScript programmer will also write Haro in a syntax they know. Two different things: a syntax brings you, and a runtime brings your libraries.

**The analyst.** The statistical layer is R's, the machine-learning layer Python's, the logic layer Prolog's. You stay in one file and one list of results. Today the R door raises, and the record says so.

**The designer.** There is nothing to design at the door itself. The record's lesson is its refusal: a language not on the list is refused by name, and "what about my language" has one answer.

**The decision-maker.** The door is the one place where the closure ends, by decision. At this commit it is not yet governed and its way back reaches host code. The vision's word "governed" is a design. Every call's duration is recorded in the trace today.

**The educator.** A learner sees which layer belongs to which language and runs a two-door pipeline. The lesson is the difference between reading a language, running it, and writing in its syntax.

**The agent.** You never reach the door by yourself. A function in an agent's file must carry a posture, and `external` fits a foreign runtime; and a model has no capability to produce effects (chapter 20).

You are building a small solution with three jobs: sorting a million numbers, deciding which of two students passes, and checking a family tree for who is an ancestor of whom. For each job, choose the language from the table in section 3 that owns its layer, and write your reason in one line. Then, without running anything, write the promise for each line of this block, and run it:

The last promise is the one that matters. If you now call the door for Prolog, will the governance have been asked? Write yes or no, and why. Then read section 7. If a promise breaks, do not change it: explain the output first.
