# Law: programmatic by default, the model last, admission over generation

Source: Chapter 17. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the domain language model's first rung; the conversation that asks; derivation with its proof; the programmatic agent; the gate that refuses an ungoverned model; designed: the thinking machine's cascade across faculties; the typed unit of prediction validated in five stages; the negotiation between an agent and its reviewer; absent: four of the library's five own agents, induction from examples among them

Run: 8 of 8 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:25 UTC+01:00.

## What the AI age changes here

In the two movements before this one, the AI age changed one thing each. In programming, it changed who writes the code. In languages, it changed who speaks the sentence. Here it changes everything, because this movement is about intelligence itself, and the field now uses the word to mean one thing: a large language model inside your software.

So this chapter does what the other two law chapters do, and states the law of its part before the evidence. It also says how the part depends on the others, because the order of this book runs from the familiar to the new, and the order of the record runs the other way. Intelligence, in Softanza, is governed by what came before it. The court that judges a model's sentence is the court of Part II, which judges code whoever wrote it. The sentence it judges is a sentence of a closed language, which is Part III. A model can only be put last because there is something solid to put first.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

Ask the field what makes an application intelligent and the answer is a model. An agent, in the field's sense, is a model in a loop with tools, a memory and a goal. The shopping list for building one in 2026 is long: a project in Python, a model provider, a framework that wires the loop as a graph, a store of vectors for memory, tools with typed schemas, a tracing service, and a chapter of safety advice. Memory is often retrieval: a collection of documents, cut into pieces, searched by similarity and pasted into the prompt.

Safety, in that picture, is a property of the agent. It is alignment, guardrails, a prompt that asks for confirmation, a box marked "human in the loop". The field has also learned to hold a model's output to a shape, with grammars that constrain what it may emit. All of this is serious work, and some of it is very good. It shares one assumption: the model is the mind, and everything else is around it.

## 2. The first principle

**The mind is programmatic by default; a model is one faculty among others, the last one called, and what it says is a proposal that the court admits or refuses.**

## 3. The Softanza form

Begin with my definition, because everything here follows from it. Intelligence is the computational ability to derive new knowledge from existing knowledge. Nothing in that sentence needs a model.

Here is a domain language model with no neurons at all. It is forged from a small knowledge graph of a restaurant, and it answers from what was recorded:

```haro
oKB = new stzKnowledgeGraph("restaurant")
oKB.Know("margherita", "dish").
    KnowRelation("margherita", "contains", "tomato-sauce").
    KnowRelation("margherita", "contains", "mozzarella")
oDLM = StzDlmQ(oKB)
? oDLM.Ask("margherita contains what?")
#--> Margherita contains tomato-sauce and mozzarella.
? oDLM.Why()
#--> recorded fact(s): 'margherita' contains tomato-sauce, mozzarella
? oDLM.AskXT("margherita contains what?")[:certainty]
#--> 1
? oDLM.Ask("who won the 1998 world cup?")
#--> That is outside the 'restaurant' domain (I know 1 entities and 1 relations).
```

> run: kept

It answers, says why, and states its certainty. And when it is asked something it does not know, it does not guess: it refuses, and it tells you how small its world is. A refusal that names its boundary is worth more than a fluent answer that has none.

Now the second piece, the one that turns the usual dialogue around. In the field, you prompt the machine and keep what comes out. Here the system asks you:

```haro
oMenu = new stzKnowledgeGraph("menu")
oMenu.Know("margherita", "dish").Know("tiramisu", "dish")
oMenu.AddConversationQ("setup").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "contains"))
? oMenu.AskIn("setup")
#--> What does 'margherita' have for 'contains'?  (why: every dish needs 'contains')
try
	oMenu.ConcludeIn("setup", "menu")
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Can't conclude: 2 gap(s) remain -- the wise-coding loop is not done. Ask the next question.
```

> run: kept

A goal was declared: every dish must say what it contains. The gap between the goal and what is known generated the question, and the question carries its reason. The world cannot be closed while it is incomplete. Chapter 18 shows the five ways you may answer: an option from a list, a data structure, a formula, a sentence, or examples. The model, if there is one, is the last of these, not the first.

The third piece is derivation, my definition at work. One law is declared, and a fact nobody recorded is proved:

```haro
oKG = new stzKnowledgeGraph("kitchen")
oKG.ConstrainRelation("contains", :Transitive)
oKG.KnowRelation("margherita", "contains", "tomato-sauce").
    KnowRelation("tomato-sauce", "contains", "tomato")
? oKG.Prove([ "margherita", "contains", "tomato" ])[:narration]
#--> proved: margherita contains tomato-sauce contains tomato
? oKG.Prove([ "margherita", "contains", "luigi" ])[:narration]
#--> not derivable: no recorded fact or lawful chain gives 'margherita' contains 'luigi'
```

> run: kept

New knowledge from existing knowledge, with its chain written out. And a "no" that is as certain as the "yes", because it is the result of a search, not of a mood. No retrieval, no training. Adding a fact makes the system know more; adding a law makes it reason further.

Then the model. It is welcome, and it is held. Wire a model's output straight to an effect, here sending a message, and the composition is refused before anything runs:

```haro
oG = new stzAgentGraph("mailer")
oG.AddLLMActor("writer")
oG.AddEffect("send")
oG.Proposes("writer", "send")
? oG.IsSound()
#--> 0
? len(oG.Violations())
#--> 3
```

> run: kept

Three laws fire: every effect must pass a gate, open text must have somewhere safe to land, and every effect must leave a witness. A model actor holds no effectful capability at all; it proposes, and that is the whole of its power:

```haro
oF = new stzLLMFunction("sum")
oF.SetPrompt("summarize: {input}")
oF.Budget(2)
oF.SeedAnswer("hello world", "a greeting")
oLLM = new stzLLMAgent("summarizer")
oLLM.SetSkillFrom(oF)
? oLLM.Propose("hello world")
#--> a greeting
? oLLM.HoldsEffectful()
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

No model is loaded in this block: the answer is seeded, the way a test fixes a golden answer, so that the page runs the same on every night. What the block proves does not depend on the model: whatever it says, it holds nothing that can act.

And the agent itself has a mind made of code. A skill is a condition and an action; it fires on what the agent perceives; and governance gates the act before it happens:

```haro
oRog = new stzPIAgent("rogue-bot")
oRog.MemoryQ().Learn("stock", "level", "low")
oRog.GovernanceQ().DeclareRisk("order-stock", 2)
oSk = new stzAgentSkill("restock")
oSk.SetWhen(func oM { return oM.Fact("stock", "level", "low") })
oSk.SetDoes(func oM { oM.Learn("stock", "level", "ordered")  return 1 })
oRog.AddGovernedSkill(oSk, "order-stock")
? oRog.Pursue()
#--> 0
? oRog.DecisionLog()[1][:why]
#--> refused: 'rogue-bot' lacks PERMISSION (can) for 'order-stock'
```

> run: kept

The stock is low, the skill wants to order, and the agent was never given the permission. Nothing happens, and the log says why, by name. The world is as it was.

Put the pieces in a line and you have the stack the vision of the platform names: perceive, decide, react, learn, without any language model. Perception classifies the world into the knowledge graph. A planner decides. Rules react. The agent scores its plans and keeps the best. A model may sit beside each step as one more faculty, and none of them depends on it.

Chapter 18 tells the five registers of an answer and the conversation that asks. Chapter 19 tells the domain language model, from its first rung, with no neurons, to its second, trained on the first one's own facts and nothing else. Chapter 20 tells the agent that cannot hurt you: it proposes, a workbench rehearses, the court judges, and only a governed actor commits. Chapter 21 tells the court itself. Chapter 22 compares all of it with the field, and prints where the field leads.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused the model as the mind.** When the models arrived, the obvious move was to put one at the centre and fence it. I wrote the opposite into the architecture: the mind is programmatic by default, and a model is a pluggable faculty, one among several, never the definition of the mind. The industry's loop is a special case of this design, with one faculty and no governance. [AUTHOR: Is there an earlier moment or sentence of yours, before the architecture of July 2026, that this chapter should open on?]

**I refused anything paid or remote as a requirement.** Every capability has a floor that needs no setup, no subscription and no network. A model may be added on top. It is never the price of admission. [AUTHOR: When a model is called last, may it be a remote frontier model, under a budget and the court, or is "last" always a local model? The record supports both readings.]

**I refused guessing.** Every ambiguity refuses rather than guesses. The field's habit of prompting and keeping what comes out has a name now; here, guessing is replaced by asking, and vibes are replaced by governance.

**I refused retrieval as memory.** Memory is a governed graph, not a pile of similar fragments. Derivation replaces retrieval: the system knows what it was told and what follows from it by law, and it can prove both.

**I refused a teacher I cannot see.** The domain language model's second rung is trained on the facts of the first rung's own knowledge base and on nothing else, and no remote model teaches it. The design goes further and says the second rung's training text is validated by the first rung's grammar before it is admitted, so that it is correct by construction. In the library the facts are rendered directly into text, with no such check yet; chapter 19 says where that stands. I gave the concept of a domain language model, first designed for one of the estate's products, to the open foundation, so that any project that supplies a knowledge base can ship its own model free.

**I refused a stub.** The library declares five agents of its own and has wired one. Asking for one that is not wired raises an error; it does not return something that looks like an agent. Pretending would be easier, and it would make every other refusal in this chapter worth less.

## 5. The court

Each block above stands on a guard. `dlm_narrated` proves the domain language model: answers with their why, refusal outside the domain, completion held to the domain's entities and relations, a self-contained bundle, and a second rung trained on its own corpus. `wisecoding_narrated` proves the conversation that asks and the refusal to conclude. `knowledge_integration_narrated` proves derivation and the certain "no". `agentgraph_narrated` proves that a model straight to an effect is not sound, that the governed shape is, and that a model actor proposes and never commits. `piagent_narrated` proves the programmatic agent and its refusal at the gate. `ownagentstack_narrated` proves the honest roster of the library's own agents.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in reach. Its frameworks connect to hosted services of every kind, its stores search open collections of documents, and its loops call the largest models available for each step. Softanza's inversion, curate a domain and derive from it, wins on a curated domain and loses on the open web. When the task is open-ended language work over material nobody can curate, summarising arbitrary documents or research over the web, the field's stack is the right tool, and the record says so in those words.

The field leads in the market. There is a labour market for its stack, and none yet for this one.

The field leads in models. The second rung of the domain language model, as built, is a small neural model of word pairs, and the step that would make it more is not started. Softanza does not build the large generators and does not compete with them.

Where I think the law stands apart is in three places. A decision here costs nothing, runs offline and gives the same answer every day, so it can be tested with ordinary promises. A model here cannot act, by construction, not by vigilance. And a model's sentence, where one is used, can be held to the grammar of a declared language before it is emitted, instead of being checked and retried after.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the domain language model's first rung, the conversation that asks, derivation with its proof, the programmatic agent, and the gate that refuses an ungoverned composition.

What is designed: the thinking machine of Chapter 9, where many faculties share one state and cascade until it settles; the typed unit of prediction, validated in five stages before it may enter the graph; the conversation between an agent and the one who reviews its plan, which reaches the plan and not yet the discussion about it.

What is absent: four of the library's five own agents. The roster says it plainly:

```haro
? len(StzOwnAgents())
#--> 5
? StzOwnAgentIsWired("wise-coder")
#--> 1
? StzOwnAgentIsWired("induction")
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

The validator, the ranker, the planner and the agent that would induce a pattern from your examples are declared and reserved. So the fifth register of an answer, examples, waits on an agent that does not exist yet.

And one limit of the first rung that you will meet the moment you rephrase a question. It recognises a relation only by the word it was declared with. Ask in other words, and it does not refuse; it answers another question:

```haro
? oDLM.Ask("what does margherita contain?")
#--> Margherita is a dish.
```

> run: kept

That answer is true, and it is not what was asked. By the law of this chapter, it should have been a refusal. I show it here rather than hide it, because a law is only as good as the page that prints where it is broken. The request to the library is to refuse a question whose verb is not a declared relation.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Your agent's mind is code you can test with ordinary promises. A model may be one faculty; it can never hold an effect.

**The analyst.** A fact or a rule you add makes the system know more, with no code change and no training, and every answer carries its why.

**The designer.** The system asks the user one accountable question at a time, and the question can be rendered in any medium: a terminal, a page, an agent.

**The decision-maker.** A decision costs nothing per call, runs offline and is the same tomorrow. The four agents still reserved are the honest gap.

**The educator.** The conversation asks and never answers first, and it uses a model's fluency only when a model is really there, so a learner always sees the floor.

**The agent.** You may propose. You may not commit. A refusal names its law, and you can learn the platform from the platform and be corrected by it.

## 9. Exercise

Build a domain of your own with three facts: a shop, a clinic, a class. Before you run anything, write under each line the promise you expect: one question inside the domain, one outside it, and, with a goal that every item must have one relation, the first question the system will ask you. Then run it.

If a promise is broken, do not change the promise to match the output. Explain the output first. You may find, as I did, that the machine answered a question you did not ask.


=====

# The five registers, and conversations that ask

Source: Chapter 18. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the goal, the gap and the question with its reason; a reply as an option, a data structure or a sentence split on commas and the word 'and'; the governed door with its refusals; the checkpoint with its lifetime; the goal fulfilled or revoked; one wired agent that stops where it would have to invent; designed: several readings of one reply, enumerated for the person to choose; the nearest alternatives named after a refusal; the example written back as a promise; absent: the formula register; the example register; a conversation whose question is worded in Arabic, Hausa or French; a reply with a space in it

Run: 15 of 15 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:25 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

Two practices meet in the idea of a conversation that asks, and a third stands behind its last register.

The first is slot filling. A dialogue framework asks the user for the missing pieces of a form until the form is full. The frameworks of the field understand free text with trained models, speak on many channels and ship connectors, and they ask the designer to write intents and flows in their own language. They take the user's reply as the answer and act on it.

The second is vibe coding, a name put into circulation in 2025: the human prompts, the model produces, the human keeps what runs.

The third is programming by example. Spreadsheets fill a column from two examples you typed. Research calls it inductive synthesis, and the model era made the example the way one steers a model. (General knowledge, not a study of the record; the dates are to be checked before print.)

## 2. The first principle

**A reply may come in any of five forms. Whatever the form, it is only a candidate, and the world's rules decide whether it becomes knowledge.**

The design document says it in one sentence I keep: expression is free, admission is governed. [AUTHOR: The record holds this sentence as "captured from the author's directive" on 13 July 2026, and no sentence of yours on why these five registers and not four or six, nor on the moment you set wise coding against vibe coding. Is there an earlier sentence or memory the chapter should open on?]

## 3. The Softanza form

Chapter 17 showed a conversation asking, and refusing to conclude while a gap is open. This chapter is about the answer. The five registers are five forms a reply can take: an option picked by its number, a data structure, a formula, a sentence, and examples. I will show you each, and I will tell you the ones that do not run.

**The first register: an option.** When the world already knows values, the question offers them as numbers, and a number picks one.

```haro
oKB = new stzKnowledgeGraph("pizzeria")
oKB.Know("pizza1", "dish").Know("pizza2", "dish").Know("pizza3", "dish").Know("pizza4", "dish")
oKB.AddConversationQ("frame").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "topping"))
oKB.AskIn("frame")
oKB.ReplyIn("frame", "cheese")
oKB.AskIn("frame")
oKB.ReplyIn("frame", "olives")
? oKB.AskIn("frame")
#--> Which 'topping' does 'pizza3' have? (1) cheese  (2) olives -- or answer freely.  (why: every dish needs 'topping')
? @@(oKB.ReplyIn("frame", 2)[:admitted])
#--> [ "olives" ]
```

> run: kept

A number nobody offered is refused, with its reason. The refusal never guesses a value, it reaches a person as a checkpoint, and the goal stays open:

```haro
? oKB.AskIn("frame")
#--> Which 'topping' does 'pizza4' have? (1) cheese  (2) olives -- or answer freely.  (why: every dish needs 'topping')
aV = oKB.ReplyIn("frame", 99)
? @@(aV[:admitted])
#--> [ ]
? @@(aV[:refused])
#--> [ [ "(99)", "no such option" ] ]
? oKB.ConversationQ("frame").Checkpoints()[1][:why]
#--> no option (99) was offered -- 2 option(s) on the table
? aV[:goalState]
#--> pursuing
```

> run: kept

Look at what the refusal says: a reason, and a count of the options on the table. It does not list them, and the next question offers the same options again. That matters for section 7.

The question is data before it is text. It carries a force, which is *what* while the world knows nothing and *which* once it knows a value, and the options. Any medium can render it:

```haro
oKF = new stzKnowledgeGraph("pizzeria2")
oKF.Know("p1", "dish").Know("p2", "dish")
oKF.AddConversationQ("f").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "topping"))
? oKF.AskInXT("f")[:force]
#--> what
oKF.ReplyIn("f", "cheese")
aQ = oKF.AskInXT("f")
? aQ[:force]
#--> which
? @@(aQ[:options])
#--> [ "cheese" ]
```

> run: kept

**The second register: a data structure.** A list of values is taken as the values, and each one goes through the same door.

```haro
oKD = new stzKnowledgeGraph("desserts")
oKD.Know("tiramisu", "dish")
oKD.AddConversationQ("setup").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "contains"))
oKD.AskIn("setup")
aV = oKD.ReplyIn("setup", [ "mascarpone", "espresso" ])
? @@(aV[:admitted])
#--> [ "mascarpone", "espresso" ]
? aV[:goalState]
#--> fulfilled
```

> run: kept

**The fourth register: a sentence.** (I skip the third for a moment, because it is one of the two that do not run.) A phrase of the form "X and Y" or "X, Y" is read as two values, each one judged. The conversation keeps a transcript in which every admission is narrated and the fulfilment of the goal is recorded:

```haro
oKM = new stzKnowledgeGraph("menu")
oKM.Know("margherita", "dish")
oKM.AddConversationQ("setup").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "contains"))
oKM.AskIn("setup")
oKM.ReplyIn("setup", "tomato-sauce and mozzarella")
? oKM.ConversationQ("setup").Transcript()
#--> SOFTANZA: Goal adopted -- this conversation is now accountable for it.
#--> SOFTANZA: What does 'margherita' have for 'contains'?  (why: every dish needs 'contains')
#--> YOU:      tomato-sauce and mozzarella
#-->   -> admitted: 'margherita' contains 'tomato-sauce'
#-->   -> admitted: 'margherita' contains 'mozzarella'
#--> SOFTANZA: Goal fulfilled: every required slot is filled in 'menu'
```

> run: kept

"Natural" here means a splitter on commas and on the English word "and". The natural layer of the library, which I will use in a moment, is not called by the conversation.

**When a reply carries several values, and the world allows one.** A relation declared unique takes the first value and refuses the others, each with the law's reason. The refusal reaches a person:

```haro
oKS = new stzKnowledgeGraph("staffing")
oKS.AddConversationQ("s").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireOne("kitchen3", "led-by"))
oKS.ConstrainRelation("led-by", :Unique)
oKS.AskIn("s")
aV = oKS.ReplyIn("s", "mario and giovanni")
? @@(aV[:admitted])
#--> [ "mario" ]
? @@(aV[:refused])
#--> [ [ "giovanni", "'kitchen3' already bears 'led-by' (to 'mario'); the relation is :Unique" ] ]
? len(oKS.ConversationQ("s").Checkpoints())
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

Be careful what you read in this. The choice between Mario and Giovanni was made by order. It was not made by a person. The design promises something else, and section 7 says what.

**The third register, the formula.** A formula typed as a reply is not evaluated. It is taken for a value:

```haro
oKP = new stzKnowledgeGraph("shop")
oKP.Know("tea", "product")
oKP.AddConversationQ("price").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("product", "price"))
oKP.AskIn("price")
? @@(oKP.ReplyIn("price", "=12*3")[:admitted])
#--> [ "=12*3" ]
? oKP.Query([ "tea", "price", "?o" ])[1]
#--> =12*3
```

> run: kept

The price of the tea is now the text "=12*3". I found this out by running it, and the code is silent about a formula register. The record calls it the next work, filed and not routed.

**The fifth register, examples.** When examples are offered as pairs, they are neither admitted nor refused. The goal stays open, and no checkpoint is written. The agent that would read them is on the roster, and reserved:

```haro
oKX = new stzKnowledgeGraph("phones")
oKX.Know("contact1", "contact")
oKX.AddConversationQ("fmt").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("contact", "number-pattern"))
oKX.AskIn("fmt")
aV = oKX.ReplyIn("fmt", [ [ "+216-21-298-374", "valid" ], [ "+33-6-12-34-56-78", "valid" ] ])
? @@(aV[:admitted])
#--> [ ]
? @@(aV[:refused])
#--> [ ]
? aV[:goalState]
#--> pursuing
? len(oKX.ConversationQ("fmt").Checkpoints())
#--> 0
try
	StzOwnAgent("induction")
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Softanza's own agent 'induction' is RESERVED, not wired yet -- refusing rather than returning a stub (LAW 3). See StzOwnAgents().
```

> run: kept

This is not the refusal the protocol describes. It is silence, and silence is the worse answer: the reader who offered the examples is not told they were not understood. I found a second form of the same fault. The same examples typed as one string, joined by a comma, are split and admitted as two literal values, and the goal reads fulfilled. The examples became the knowledge. Nothing was inferred.

So three of the five registers are wired, one of them in its simplest form, a splitter. Two are not. I would rather you meet that here than on a page of mine that said five.

### What happens to a refusal

A refusal waits for a person, with its context. A lifetime can be set so that a stale one leaves the live list without being deleted:

```haro
oKT = new stzKnowledgeGraph("ttl")
oKT.AddConversationQ("t").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireOne("kitchen", "led-by"))
oKT.AskIn("t")
oKT.ReplyIn("t", 7)
? len(oKT.ConversationQ("t").Checkpoints())
#--> 1
oKT.ConversationQ("t").SetCheckpointTTL(1)
oKT.AskIn("t")
oKT.AskIn("t")
? len(oKT.ConversationQ("t").Checkpoints())
#--> 0
? len(oKT.ConversationQ("t").AllCheckpoints())
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

Nothing is deleted. Expiry is about what still needs a person, and the audit record keeps all of it. Every call here is chained from `ConversationQ`, because a held copy would silently do nothing: the guard's comment explains it.

A conversation is accountable for one goal, and it ends by fulfilling the goal or by revoking it on the record. A revoked conversation asks nothing and cannot conclude:

```haro
oKG = new stzKnowledgeGraph("lifecycle")
oKG.Know("d1", "dish").Know("d2", "dish")
oKG.AddConversationQ("s1").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "sauce"))
oKG.AddConversationQ("s2").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "garnish"))
? oKG.ConversationQ("s1").GoalState()
#--> pursuing
oKG.AskIn("s1")
oKG.ReplyIn("s1", "pesto")
oKG.AskIn("s1")
oKG.ReplyIn("s1", "pesto")
? oKG.ConversationQ("s1").GoalState()
#--> fulfilled
? oKG.ConversationQ("s1").GoalWhy()
#--> every required slot is filled in 'lifecycle'
oKG.AskIn("s2")
oKG.ConversationQ("s2").RevokeGoal("out of scope tonight")
? oKG.ConversationQ("s2").GoalState()
#--> revoked
? oKG.ConversationQ("s2").GoalWhy()
#--> out of scope tonight
? len(oKG.OpenConversations())
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

### Programmatic by default: the conversation with no one in it

Chapter 17's law says conversations happen in code first. Here is the conversation with no person and no model. An agent of the library asks, an answer source (a plain function) replies, and every reply passes the same door. Where the source has no answer, the agent stops and leaves the gap for a person. It does not invent:

```haro
oKW = new stzKnowledgeGraph("menu")
oKW.Know("margherita", "dish").Know("tiramisu", "dish")
answers = [ [ "margherita", "tomato-sauce" ], [ "tiramisu", "mascarpone" ] ]
aR = StzOwnAgent("wise-coder").PursueGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "contains"), oKW, func subj, rel {
	for a in answers
		if a[1] = subj
			return a[2]
		ok
	next
	return ""
})
? aR[:filled]
#--> 1
? aR[:asked]
#--> 2
? aR[:why]
#--> goal filled in 2 question(s), governed admission throughout
oKV = new stzKnowledgeGraph("desserts")
oKV.Know("panna-cotta", "dish")
aR2 = StzOwnAgent("wise-coder").PursueGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "contains"), oKV, func subj, rel { return "" })
? aR2[:filled]
#--> 0
? aR2[:why]
#--> stopped: no answer for 'panna-cotta' / 'contains' -- gap left for a human
```

> run: kept

Of the library's five own agents, this is the one that is wired. The other four are reserved, and asking for one raises. It is never a stub.

### In Arabic, and in Hausa

Chapters 2 and 11 showed the natural layer in Hausa. Here is what the conversation can and cannot do with languages, said in three blocks, because the three are different.

First, the data. Names, values and lists in Arabic script pass the gate exactly as Latin ones do. The words around them, the frame of the question, stay English:

```haro
oKA = new stzKnowledgeGraph("مطعم")
oKA.Know("بيتزا", "dish")
oKA.AddConversationQ("c").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "contains"))
? oKA.AskIn("c")
#--> What does 'بيتزا' have for 'contains'?  (why: every dish needs 'contains')
aV = oKA.ReplyIn("c", [ "جبن", "طماطم" ])
? @@(aV[:admitted])
#--> [ "جبن", "طماطم" ]
? aV[:goalState]
#--> fulfilled
```

> run: kept

Second, the candidate. A Hausa sentence can produce a value in the natural layer, and the value, sent as a reply, goes through the same door. The two steps are the caller's: the conversation does not call the natural layer itself. This is the principle of the chapter in a small space, since the expression is free and the admission is governed. The graph folds the case of the value, so the capitals become 'jibini':

```haro
oKH = new stzKnowledgeGraph("menu")
oKH.Know("pizza", "dish")
oKH.AddConversationQ("c").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "contains"))
oKH.AskIn("c")
cCandidate = NaturallyIn("ha", "Yi rubutu dauke 'jibini' Maida shi").Result()
? cCandidate
#--> JIBINI
aV = oKH.ReplyIn("c", cCandidate)
? aV[:narration]
#--> admitted: 'pizza' contains 'jibini'
```

> run: kept

Third, the question in the learner's language. The tutor is a consumer of the conversation: it uses the gap the conversation names, and it chooses the words to say it in four languages. The sentences are templates. The code's own comment says that the French, Arabic and Hausa wording is a draft awaiting a native reviewer, so read the three below as drafts, and not as reviewed text:

```haro
oEx = StzProgramQ("../../education/program").CourseQ("elementary-introduction").ExerciseQ("ex-01-01")
? oEx.GapText("finds", "en")
#--> Where are the repeated items? Which line of your program asks for their positions? (why: the mental model finds before it applies)
? oEx.GapText("finds", "fr")
#--> Où sont les éléments répétés ? Quelle ligne de votre programme demande leurs positions ? (pourquoi : le modèle mental trouve avant d'agir)
? oEx.GapText("finds", "ar")
#--> أين العناصر المكررة؟ أيّ سطر في برنامجك يسأل عن مواضعها؟ (لماذا: النموذج الذهني يبحث قبل أن يطبّق)
? oEx.GapText("finds", "ha")
#--> Ina abubuwan da aka maimaita? Wane layi a shirinka yake tambayar wurarensu? (dalili: tsarin tunani yana nemo kafin ya aiwatar)
```

> run: kept

So the library cannot ask in Arabic or in Hausa through `AskIn`. What it can do is hold the data in any script, take a candidate computed in any pack, and let the tutor say the gap in four languages. A reply that has a space in it raises at the moment, and so the connectors "et" in French, "da" in Hausa and "و" in Arabic are not connectors. [AUTHOR: The tutor's French, Arabic and Hausa wording is, in the code's words, a draft awaiting a native reviewer. May the chapter print it as it stands, with that caveat, or will you give the Arabic and the Hausa you want shown?]

### A model is a fluency, never the mind

Without a model, a conversation stays on its deterministic floor and says so. The word "neural" can be set, and it is claimed only when a model is really there:

```haro
oKF2 = new stzKnowledgeGraph("fluency-space")
oKF2.AddConversation("f")
? oKF2.ConversationQ("f").Fluency()
#--> plain
oKF2.ConversationQ("f").SetFluency(:neural)
? oKF2.ConversationQ("f").Fluency()
#--> neural
? oKF2.ConversationQ("f").IsFluencyNeural()
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused to guess.** The record's phrase is: guessing is replaced by asking, and vibes are replaced by governance. A gap is a question. A reply is a candidate.

**I refused a different gate for each way of answering.** A formula is not trusted because it is code, and a structure is not trusted because it is typed. The form does not buy trust. All five registers land in one funnel.

**I refused a stub.** A reserved agent raises when it is asked for. The design document says it plainly: the raise is the law working, and it should not be read as a defect. I refused to pretend the fifth register works.

**I refused an agent that fills a gap to finish the job.** The wise coder stops, and leaves the gap for a person.

**I refused to let a conversation own a world.** It happens inside a knowledge space and never owns one, and a copy of the knowledge held in a session goes stale. That is the reason every call above is chained from the space.

**I refused a deleted checkpoint.** Expiry takes a refusal off the live list. The record keeps it.

**I refused to let the example look like the answer.** The record's closing law about the fifth register is that an admitted candidate writes its example back as a promise. That law is designed, and not built.

## 5. The court

`wisecoding_narrated` proves that the gap is computed from the space; that the question is led by the system, names its subject and says why it asks; that "X and Y" and a data structure admit; that a unique law refuses through the conversation and the refusal reaches a checkpoint; and that concluding with a gap open is refused. `wisecoding_rich_narrated` proves the goal monitored to fulfilled or revoked, the frame, the numbered options and the refusal of a number nobody offered, the lifetime of a checkpoint, and the fluency that is claimed only with a model. `ownagentstack_narrated` proves the roster of five, the wired wise coder, the reserved agent that raises, and the gap left for a person. `tutor_gaps_narrated` proves the exercise's gap question in each language, and that a step with no question in a language is red, never another language. It writes folders, so the book counts it and does not run it; the last block above only reads the course.

The guards end by calling a function that prints the total and then stops the run with a raise. That is the guard's way to end.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in understanding. A dialogue framework reads free text with trained models, speaks on many channels and ships connectors. The record says what Softanza lacks, in its own count: the word "WhatsApp" appears in no code file and no design document of the library. The conversation asks one slot at a time, over a graph the user must first have declared, and it does not understand an open reply.

The field leads on examples. Programming by example is shipped in spreadsheets today. Softanza has no induction at all, and the record says so: nothing induces yet. A search for "induce", "from examples" and "infer" over the regular-expression folder finds nothing.

The field is honest about costs, and so is the record. Several candidates is the normal case when you ask for a rule from examples. A pattern induced from three examples overfits them. The remedy the record names is the question that tells two candidates apart. And the index that would carry the examples is as clean as the tags of the methods, with about eighty-five misspellings.

Where I think the record stands apart: every reply, in whatever form, is judged by the world's laws before it counts, with the reason, and the session keeps a transcript and a checkpoint that a person can read. The field's reply is a prompt, and the human keeps what comes out.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the goal, the gap, the question with its reason, the frame; the first register, the second, and the fourth in the form of a splitter; the governed door with its refusals; the checkpoint and its lifetime; the goal fulfilled or revoked; the transcript; one wired agent; the tutor's four-language gap sentence.

What is designed: the *several acceptable* branch as the design writes it. The protocol says that when several readings of a reply are acceptable, the system enumerates them and the person chooses. What is built is two different things: the values already known become numbered options in the question, before the reply; and a reply that carries several values has each one judged on its own, with the first kept under a unique law. No step presents competing readings of one reply. And the *none acceptable* branch as written: the reason is built, and the nearest acceptable alternatives are not. The refusal states a reason, and for a number, how many options were on the table. The site's two pages on this, *wise* and *by example*, say "with the nearest alternatives" and "the person chooses", and the pages say more than the code does. [AUTHOR: May the chapter say outright that the two pages promise more than the code, so that the page or the code is corrected? And the code wires three of the five registers, while the memo that names the formula and the examples calls them filed and not routed: does the chapter say they are the next work you have filed, or only that they are absent?]

What is absent: the formula register; the example register, and the induction agent behind it; a conversation worded in Arabic, Hausa or French; and a reply with a space in it. In the version of the library this book runs, `ReplyIn` raises, with an uninitialised variable, on any value containing a space: "tomato sauce", "fromage et tomate", "jibini da tumatir". The direct call to the door returns the refusal properly. The conversation should have refused with the reason and written a checkpoint, and it doesn't. It is in the memo as a request, and the exercise below does not use a reply with a space.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** A reply is a verdict you can test with ordinary promises: what was admitted, what was refused, and the state of the goal.

**The analyst.** The goal is the schema of a complete model. The gaps are the list of what is missing. The transcript and the checkpoints are the evidence of how each fact got in.

**The designer.** The question comes back as data: force, subject, relation, options, reason. Any medium can render it. The library renders English, and the tutor shows the pattern for four languages.

**The decision-maker.** A reply is judged before it counts. A refusal reaches a person with its context and a lifetime. The roster is honest: one agent of five is wired, and two of the five registers are not available.

**The educator.** The tutor is wise coding. It names the missing step as a question and never as the answer, in the learner's language, and its wording in three of those languages awaits a native reviewer.

**The agent.** You can drive the loop yourself with an answer source, and you must stop where you would have to invent. You cannot offer examples and be understood, and you are told by name when you ask for a reserved agent.

## 9. Exercise

Choose a small world of your own, a shop, a class, a clinic, with four entities of one type and a goal that each needs one relation. Before you run anything, write under each of the replies below the verdict you expect, admitted, refused with a reason, or neither:

1. a plain word;
2. a second word, offered as a number after the first is known;
3. a number nobody offered;
4. two values at once, for a relation you have declared unique;
5. a list of two pairs of examples.

Then run it. If a promise breaks, do not change the promise: explain the output first.

```haro exercise
oWorld = new stzKnowledgeGraph("my-world")
oWorld.Know("item1", "thing").Know("item2", "thing")
oWorld.AddConversationQ("mine").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("thing", "colour"))
oWorld.AskIn("mine")
# your five replies here, each with the verdict you expect
```

> run: exercise

The fifth reply produces silence today, and that is the lesson of the exercise. If the library later turns it into a refusal, the expected answer changes. Run it on the date and commit shown at the foot of the page.

# The domain language model

Source: Chapter 19. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the domain model's first rung with no neurons; its facts rendered as a corpus; a closed vocabulary; completion by lookup; a plain-text bundle that travels with its tests; a small second rung, a bigram, exported to a model file; the admission of a fact by a law; designed: a grammar derived from a knowledge base by one call; the typed unit of prediction validated in five stages; a second rung whose corpus is checked before it trains; the foundry that would make it larger; absent: a pipeline that chains the five stages on any unit; an end-of-sentence word for the second rung; a completion that sees the sentence so far

Run: 7 of 7 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:25 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

A language model predicts the next token. A token is a fragment of text with no type, no role and no constraint: it can be the end of a word, a space, a bracket. Everything a model says is built by choosing tokens one after another, and everything that makes the result useful, that it is a valid sentence, that it is true, that it fits a schema, is checked afterwards or enforced from outside.

The field has made this work with three tools. You adapt a general model to a domain by training it further on the domain's documents. You give it the relevant documents at the moment of the question, retrieved by similarity. And you constrain its decoding with a grammar, so that it cannot produce a malformed sentence, as chapter 11 showed. All three keep the unit the same: the token.

## 2. The first principle

**The next thing the model says is a checkable unit of your world, a fact your rules admit or refuse, never a token.** And the gate is the same for every model of this kind: generations are proposals, and the graph and its rules decide admission.

## 3. The Softanza form

I call it a domain language model, and it has two rungs. The first has no neurons at all and was shown in chapter 17: it answers from recorded facts, says why, and refuses outside its domain. The second is a small neural model, optional, which gives it fluency. Both end at the same gate.

Begin with where the training text comes from. No one writes it. Each sentence is a fact of the knowledge base, rendered, and the vocabulary is closed: every word the domain holds, with a first entry for the unknown.

```haro
oKB = new stzKnowledgeGraph("restaurant")
oKB.Know("margherita", "dish").
    Know("tiramisu", "dish").
    Know("chianti", "wine").
    KnowRelation("margherita", "contains", "tomato-sauce").
    KnowRelation("margherita", "contains", "mozzarella").
    KnowRelation("margherita", "pairs-with", "chianti")
oDLM = StzDlmQ(oKB)
? @@( oDLM.GenerateCorpus() )
#--> [ "margherita is a dish.", "tiramisu is a dish.", "chianti is a wine.", "margherita contains tomato-sauce.", "margherita contains mozzarella.", "margherita pairs-with chianti." ]
? @@( oDLM.Tokenizer() )
#--> [ "<unk>", "margherita", "tiramisu", "chianti", "dish", "wine", "contains", "pairs-with", "is", "a", "tomato-sauce", "mozzarella" ]
? @@( oDLM.Tokenize("margherita contains something-new") )
#--> [ 2, 7, 1 ]
```

> run: kept

Twelve words, and a word outside the domain maps to the first one. "Correct by construction" holds here in the one sense the code gives it: each sentence is a recorded fact. It does not yet hold in the sense the design intended; section 7 says where.

Completion is constrained to the domain: after an entity, only its relations; after a relation, only its objects; a fragment completes to entities; a word that the domain does not hold completes to nothing.

```haro
? @@( oDLM.Complete("margherita") )
#--> [ "contains", "pairs-with" ]
? @@( oDLM.Complete("contains") )
#--> [ "tomato-sauce", "mozzarella" ]
? @@( oDLM.Complete("tira") )
#--> [ "tiramisu" ]
? @@( oDLM.Complete("pizza") )
#--> [ ]
```

> run: kept

I have to say what this is, because the guard that proves it calls it "the grammar spirit". It is a lookup in the facts, not decoding under a compiled grammar. And it sees one word, not the sentence so far: `Complete("contains")` returns the objects of every `contains` fact, whatever its subject.

The grammar itself can be derived from the knowledge base, and here the reader of this book has to write the derivation. There is no call in the library that turns a knowledge base into a grammar, and the design lists it as the first rung's task. But the pieces compose. The domain's vocabulary becomes the choices of a schema from chapter 11, the schema compiles to a grammar, and the grammar has no path for a word outside the domain:

```haro
aLex = oDLM.Lexicon()
acObjects = []
for aFact in oKB.Facts()
	if aFact[2] != "is-a" and find(acObjects, aFact[3]) = 0
		acObjects + aFact[3]
	ok
next
oS = StzOutputSchemaQ([
	[ :field = "subject",  :type = :oneof, :choices = aLex[:entities] ],
	[ :field = "relation", :type = :oneof, :choices = aLex[:relations] ],
	[ :field = "object",   :type = :oneof, :choices = acObjects ]
])
? oS.ToGBNF()
#--> root ::= subject-line relation-line object-line
#    subject-line ::= "subject: " ("margherita" | "tiramisu" | "chianti") "\n"
#    relation-line ::= "relation: " ("contains" | "pairs-with") "\n"
#    object-line ::= "object: " ("tomato-sauce" | "mozzarella" | "chianti") "\n"
#    text ::= [^\n]+
#    number ::= "-"? [0-9]+ ("." [0-9]+)?
#    boolean ::= ("yes" | "no" | "true" | "false")
cGood = "subject: margherita" + nl + "relation: contains" + nl + "object: mozzarella" + nl
cOut  = "subject: pizza" + nl + "relation: contains" + nl + "object: mozzarella" + nl
cLie  = "subject: tiramisu" + nl + "relation: contains" + nl + "object: mozzarella" + nl
? oS.Accepts(cGood)
#--> 1
? oS.Accepts(cOut)
#--> 0
? oS.Accepts(cLie)
#--> 1
? oDLM.Ask("tiramisu contains what?")
#--> I hold no 'contains' knowledge about 'tiramisu'.
```

> run: kept

Look at the last four lines together. The grammar accepts the sentence that says a tiramisu contains mozzarella, because it has the right shape and every word is in the domain. The model refuses it, because the graph holds no such fact. A grammar is a gate for shape; the truth belongs to the graph.

A domain language model is a file that travels. It is saved as plain text, readable and diff-able, with no binary and no weights, and a second model that has never seen the knowledge base can answer from it and run its own tests:

```haro
oDLM.AddGolden("what is margherita", "Margherita is a dish.")
oDLM.AddGolden("what is the weather today?",
	"That is outside the 'restaurant' domain (I know 3 entities and 2 relations).")
cFile = oDLM.Save("probe_dlm")
? cFile
#--> probe_dlm.zdlm
? read(cFile)
#--> dlm "restaurant"
#    entities
#        margherita | dish
#        tiramisu | dish
#        chianti | wine
#    relations
#        contains
#        pairs-with
#    facts
#        margherita | contains | tomato-sauce
#        margherita | contains | mozzarella
#        margherita | pairs-with | chianti
#    laws
#    goldens
#        what is margherita | Margherita is a dish.
#        what is the weather today? | That is outside the 'restaurant' domain (I know 3 entities and 2 relations).
oShipped = StzDlmQ("")
oShipped.LoadFrom(cFile)
? oShipped.Ask("what is tiramisu")
#--> Tiramisu is a dish.
? @@( oShipped.RunGoldens() )
#--> [ [ "total", 2 ], [ "passed", 2 ], [ "failed", [ ] ] ]
remove(cFile)
? fexists(cFile)
#--> 0
```

> run: kept (touches files)

The refusal travels with the bundle as a golden test, and the second model, which has never seen the graph, passes it. The laws section is empty because no law was declared before the model was forged; declare that a relation is unique, forge again, and the line appears.

Now the second rung. It is a small neural model trained on the corpus you saw, with no teacher, and it can be exported as a model file. I will tell its size in the same breath as its result, because it is small: a single layer that learns to predict the next word from the previous one, over twelve words, trained on fifteen pairs.

```haro
oDLM.TrainNeuralRung(500)
? oDLM.NeuralWhy()
#--> neural bigram rung: 15 pairs over a 12-word vocab; trained 500 epoch(s), lr 0.50: loss 2.57 -> 0.70
? oDLM.NextToken("is")
#--> a
? oDLM.NextTokenConfidence("is")
#--> 1.00
cGguf = oDLM.ExportNeuralGguf("probe_bigram")
? cGguf
#--> probe_bigram.gguf
? StzEngineNeuralGgufInspect(cGguf)
#--> 2
remove(cGguf)
```

> run: kept (touches files)

The file holds two tensors, a weight and a bias, and the library can read it back. I do not claim another runtime loads it as a text generator. It trains in a fraction of a second, offline, and gives the same numbers on every run.

What does a model like this say? It is fluent in the grammar of the domain, and it is wrong about facts. That is the reason for the gate:

```haro
aCorpus = oDLM.GenerateCorpus()
for cSeed in [ "tiramisu", "chianti", "margherita" ]
	cSaid = oDLM.NeuralGenerate(cSeed, 3)
	? cSaid + "  ->  " + ( find(aCorpus, cSaid + ".") > 0 )
next
#--> tiramisu is a wine  ->  0
#    chianti is a wine  ->  1
#    margherita contains mozzarella is  ->  0
```

> run: kept

"Tiramisu is a wine" is false, because tiramisu is a dish. "Chianti is a wine" is true. And "margherita contains mozzarella is" is a run-on, because the bigram has no word that ends a sentence. The test against the corpus is a crude gate, and it would refuse a true fact nobody recorded. The real gate is the graph and its laws:

```haro
oKB.ConstrainRelation("is-a", :Unique)
aVerdict = oKB.Admit("tiramisu", "is-a", "wine", [ :source = "rung 2" ])
? aVerdict[:admitted]
#--> 0
? aVerdict[:why]
#--> 'tiramisu' already bears 'is-a' (to 'dish'); the relation is :Unique
? @@( oKB.Contradictions() )
#--> [ [ [ "subject", "tiramisu" ], [ "relation", "is-a" ], [ "existing", "dish" ], [ "attempted", "wine" ], [ "source", "rung 2" ] ] ]
? oKB.Admit("pizza", "is-a", "dish", [ :source = "rung 2" ])[:why]
#--> admitted: 'pizza' is-a 'dish'
```

> run: kept

The false proposal is refused with its reason, and the refusal is kept, with its source: the model's second rung. A new fact is admitted. That is the whole of the principle in seven lines: a proposal, a law, a refusal with its why, and the refusal on the record.

The design describes the unit of prediction in three levels. A token is a fragment with no type. An embedding is a vector without a role. And a chunk is a typed node of a graph, with a tag, a payload and edges: a declaration, a relationship, a constraint. A chunk is validated in five stages before it enters the graph: type, pattern, graph integrity, compliance, and constructive contribution. I tell it as design, because there is no chunk in the library. What exists are pieces: the court of chapter 11 checks a text's type and shape; the admission above enforces a law, which is compliance; and adding a fact twice is a harmless no-op, which is a small form of the last stage. Nothing chains them.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

On 13 July 2026, I ruled on this. The concept of a domain language model had been designed for one of the estate's products; I donated it to the open foundation, so that any Softanza project that supplies a knowledge base can ship its own model free to the users of its domain. The format is `.zdlm`. The floor is the first rung, in every model; the second is optional fluency. [AUTHOR: The record has your ruling that a model is free to ship, and no licence text to say so. May the book tell "free" as: a plain file, in the open foundation, with no licence fee or meter?]

**I refused the token as the unit.** The unit is a chunk of the world, which a graph can admit or refuse.

**I refused a teacher I cannot see.** The earlier design of the idea used a large commercial model to write its training corpus. I put a law against it, and this model's corpus is rendered from the domain's own facts: the library's second law says a capability never requires anything paid or remote.

**I refused neural first.** The finding the section starts from is that the canonical model is not neural. A review of the earlier product archived a transformer of one to three billion parameters, in favour of rule-based prediction, on the ground that hallucination is impossible when the model cannot generate almost-valid text. So the first rung has zero training and zero cost, and the neural rung comes second.

**I refused to let the shape stand for the truth.** The grammar says structure kills malformedness, not falsehood. The truth belongs to the graph, to its laws, and to a person.

## 5. The court

`dlm_narrated` proves the model forged from a graph, its answers with their why, its refusal outside the domain, the completion, the plain-text bundle with its goldens, the closed tokenizer and the second rung with its model file. One of its assertions on the second rung checks only that the generated sentence begins with a fixed prefix, so it would also pass the false sentence above. `structuredoutput_narrated`, `schema_grammar_narrated` and `constrained_decoding_narrated` prove the court of chapter 11, the grammar compiler and the sampler; the last of them has two scenes that need a generative model file, which this machine does not hold, so only part of it ran for this page. `knowledge_integration_narrated` proves derivation and the refusal by a law. And I should say that the admission I show in the last block is not called by its name in any guard: it is reached through the conversation, and by the strict form of adding a fact, which the guard does assert.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in scale. Training and serving models of every size is a mature practice; this second rung is a single layer over twelve words, and the steps that would make it more, a validated corpus, deduplication, a trained tokenizer, a larger network, quantisation, are not done. I do not compete with large models.

The field leads in fluency, which is the point of a model. A first rung that answers from facts will say "I hold no knowledge about that" where a general model would write something plausible, and for a customer the plausible thing is often more useful. The record's own answer is the second rung, and its present state is the table above.

Where I think this stands apart is in what the output is. A general model's answer is text, and every check on it is after the fact. Here every answer of the first rung carries its why and a certainty of one, and every proposal of the second meets a gate that can refuse it with a reason. A model that can only say what the domain holds, and a gate that remembers what it refused: that is what a bank, a ministry or a school can sign.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the first rung and its answers; the facts rendered as a corpus, with a closed vocabulary; completion by lookup; the bundle that travels with its tests; a second rung that trains in a fraction of a second and exports a small model file; and the admission of a fact by a law.

What is designed, and the page must say each:

- The grammar derived from a knowledge base by one call. Chapter 19's composition is the reader's, not the library's.
- The second rung's corpus validated by the first rung's grammar before it is admitted. The design says so; the code renders the facts directly, with no such check. So "correct by construction" holds for the training text, and not for what the model says.
- The five-stage validation of a chunk. None exists as a pipeline.
- The foundry's steps beyond the bigram.

What is absent: an end-of-sentence word for the second rung, which is why one of its sentences runs on; and a completion that sees the sentence so far, which is why a completion after a relation lists the objects of every fact of that relation. The first rung also answers a rephrased question with the type, as chapter 17 showed, where the law of this part says it should refuse.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** A model is a file you can read, diff and test: its facts, its laws and its goldens.

**The analyst.** A fact you add makes the model know more, and a law you add makes it refuse more, with no training.

**The designer.** The model's refusal is part of its contract: the golden test says what it must decline to answer.

**The decision-maker.** The model can only say what the domain holds, and the gate keeps the record of what it refused and why.

**The educator.** Rung one is a model a learner can read in full. Rung two is small enough to train in front of a class.

**The agent.** What you propose is a fact the graph can admit or refuse, with a reason you can read.

## 9. Exercise

Forge a domain of your own with three entities and two relations. Before you run anything, write what you expect from three calls: the completion of an entity, the completion of a relation, and the answer to a question about a thing the domain does not hold. Then declare that one relation is unique and propose, in the form of a fact, something that contradicts it.

Run it. Then write a sentence of the right shape and the wrong truth, and say which of the grammar and the graph refuses it, and which does not.
