# Agents that cannot hurt you

Source: Chapter 20. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: reversibility as a declared contract and a registration gate; posture set against reversibility, at the door and at load; the workbench, the plan, the reviewer's rejection, the committing actor under a scope; the containment drill; the engine's bounded slots, inbox and queue; designed: the agent's side of the negotiation over a plan; the examination hall; branches as parallel minds; twins for databases, interfaces and networks; absent: a plan with no executor that is refused; a ranking of a plan's risks; code already running in the process, which is out of scope; the Right to a Human as a machine check

Run: 9 of 9 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:26 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

In the field, safety is a property of the agent. The doctrine document of the library lists what the field offers: alignment, guardrails, confirmation prompts, a checkbox that says a human is in the loop. Tool-using agents, and the families that descend from the first of them, let the model's tool calls touch reality immediately, with a prompt for confirmation. Computer-use agents run in a sandboxed virtual machine, which contains a process, and does not tell a file moved from random bytes churning.

The best advice of the field, repeated in every serious guide of 2026, is to separate planning from execution and put a human approval between them. It is good advice, and it is advice: a pattern you implement, and can forget. The threat lists of the field, the OWASP list of risks for agentic applications among them, are lenses, and the record uses them so.

So the field's posture, in a line, is that the agent is a model in a loop, and safety is vigilance around it. (General knowledge, not a study of the record, except where I name the document.) Chapter 17 said this of the whole mind. This chapter takes the action half: what happens when the proposal meets the world.

## 2. The first principle

**Do not build safe agents. Build a safe world, and let ordinary agents loose inside it.**

The sentence is in the library's doctrine document: "We will not build safe agents. We will build a safe world, and let ordinary agents loose inside it." The document gives its structural half in one more: the only artifact that ever leaves its world is an update plan, and plans are executed by someone else. [AUTHOR: The doctrine document carries no date and no author line in its header, and git first records it in a commit of 9 July 2026. Is the sentence yours, may the chapter open on it in the first person, and when was it written?]

## 3. The Softanza form

Four steps, in the form the study of the record gives them: the agent proposes, the workbench rehearses, the court judges, and only a governed actor commits. I will show each in code, and then the cases where a step does not hold.

**May this agent exist?** Before an agent runs a single tick, the governance asks the question, and it asks it when the answer is free. An agent that declares no coverage, one sentence saying what it covers, is refused. An agent that declares no reversibility class is refused too: reversible, compensable or irreversible. Both refusals are in the engine's own sentences, and the Ring door repeats them.

```haro
oGov = new stzGovernance("shop")
oGov.DeclareReversibility("restocker", :Compensable)
? oGov.ReversibilityOf("restocker")
#--> compensable
? oGov.MayRegister("mystery", "", "reversible")
#--> 0
? oGov.Why()
#--> AGENTLOOP-R4: agent 'mystery' declares no coverage statement. Registration refuses it. Say what this agent covers, in one sentence, before the loop will run it. Law 18: nothing schedules what nobody has stated the reach of.
? oGov.MayRegister("mystery", "reorders stock", "")
#--> 0
? oGov.Why()
#--> AGENTLOOP-R5: agent 'mystery' declares no reversibility class. Registration refuses it. Declare one of: reversible | compensable | irreversible. Law 18: an agent whose reversal nobody stated cannot be scheduled by something that cannot undo it.
? oGov.MayRegister("mystery", "reorders stock", :Compensable)
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

Reversibility is the sixth contract of the governance, beside risk, authority, commitment, decommission and lineage. It is orthogonal to risk: an act of small risk that cannot be undone deserves more ceremony than a large one that can.

**The harder an act is to undo, the more trusted the code that does it must be.** Here is the whole table, the posture of the code against the class of the act, printed by a loop of the book's own:

```haro
aPostures = [ "trusted", "external", "sandboxed" ]
aClasses = [ "reversible", "compensable", "irreversible" ]
for cP in aPostures
	cRow = cP + ":"
	for cC in aClasses
		if StzPostureReversibilityRefusal(cP, cC) = ""
			cRow += " yes"
		else
			cRow += " no"
		ok
	next
	? cRow
next
#--> trusted: yes yes yes
#--> external: yes yes no
#--> sandboxed: yes no no
? StzPostureReversibilityRefusal("sandboxed", "irreversible")
#--> a 'sandboxed' posture does not cover 'irreversible' work -- the harder an act is to undo, the more trusted the code performing it must be (reversibility x posture, ruling 3.2)
```

> run: kept

**The agent runs, believes it acted, and the disk does not move.** An agent is a file and a few functions. Its function writes a price list. It is given a workbench, and the function reaches the bench as an ambient object during one cycle, with no change in its signature. The skill verifies its own work; the fact is learned; and the real file does not exist, because the file lives in the twin:

```haro
StzAllowAgentFunction("ScribblePrices", "trusted")
cFx = StzReplace(WorkingDirectory(), "\", "/") + "/_fixture"
StzDirCreatePath(cFx)
$cPrices = cFx + "/prices.csv"
cPia = "pia: 2
name: scribbler
kind: pi
coverage: writes a price list and records that it did
reversibility: compensable
schedule:
  timer: 20
skills:
  - name: scribble
    when: always
    does: ring:ScribblePrices
    verify: fact prices were written
    posture: trusted
"
oAg = StzAgentDeclarationQ(cPia).ToAgent()
oAg.GiveWorkbench()
? oAg.Cycle()
#--> 1
? oAg.MemoryQ().Fact("prices", "were", "written")
#--> 1
? StzFileExists($cPrices)
#--> 0
? oAg.WorkbenchQ().Exists($cPrices)
#--> 1
? oAg.WorkbenchQ().ContentOf($cPrices)
#--> espresso,2.40
#--> flat-white,3.10

func ScribblePrices(poMemory)
	StzActiveWorkbenchQ().WriteFile($cPrices, "espresso,2.40" + char(10) + "flat-white,3.10")
	poMemory.Learn("prices", "were", "written")
```

> run: kept

Two lines of the file decide who may act. `posture: trusted` is there because a `ring:` clause must carry a posture, and `StzAllowAgentFunction` is the platform's code, and not the agent's file, deciding which functions a file may call.

**The only export is a plan, and it narrates itself.** The tick exports toward reality a plan and nothing else. The plan says what it would do before anything is attempted. A language model set as its executor is refused before any attempt, because it lacks the capability to produce an effect. A named actor under a scope that does not cover the path commits nothing: the disk still did not move. The same actor under a scope that covers the path commits once, and the audit trail names the actor. Reality changes here and only here:

```haro
oPlan = oAg.GenerateUpdatePlan()
? StzReplace(oPlan.Narration(), cFx, "<fixture>")
#--> Update plan (1 of 1 operations to commit):
#-->   * 1. write 29 bytes to '<fixture>/prices.csv'
oPlan.SetExecutor(LLMActor("eager-llm"))
? oPlan.MayCommit()[2]
#--> actor 'eager-llm' cannot commit -- it lacks the 'effectful' capability (required by operation 1)
oScope = new stzCommitScope()
oScope.AllowUnder("/somewhere/else")
oPlan2 = oAg.GenerateUpdatePlan()
oPlan2.SetExecutor(PIActor("shop-committer"))
oPlan2.SetScope(oScope)
oPlan2.Execute()
? StzFileExists($cPrices)
#--> 0
oScope3 = new stzCommitScope()
oScope3.AllowUnder(cFx)
oPlan3 = oAg.GenerateUpdatePlan()
oPlan3.SetExecutor(PIActor("shop-committer"))
oPlan3.SetScope(oScope3)
oPlan3.Execute()
? StzFileExists($cPrices)
#--> 1
? @@(oPlan3.AuditTrail())
#--> [ [ 1, "committed", "write_file", "shop-committer" ] ]
StzDirDeleteAll(cFx)
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

I replaced the absolute path in the narration so the output is stable. A plan embeds the path of the file it would write.

**The court at load.** The composition of the table above is enforced when the file is loaded, before the first tick. Take the same file, make the agent irreversible, and make its code sandboxed:

```haro
cIrrev = StzReplace(StzReplace(cPia, "reversibility: compensable", "reversibility: irreversible"), "posture: trusted", "posture: sandboxed")
oD = StzAgentDeclarationQ(cIrrev)
? oD.IsValid()
#--> 0
? oD.CiteFindings()
#--> [pia-posture-reversibility @ skills.scribble.posture] a 'sandboxed' posture does not cover 'irreversible' work -- the harder an act is to undo, the more trusted the code performing it must be (reversibility x posture, ruling 3.2)
```

> run: kept

**A proposal gone wrong.** An agent that tidies a folder by deleting every file proposes thirty deletions. The plan lists thirty operations and flags thirty as risks. A language model that asks to commit is refused. And when it calls `Execute()` anyway, all thirty files are still there:

```haro
cDir = StzReplace(WorkingDirectory(), "\", "/") + "/_course"
StzDirCreatePath(cDir)
for i = 1 to 30
	StzFileWrite(cDir + "/lesson" + i + ".txt", "lesson " + i)
next
oBench = new stzVirtualFileSystem()
oBench.SetActor("tidy-bot")
for i = 1 to 30
	oBench.MirrorFile(cDir + "/lesson" + i + ".txt")
	oBench.DeleteFile(cDir + "/lesson" + i + ".txt")
next
oBig = oBench.GenerateUpdatePlan()
? oBig.NumberOfOperations()
#--> 30
? len(oBig.Risks())
#--> 30
oBig.SetExecutor(LLMActor("helper-llm"))
? oBig.MayCommit()[2]
#--> actor 'helper-llm' cannot commit -- it lacks the 'effectful' capability (required by operation 1)
oBig.Execute()
nLeft = 0
for i = 1 to 30
	nLeft += StzFileExists(cDir + "/lesson" + i + ".txt")
next
? nLeft
#--> 30
```

> run: kept (touches files, the system)

The site shows this with a larger course, in a demo that I did not run for this book, and prints a count of six hundred and ten. I print thirty, because thirty is what this block runs. [AUTHOR: May the chapter quote the site's six hundred and ten, as measured by the education demo on 30 September 2026, or keep only the number the book runs?]

**A person can argue with a plan.** The plan is a medium. A reviewer rejects one step, with a reason; a named actor under a scope commits the other twenty-nine; one is skipped; and the file the reviewer protected is the one file still on disk:

```haro
oRev = oBench.GenerateUpdatePlan()
oRev.RejectOperation(2, "reviewer: keep lesson 2")
oRev.SetExecutor(PIActor("oncall"))
oScopeC = new stzCommitScope()
oScopeC.AllowUnder(cDir)
oRev.SetScope(oScopeC)
aRes = oRev.Execute()
? aRes[1][2]
#--> 29
? aRes[2][2]
#--> 1
nLeft = 0
for i = 1 to 30
	nLeft += StzFileExists(cDir + "/lesson" + i + ".txt")
next
? nLeft
#--> 1
StzDirDeleteAll(cDir)
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

**One rule, two layers, the same words.** The refusal that chapter 17 showed at the graph, and that the file showed above, is also enforced in the engine's own tick loop, at registration, in the graph rule's sentence. A model with the capability to produce effects is refused. A model without it registers like any other slot:

```haro
StzResetEngineAgentLoop()
nL = stzenginezutercreate("summarizer")
? stzengineagentloopregister(nL, 1, 1, 0, 1, 0, 10, "summarises tickets")
#--> -6
? stzengineagentlooplastrefusal()
#--> AGENTLOOP-R6: agent 'summarizer' (slot 0) breaks the rule no-llm-effectful -- llm actor holds 'effectful' -- an LLM proposes, only a pi-gate commits. Registration refuses it. Drop the effectful capability, or register it as a pi-gated composition rather than an llm actor.
nL2 = stzenginezutercreate("proposer")
? stzengineagentloopregister(nL2, 1, 1, 0, 0, 0, 10, "proposes summaries")
#--> 0
StzResetEngineAgentLoop()
```

> run: kept

The arguments after the slot are, in the order the guard gives them: the kind (1 is a model), the reversibility class, the trigger, the capability, and what remains. They are the engine's low-level names, and I show them so you can see the refusal come from the engine, in the same words.

**An agent of the estate's own roster, rehearsed.** Until 22 August 2026, an agent of the estate's own roster, the one that rolls the ledger, moved a real journal file directly, on a tick, with no plan, no scope and no committing actor. The doctrine had been written before it was true, and the record caught it the same day. Now the agent relocates the file in the twin: two skills fire and verify; the real journal file is where it was; no archive exists; the tick has exported a plan; and only the committing actor, under a scope, moves the file:

```haro
cEstate = StzReplace(WorkingDirectory(), "\", "/") + "/_estate"
StzDirCreatePath(cEstate + "/dashboard")
StzDirCreatePath(cEstate + "/journal")
SetEnvVar("STZ_SOFTANZA_ROOT", cEstate)
StzFileWrite(cEstate + "/journal/2000-01-scar.md", "an old journal entry" + char(10))
StzFileWrite(cEstate + "/dashboard/SESSION-LOG.md", "# SESSION-LOG" + char(10) + char(10) + "2000-01-15 09:00 | someone | long ago" + char(10))
StzAllowAgentFunction("RollRelocateMonths", "trusted")
StzAllowAgentFunction("RollWritePointer", "trusted")
cRoll = "pia: 2
name: ledger-roll
kind: pi
coverage: moves journal months before the current one into archive and leaves a pointer
reversibility: reversible
schedule:
  event: roll
governance:
  authority: delegated
  risks:
    relocate-evidence: 2
  grants:
    - relocate-evidence
skills:
  - name: relocate
    when: always
    does: ring:RollRelocateMonths
    verify: fact archive holds month
    effect: relocate-evidence
    posture: trusted
  - name: pointer
    when: fact archive holds month
    does: ring:RollWritePointer
    verify: fact ledger holds pointer
    effect: relocate-evidence
    posture: trusted
"
oRoll = StzAgentDeclarationQ(cRoll).ToAgent()
oRoll.GiveWorkbench()
? oRoll.Cycle()
#--> 2
? StzFileExists(cEstate + "/journal/2000-01-scar.md")
#--> 1
? StzFileExists(cEstate + "/journal/archive/2000-01-scar.md")
#--> 0
oRP = oRoll.GenerateUpdatePlan()
? oRP.NumberOfOperations()
#--> 7
? len(oRP.Risks()) >= 1
#--> 1
oRP.SetExecutor(PIActor("roll-committer"))
oScopeR = new stzCommitScope()
oScopeR.AllowUnder(cEstate)
oRP.SetScope(oScopeR)
oRP.Execute()
? StzFileExists(cEstate + "/journal/2000-01-scar.md")
#--> 0
? StzFileExists(cEstate + "/journal/archive/2000-01-scar.md")
#--> 1
StzDirDeleteAll(cEstate)
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

The count of seven operations is what this run measures. The library's own guard only promises at least five.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused safe agents.** The doctrine's reason is that the field's safety tries to make a fundamentally unpredictable actor trustworthy. Asking whether the agent might escape is like asking whether a chess engine might move a piece on a physical board: the agent's tool surface contains no operation that touches the disk, a database or the network.

**I refused a permission check as the boundary.** It is not a check that the agent might route around. The powers are separated: creativity without authority, authority without creativity.

**I refused a second workbench for machines.** The sandbox is the same workbench that the human programmer already uses. The framework was conceived for human programmers, and everything designed to liberate one is exactly what is needed to contain an artificial one.

**I refused to leave the gate optional.** The ruling of 22 August 2026 says the registration gate is doctrine, and that opting in is a migration state, not the design. The record is honest about the finding behind the ruling: where the doctrine of agents that cannot hurt you bound that day was nowhere. The safe world existed, and the agentic folder did not mention any of it. The ruling closed it the same day.

**I refused a stub.** Asking for a reserved agent raises. The architecture says that this is the law working, and not a defect.

**I refused to drop quietly.** In the engine, a send to a full inbox is refused with a code and counted. When the run queue is full, further visits are dropped and counted. The loop also states what it cannot see: the inside of a callback, the clock, and whether a coverage statement is true.

**I refused to let a machine be the last resort.** The interface constitution of the estate has a right, the sixth of six: the Right to a Human. Wherever a machine serves, a person is reachable, clearly, and without penalty. It is enforced by judgment, a person's, and not by a guard that runs; the library does not mention it. [AUTHOR: Do you want the chapter to say so in those words and to state the right as the human end of the loop, or to leave a one-line pointer to the interface constitution?]

## 5. The court

`safeworld_narrated` has seven scenes: reversibility as the sixth contract; the registration gate in the engine's sentences; the table of posture against reversibility; the file's court at load; what the file declares landing in the agent's governance; the workbench, from the disk that did not move to the audit trail; and the roster's agent rehearsed and then committed. `agentfile_narrated` proves that an agent is a file judged at load, with one refusal per gate, and that a folder is a deployment. `agentloop_engine_narrated` proves that the engine loop states its coverage at run time, that registration is a gate, that one rule gives the same words in two layers, and that an over-full queue drops visits and counts them. `containment_drill_narrated` proves a drill against a real target on loopback: a model investigator proposes the containment and commits nothing; a human commits, and containment holds when verified from outside. In the run I made on 8 October 2026 the drill read 443 ms to detect and 61 ms to contain, on this machine. The site prints other numbers from its own run, and the threat model others from its own, and the three differ because the drill measures a clock. The limit of the drill is one attack shape, on loopback, on one machine, run on demand. `virtual_system_twin_narrated` proves the twin: a rehearsal touches nothing real; the plan narrates and flags its risks; a scope refuses; a reviewer rejects a step; snapshots roll back.

The guard `roster_narrated` is not in the front matter, because it cannot run from a snapshot of the library: it loads the estate's roster files from a sibling repository. The rehearsal of the roster's agent is proved by `safeworld_narrated`, whose seventh scene is the last block above.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The record's own table is honest, and I carry its rows in prose.

The field leads in connectors: chat, drives and trackers a few clicks away, hosted vector stores, streaming interfaces. They are reachable from Softanza and not paved.

The field leads in retrieval over open corpora. Softanza wins on a curated domain and loses on the open web.

The field leads in frontier reasoning. That stack orchestrates the largest models on every call.

The field leads in ecosystem and hiring. There is a labour market for that stack, and none for this one.

When to reach for which, in the narration's words: the mainstream stack, when the task is open-ended language work over material you cannot curate, such as summarising arbitrary documents, conversational products, research over the open web. The Softanza layer, when actions have consequences and someone will eventually ask why it did that: regulated work, anything unattended, anything offline at zero marginal cost. The two are not exclusive, and a model can sit inside a Softanza system as a proposer.

The twin is a model of reality, not reality. The framework asks for fidelity and honesty, and the plan re-validates against reality before it is committed: after the real file is deleted behind the twin's back, the validation says the target is absent in reality. I saw it warn, and did not test whether it blocks the commit.

And the largest honest loss. The threat model puts *code already running in the process* out of scope: it can do anything the process can, wipe the ledger or read a secret. The workbench contains what is written through the bench. A function that does not ask for the bench is not confined. Reads are free. The architecture says that the escape is not a defect to be closed by removing it, and it narrows it with a declared posture, and with code, not the file, deciding which functions an agent may call. I did not run a trusted function that bypasses the bench: I read this limit in the architecture and the threat model.

## 7. The limit

What is built: reversibility as a declared contract, and the registration gate, in the Ring door and in the engine; posture set against reversibility at the door and at load; the workbench, with a plan that narrates, flags, validates, takes a rejection and keeps an audit trail; the roster's agent rehearsed and then committed; the drill; the engine's bounded structures, which are 64 agent slots, an inbox of 8 messages and a run queue of 256.

What is designed: the agent's side of the negotiation, in which the plan reaches the agent but the conversation about the plan does not yet. The architecture says so, and the threat model repeats it. I searched the library's code and found no revising of a plan. The examination hall, which would certify an agent for a commit scope the way you certify a driver for a licence class: it occurs in documents and in no code. Branches as parallel minds: snapshots and rollback are built, and I found no branch call. Twins for databases, interfaces and networks: the framework names them. The twin of files is built, and so is the twin of the process environment, with its variables, its working directory and its queued spawn (chapter 24). The other three are not.

What is a gap, found while running and reading:

- **An unset executor is unguarded.** A plan with no executor answers that no executor is set, and that a human at the keyboard is unguarded, and `Execute()` is allowed. The refusal of the block above applies to a model set as the executor. So "only a governed actor commits" is exact when an executor is set, and a person at a keyboard may commit a plan that names none.
- **A plan flags; it does not rank.** The narration says that a plan ranks its own risks. The code returns one flag per destructive step, in the order of the operations, with a label such as DESTRUCTIVE. There is no score and no order of severity. The chapter says "flags".
- **The gate is opt-in at the host.** The default flips when the last host in the tree declares. A fresh host runs the older pump.
- **The loop checks that a coverage statement exists, not that it is true.**
- **The drill's limits:** one shape of attack, on loopback, on one machine.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Your agent is a file and a few functions. It runs and believes it acted, and the disk does not move. You read the plan, choose the committing actor and the scope, and the court tells you in one sentence which rule refused you.

**The analyst.** Every proposal is a narrated plan of operations with the destructive steps flagged, and an audit line for each commit. You can read what the agent meant to do, step by step, without running it. The flags are by kind, not scored.

**The designer.** The plan is the one artefact that crosses from the agent to the person, and a person can reject one step. The Right to a Human says a person is reachable wherever a machine serves, and the right is held by judgment.

**The decision-maker.** You declare what each agent covers and how reversible it is before it may exist, and you decide the scope and the actor that commits. A model is structurally unable to commit, even when fooled; the threat model assumes it will be. The hardest part is the one the design leaves to you: which functions code allows an agent to call.

**The educator.** A learner's agent that tidies a course folder by deleting everything is a lesson in what a plan is. She sees what it would have done, and the files are still there.

**The agent.** You may rehearse without limit, inside the twin. Your only export is a plan, and you can be argued with over it. You may not commit. Today the argument is one-way: you do not yet receive the reviewer's objection and revise.

## 9. Exercise

Choose a folder of ten files you could lose. Write an agent file for a tidy-up job on it: what it covers, how reversible it is, one skill. Before you run anything, write under each line the promise you expect: how many files exist on disk after one cycle; how many operations the plan holds; how many of them the plan flags as risks; and what happens when you set a language model as the executor.

```haro exercise
cDirMine = StzReplace(WorkingDirectory(), "\", "/") + "/_mine"
StzDirCreatePath(cDirMine)
for i = 1 to 10
	StzFileWrite(cDirMine + "/file" + i + ".txt", "file " + i)
next
oMine = new stzVirtualFileSystem()
oMine.SetActor("my-tidy-bot")
# your tidy-up proposal here, with the promise you expect under each line
StzDirDeleteAll(cDirMine)
```

> run: exercise

Run it. Then give the plan to a committing actor under a scope that does not cover the folder, and write the promise again. If a promise breaks, do not change the promise: explain the output first. A good learner is surprised once. The plan flags its destructive steps and does not rank them, and a plan with no executor at all is allowed.


=====

# The court: promises, perception, security and performance

Source: Chapter 21. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the promise judged as a claim about a stop; the security plane with its ledger, sentinel, drill, response and secrets, and its table of guarantees tied to guards; the performance plane with its instrument and its governed loop; the perception law, adopted; designed: routes, the clock and weather, and perception for the promise runner; the plugin plane; the negotiation over a rejected plan; absent: a human verdict on any picture of the gallery; seven of the eight musical traditions heard by a person from them; a real vault and a real sandbox as proofs; a time-series store

Run: 7 of 7 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:26 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

Four practices of the field are four courts, and they are rarely seen as one.

The first is the example that runs. Python's doctest runs the interactive session found in a docstring and compares the printed text. Rust runs the examples in its documentation, and Go has testable examples with an expected output in a comment. The example is the specification. Its weakness is that the comparison is text against text, and the harness decides what a failure is.

The second is the snapshot test. A screenshot or a rendered tree is stored and compared on the next run. It proves that today's picture equals yesterday's.

The third is the audit against a list. The field's lists are OWASP's, NIST's and the compliance frameworks'. For detection and evidence, the toolchain is the log aggregator, the rule format, the orchestration of responses.

The fourth is the instrument. Micrometer, OpenTelemetry, Prometheus and the profilers of every runtime measure a program from the outside. (General knowledge, not a study of the record, except where I name the file.)

## 2. The first principle

**A claim is only as good as the court that can refuse it. And where no court can see, the record says so.**

The record has it in two sentences that meet. The threat model: every guarantee names the guard that proves it, and a guarantee without a guard is listed as an open risk. And the perception law: a comparison proves sameness and never rightness, and if a person is the last consumer, a person is the last gate. Under both lies the doctrine that the library repeats in every plane: expression is free, and admission is governed.

## 3. The Softanza form

Chapter 5 told the court that judges whoever wrote the code. This chapter has four courts, one per kind of claim. I show each by something it refuses.

### The court of promises

A promise in this book is the line `#-->` under an expression, and it is a claim about a stop of the program. The tool that reads it, called the traveller, says a promise is kept, diverged, unreached or unjudged. A promise that argues with itself, prose after a value, is never accused. The runner's exit code is the runner's, and not the language's:

```haro
write("tour.haro", '? 1 + 1' + nl + '#--> 2' + nl +
	'? 1 + 1' + nl + '#--> 3' + nl +
	'? "C"  #--> "C" but should be "sm_AS"' + nl)
oTour = TravellerQ().Silent().Walk("tour.haro")
aClaims = oTour.Records()[1][:run][:stops][1][:claims]
? aClaims[1][:state]
#--> kept
? aClaims[2][:state]
#--> diverged
? aClaims[3][:state]
#--> unjudged
? oTour.ExitCode()
#--> 1
remove("tour.haro")
```

> run: kept (touches files)

A promise can be that the line raises. That is read as a raise that was promised. When it happens, the promise is kept and the tour has finished as it said it would, even though the language process ended with an error:

```haro
write("raise.haro", '? "before"  #--> before' + nl +
	'? Boom()  #--> ERROR: boom happened' + nl +
	'func Boom()' + nl + char(9) + 'raise("boom happened")' + nl)
oRaise = TravellerQ().Silent().Walk("raise.haro")
aRun = oRaise.Records()[1][:run]
? aRun[:stops][1][:claims][2][:state]
#--> kept
? aRun[:end]
#--> finished
? oRaise.ExitCode()
#--> 0
remove("raise.haro")
```

> run: kept (touches files)

Every rule of this judge was learned by watching the harness accuse the library falsely. The guard's header says it. A short promise `6` once hid inside a later `[ 6, 28 ]`, and that is why the rule on spellings exists. This book's own judge is a copy of the same grammar.

### The court of perception

The record's sentence is that a comparison proves sameness, and that it never proves rightness. The library holds the case. A bar plot drew vertical bars for horizontal data while all of its seventy-three examples went on passing. This is why the plots now compare the picture exactly. That makes sameness strict; it does not make it rightness. Rightness is a person.

So a declared world carries its perception state. Eight musical traditions are declared as data in the library. Seven of them are unperceived. One was heard, and its record carries a name, a date and a verdict:

```haro
nUnheard = 0
for cName in StzSoundUniverses()
	if StzSoundUniverseQ(cName).Listener() = "UNPERCEIVED"
		nUnheard++
	ok
next
? len(StzSoundUniverses())
#--> 8
? nUnheard
#--> 7
? left(StzSoundUniverseQ("tunisian").Listener(), 5)
#--> HEARD
```

> run: kept

There are no counters in this block. The one tradition that was heard was heard by the author on 26 September 2026, through five original examples, and the record's words are that it is "far from being qualified". Which part fails, whether the tuning, the phrasing, the rhythm or the timbre, is not yet said. [AUTHOR: May the book quote those four words as yours, as the clearest recorded perception that is a refusal?]

The law is the estate's, adopted on 25 September 2026 at the author's instruction, and written by a session. It requires that the record names the perceiver and quotes the verdict. Its second line is the one I take with me: unperceived is a state, not a failure. It refuses two things as well. It does not say that a human must gate every commit, because it binds at the granularity of a phase. And it does not say that it generalises from one plane without further evidence, and it was held for a month on that account.

The gallery of pictures is where it is unmet. The library holds ninety-five judgements of pictures and text sections, in three index files, committed on 5 October 2026. Every one is by a model, reading the picture or reading the text the program printed. None has a human name. The first index says it in a sentence: no human has looked at these pictures. The register of findings ends with a line I repeat: a human verdict on a sample is still owed. [AUTHOR: Is there a human verdict on any gallery picture since 5 October? If so, give the name, the date and your words, and the chapter prints them. If not, it stays "unperceived".]

### The court of security

The security plane starts from a claim. The threat model assumes that a hijacked language model will get through, and makes it harmless. The model never holds the capability, and so an LLM that is fooled still cannot act. The document does not try to detect every malicious prompt.

The evidence of what happened is a hash chain. Edit one line, and the chain says which entry broke. A wrong key and an edited line are two different accusations:

```haro
oLed = StzSecurityLedger(256)
oLed.Record(StzSecurityRefusal("auth.login.failed", HumanActor("admin"), "user:admin", "wrong credentials"))
oLed.Record(StzSecurityRefusal("secret.reveal.refused", LLMActor("advisor"), "secret:payments-key", "actor is not effectful"))
oLed.SealTo("evidence.txt", "the-evidence-key")
? StzVerifySealedLedger("evidence.txt", "the-evidence-key")[:why]
#--> chain intact over 2 entries
? StzVerifySealedLedger("evidence.txt", "another-key")[:why]
#--> the chain is intact but the SEAL does not match this key
write("evidence.txt", StzReplace(read("evidence.txt"), "actor is not effectful", "routine maintenance"))
? StzVerifySealedLedger("evidence.txt", "the-evidence-key")[:why]
#--> the chain breaks at entry 2 -- that record (or one before it) was edited
remove("evidence.txt")
```

> run: kept (touches files)

Notice what I chose as the reason to edit. A reason written as "bad password" is redacted when the record is built, so editing it leaves the chain intact. The redaction is itself a guarantee, and it hid my first attempt. The narration states the limit in its own words: this is not protection against an attacker already running code in the process.

A secret is the same law applied to a key. Its only public face is its descriptor. A hand that may commit can read it. A language model cannot, and the refusal goes into the ledger without anyone calling the ledger:

```haro
StzOpenSecurityLedger(64)
oKey = new stzSecret("deploy-key")
oKey.FromLiteralQ("ssh-ed25519-AAAA")
? oKey.Descriptor()
#--> <secret 'deploy-key' (secret) from literal>
? oKey.Reveal(HumanActor("ops"))
#--> ssh-ed25519-AAAA
try
	oKey.Reveal(LLMActor("planner"))
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Refused: actor 'planner' (posture sandboxed) may not reveal secret 'deploy-key'. Only an effectful, non-sandboxed actor can read a secret.
? len(StzSecurityLedgerQ().Refusals())
#--> 1
StzCloseSecurityLedger()
```

> run: kept

This is chapter 17's law, that a model does not hold the capability to produce effects, applied to a key, with the record added.

What does the table of guarantees tie to a guard? The meta-guard of the threat model checks that the document exists with its sections; that every guarantee row contains a proof, which is a guard's path, a fuzz run, a bill-of-materials check or a runbook; that every guard path in the rows exists on disk; and that a planted fake row with an unproven guarantee is caught. It does not run the guards, and it does not check that a guard asserts what its row says. That is the security gate's job. So "each tied to a guard" is, exactly, "each names at least one proof, and every guard file it names exists".

At this commit the table holds 43 guarantees. The site says 38, on three pages. Both are true of their dates: the document had 38 rows on 30 September 2026 at nine in the morning, and the rows from 39 to 43 were added on 5 October, the last at 16:31. The site's number is the site's to update.

### The court of performance

An instrument owes a number. The honest stopwatch gives a number from a clock that only moves forward, excludes a pause, and ships two spans of one trace in one batch for an observability collector:

```haro
w = StzStopwatchXT("import-orders")
w.Lap("parsed")
w.Pause()
StzEngineTimeSleepMs(200)
w.Resume()
w.Stop()
? isnumber(w.ElapsedMs())
#--> 1
? w.ElapsedMs() < 200
#--> 1
? w.Record()[:running]
#--> 0
w2 = StzStopwatchXT("notify-customer")
w2.JoinTrace(w.TraceParent())
w2.Stop()
? w2.TraceId() = w.TraceId()
#--> 1
oBatch = StzOtelBatch("shop")
oBatch.AddSpan(w).AddSpan(w2)
? Q(oBatch.ToJson()).NumberOfOccurrence(w.TraceId())
#--> 2
```

> run: kept

Careful with what this does not claim. The narration says the joined stopwatch gets a new span. I checked: it gets the same trace and the same span identifier. The sentence holds for the server's echo, which I did not run, and not for the stopwatch. I list it among the requests.

The instrument is not the end of the court. Judgment and action live inside the program. A performance plan is proposed by a machine, and only an effectful actor commits it. The same action is judged twice, and both judgments stay in the audit:

```haro
oPlan = StzPerfPlan("relieve-queue")
oPlan.Propose(:ScaleUp, "nlp", "queue.depth: measured 900 (must be <= 100)")
oFleet = new FakeFleet
? oPlan.MayCommit(LLMActor("advisor"))
#--> 0
? oPlan.ExecuteOn(oFleet, LLMActor("advisor"))
#--> 0
? oFleet.CallCount()
#--> 0
? oPlan.ExecuteOn(oFleet, HumanActor("mansour"))
#--> 1
? oFleet.CallCount()
#--> 1
oPlan.Show()
#--> Perf plan relieve-queue -- 1 proposed action(s).
#      1. scaleup 'nlp' -- queue.depth: measured 900 (must be <= 100)
#    Audit (2):
#      #1 REFUSED scaleup 'nlp' by advisor -- actor is not effectful -- expression is free; admission is governed
#      #1 COMMITTED scaleup 'nlp' by mansour -- queue.depth: measured 900 (must be <= 100)

class FakeFleet
	aFleetCalls = []
	def ScaleUp(pcTag)
		aFleetCalls + [ "scaleup", pcTag ]
	def CallCount()
		return len(aFleetCalls)
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

The fleet here is a rehearsal double, any object that answers the same effectful verbs. The loop is proved against the double and not against a real fleet. A model asking to commit is refused before any attempt, and the double counts zero calls.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused the word for the thing it was not.** The structure of this book and the closing assessment of the study call the articles "narrations that cannot lie". My own ruling of 7 October 2026, the study's 12.20, says that "narration" names the articles under the library's documentation folder, and not the executable suites. The site's runner annotates an article and no longer decides whether it is shown. The site's own record of 8 October 2026, run on a later commit than this book's, says that of 135 articles 55 ran, and of the 423 blocks in those, 123 kept their promise. So the articles can drift. What cannot lie are the executable guards, and this book's own build, which fails when a block does not keep its promise. [AUTHOR: May the chapter say that, or do you want it to defend the older phrase?]

**I refused a second assertion library.** The promise runner's rulings refuse a second library, a rewrite of the test files, a coverage percentage before there are routes, the word "verified" on a perceptual claim without a name and a sentence, and a mock written for a platform's production.

**I refused to fold unjudged or unperceived into pass or fail.** A stop has five states and no sixth. A runner that folds the fourth or the fifth into either of the others is wrong.

**I refused to analyse tampered evidence.** A tool that verifies, warns, and then analyses anyway teaches its users to ignore the warning.

**I refused to detect everything.** Detections are declared. The sentinel speaks on transitions only: quiet, one alarm after five wrong passwords, and nothing new after a sixth, since the story did not restart. It does not write its own firings into the ledger, because detections over detection events invite feedback loops.

**I refused to compete with the field's ecosystem.** The performance study of 29 July 2026 says the plane does not compete with Grafana, Datadog or the ecosystem of OpenTelemetry, and should not pretend to. It exports.

**I refused a guarantee without a guard.** The threat model's last rule is that a guarantee is added only together with its guard.

## 5. The court

`testoor_promises_narrated` proves each promise rule planted both ways: a lie is diverged; two lies are both caught; a short lie does not hide in a later list; the spellings of a true value are all kept; an expected raise is honoured; prose is unjudged; a program that does not compile is not diverged; a raise part-way leaves the later promises unreached. `threat_model_narrated` proves the meta-guard described above, including its negative sibling. `security_ledger_narrated`, `secretstore_narrated` and `secret_narrated` prove the chain and the seal, the store and the secret. `security_sentinel_narrated` proves the edge-triggered sentinel. `stopwatch_narrated` and `tracing_tail_narrated` prove the instrument and the batch. `governed_loop_narrated` proves the plan, the preflight, the commit and the audit.

I ran `threat_model_narrated` alone on 8 October 2026, and it passed all of its assertions. The others are counted by the build and not run here. The performance plane has twelve guards. I do not print their sum, because the build counts them.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The record's own honest sections, each loss beside its win.

**In performance.** There is no time-series database, no query language and no dashboards. By design, the plane exports. Its sampling answer is bounded rings that drop the oldest, which is honest and cruder than head or tail sampling. Distributed context stops at HTTP. The ecosystem of integrations and auto-instrumentation for every framework is the field's. The profiler is cooperative: it sees what you bracket, where a native profiler sees everything. The win it claims is that judgment and action live inside the program: budgets that fail the build, tuning that passes governance, monitoring that prices itself.

**In security.** The ledger is a bounded ring, and bounded means forgetting. There is no telemetry from the host, the kernel or the network. Detections are declared and not learned, a gap partly closed by one detection of rates on 30 September 2026. The record's own comparison is that a rule corpus of the field's format is larger than eight shipped detections. There is no workflow of a security operations centre. The largest gap, in the record's words, is that a detection can only see what a seam emits. The win is that the event is typed at birth, where the door refuses, so no parser stands between what the program knew and what the detector judges.

**In promises.** The field's doctests sit in the host's toolchain and answer pass or fail. The house's judge has five states. Where the field leads is instruments: the Testoor launch memo has coverage instruments of any kind at zero, and route coverage is planned.

**In perception.** The field's visual snapshot services are mature and automatic. The house has no automation of the verdict, and says it should not: it does not replace a number, and no number replaces it.

The study of the record puts the tone I try to echo. A grid of two hundred and eighty-six ticks against two hundred and sixty-nine crosses is, in its words, the shape of a brochure.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the promises as stops; the security plane, with its ledger, sentinel, drill, response, attestation and secrets, and a table of 43 guarantees that name 57 distinct guard files, none missing; the performance plane from its first phase to its last; the perception law, adopted; the declared verdict tables for the singing voice and the eight universes.

What is designed:

- The promise runner's routes and map, its clock and weather, its excursions, perception and mutation, and its inherited tours. All are planned. The state *unperceived* is named in the runner's header, and the reader assigns none of them.
- The negotiation over a rejected plan, which the threat model calls design only.
- The plugin plane: plugins are not shipped, and foreign code is not yet governed.

What is absent:

- **A human verdict on any gallery picture.** Zero of ninety-five. The law's second requirement, a name, a date and what they said, is unmet there. The chapter says unperceived, and does not say verified.
- Of the eight musical traditions, seven unheard.
- A real vault and a real sandbox as proofs. The vault resolver is tested against a stand-in, over real HTTP. There is no cloud key-management adapter.
- Paths on POSIX systems, unverified; scheduled fuzzing; continuous integration; signed commits. The author deferred them for the development phase.
- A time-series store. A coverage percentage, which is refused until there are routes.

Three more things belong here, found while running the court.

**The drill's numbers differ by source.** The threat model's measure of responsiveness is a detection in a little over three hundred and fifty milliseconds and a containment in a little over fifty, in three runs on 29 September 2026. The site prints 395 and 50, from a replay of 1 October. They differ because the drill measures a clock. One attack shape, on loopback, on one machine, run on demand. Whichever number you print, you name its source.

**A possible defect in the promise judge, which I report and have built nothing on.** A file whose first promise is a lie and whose last is a promised raise reads the lie as unreached, the raise as kept, the tour as broken, with zero diverged, and an exit code of 1. I reproduced it twice. Each rule alone behaves as the guard plants it. Whether a divergence above a promised raise should read *diverged* is a question for the Testoor plane.

**Counts that disagree between sources.** The study's closing says 387 narrated guards. My `find` on the pinned snapshot says 529 files named so. The build's own count of guards is the one to use, and neither of the two.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Your examples already carry their expected output. The traveller reads them as promises and says kept, diverged or unjudged, and prose that argues with itself is never accused.

**The analyst.** The threat model is a table you can read: each row with the guard that proves it and its honest limit. The performance judgment is declared before the code runs.

**The designer.** A picture or a sound is a claim only a person can close. The gallery says a model looked and no human has. Your verdict is a named line in the record.

**The decision-maker.** "Who decided, and what is written down" has a mechanical answer in both planes: the same action refused for the model and committed for the human, both in one audit, and the evidence sealed so that an edit is named.

**The educator.** The tutor's rule meets the court. Predict the verdict of a promise before the traveller walks it.

**The agent.** You may propose a containment plan or a tuning plan and you may commit neither. Your refusal is itself a recorded event. Do not tell a person that a picture is right. Say "unperceived".

## 9. Exercise

Predict, in writing, before you run anything. The file below holds three promises. Which state will each one have?

```haro exercise
write("mine.haro", '? 2 * 3' + nl + '#--> 6' + nl +
	'? 2 * 3' + nl + '#--> 7' + nl +
	'? "ok"  #--> "ok" but see why' + nl)
# before you run: which state will each of the three promises have?
oMine = TravellerQ().Silent().Walk("mine.haro")
aMine = oMine.Records()[1][:run][:stops][1][:claims]
# your three predictions, then:  ? aMine[1][:state]   ? aMine[2][:state]   ? aMine[3][:state]
remove("mine.haro")
```

> run: exercise (touches files)

Run it. The states are kept, diverged and unjudged. Then add a fourth line that raises, promise it with `#--> ERROR:`, and predict first how the tour will end. Do not change your prediction to match what you see. Explain the output first.
