# Compared, honestly

Source: Chapter 22. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: five comparisons run on both sides, with the other side's version and the date; the four code pairs of the site, re-run; four field studies that end on what the field has and the library lacks; designed: a comparison register as data; a comparison court whose rule is that a row with no source or date does not publish; both benchmarks re-run on the present engine; absent: locale collation; locale casing; a grapheme count on the public string; a time-zone database; the benchmark of 7 July 2026 re-run; the source of the coverage matrix, which I could not locate

Run: 5 of 5 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:26 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

Comparison, in the field, is a genre of marketing with a few honest exceptions. The record counts the genre: a "Compare" page for each rival, and a comparison block on each feature page. Its commonest form is the grid, with rows of features, columns of tools, ticks and crosses.

The honest exceptions are practices and not products. Benchmarks published with the method, the machine and the build mode. And sections that say what the other tool has that we lack. (General knowledge, not a study of the record. I name no tool, since the author names none.)

## 2. The first principle

**A comparison is worth nothing until the other side's version and the date are on the page, the other side has been run, and the loss is printed beside the win.**

It is built from three sentences of the record. The study of 7 October 2026: every claim about another tool is cited, dated and run, and every loss is published with the wins. The estate's study of a browser runtime, run on 8 August 2026: losses are published with the wins. And the site's page of coverage: a platform that shows its gaps is believed on its strengths. The author's request, as the study reports it, is a comparative tone that is "a motor force, to learn from the best and beat them when possible". The study adds: with "when possible" measured.

## 3. The Softanza form

Chapter 3 said "I refused the grid alone". This chapter is the method that replaces it, with its evidence. Here is what the record measured about the grid. The study scanned the library's text for tables whose header names Softanza and at least one other tool. It found sixty-four tables, in thirty-one documents. In them, the column of Softanza carries 286 ticks, 2 warnings and 2 crosses. The other columns carry 204 ticks, 207 warnings and 269 crosses. The study's judgement is that this is the shape of a brochure, and that the record itself does not trust it. I could not reproduce those figures: a narrower scan of my own, with a header rule I wrote, found thirty-seven tables in twenty-nine documents. The study does not print its header rule. I cite its numbers as its own, and dated.

The form that replaces the grid is a pair: the same task, both sides run, the other side's version on the page, and a line where the other side leads. I show five. Each was run on 8 October 2026, between a quarter past ten and half past ten in the evening, on a Windows machine. The other side is Python 3.13.15, and Node v22.20.0 with its V8 12.4 engine and ICU 77.1. R and Julia are not installed on this machine, and no pair uses them.

**One: an integer is exact at any size.** Forty digits, from two numbers of twenty:

```haro
oN = new stzNumber("12345678901234567890")
? oN.MultiplyByQ("98765432109876543210").Content()
#--> 1219326311370217952237463801111263526900
```

> run: kept

The other side, Python 3.13.15, prints the same digits for `12345678901234567890 * 98765432109876543210`. Python's integer is exact by default, and Softanza is level with it. The Softanza line is the longer one: a literal this long cannot be a native number, so it is given as a string. The other side, Node v22.20.0, prints `1.219326311370218e+39`, because its default number is a 64-bit float. It is exact only when you ask for it with the suffix `n`, a second kind of number. So the win here is over JavaScript's default, and not over Python.

**Two: exact decimals.** `0.1` plus `0.2` is `0.3`, and a price times a rate keeps its cents:

```haro
? (new stzNumber("0.1")).AddQ("0.2").Content()
#--> 0.3
? (new stzNumber("19.99")).MultiplyByQ("0.15").Content()
#--> 2.9985
```

> run: kept

The other side, Python with floats, prints `0.30000000000000004` and `2.9984999999999995`. Node prints the same two, because JavaScript has no decimal type. Python's standard `decimal` module, imported and fed strings, prints `0.3` and `2.9985`: level with Softanza, and with the same cost per line. So Softanza wins on the default, since a number made from a string is exact decimal, and is level with the one library that does it deliberately. The guard's comment says why: the value is stored as a string, and the computation is done on scaled integers by the engine. Its limit is in the record's words: the rungs for rationals and for complex numbers are named in the plan and, in the guard of numbers that are not lost, "not built yet".

**Three: sorting accented words. Here the field leads.** Softanza sorts by code point. The field's locale-aware sort puts `étoile` between `eau` and `zèbre`:

```haro
? @@( Q([ "zèbre", "étoile", "eau", "zoo" ]).SortedInAscending() )
#--> [ "eau", "zoo", "zèbre", "étoile" ]
```

> run: kept

The other side, Python's `sorted`, prints the same list: level, and both wrong for French. With the standard `locale` module set to `fr_FR.UTF-8`, sorted with `locale.strxfrm`, Python prints `eau, étoile, zèbre, zoo`. That worked on the machine of the run. The locale must exist on the host, and the call is process-wide. Node, with `Intl.Collator('fr')`, prints the same French order with a built-in library. So the field leads: JavaScript with one argument, Python with the host's locale. Softanza has no collation. The culture plan of 18 August 2026 says the sorting is the engine's ordinal sort. And the coverage page rates internationalisation partial, against deep for two other platforms, with a note: a deliberate bet against the big international library.

This block is a pinned gap. Its promise records the library's present, weaker answer, so the page fails the day the library gains collation. That is by design. The culture plan's own rule says that pinning a known gap is legitimate only when the assertion fails the day the gap is fixed, and when the label says so. [AUTHOR: Blocks three and four pin a gap. Do you want them as running blocks, so that the page fails the day it is fixed, or shown as recorded text with their run date and not judged?]

**Four: Turkish uppercase. The field leads.** A locale argument that is accepted and ignored:

```haro
? Q("istanbul").UppercasedInLocale("tr_TR")
#--> ISTANBUL
```

> run: kept

Python prints `ISTANBUL`: level, since the standard library has no locale casing. Node, with `toLocaleUpperCase('tr')`, prints `İSTANBUL`. Turkish needs the dotted capital. This is the loss most worth printing, and it is not that the engine lacks Turkish. It is that the method takes a locale and returns the wrong answer silently. The source comment says: plain uppercase when it has no rule. The culture plan measured the same on 18 August 2026 and called it the code equivalent of the fake progress bar. Its law is the reason for this chapter: no facades. An operation either honours the culture it was given, or refuses.

**Five: an instruction in your language.** A natural-language instruction is dictionary-driven and local: no network, no model, no key.

```haro
o1 = Naturally("Make a list holding [ 3, 1, 3 ] then drop its duplicates")
? @@( o1.Result() )
#--> [ 3, 1 ]
? o1.Understood()
#--> create a list with [ 3, 1, 3 ] -> remove duplicates
```

> run: kept

I did not run the other side. The field's equivalent of "take this sentence and do it" is a model call. What it needs is a model: a hosted one needs a network, an account, a key and a per-token cost; a local one needs a downloaded model and a GPU or a patient CPU. It needs a prompt, and a check that the answer is what was asked, and the answer is not guaranteed to be the same twice. The site's own pair of this kind writes the other side as a comment, which says that there is no counterpart without an external language model.

One more loss, which is not a block. A string of a family emoji, five code points, joined by joiners, shows a weakness in what the public string exposes. The counter class answers `1` for the grapheme count; `NumberOfChars` answers `5` and `NumberOfBytes` answers `18`; and `GraphemeCount()` on the string itself raises, because the method is not defined. The engine knows, and the string does not say. Python's `len` gives `5`, and Node's `.length` gives `8`.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused the grid alone.** Two hundred and eighty-six ticks against two hundred and sixty-nine crosses is the shape of a brochure.

**I refused to keep a headline that does not reproduce.** The estate's memo of 13 September 2026 re-measured the runtime beneath the library against a fast runtime of the field on six matched kernels: identical algorithm and sizes on both sides, each side its fast idiom, cross-language checksums asserted before any number, minima over repeated runs. The field leads every row, by a factor between 1.04 and 1.88. The memo says the claim to retire is that the runtime beats it, in the present tense, on x64. The headline was never re-measured after the spike became a product. The memo asked the author to correct the runtime's own notes. [AUTHOR: As far as I read on 8 October 2026, the runtime's notes still say that the spike beats the fast runtime on every row, and the suggestion of a dated correction is unanswered. Will the correction be made before the book says the headline was withdrawn, or should the book say "withdrawn in the estate memo of 13 September 2026", and no more?]

**I refused a retraction that deletes its evidence.** The runtime's own notes of 29 August 2026 retracted a published table in the other direction, a loss on a phone, because the run had executed on the phone's slow cores while the peer ran on fast ones. The notes keep the table, struck through, and say that a retraction that deletes its own evidence is not a retraction. So the record has withdrawn a win and withdrawn a loss. The rule is to reproduce before you print, and not to print losses.

**I refused a comparison that cannot be redone.** The memo's hand-off says the spike's kernels died with the working tree, which is why an outside session had to reconstruct the measurement from prose.

**I refused a facade.** Blocks three and four.

**I refused the large international library.** The culture plan's table says it is killed, a decision made and standing in the first layer. The cost is blocks three and four. The coverage page prints it as a deliberate bet.

**I refused to compare on the tool's strongest ground.** The estate's study of a browser runtime has three honesty rules: each language gets idiomatic code; losses are published with the wins; every engine is a release build. It says, of its own page, that without them it is marketing. Its scoreboard has nine scenarios, four won and five lost. It is not a regression gate: its numbers are for learning, and not for gating.

## 5. The court

The comparison court is proposed and not built. The study's plan names it: a comparison row with no source or no date does not publish, and a code pair whose other side did not run, or ran in a debug build, does not publish. I found no file in the library or the site that implements it. So what exists is what proves the blocks and bears on the method:

`numeric_exactness_narrated` proves exact decimal arithmetic, integers past 2 to the power of 53, the forty-digit product, and exact division when it terminates. `numeric_no_loss_narrated` proves that a number is not destroyed on its way in, and that the representation ladder is observable; it is also where the rungs not built are named. `semantic_resolver_narrated` proves that paraphrases resolve to canonical operations through the local lexicon, and block five's sentence is one of its cases. It does not pass at this commit: I ran it on 9 October 2026 and it stops with an error raised from a string-splitting function, before it ends, so the page counts its assertions and does not claim its verdict. `engine_is_optimised_narrated` proves that the engine is compiled with optimisation, by a time gate: it is the library's own guard for the release-build rule.

No guard asserts blocks three and four as pinned gaps. No guard exists for a code pair whose other side did not run. I list both among the requests.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The record already keeps the list of what the field has and Softanza lacks. I print it, by file and date, beside the wins.

**Performance, against the field, 29 July 2026.** No time-series store, no query language, no dashboards: by design, the plane exports. No distributed context beyond HTTP. Sampling that is honest and cruder. Decades of integrations, which the incumbents own. The study's own words are that on breadth the incumbents win decisively, and that the plane does not compete with them and should not pretend to. Four losses are struck through with the date they closed.

**Incident analysis, against the field, 1 August 2026.** Scale, retention and search, where bounded means forgetting. No telemetry from the host, the kernel or the network. A small set of shipped detections against a community's corpus. The workflow of an operations centre. And the unwired seams, which the study calls the system's largest honest gap, listed and not hidden.

**The plugin study, 16 August 2026.** The field is ahead on permission granularity, on dependency resolution, on distribution and signing. The study's header says it is analysis and not rules.

**The culture plan, 18 August 2026.** Every row was verified in that session by running it. Casing is a facade four levels deep. Collation, digit shaping, non-Gregorian calendars and time zones do not exist. The plan's status then was not started.

**The estate's browser-runtime study, 8 August 2026.** Four of nine rows won, five to the peers, and the fused loop opcode of one peer stays a design generation ahead.

**The runtime against a fast peer, 13 September 2026.** Six kernels, checksums first, the peer ahead on every row.

**The string benchmark of 7 July 2026**, and the polyglot battle, in a narration dated 25 April 2025: the former host language came last of seven in two of three rounds and in the total. Both carry caveats the later record has put on them. I did not re-run the benchmark. The engine's default build was a debug build until 26 July 2026, and the library's own build file says every shared library it had ever loaded was compiled unoptimised. So the benchmark probably ran unoptimised. That is an inference, and I state it as one. I could not read the engine's build mode here, and the clock of the host language resolves ten to fifteen milliseconds. [AUTHOR: May the chapter cite the 7 July benchmark as a dated record with that caveat, or do you want it re-run on the present engine first, in a session that can read the build mode?]

**The coverage matrix.** Thirty domains in seven groups, against four other platforms, read on 17 September 2026. Softanza's tally is seven deep, fourteen solid, seven partial, two absent, present in twenty-eight of thirty. I recounted it from the rows and it matches. The two absent rows are symbolic mathematics ("no computer algebra") and package, build and deploy ("a library, not a registry"). Among the seven partial rows is internationalisation, "a deliberate bet against ICU", and the neural row is "inference only; no training foundry". The stored tallies of two of the other platforms do not match their own rows, by one in two places. I print Softanza's tally only. The matrix cites a compass file as the source of the ratings, and I could not locate the file in the snapshot, in the live checkout or in the estate's repository.

**The graph comparison, a narration dated 26 December 2025.** A model wrote it, and says so in its byline. Its column for Softanza rates itself with a cross on scalability and on transactions and with a warning on performance, and the file ends with a section titled with where traditional solutions win.

Where Softanza stands apart, by the record, without a number: the SLA; the governed act; the typed event; the matrix's one row of governance and safe execution; exact numbers by default, against two floats; an instruction that resolves locally. And a method that prints each of them beside a loss.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the five pairs, run on both sides; the four code pairs of the site, which I re-ran on 8 October 2026, both sides, and every output reproduced; the library's four field studies with their loss sections; the harness of the string benchmark; the guard that the engine is optimised.

What is designed or proposed: a comparison register as data; a comparison court; the re-run of both benchmarks. All three are in the study and nowhere else. I searched for them in the library and in the site and found nothing.

What is absent: a *compared* column on the Atlas, on the register of innovations and on the narrations; the field studies, the sixty-four grids, both benchmarks and the runtime table on the site; locale collation; locale casing; a grapheme count on the public string; a time-zone database; the re-run of the benchmark of 7 July 2026; the source of the coverage matrix.

And something that is unreproduced: the study's sixty-four, six hundred and fifteen and thirty-one, which my scan did not reproduce, and the symbol counts.

The method is stricter than the record it is applied to. Older comparisons in the library fail one or more of its rules. The string benchmark probably ran on a debug engine. The polyglot battle has no versions and no dates inside its text, and says its times are relative to a specific computer environment. The graph grid has no versions, dates or sources for its cells. The cognitive-load grid of another design article makes claims of sixty to seventy percent with no method printed beside them. That article then ends on an honest positioning that names four things the library does not compete on.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Copy a pair. Run both sides in your own versions, keep the output as it came, and add the line where the other tool is ahead.

**The analyst.** The coverage matrix is a buying aid only because it shows two absent rows and seven partial ones in its own colours. Read those first.

**The designer.** The loss goes in the same block and in the same type as the win. The stage word is part of the interface.

**The decision-maker.** Ask any vendor, this one included, for the other side's version, the date, whether it was run, and the line they lose. The memo of 13 September 2026 is a model of a headline withdrawn.

**The educator.** A code pair is a lesson in what a line costs. The learner writes the other side's output before running it.

**The agent.** When you repeat a claim about another tool, carry its version and date, and say if you did not run it. The register that would make this checkable by an agent is proposed and not built.

## 9. Exercise

Pick one task where you believe your own tool is ahead, and one where you believe the other is. Make a comparison that obeys the three rules: the other side's version and the date, both sides run, the loss printed.

```haro exercise
? YourSide(the_input)
#--> the line your tool prints
```

> run: exercise

Under it, write as text: the other tool's name and version, the date, the exact line it prints, and one line of the form "here the other leads because...". A reader checks the three text lines, since a runner cannot.

A variant that a machine can judge: take the five lines of block three's other side and predict, before running, the order each one prints. I will not tell you which order is right.


=====

# A world, a constellation, a platform, and the architect

Source: Chapter 23. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: a world as a declaration with a being, a becoming and a way of being met; a goal whose gap is measured; a constellation of worlds with bonds and three gates on every crossing; the commons as a platform; the capability seam; the profile of a solution as a text file, and the three system scopes; designed: the near-natural idiom of the constellation; a call routed into a nested constellation; shells that embed the engine; the architect's certification, which is a proposal; absent: a world that judges its own truths; a narration for the constellation or the platform envelope; a page for the architect; the five-row guarantee sheet

Run: 10 of 10 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:26 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

Three habits of the field have each a home, and I take them from general knowledge, not from a study of the record. The record holds no field study of super-apps, of platform engineering or of context maps. Its only named field is one line of a design document: a famous messaging super-app, and its kin.

The first is the super-app and the mini-program. One large application embeds a runtime and runs small applications inside it, sharing its services and its store. The host is privileged, the mini-app second-class, and the isolation a sandbox.

The second is the bounded context and the context map of domain-driven design, of 2003. A large system is cut into models, each with its words, and a map shows how they relate. It is the field's nearest idea to a world and a bond. It lives in diagrams and in review, and not in a running registry that refuses a call.

The third is the platform team and the deployment declared as code. A team builds the internal base that others deploy onto, and tools declare machines before they exist. The person who works across the floors is, in the field, a software architect, a solutions architect or a platform engineer: a title held by a person, whose artefacts are diagrams and manifests.

For the scopes, the field's home is the compiler: the target of a build, and the check that flags a call the minimum platform level lacks. Each decides which system the code is for. None lets the program name that system at the call site and refuse the act.

The shared shape: the field draws the picture, a host, a map, a diagram, and keeps the judge outside it. In all three the program knows nothing of its own composition.

## 2. The first principle

**The solution is declared as worlds, bonds and an envelope before any of it is built, and every crossing between them is governed.**

The sentences of the record: an application is a living world of meaning; worlds, not mini-apps, a governed graph and not a host container; and a Softanza architect models the deployment architecture first, as a declarative artifact, before a line of system feature code. The sentence above is built from the three and from the book's method, which is to declare and then govern. [AUTHOR: Is the sentence yours, or do you keep one of the three as it stands?]

## 3. The Softanza form

Three levels, then. A world is one application. A constellation is a graph of worlds. A platform is the envelope around a solution and the ground a constellation stands on. And one person declares all three.

### A world

A world has a being, a becoming, and a way of being met. Chapter 4 showed its being. This is the rest, for an agency that visits clients:

```haro
oApp = new stzApp("FieldVisits")
oApp {
    AddThingQ(:Account) { Has([ :number, :chapter, :balance ])  IsTrue(:balance, "@ >= 0") }
    AddThingQ(:Client)  { Has([ :code, :name, :city ])  Owns(:Account) }
    AddThingQ(:Visit)   { Of(:Client)  Has([ :agent, :date, :subject ]) }
    AddRelation(:Account, :belongsTo, :Client)
    AddFlowQ(:agent, :records, :Visit) { Require(:subject) }
    AddReactionQ(:Client).Unseen(90, :Days) { Propose(:Visit) }
    AddGoalQ(:EveryClientSeenThisQuarter) {
        Means      = "every :Client Has(:visit) Since(:quarterStart)"
        ReachedBy  = :planning
    }
    SetBody([ :GraphDB, :Files ]) { Graph = ".stzapp/world.stzgraf"  Files = "./" }
}
oApp.AddScreenQ(:ClientFile) { ToUnderstand(:Client)  Shows([ :identity, :accounts, :visits ]) }
oApp.AddRefinementQ(:balance).Bounds(0, 1000000)
oApp.AddReaches([ :mobile, :desktop, :web ])
oApp.Explain()
#--> WORLD FieldVisits   lives in: graphdb + files
#      BEING
#        account (number, chapter, balance)
#            true when balance @ >= 0
#        client (code, name, city)
#        visit (agent, date, subject)
#      RELATIONS
#        client owns account
#        visit of client
#        account belongsto client
#      BECOMING
#        when agent records visit require subject
#        whenever client unseen 90 days -> propose visit
#        wants everyclientseenthisquarter -> reached by planning
#      MET FROM WITHOUT
#        screen clientfile: understand client shows identity, accounts, visits
#        refine balance bounds [0..1000000]
#        reaches mobile, desktop, web
```

> run: kept

`SetBody` only declares. No file was written by this block.

A world strives. A goal is a wanted state of the graph, and the gap is measured. A goal with no pattern to evaluate is refused, and never faked:

```haro
oApp = StzAppQ("visits")
oApp.AddThingQ(:Client) { Has([ :code, :name ]) }
oApp.AddThingQ(:Visit)  { Of(:Client) }
oApp.AddGoalQ(:EveryClientVisited) { Means = "every :Client Has(:visited)"  ReachedBy = :planning }
oApp.AddInstance("anna", :Client)
oApp.AddInstance("bilal", :Client)
oApp.Relate("anna", :visited, "visit1")
? @@( oApp.Pursue(:EveryClientVisited) )
#--> pursuing everyclientvisited via planning -- 1 proposal(s)
#    [ [ "attend", "client", "for", "bilal" ] ]
? oApp.GoalSatisfied(:EveryClientVisited)
#--> 0
oApp.Relate("bilal", :visited, "visit2")
? @@( oApp.Pursue(:EveryClientVisited) )
#--> pursuing everyclientvisited via planning -- 0 proposal(s)
#    [ ]
? oApp.GoalSatisfied(:EveryClientVisited)
#--> 1
oApp.AddGoalQ(:Vague) { Means = "make things nice" }
try
    oApp.Pursue(:Vague)
catch
    ? cCatchError
done
#--> stzGraphGoal.FromMeans: no 'every :Type' clause in 'make things nice'.
```

> run: kept

`Pursue` prints its own first line, and then the `?` prints the list it returns. With a reaction declared, the same gap proposes a *visit* for Bilal, and without one the default act is *attend*.

### A constellation

Nothing crosses between worlds unless it is declared and governed. A bond must exist, and then three gates stand on the crossing: the act must have a declared risk tier, the caller must hold the permission, and the caller's authority must cover the risk. Each refusal names its gate. The bond exists in all four calls below, so the three refusals are the governance's:

```haro
oResto = StzAppQ("resto")
oResto.AddThingQ(:dish) { Has([ :name, :price ]) }
oSupplier = StzAppQ("supplier")
oSupplier.AddThingQ(:crate) { Has([ :produce, :qty ]) }
oCon = new stzSuperApp("acme-group")
oCon.AddWorld("resto", oResto)
oCon.AddWorld("supplier", oSupplier)
oCon.Bond("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
? oCon.CallAcross("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
#--> 0
? oCon.Why()
#--> governance refused: refused: 'order-produce' has NO declared risk tier (undeclared actions never proceed)
oCon.GovDeclareRisk("order-produce", 2)
? oCon.CallAcross("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
#--> 0
? oCon.Why()
#--> governance refused: refused: 'resto' lacks PERMISSION (can) for 'order-produce'
oCon.GovGrant("resto", "order-produce")
? oCon.CallAcross("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
#--> 0
? oCon.Why()
#--> governance refused: refused: 'resto' holds '' authority (level 0) but 'order-produce' is risk tier 2 (SHOULD does not cover it)
oCon.GovSetAuthority("resto", :Delegated)
? oCon.CallAcross("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
#--> 1
? oCon.Why()
#--> allowed: active + bonded + governed
? oCon.GovernanceQ().NumberOfDecisions()
#--> 1
? @@( oCon.GovernanceQ().DecisionsOf("resto")[1][2] )
#--> [ "rationale", "cross-world call cleared" ]
```

> run: kept

The doubled word "refused: refused:" is the library's own text. The cleared crossing wrote a decision by itself. Its record holds the authority and the risk of that moment, and a clock read, so the block prints only the rationale. That is the lineage chapter 30 uses.

The shared ground is a world too. Inside a constellation the commons is itself a platform, and it holds an identity, a session and a store. The commons is not a special kind of node; the design's own words are that it is uniform and recursive:

```haro
oCon = new stzSuperApp("acme-group")
oDb = new stzDatabase(":memory:")
oCon.OpenCommonsOn(oDb)
? classname( oCon.CommonsQ() )
#--> stzplatform
? oCon.RegisterIdentity("owner", "s3cret")
#--> 1
? oCon.RegisterIdentity("owner", "other")
#--> 0
? len( oCon.OpenSession("owner", "s3cret") ) > 0
#--> 1
? oCon.OpenSession("owner", "wrong") = ""
#--> 1
oCon.StorePut("currency", "EUR")
? oCon.StoreGet("currency")
#--> EUR
oDb.Close()
```

> run: kept

This block uses an in-memory database, so no file and no network. The session token is random, and only its presence is printed. Secrets are stored salted and hashed, with the engine's key-derivation function. The point is the first line: a platform is not only the envelope around a solution. It is what a constellation stands on. In code the commons is not a node of the constellation graph: when I probed it the graph held two nodes for two registered worlds. World zero is a role the platform plays.

A world is swapped live and keeps its bonds. A constellation nests in another. A name is taken once:

```haro
oResto = StzAppQ("resto")
oResto.AddThingQ(:dish) { Has([ :name, :price ]) }
oCon = new stzSuperApp("acme-group")
oCon.AddWorld("resto", oResto)
oCon.AddWorld("supplier", StzAppQ("supplier"))
oCon.Bond("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
oCon.GovDeclareRisk("order-produce", 2)
oCon.GovGrant("resto", "order-produce")
oCon.GovSetAuthority("resto", :Delegated)
oNew = StzAppQ("supplier-v2")
oCon.Swap("supplier", oNew)
? oCon.NumberOfWorlds()
#--> 2
? oCon.WorldQ("supplier").Name()
#--> supplier-v2
? oCon.CallAcross("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
#--> 1
oHolding = new stzSuperApp("holding")
oHolding.AddConstellation("acme", oCon)
? oHolding.KindOf("acme")
#--> super
? oHolding.WorldQ("acme").NumberOfWorlds()
#--> 2
? oCon.KindOf("resto")
#--> app
try
    oCon.AddWorld("resto", oResto)
catch
    ? cCatchError
done
#--> stzSuperApp: world 'resto' already in the constellation.
```

> run: kept

Chapter 28 used `Retire` and `Revive`, and this block is `Swap` and `AddConstellation`. Nesting is a fact of the registry. Nothing in the code routes a call into a nested constellation, so the recursion of the design is a registry's recursion and not yet a call's.

One trap. A registered world is a copy. The host language copies an object by value when it is stored, so the world you register is not the world you hold. The header of the class says it: world objects are reached through the registry index, the live path. Configure the world fully, register it, and reach it through the constellation from then on. The envelope has the same doctrine: setting a world on the platform harvests its declarations at call time, and setting the governance snapshots the regime.

### The envelope, and the seam

A platform grants a capability of a device only if governance clears its risk. A permission is held and the world is refused anyway. The risk tiers, two for the camera and four for payments, are the library's declared ones:

```haro
oGov = new stzGovernance("resto-regime")
oGov.GrantPermission("resto", "use-camera")
oGov.GrantPermission("resto", "use-payments")
oGov.SetAuthority("resto", :Delegated)
oResto = StzAppQ("resto")
oResto.AddThingQ(:dish) { Has([ :name, :price ]) }
oPlat = StzPlatformQ("resto-envelope")
oPlat.SetWorld(oResto)
oPlat.SetGovernedBy(oGov)
oPlat.AddCapabilityQ(:camera).SetPurposes([ :scan_dish_photo ])
? oPlat.Granted(:camera)
#--> 1
oPlat.AddCapabilityQ(:payments).SetPurposes([ :charge_guests ])
? oPlat.Granted(:payments)
#--> 0
? oPlat.Why()
#--> refused: 'resto' holds 'delegated' authority (level 2) but 'use-payments' is risk tier 4 (SHOULD does not cover it)
```

> run: kept

The class header says what the world lacks: the world models the world, and deliberately lacks the operational envelope. The envelope's duties are generation, the capability seam, the commons runtime, a networked body and a registry with enforcement. Generation is real, and refuses to generate for a world that declares no reach. The success path writes real files, so I do not run it here. I ran it once and removed what it wrote: the web shell is a page of 318 bytes with the world's screens and things as list items, and the desktop shell a launcher of 251 bytes. That is the limit of "per-platform shells": generation is real, and tiny.

### The architect, and the three scopes

By *scope* I mean the System Foundation's: development, deployment, and runtime-current. Every system act is written in a named scope. A Softanza architect models the deployment architecture first. The same verb, in two scopes, gets two verdicts, and the target's own words say why:

```haro
oSol = new stzPlatformProfile("greenhouse")
oSol.DevelopedOn(:Windows)
oSol.WithServer(:backend, :LinuxServer)
oSol.WithSuperApp(:phone, :Android)
oSol.WithApp(:sensor, :ESP32)
? oSol.DevelopmentSystem().OSName()
#--> windows
? oSol.App(:phone).System().OSName()
#--> android
? oSol.App(:sensor).System().OSName()
#--> espidf
? @@( oSol.App(:sensor).System().System().Capabilities() )
#--> [ "gpio", "network", "clock", "threads" ]
? oSol.CurrentSystem().IsRuntime()
#--> 1
? oSol.App(:backend).System().Spawn("cron")
#--> native
try
    oSol.App(:phone).System().Spawn("cron")
catch
    ? cCatchError
done
#--> scope 'android' (android) forbids 'process' -- spawn 'cron'. This target has no such capability.
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

The development system's name is the declared one, so it does not depend on the machine. The line `native` is host-dependent: it holds only on a host that can spawn a process, as chapter 31 says of its own word. The Android refusal and the capability list of the board are the target's, whatever the host. The three scopes are `new stzDevSystem()` or `DevelopmentSystem()` for development, which is tooling only; `App(:x).System()` for deployment, where feature code is written and checked; and `new stzCurrentSystem()` or `CurrentSystem()` for runtime-current, which is what runs now. The scope object has no `Capabilities()` of its own: the call goes through `.System().System()`. A request.

The architect's artefact is a text file. An unsound solution cannot be built:

```haro
oSol = new stzPlatformProfile("greenhouse")
oSol.DevelopedOn(:Windows)
oSol.WithServer(:backend, :LinuxServer)
oSol.WithSuperApp(:phone, :Android)
oSol.WithApp(:sensor, :ESP32)
? oSol.ToStzPlatform()
#--> # .stzplatform -- a Softanza solution profile
#    platform: greenhouse
#    developed_on: windows
#    server: backend -> linux
#    superapp: phone -> android
#    app: sensor -> espidf
oBack = StzPlatformProfileQ()
oBack.FromString( oSol.ToStzPlatform() )
? oBack.App(:sensor).DeploymentOSName()
#--> espidf
oBad = new stzPlatformProfile("empty")
? @@( oBad.Validate() )
#--> [ "no development system declared (use DevelopedOn)", "the solution deploys no apps" ]
oPlat = StzPlatformQ("empty")
oPlat.SetProfile(oBad)
try
    oPlat.Build()
catch
    ? cCatchError
done
#--> stzPlatform.Build: the solution is not sound -- no development system declared (use DevelopedOn); the solution deploys no apps
```

> run: kept

The file form is plain text that the architect owns, and it round-trips from a string. The order of building and deploying is chapter 33's, and the gate on who deploys is chapter 24's.

Who is the architect? The record names the person three times in three unrelated places: the System Foundation, a private book of the estate on platform modelling, and Central's memo for a school that would certify architects of Softanza, with three levels. That certification is a proposal of 30 September 2026, not a delivery, and the site must not announce it. The estate's protocol defines a role as a profile held in a scope. Applied here, which is my application and not the record's, the architect is not a rank. It is what one holds in the scope of a solution: the profile, the worlds, the governance. The site has no page for the architect. [AUTHOR: Is the platform architect told as what one declares, with the certification left out, or with one line saying it is proposed? Naming the school needs your word.]

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused the host and the mini-app.** Worlds, not mini-apps; a governed graph, not a host container. Chapter 4 told it, and I point back.

**I refused to make the commons a special kind of node.** It is a world, and a world is uniform and recursive.

**I refused the sandbox as the definition of isolation.** Isolation is a declared, inspectable meaning and not a black-box virtual machine. A world's sovereign body plus norm-gated bonds. A runtime sandbox is only an implementation for untrusted worlds.

**I refused to make governance a fifth facet.** It is the ambient. In code, one governance per constellation, configured through it, so that there is one live governance and never a stale copy held by a caller.

**I refused to give the world the envelope.** `stzApp` models the world, and deliberately lacks the operational envelope.

**I refused to freeze *current* under a better name.** The cure is not a better name: every system act is written in a named scope. The attempts it refuses are a pair of words, this system and the target system, and a triple, host, runtime and target.

**I refused to deploy a profile.** We do not deploy a profile; we deploy a platform and its parts. The profile only describes. The platform owns it and drives the building and then the deployment.

## 5. The court

`06_stzsuperapp_narrated` proves that worlds register as nodes; that the commons holds an identity and a store; that a crossing is refused for an undeclared risk, accepted when governed, and refused when unbonded with the bond named; that a retired world breaks its bonds and revives; that a swap keeps the node and the bond; and that a constellation nests in another. `01_platform_narrated` proves that shells are generated per declared reach and a world without a reach is refused; that a capability is granted only when governance covers its risk tier, and a capability never requested is refused with a reason; that the commons registers identities, opens sessions, posts and reads messages, and stores; that the world is served over a real port; and that the registry refuses a call to an unbonded or retired world. It writes shells beside itself and opens a loopback port. `full_scope_model_narrated`, which the guard calls the architect's common ground, proves that the solution is modelled first, that an unsound one is flagged, that the three scopes are distinct, that the scope refuses what the target forbids and rehearses what the host lacks, that the text file round-trips, that building precedes deploying, and that a deploy by a language-model actor is refused. `04_stzapp_pursue_narrated` and `05_stzapp_live_narrated` carry the goal and the reactions.

The guard the chapter does not have: none judges a world's truths, a flow, or the time of a reaction.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The super-app leads in users. The design names its model and replaces it; chapter 4 says the record measures nothing against them. The Platforms page prints the other half: the customer platforms run today on ordinary web stacks. No platform runs on the constellation in production.

The field leads in shells. The design promises a progressive web app, a desktop shell, a mobile shell. The code generates a page and a launcher of a few hundred bytes. The shells are designed. Generation is built, and tiny.

The field leads in what a bounded context carries: a team and a language. A world carries a graph and a narration. The persona a world is for is not a field of the class: a world has the list of surfaces it reaches and nothing else about whom it serves. The site's five things, a shared ground, a world for each person and device, bonds, rules, roles, are true of the commons, the bonds and the governance. The roles are the actors, permissions and authority of the governance. There is no role class, and a world per person and device is a naming convention.

The Atlas does not rate this plane. `stzApp` is named once, in a lane of the area for interfaces, rated solid, with the note that there is no multi-window orchestration, dialogs, menus or tray. The platform in a lane of the area for the system. The constellation in none.

The compiler leads on scopes. It checks against a target in daily use at scale; Softanza's scope is a runtime object that a program asks. What it adds is a verdict at the call site in the target's own words, and a twin that rehearses what the host lacks, which is chapter 24.

Where the field and the record meet: a super-app is a graph of graphs. The field's context map is a diagram. Softanza's is a registry that refuses.

## 7. The limit

A world's truths are narrated and not judged. The number in a reaction is the first case. Ninety days and one day give the same proposals: a reaction fires for each instance that lacks a relation named after the thing it proposes. The comment above `Pulse()` says so.

```haro
oA = StzAppQ("clinic-a")
oA.AddThingQ(:Patient) { Has([ :name ]) }
oA.AddThing(:Checkup)
oA.AddReactionQ(:Patient).Unseen(90, :Days) { Propose(:Checkup) }
oA.AddInstance("alice", :Patient)
oA.AddInstance("bob", :Patient)
oA.Relate("alice", :checkup, "checkup-1")
oB = StzAppQ("clinic-b")
oB.AddThingQ(:Patient) { Has([ :name ]) }
oB.AddThing(:Checkup)
oB.AddReactionQ(:Patient).Unseen(1, :Days) { Propose(:Checkup) }
oB.AddInstance("alice", :Patient)
oB.AddInstance("bob", :Patient)
oB.Relate("alice", :checkup, "checkup-1")
oA.Live()
#--> [clinic-a] is live -- 2 thing(s), 0 flow(s), 1 reaction(s), 0 goal(s); 1 proposal(s)
oB.Live()
#--> [clinic-b] is live -- 2 thing(s), 0 flow(s), 1 reaction(s), 0 goal(s); 1 proposal(s)
? @@( oA.Proposals() )
#--> [ [ "propose", "checkup", "for", "bob" ] ]
? @@( oB.Proposals() )
#--> [ [ "propose", "checkup", "for", "bob" ] ]
```

> run: kept

The same reading holds for `IsTrue`, which is stored and narrated while instances carry no fields to judge it against; for a flow's `Require` and `Then`, stored and narrated, where I found no executor; and for the bounds of a refinement. A world today declares that a balance must not be negative. It does not judge a balance. What it does judge is a goal's gap, a crossing and a capability. So I say "a world that wants" for the goal, and "narrated" for the truth. Chapter 4's `true when balance @ >= 0` is a declaration only. [AUTHOR: May the chapter say "declared and narrated; judged only for goals, crossings and capabilities", and may the memo request that truths be evaluated on instances?]

The design writes more than the class speaks. The design's near-natural idiom for the constellation is not in the class:

```haro
oSuper = new stzSuperApp("acme-group")
try
    oSuper.Hosts(:VisitApp)
catch
    ? cCatchError
done
#--> Error (R14) : Calling Method without definition: hosts
try
    oSuper.Admits(:VisitApp)
catch
    ? cCatchError
done
#--> Error (R14) : Calling Method without definition: admits
try
    oSuper.Explain()
catch
    ? cCatchError
done
#--> Error (R14) : Calling Method without definition: explain
```

> run: kept

`Shares`, `Provides`, `Norm`, `Reaches` and `Generate` on a constellation fail in the same way. The class has `AddWorld`, `AddConstellation`, `Bond`, `Retire`, `Revive`, `Swap`, the `Gov*` family, `CallAcross`, `Crossings` and the commons calls, and that is all. The design's three aspects are reached today by `AddWorld`, `Bond` and `OpenCommonsOn`, and governance by `Gov*`; reach and presence are the part that is not there. The design's own header calls the document a forward design.

What is built: the world; the constellation with its gates, its ledger of crossings with timings, a decision written on a cleared crossing, and a refused crossing noted as evidence; the platform's capability seam, its commons, its registry, its shells, its profile with building then deploying; the three scopes.

What is designed: the near-natural idiom; a call routed into a nested constellation; loading one world without rebuilding a host; shells that embed the engine.

What is absent: a narration for the constellation or the envelope, since none exists. The narration for building a first app uses verbs the class does not have: seven of its calls fail at this commit, and I cite it for the idea and not the syntax. A customer platform on the engine as a constellation. A page on the site for the architect. The five-row guarantee sheet. A lane of the Atlas for the world, the constellation or the envelope.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** You declare a world with braces, ask it to explain itself, and cross between worlds with a call that returns 0 and a `Why()` that names the gate. Watch the copy: configure, then register.

**The analyst.** You declare things, relations and a goal as a wanted state of the graph, and the gap is measured. Your truths and durations are narrated and not yet judged.

**The designer.** A world declares screens as intents, a refinement as a bounded knob, and where it appears. The shells generated today are stubs, and the persona a world serves is not a field.

**The decision-maker.** You declare who may cross where and at what risk, in three gates, and every cleared crossing leaves a decision with the actor, the authority and the risk of its moment. Your architect's file is text you own.

**The educator.** The record is thin. The education plan lists a ladder from practitioner to architect to contributor among six competing level schemes, and I say so. The exercise below is judged by running.

**The agent.** A world can be wrapped as an agent that the host ticks. A deploy by a language-model actor is refused, and nothing is deployed. The crossing between worlds is governed whoever calls.

## 9. Exercise

Declare two worlds for a place you know: a shop and its supplier, a class and its library, a clinic and its pharmacy. Give each a thing. Join them with one bond for one act. Before you run anything, write what you expect `CallAcross` to return, and which reason you expect to read first.

```haro exercise
? YourConstellation.CallAcross("a", "b", "act")
#--> 1
```

> run: exercise

Then ask for the call, read the reason, and repair the declaration, not the court, one gate at a time, until it returns 1. Count the refusals. Which gate would you have forgotten without the court? The court tells you which gate refuses: a missing risk tier, a missing permission, an authority that does not cover the risk. It does not write the declaration. A harder one: retire the second world and predict the new reason before asking; then register a copy of the first, change your original, and predict what the constellation sees.


=====

# Rehearse, then commit

Source: Chapter 24. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: a twin of files and a twin of the process environment, each with its history, snapshots, a narrated plan, its risks and a validation against reality; the placement plan; the emulator's bundle, wired into the production attach; a deployment as a graph of steps with a rollback; the registry of services with its postures and the gate that refuses a fake at production; designed: the engine seen running in a browser; a drift check for recorded answers; branches of a twin; twins for databases, interfaces and networks; the emulated phase set by the emulated deploy; absent: any live adapter for payments, a model, mail, SMS or an object store; live verification of a remote host; a person's look at the emulator's surface; a customer solution emulated and deployed through this path

Run: 11 of 11 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:26 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

Three practices of the field stand beside the program, each with its own configuration.

The first is plan and then apply: infrastructure as code and deployment pipelines. The record's own references are the dependency graph of a pipeline's jobs, a tool's resource graph with apply-or-revert, and a cluster scheduler that places work by the resources it asks for. The field's habit is that the plan is a file for a tool, and whether the wrong actor may apply it is a policy. The record states the loss it answers: it's a convention, enforced by hope.

The second is service virtualization and the test double: a vendor's test mode with published test cards, a stub server for HTTP, and the libraries that record a call once and replay it. The field's habit is that the double lives in the test suite, and production is a different file. The record's complaint: no gateway will decline a card because you asked nicely.

The third is the emulator and the simulator: a phone emulator, a board emulator, a local stand-in for a cloud. The record's own sentence on the genre is that simulators lie. They mock the database, stub the device, approximate the compute.

What the three share is that each is a separate tool, and the program itself knows nothing of it. (General knowledge for the tools, not a study of the record, except where I quote the documents.)

## 2. The first principle

**Reality is reached through exactly one door: rehearse on what touches nothing, commit through a governed actor, and let production refuse anything that is fake.**

The record's first principle for this ground is the first clause, and the library's own label calls it a first principle: reality is reached through exactly one door. The default path for consequential change is to rehearse in a twin, generate a plan, and commit the plan. The plan is the door. The framework's summary is three words: rehearse, plan, commit. The structure line of this book, which is mine, says the last clause: production refuses a fake. [AUTHOR: Is the chapter's sentence the first clause, which is a library document without an author line, the second, which is yours, or your own? I have joined them.]

## 3. The Softanza form

### The twin

The one artifact that crosses toward reality is a plan. It names each step. It flags the one that removes something. And asked to validate, it re-reads reality and says that the source it was going to move is not there. Chapter 20 said "I saw it warn" and did not show the words. Here they are. Nothing is executed:

```haro
oBench = new stzVirtualFileSystem()
oBench.CreateFolder("config")
oBench.CreateFile("config/pump.ini", "threshold=40")
oBench.MoveFile("old_settings.txt", "config/legacy.ini")
oPlan = oBench.GenerateUpdatePlan()
? oPlan.Narration()
#--> Update plan (3 of 3 operations to commit):
#-->   * 1. create folder 'config'
#-->   * 2. create file 'config/pump.ini' (12 bytes)
#-->   * 3. move 'old_settings.txt' -> 'config/legacy.ini'
? @@(oPlan.Risks())
#--> [ [ 3, "MOVES (removes source): move 'old_settings.txt' -> 'config/legacy.ini'" ] ]
? @@(oPlan.Validate())
#--> [ [ 3, "source absent in reality: old_settings.txt" ] ]
```

> run: kept

The library's narration prints a closing line, that reality is untouched until I choose to commit this plan. The run does not print it, so I do not show it.

The twin is not only a twin of files. A variable set, a directory changed and a child process queued are recorded, narrated and flagged, while the real environment does not have the variable and no process started:

```haro
oEnv = new stzVirtualEnvironment()
oEnv.SetActor("agent-42")
oEnv.SetVar("SHOP_MODE", "rehearsal")
oEnv.Cd("/srv/shop")
oEnv.Spawn("backup-database --all")
? oEnv.Var("SHOP_MODE")
#--> rehearsal
? oEnv.NumberOfPendingSpawns()
#--> 1
? (new stzEnvironment()).Has("SHOP_MODE")
#--> 0
oEnvPlan = oEnv.GenerateUpdatePlan()
? oEnvPlan.Narration()
#--> Update plan (3 of 3 operations to commit):
#-->   * 1. set env var 'SHOP_MODE' = 'rehearsal'
#-->   * 2. change directory to '/srv/shop'
#-->   * 3. spawn process: backup-database --all
? @@(oEnvPlan.Risks())
#--> [ [ 3, "RUNS an external command (side effects): spawn process: backup-database --all" ] ]
```

> run: kept

Chapter 20's limit paragraph said that only files are built. That was wrong, and I have corrected it there.

The twin has a second job. A developer on a laptop with no pins writes pin code for a board. The scope says *rehearsed*, as chapters 8 and 31 showed. At deploy, the lowering bridge turns the rehearsal into the source of firmware, and an assistant may propose that deploy but not perform it. `Build()` records the build command and does not run the toolchain, and `Deploy` lowers in memory, so no file is written:

```haro
oProduct = new stzPlatformProfile("greenhouse")
oProduct.DevelopedOn(:Windows)
oProduct.WithApp(:sensor, :ESP32)
? oProduct.ReadPinIn(:sensor, 34)
#--> rehearsed
? oProduct.WritePinIn(:sensor, 26, 1)
#--> rehearsed
oPlatform = StzPlatformQ("greenhouse")
oPlatform.SetProfile(oProduct)
oPlatform.Build()
try
	oPlatform.DeployAs(LLMActor("copilot"))
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> stzPlatform.Deploy: actor 'copilot' lacks the 'effectful' capability -- it may propose a deploy, not perform it.
? oPlatform.IsDeployed()
#--> 0
oPlatform.DeployAs(HumanActor("me"))
? oPlatform.ArtifactFor(:sensor)
#--> // firmware generated by the Softanza lowering bridge
#--> // target: espidf
#--> void setup() {}
#--> void loop() {
#-->   digitalRead(34);
#-->   digitalWrite(26, 1);
#--> }
```

> run: kept

The limit of this block is in section 7: only two verbs are lowered.

### The plan of placement

Before a byte is built, the delivery planner states, for each capability a part needs, whether the target does it, the engine does it, a construct does it, or the server does it, and gives the reason in a sentence. The figure in parentheses is the catalogue's estimate and not a measure, so do not quote it as a claim. "1 parts" is the library's own grammar:

```haro
oShip = new stzDelivery("shop")
oShip.AddSuperApp(:phone, :Android)
oShip.NeedsIn(:phone, [ :Unicode, :PivotTable, :Collection, :Neural ])
oShip.Plan().Show()
#--> Solution 'shop' -- placement & scope plan (rehearsal; nothing built yet)
#--> ==============================================================================
#-->
#-->   Part 'phone' [superapp] -> android (mobile / edge)
#-->      Unicode            [target]    Unicode: the target-platform is strong at it -> use its own
#-->      PivotTable         [stz.wasm]  PivotTable: Softanza-differential, mobile weak -> the on-device engine
#-->      Collection         [stz.js]    Collection: ergonomic -> a Softanza construct in the target language
#-->      Neural             [server]    Neural: heavy -> runs on the backend, not the edge
#-->      -> stz.wasm carries: [ "PivotTable" ]  (~12 KB, 1 of 4)
#-->
#-->   Summary: 4 capabilities across 1 parts -> target 1, engine 1, construct 1, server 1.
#-->   Build() will compile exactly this scope; Deploy() will commit it.
```

> run: kept

The rule behind the plan, in the emulation design: the edge carries a capability only if it is critical, and either unique to Softanza or weak or absent on the target platform. A regular expression is a construct over the platform's own, and never the engine's matcher compiled for the browser.

### The emulation

The emulator turns the plan into a bundle, and the bundle is what ships. In the next block the bundle generated for the rehearsal is wired straight into the production attach, and the actor decides. An assistant calling `Deploy(:Production)` leaves the site `absent`. A person calling it makes the site `launched`. The manifest that lands names the engine group the part carries. The target here is a local folder, the one kind of site that stores for real without a network:

```haro
cHere = StzReplace(WorkingDirectory(), "\", "/")
oFront = new stzDelivery("shopfront")
oFront.AddSuperApp(:web, :Browser)
oFront.NeedsIn(:web, [ :Unicode, :PivotTable, :Collection ])
oBundle = new stzEmulator(oFront)
oBundle.CompileEngineQ(FALSE)
oBundle.SetOutDirQ(cHere + "/_bundle")
oBundle.Build()
oSite = new stzDeploymentSite("cdn")
oSite.SetKindQ(:LocalRepo).SetStoreAtQ(cHere + "/_live")
oFront.DeployTo(oSite, :web)
oFront.ShipSlice(:web, oBundle)
oFront.SetActor(LLMActor("assistant"))
oFront.Deploy(:Production)
? oSite.Status()
#--> absent
oFront.SetActor(HumanActor("ops"))
oFront.Deploy(:Production)
? oSite.Status()
#--> launched
? @@(StzEngineDirListFiles(cHere + "/_live"))
#--> [ ".stzsite", "deploy.json", "index.html", "stz.js" ]
? read(cHere + "/_live/deploy.json")
#--> {
#-->   "solution": "shopfront",
#-->   "part": "web",
#-->   "engine": "aggregation",
#-->   "artifacts": "index.html,stz.js"
#--> }
StzDirDeleteAll(cHere + "/_bundle")
StzDirDeleteAll(cHere + "/_live")
```

> run: kept (touches files)

The honest half: no engine file for the web part is in the manifest, because the engine compile was off. The block says what the run shipped, and not what a full run would. A bare `Deploy(:Production)` with no actor at all returns a rehearsal and writes a warning, "no effectful actor -- returning a rehearsal (nothing committed)". I ran it too: the site stayed absent and nothing was written.

I did not run `Deploy(:Emulated)`, because it builds each part's subset through the engine toolchain, and the toolchain's build is something this machine must not run. What I ran is the bundle without the compile, and its map names an engine file that is not there. Section 7 says what that means for the claim that the engine runs in a browser.

### The deployment

A rollout that fails undoes what it had done. The web part deploys and passes its gate. The api part's store fails. The remaining steps are skipped, the deployment does not commit, and the web site, already live, is rolled back. The failing site is a server with no storage location, which fails without spawning anything:

```haro
cHere = StzReplace(WorkingDirectory(), "\", "/")
oShop10 = new stzDelivery("shop")
oShop10.AddBackend(:api, :LinuxServer)
oShop10.AddSuperApp(:web, :Browser)
oGood = new stzDeploymentSite("cdn")
oGood.SetKindQ(:LocalRepo).SetStoreAtQ(cHere + "/_site_cdn")
oBad = new stzDeploymentSite("prod-api")
oBad.SetKindQ(:Server)
oRun = new stzDeployment(oShop10)
oRun.SetActor(HumanActor("ops"))
oRun.SetTarget(:web, oGood)
oRun.SetTarget(:api, oBad)
oRun.RunAfter(:api, :web)
aRun = oRun.Run()
? aRun[1]
#--> 0
for aLine in aRun[2]
	? aLine[1] + " -> " + aLine[2]
next
#--> store:web -> done
#--> launch:web -> done
#--> verify:web -> done
#--> store:api -> FAILED
#--> launch:api -> skipped
#--> verify:api -> skipped
? oGood.Status()
#--> rolledback
StzDirDeleteAll(cHere + "/_site_cdn")
```

> run: kept

With an assistant as the actor, the same run returns every step as *rehearsed*, the site stays absent, and no folder is created. I ran it and do not print it.

### Code first, subscribe later

The code asks for a service by name and never learns which implementation it got. The sandbox sees the first call and none after the flip, the second call goes to the live adapter, and the surface is sound once every live binding names a secret that the store holds. The live gateway in this block is a stand-in class written here, since the library ships no payments adapter. A stand-in named `LiveGateway` that returns "authorized" is a fake in everything but its name; the registry takes it as live because it declares nothing, and section 7 says why that matters:

```haro
oPay = new stzPaymentsSandbox()
oReg = new stzServiceRegistry("shop")
oReg.DeclareMany([ :payments, :mail ])
oReg.Bind(:payments, oPay)
oReg.Bind(:mail, new stzMailSandbox())
? Checkout(oReg, 4200)
#--> authorized
? oPay.NumberOfAuthorizations()
#--> 1
oStoreCf = new stzSecretStore("shop")
oStoreCf.Register( (new stzApiKey("stripe-key")).FromLiteralQ("sk-test-123") )
oStoreCf.Register( (new stzApiKey("mail-key")).FromLiteralQ("mk-test-123") )
oReg.BindLive(:payments, new LiveGateway, "stripe-key")
oReg.BindLive(:mail, new LiveMailer, "mail-key")
? Checkout(oReg, 4200)
#--> authorized
? oPay.NumberOfAuthorizations()
#--> 1
? oReg.PostureOf(:payments)
#--> live
oReg.SetPhase(:production)
? oReg.IsSoundVia(oStoreCf)
#--> 1

func Checkout poReg, nCents
	aRes = poReg.Service(:payments).Authorize(nCents, "tok_visa")
	poReg.Service(:mail).Send("dana@example.com", "Receipt", "Thank you.")
	return aRes[:status]

class LiveGateway
	def init
		return
	def Authorize nAmount, cToken
		return [ :status = "authorized" ]

class LiveMailer
	def init
		return
	def Send cTo, cSubject, cBody
		return 1
```

> run: kept

The day you do subscribe, you change a binding and not a line. The key is the test key of a vendor and not a real one.

### Production refuses

One report names every way a surface is not fit for production: a declared service never bound, a fake, a real database that vanishes on restart, a live adapter whose key is not in the store, and, as a warning, a live adapter that names no secret at all. Chapter 31 showed the unbound one and the raise. This is the rest of the list. In the development phase the surface is already not sound, since an unbound service and a missing key are errors in any phase. The other two errors appear only in the production phase. The code lists eight invariants, and the plan and the narration list five:

```haro
oStoreSf = new stzSecretStore("shop")
oSurf = new stzServiceRegistry("shop")
oSurf.Bind(:payments, new stzPaymentsSandbox())
oSurf.Bind(:scratch, StzMemoryDataSourceQ())
oSurf.BindLive(:cache, new stzString("cache-adapter"), "redis-url")
oSurf.BindLive(:sms, new stzString("sms-adapter"), "")
oSurf.Declare(:reporting)
? oSurf.IsSoundVia(oStoreSf)
#--> 0
oSurf.SetPhase(:production)
oSurf.ReportVia(oStoreSf)
#--> Service registry 'shop' [production] -- 4 bound: 1 sandboxed, 1 local, 2 live
#-->   [ERROR] unbound-service @ shop/reporting -- declared but never bound -- asking for it will raise
#-->   [ERROR] sandbox-in-production @ shop/payments -- still bound to a SANDBOX in a production phase -- a fake must never ship
#-->   [ERROR] ephemeral-in-production @ shop/scratch -- a LOCAL source that vanishes on restart (in-memory) is bound in a production phase
#-->   [ERROR] live-without-secret @ shop/cache -- credential 'redis-url' is not in the secret store
#-->   [WARN] inline-credential @ shop/sms -- live adapter bound without naming a store secret
```

> run: kept

Two different questions are asked at the door. Who is refused, and what is refused. An assistant is refused for who it is. A person is refused for what the surface is. And asking leaves the phase where it was; the question is asked in the production frame even in the development phase:

```haro
oStoreWw = new stzSecretStore("shop")
oOne = new stzServiceRegistry("shop")
oOne.Bind(:payments, new stzPaymentsSandbox())
? oOne.Phase()
#--> development
? oOne.MayGoLive(LLMActor("assistant"), oStoreWw)
#--> 0
? oOne.WhyNotLive(LLMActor("assistant"), oStoreWw)
#--> actor 'assistant' is not effectful -- it may propose, not commit
? oOne.MayGoLive(HumanActor("dana"), oStoreWw)
#--> 0
? oOne.WhyNotLive(HumanActor("dana"), oStoreWw)
#--> sandbox-in-production: still bound to a SANDBOX in a production phase -- a fake must never ship
? oOne.Phase()
#--> development
```

> run: kept

And the registry knows services while the delivery knows parts. Joined, they answer the question neither can: which part is about to ship on a double. The registry itself reports nothing, since the phase is development and there are no findings. The part-level check answers anyway, by destination, and names the part:

```haro
oS3 = new stzServiceRegistry("shop")
oS3.DeclareMany([ :payments, :cache ])
oS3.BindSandbox(:payments, new stzPaymentsSandbox())
oS3.BindLocal(:cache, StzMemoryBlobStoreQ())
oD3 = new stzDelivery("shop")
oD3.AddBackend("api", "linux")
oD3.AddApp("web", "browser")
oD3.UseServicesQ(oS3)
oD3.NeedsServiceInQ("api", [ :payments, :cache ])
oD3.NeedsServiceInQ("web", [ :reporting ])
oD3.DeployTo(StzDeploymentSiteQ("host1"), "api")
? oS3.Phase()
#--> development
? len(oS3.Findings())
#--> 0
for aF in StzCheckServiceDelivery(oD3)
	? aF[:rule] + " @ " + aF[:where] + " [" + aF[:severity] + "]"
next
#--> production-part-uses-sandbox @ api [error]
#--> production-part-uses-ephemeral @ api [error]
#--> part-uses-undeclared-service @ web [warning]
```

> run: kept

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

Everything below is from the library's design documents and narrations, written in sessions with no author line. The record holds no ruling of my own for this chapter. Its decisions are the documents'.

**I refused the simulator that lies.** The emulator does not mock. Each part runs the same compiled engine artifact that will ship to its target. That claim has a caution, in section 7: the proof that the engine runs in a browser is not in the pinned tree.

**I refused to re-ship what the target does well.** The edge carries a capability only if it is critical and either unique or weak on the target.

**I refused tool sprawl, and the convention enforced by hope.** The target should be one config away, and deploying to it should be one governed act.

**I refused an inline secret.** A site carries an auth reference, not a key.

**I refused to touch reality by default.** Production commits only if the actor may commit. A bare `Deploy(:Production)` with no actor touches nothing.

**I refused the agreeable fake.** An agreeable fake is more dangerous than no fake at all. Silence is the worst answer: the lenient mode answers 501, because a miss must never be mistakable for success.

**I refused "fake versus real" as the whole truth.** It was too coarse. There are four postures now: sandbox, local, live, and conformance. The third was added when the database exemplar showed that a SQLite file is a real database in a file you own.

**I refused a gate that fails open.** The registry once kept its state in attributes, so attaching it to a delivery took a snapshot, and the gate would have passed an unsound surface. A gate that fails open is worse than no gate. The state now sits in a table keyed by identity, so every copy is the registry. Anything that governs others must survive being copied.

**I refused to virtualize the quality of a model.** Frontier-model quality cannot be virtualized. What the sandbox sells instead is determinism, a cost you can assert, and working offline.

**I refused to ship the live adapters.** The live payments adapters are deliberately not shipped, since an account, a key and a network are infrastructure-gated. The contract is defined, and the registry refuses to let the fake ship in its place.

**I refused to ask a question in the wrong frame.** `MayGoLive` once asked about soundness in the current phase, so with a fake bound it answered yes. Now it is asked in the production frame whatever the phase.

**I refused to say the restaurant's need was the design's origin.** The site says that the emulation and deployment designs take a solution as an example, and that it is an example, not the origin of the design. The sample solution in the documents carries a customer's product name, and I have renamed it in every block. Chapter 28 told the story, and I do not repeat it.

[AUTHOR: The service plan is headed "written 2026-07-23 in answer to the user's question", which asks whether emulation covers databases, business APIs, frontier models and cloud providers, so that a programmer can code a whole program without subscriptions or fees and still get a working solution ready to deploy. Was that your question? May the chapter tell it in the first person as the origin of "code first, subscribe later"?]

## 5. The court

`service_registry_narrated` proves the declared surface; that an unbound service raises; that the application asks by name; that a sandbox in production is a violation, with one finding per fake; that the credential must be in the store; that going live is a governed crossing, with an assistant refused and a person refused over a fake; that asking leaves the phase; the CI helpers; the fourth posture; and a live adapter that needs a certificate. `service_delivery_narrated` proves the external surface rehearsed in the plan; the pre-flight; the gate, which in a production deploy refuses a fake and logs every reason, and opens once the surface is real; that the registry is one registry however many copies are made; and the question of which part depends on a fake. I ran both on 8 October 2026: 93 of 93 and 60 of 60. The second left a file of zero bytes beside itself, and I removed it.

`virtual_system_twin_narrated` proves, in twelve scenes, that the twin touches nothing real, that the plan narrates and flags, that commit is the one door, that a scope refuses, that a reviewer rejects a step, that a hundred operations stay in memory until one commit. `virtual_environment_twin_narrated` proves the same shape for variables, a directory and queued spawns. `emulator_narrated` proves that the bundle generates, the map renders every part by role, and each part carries only its subset. `deployment_narrated` proves the site as config, the governed commit, the step plan, the rollback, feasibility, and the git backend; it spawns `git`, so the book does not run it. `full_scope_model_narrated`, scenes 13 to 16, proves that building precedes deploying, that an unsound solution cannot be built, the lowering bridge, and the governed deploy.

The refusal that production makes is proved by the first two guards, which ran. That the rehearsed engine runs in a browser is proved by no guard that runs.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

Real services lead. The plan says it: a double is not the real service, and you still need at least one real run before production. A scripted gateway is not a vendor's edge cases. Recorded answers drift, and a replay suite needs re-recording and contract tests against the real API. The library has the recording call and no drift check.

Frontier models lead, and cannot be emulated. The model is judged against the real model, deliberately, as its own activity.

Live infrastructure leads. The deployment is infrastructure-gated: live verification against a real host or account. The commands are generated, and one reachable host away from literal.

The mature deploy tools lead. The narration says it plainly: this does not replace every mature deploy tool, it unifies the seams those tools leave exposed.

Fidelity is limited, and says so. Emulation is faithful but not omniscient, so it says which is which. What a double proves is narrower still: a capture sink proves what you would have sent, and a directory is not a CDN.

Where I think the record stands apart is the coupling. The double, the plan, the credential names, the gate and the actor are declared in the same program that ships, and one gate judges them.

## 7. The limit

**A hand-written fake that says nothing about itself passes the gate.** The registry reads what the object says it is. A double that declares neither that it is a sandbox nor that it is local and real is taken for live: no opinion means live. So "production refuses a fake" is exact for the library's own doubles, and for any double that declares itself. An undeclared fake passes:

```haro
oUndeclared = new stzServiceRegistry("shop")
oUndeclared.Bind(:payments, new AlwaysYes)
oUndeclared.SetPhase(:production)
? oUndeclared.PostureOf(:payments)
#--> live
? oUndeclared.IsSound()
#--> 1

class AlwaysYes
	def init
		return
	def Authorize nAmount, cToken
		return [ :status = "authorized" ]
```

> run: kept

The block-above stand-in, `LiveGateway`, is such an object.

**The lowering knows two verbs.** Only `read_pin` and `write_pin` become code. Any other rehearsed operation becomes a comment line. I rehearsed `UseIn(:sensor, :gpio, "blink the status led")` and the firmware got a comment. For a server or a phone, the artifact is a header with no lines when nothing was rehearsed. The narration's "the board is the last inch" holds for reads and writes of pins only.

**The fidelity contract is by target class.** The design says each part declares what is faithful and what is approximated. The code returns one fixed text for a microcontroller, listing two approximated things, and one for everything else, "faithful".

**The sizes are estimates.** The plan prints a figure from a catalogue, and the narration prints two different figures for the same part. Do not print a size as a claim.

**`Deploy(:Emulated)` never moves the registry.** The plan says it resolves services to sandboxes. The code builds the emulator and returns; only the production deploy touches the registry's phase, and the `:emulated` phase value that the registry has is set by nothing.

**A refused `Deploy(:Production)` leaves the registry in the production phase.** After my refused deploy, `Phase()` printed `production`. The pre-flight restores the phase, and the deploy does not.

**A plan flags, it does not rank.** Chapter 20 said it, and the framework document says "ranked risk assessment".

What is designed: the engine running in a browser. The documents say that the phone's `solve 2 -8` returns `x = 4` in a page. The guard that checks it, `wasm_narrated`, says in its own header that it checks the wiring and the build, since the host language cannot execute wasm, and it cites an in-browser page with "8/8 checks". That page is not in the pinned tree: I searched for it and found none. And I have not seen the emulator's surface in a browser, and the record names no one who has. By the law of chapter 33, that surface is unperceived. [AUTHOR: Do you hold a run of that browser proof with a date and a person who saw it? If not, the chapter says: wired, built by the toolchain, not seen in a browser by anyone the record names.]

Also designed: branches of a twin and the plan's alternatives and feedback, for which no method exists; twins for databases, interfaces and networks; and a drift check for recorded answers.

What is absent: a live adapter for payments, a frontier model, an object store, SMS or mail. The contracts and doubles ship, and the live clients do not, deliberately. Live verification of a remote host or account: commands are generated, and nothing ran against a host. A customer solution emulated and deployed through this path: nothing in the record, which chapter 33 carries as the one line still owed.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** You code the whole integration against names, with doubles that raise when you forgot something and a counter that lets you assert a fee. The day you subscribe, you change a binding and not a line. The loss: the always-yes double you wrote yourself passes the gate.

**The analyst.** Before anything moves you can read the placement plan, the external dependencies with the credentials production will ask for by name, the step graph, and which part depends on a fake. The sizes are estimates, and the findings are by part.

**The designer.** The emulator generates a surface from the plan, with a parts grid by role and one window per part. I checked the generated text only, and never the look. Say "generated", and not "seen".

**The decision-maker.** "Who may deploy?" is a property. An assistant may plan the release and the site stays absent. A person may commit. Neither may ship a fake. The loss to print: live hosts and real providers are gated by infrastructure, and the record names no live run.

**The educator.** A learner can build a whole shop's integration with no account, no key and no fee, and watch the double refuse what she forgot. Her deployment fails safely and rolls back.

**The agent.** You may rehearse the whole integration and plan the whole release, in a world where nothing real can move: a sandbox payment moves no money and a mail sink sends no mail. You may ask whether you may go live and be told why not. You cannot commit. The loss: that guarantee covers the library's own doubles, and a surface bound to something live is outside it.
