# Your users keep their medium

Source: Chapter 25. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the governed conversation with its question as data; the SMS port and its sandbox, with the bill per segment in two alphabets; a verifier for the body of an inbound message; five semantic values rendered as colour, earcon and phrase; a voice that speaks, and hears a closed grammar; a closed set of sentences a business agrees to say; a message of three fields between identities of one platform; designed: the commons service of messaging; conversational surfaces for a messaging app, SMS, USSD and voice, with a satisfiability matrix as a court; templates as a grammar; the twenty-two verbs as a list the library holds; absent: a messaging app, USSD or a telephone line, in any form; a conversational projection of a world; a channel, a session, a template, consent; the question in a language other than English; a voice for Hausa, Zarma or Fulfulde; a reply with a space in it

Run: 11 of 11 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:26 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

Conversational commerce, or omnichannel: a business reaches its customers in the channels they already use, and a dialogue engine sits behind them. These are the practices, from general knowledge and not from a study of the record. Every date and every provider rule is to be re-read before print, as the study of the record says in its own "Not measured".

The dialogue frameworks own the dialogue model. The designer writes intents and flows in the framework's notation, and the framework understands free text with trained models and ships connectors to channels.

The business platform of a messaging app: messages arrive by a webhook, and the business may answer freely inside a service window that the customer opened, and outside it only with approved templates, through a verified business account.

The operators' gateways: SMS and USSD. SMS is billed per segment, and I measure this myself below. USSD is a session of short screens with a timeout, through an operator's gateway, country by country, on any phone without data. No file of the snapshot says anything about USSD.

And platform speech, in the library's own plan: Windows has one speech interface, macOS has another, and every modern browser has a third.

What they share is that the dialogue is written once per channel or once per framework, and the channel is a connector, a deployment detail. The chapter turns that picture around. The world is declared once, and the medium is a rendering of it.

## 2. The first principle

**A new medium adds renderings, never verbs.**

It is a sentence of the interface constitution, printed on the site: the grammar is closed, a new medium adds renderings and never verbs. The record is silent on whether the wording is mine. I quote it as the constitution's. [AUTHOR: Is the bridge sentence yours, to be told in the first person, or do I only quote it as the constitution's? And may the chapter list the twenty-two verbs while that repository is not public yet?]

The same sentence, built, for one family of values and not for the twenty-two verbs: the value is shared, and how it is rendered is each medium's own. It is a ruling by Central on my delegation, of 30 September 2026. It proves that the principle is not only a slogan. It is the shape of three working faces: colour, earcon, phrase.

For the floor of the dialogue, the code's own sentence: deterministic floor, no model needed. Chapter 17's law, applied to a medium.

## 3. The Softanza form

The library ships the question as data: a force, a subject, a relation, options and a reason. It ships the verbs, ask, reply, refuse and conclude, and the court. A medium is a rendering of the data and a reader of the reply, both written by the crafter in a few lines. Nothing in the library renders a question to a phone. What it ships is an English line, `AskIn`, which is already a text menu: "(1) cheese (2) olives". I say that in those words.

Two small functions of my own are the whole of the medium: one that renders the question to a line of text, and one that reads the reply. The reading matters, and I show why in the third block:

```haro
aEn = QuestionAfter("couscous", "brik")
aAr = QuestionAfter("كسكس", "بريك")
? ToText(aEn)
#--> Which dish for order3? (every order needs 'dish') 1) couscous 2) brik. Reply with the number.
? StzSmsSegments(ToText(aEn))[:segments]
#--> 1
? StzSmsSegments(ToText(aAr))[:encoding]
#--> ucs2
? StzSmsSegments(ToText(aAr))[:segments]
#--> 2
oBill = new stzSmsSandbox()
oBill.Send("+22790112233", ToText(aEn))
oBill.Send("+22790112233", ToText(aAr))
? oBill.TotalSegments()
#--> 3

func ToText(aQ)
	cOut = "Which " + aQ[:relation] + " for " + aQ[:subject] + "? (" + aQ[:why] + ")"
	for i = 1 to len(aQ[:options])
		cOut += " " + i + ") " + aQ[:options][i]
	next
	return cOut + ". Reply with the number."

func ReadReply(cText)
	for c in cText
		if NOT isdigit(c)
			return cText
		ok
	next
	return 0 + cText

func QuestionAfter(cA, cB)
	oW = new stzKnowledgeGraph("stall")
	oW.Know("order1", "order").Know("order2", "order").Know("order3", "order")
	oW.AddConversationQ("c").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("order", "dish"))
	oW.AskIn("c")
	oW.ReplyIn("c", cA)
	oW.AskIn("c")
	oW.ReplyIn("c", cB)
	return oW.AskInXT("c")
```

> run: kept

The question is data, so any medium renders it. And the medium's own law, here that SMS is billed per segment and the alphabet decides, is a test and not a surprise. The English text is 93 characters and one segment. The same frame with two Arabic dish names is 89 units, in two segments, because the encoding changes. The telephone number is invented, and only the country code, which is Niger's, is real.

Now the whole conversation, rehearsed over the SMS sandbox before any account exists. A conversation is a loop of the library's verbs: the medium carries each question out and each reply in. The assertions are the bill and the refusal. The refusal of "7" is the library's own, as in chapter 18; the difference is that it arrives from a phone, and the sandbox counted four messages and four segments. Nothing in the loop is a model:

```haro
oKB = new stzKnowledgeGraph("stall")
oKB.Know("order1", "order").Know("order2", "order").Know("order3", "order")
oKB.AddConversationQ("c").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("order", "dish"))
oSms = new stzSmsSandbox()
for cIn in [ "couscous", "brik", "7", "2" ]
	oSms.Send("+22790112233", ToText(oKB.AskInXT("c")))
	oKB.ReplyIn("c", ReadReply(cIn))
next
? oKB.ConversationQ("c").GoalState()
#--> fulfilled
? oSms.NumberOfMessages()
#--> 4
? oSms.TotalSegments()
#--> 4
? oKB.ConversationQ("c").Checkpoints()[1][:why]
#--> no option (7) was offered -- 2 option(s) on the table
? @@(oKB.Query([ "order3", "dish", "?o" ]))
#--> [ "brik" ]
```

> run: kept

**The reader of the medium is the medium's job.** An inbound SMS is a string. The library separates numbers, which are option indices, from strings, which are values, and it does not guess. Its own comment: an index can never be mistaken for a literal value. So the medium's reader must decide which it is. With no options on the table, the text "2" is admitted as a dish named 2:

```haro
oKT = new stzKnowledgeGraph("trap")
oKT.Know("order1", "order")
oKT.AddConversationQ("c").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("order", "dish"))
oKT.AskIn("c")
? @@(oKT.ReplyIn("c", "2")[:admitted])
#--> [ "2" ]
```

> run: kept

In the block before, my `ReadReply` turned the text "2" into the number 2, and the option was picked. This is the sharpest sentence about the bridge: a medium adds renderings, and it adds a reading too. The reading is where a user's culture lives, the digits, the words, a local "OUI".

**An outage is not a rejection.** The medium refuses in its own terms. A malformed number, with no `+`, is not an E.164 number, and it is rejected: fix the data. An outage is refused: retry:

```haro
oPh = new stzSmsSandbox()
? oPh.Send("0612345678", "ready")[:status]
#--> rejected
oPh.FailNextQ()
? oPh.Send("+22790112233", "ready")[:status]
#--> refused
? oPh.NumberOfMessages()
#--> 0
? oPh.Send("+22790112233", "ready")[:status]
#--> sent
? oPh.NumberOfMessages()
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

**An inbound message is believed only after its signature is verified.** The way in is the pattern the payments plane built, and the verifier is generic, over the body. No provider's header or body format is modelled, and the JSON is invented. Times are passed in, so no clock is read:

```haro
oSig = new stzRequestSigner("inbound")
oSig.AddKey("provider", "a-shared-secret")
cBody = '{"from":"+22790112233","text":"2"}'
cSig = oSig.SignBody("provider", cBody)
? oSig.VerifyWebhook(cBody, cSig, 1000, 5000)
#--> provider
? "[" + oSig.VerifyWebhook('{"from":"+22790112233","text":"1"}', cSig, 2000, 5000) + "]"
#--> []
? oSig.Why()
#--> signature mismatch (tampered body, or a secret this receiver does not hold)
```

> run: kept

A replayed body inside the window gave "replay detected" in my probe.

**One value, many renderings.** The bridge's shape, built for the five semantic values of the interface constitution. Colour, earcon and phrase render one value. `muted` is silence in sound and a treatment in colour. A value the law does not know is refused:

```haro
? @@(StzSemanticValues())
#--> [ "danger", "warning", "info", "success", "muted" ]
oE = new stzEarcons()
? oE.WouldFireAt("Catastrophe", 1)
#--> 0
? StzColorToNumber("danger.muted") != StzColorToNumber("success.muted")
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

**The voice: spoken, heard, meant.** Text to sound, sound to text, text to meaning. The medium called voice renders and reads with a closed grammar, and the meaning is my declaration. This block needs a French voice and a French recogniser. This machine speaks two languages and hears one, and a machine without them would give another answer, so the output shown is the one recorded on this machine. No microphone, no file, no network: the recogniser is the in-process one:

```haro
oV = StzVoiceQ()
oV.UseLanguage("fr-FR")
oL = new stzListener()
oL.AcceptMeanings([ [ "disque plein", :Danger ], [ "sauvegarde terminee", :Success ] ])
oL.HearSound( oV.ToSoundOf("disque plein") )
? oL.MeaningHeard()
#--> danger
oL.HearSound( oV.ToSoundOf("je voudrais un cafe au lait") )
? oL.WasNoMatch()
#--> 1
? oL.AcceptMeanings([ [ "tout brule", :Catastrophe ] ])
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

The refusal's reason says that `catastrophe` is not one of the five semantic values. I do not print the recogniser's confidence, which varies by machine.

**The world declares its verbs and its reach, and its code names no medium.** A world holds screens declared by intent, five of the twenty-two verbs, and a list of what it reaches. Nothing refuses `:whatsapp` when it is declared:

```haro
oApp = new stzApp("stall")
oApp.AddThingQ(:Dish) { Has([ :name, :price ]) }
oApp.AddScreenQ(:Menu) { ToDiscover(:Dish)  Shows([ :name, :price ]) }
oApp.AddScreenQ(:Basket) { ToSelect(:Dish)  Acts(:Confirm, :Order) }
oApp.AddReaches([ :web, :whatsapp ])
oApp.Explain()
#--> WORLD stall   lives in: memory (not persisted)
#      BEING
#        dish (name, price)
#      MET FROM WITHOUT
#        screen menu: discover dish shows name, price
#        screen basket: select dish
#        reaches web, whatsapp
```

> run: kept

`Acts(...)` is stored and not narrated. And the stage as a run: the platform refuses a surface it cannot generate, and the refusal names the three surfaces it has. This block creates an empty folder in the working directory:

```haro
oPlat = StzPlatformQ("stall-platform")
oPlat.SetWorld(oApp)
try
	oPlat.Generate(:whatsapp)
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> stzPlatform.Generate: unknown reach surface 'whatsapp' (web/desktop/mobile).
```

> run: kept

The same refusal answers `:ussd`.

**What may be said to a customer is a closed set.** The court of what is said has a mechanism today: a closed enumeration compiled to a grammar, which chapters 11 and 19 use for a language and for a model's output. Here it holds the replies that a business is willing to send. It is not a messaging-template unit, and there is none. Two things to print with it: the message says "the model answered" whoever the speaker is, and the enumeration is case-folded:

```haro
oSay = StzOutputSchemaQ([ [ :field = "reply", :type = :oneof, :choices = [ "Your order is received.", "Your order is ready.", "We are closed tonight." ] ] ])
? oSay.ParseOutput('{"reply": "Your order is ready."}')[:ok]
#--> 1
aNo = oSay.ParseOutput('{"reply": "Your order is free, tonight only."}')
? aNo[:ok]
#--> 0
? oSay.CiteFindings(aNo[:findings])
#--> [oneof @ reply] field 'reply' is a closed enumeration (your order is received. | your order is ready. | we are closed tonight.) and the model answered 'Your order is free, tonight only.'.
```

> run: kept

### The restaurant's medium

"Simple like a messaging app" is simulated in the project, as chapter 28 already found.

So the restaurant is the first form of the sentence of this chapter, and not an example of the messaging medium. The customer keeps their medium: a link that opens in the browser they already have. ## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**Messaging, the question.** The study's report of my ask: conversations happen programmatically by default, or through minimal configuration, without obliging the users to change their usability cultures. The study's section is titled "ruled" and not "ruled by the author", and its proposals for the conversational surfaces are a session's. The record holds no ruling of mine that adopts them. I say *proposed*, not *decided*.

**A conversation needs no model.** The floor is deterministic. A model can upgrade the fluency behind the same surface.

**Voice is not a paradigm.** The plan of record says out until asked in writing: all of dialogue management, wake words, intent parsing and barge-in.

**Commands, not transcription: a measured refusal.** The gate was set before the numbers: free dictation ships at eighty per cent exact and 0.60 mean confidence. The run gave 66.7 per cent and 0.600, which failed. The closed grammar gave a hundred per cent and 0.898, which passed. So this face has no `Transcribe()`, and never will until a better recogniser exists.

**Refuse, never substitute.** Speaking French to an operator who asked for English is worse than saying nothing.

**No inferred meaning.** The author still declares the meaning. A sixth value would be a constitutional amendment wearing a library's clothes.

**A sink proves what you would have sent.** The SMS port's honest limit: carrier filtering, sender-ID rules, per-country alphabet quirks, and asynchronous delivery receipts, none of which is modelled here.

**The screen acknowledges, and the sound is not the acknowledgement.**

## 5. The court

`sms_port_narrated` proves the segment law, with its boundaries at 160 and 161, 306 and 307, the emoji cliff at 100 to 102 units, 71 Arabic letters making two segments, and the nine extension characters; E.164; the sink and its bill; the outage against the rejection; and the refusal in production. I ran it once, and 53 of 53 assertions passed. `wisecoding_rich_narrated` is chapter 18's: the frame, the numbered options, the refusal of a number nobody offered, the checkpoints with a lifetime. `payments_webhooks_narrated` proves that an inbound signature is verified before it is believed: unsigned, forged, replayed. `sound_semantics_narrated` proves that the five values are the law's, that four sound and `muted` is silent, and that an invented value is refused. `sound_listener_narrated` proves the closed grammar, that no match is a result, and that the confidence is a separate call; I ran it once, and 21 passed. `sound_convergence_narrated` proves that the loop closes for the four states, that the language is negotiated per direction, that a sixth value is refused at declaration, and that a stranger's phrase gives no meaning.

A caveat for the court line. The sound guards need the engine's sound library, and for the loop, a voice and a recogniser. On a machine without them the voice guards print "skipped" and pass. A pass there proves nothing about speech. The guards for SMS and for the conversation have no such dependency.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The record's own count, in the study of 12.25: of the constituent units of messaging, eighteen in all, what is missing. Its sentence that the word for a messaging app appears in no code file and no design document of the library, and USSD in none, I re-measured at this commit and confirmed. The only hits are word-list data files.

The frameworks lead in understanding and in connectors. Chapter 18 states Softanza's side from the code: the conversation asks one slot at a time, over a graph the user must first have declared, and it does not understand an open reply.

The field leads in channel coverage and in delivery truth, receipts and provider-approved templates. The SMS port says it itself: a sink proves what you would have sent, not that a carrier would accept it, and the live adapter is infrastructure-gated. No live client for SMS ships.

The voice plan: a declaration is portable, and a voice is not. A browser's voice has no buffer, so the loop closes on the native tier only. This machine can speak English and cannot hear it.

What the field has in cloud dictation and in trained recognisers for many languages, the record chose not to ship. Free dictation reached 66.7 per cent on the plane's own clean synthetic audio.

What the record claims instead: the dialogue is a rendering of a declared world judged by a court, deterministic by default. The medium is a port with a sandbox, so the conversation and its bill are rehearsed before an account exists.

## 7. The limit

The study (12.25) has two errors that this chapter's runs correct. The first: it says speech to text is missing. At this commit it exists, by decision, for commands and not for dictation. The second: it says no message exists. There is a message of three fields, between identities of one platform, and I show it:

```haro
oDb = new stzDatabase(":memory:")
oCom = StzPlatformQ("neighbourhood")
oCom.OpenCommonsOn(oDb)
oCom.PostMessage("customer", "kitchen", "one brik, no egg")
? @@(oCom.Inbox("kitchen"))
#--> [ [ "customer", "one brik, no egg" ] ]
```

> run: kept

It is a sender, a recipient, a body and a time in a SQL table. It has no medium, no status and no parts. It is the message the study found absent, in its smallest form.

What is built: the governed conversation; the SMS port and its sandbox; a body-signature verifier with replay protection, wired to no messaging provider; the semantic layer of five values rendered as colour, earcon and phrase; the voice; a minimal message in the commons; a closed set of sentences with a refusal; and the world's declared intents and reaches.

What is designed: `Provides(:Messaging)` as a commons service of the constellation. The design says so in two places, and the word `Provides` is in no code file of the application folder. The commons runtime as the operational counterpart of the declared provides and shares. Conversational surfaces for a messaging app, SMS, USSD, voice and web chat, the satisfiability matrix as a court, templates as a grammar, a channel in the platform profile: these are the study's five proposals, a session's, and not the library's design. A spoken analysis and a spoken transport: demos and compositions, and not a unit.

What is absent:

- Ports for a messaging app, Telegram, USSD, and the telephone call: zero hits. A USSD session has no shape in the library.
- A conversational projection of a world. The only projection is `Generate`, which knows three surfaces and refuses a fourth. No shell renders a question or a flow.
- The messaging units: channel, session with a window or a timeout, template, consent. The word *consent* appears in the snapshot only as the microphone's boundary, in comments.
- The twenty-two verbs as a library list. `stzApp` holds five of them as screen intents, unchecked, since any word is accepted. The other seventeen are in the constitution and not in the library. The bridge sentence is applied by hand today.
- The question in a language other than English. `AskIn` is English, and the tutor shows the pattern in four languages. `NaturallyIn` asked for Zarma raises the opaque "Unsupported object type!" and not "no pack for this language".
- A voice for the languages that the product designs name, Zarma, Hausa and Fulfulde. None here; the library would refuse.
- **A reply with a space in it, from a phone.** I ran `ReplyIn("c", "grand couscous")` and it raised, with an uninitialised variable. With a hyphen it is admitted. This is chapter 18's known limit, and it is decisive here, because people type spaces in a text message. The conversation above works only because the replies are digits and one-word dishes.
- Live adapters for SMS, for a messaging app and for a telephone line; delivery receipts; sender-ID rules.
- Nothing in the record runs on a real phone as a conversation.

[AUTHOR: May the chapter present `On(:whatsapp)`, `On(:ussd)`, `On(:voice)` and the satisfiability matrix as what you mean to build, with the limit beside them, or only as what a study proposed?]

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** The question comes back as data. You write the rendering and the reader for your medium in ten lines, and every reply goes through the same door whatever carried it.

**The analyst.** The goal is the schema of the conversation. The transcript says which message produced which fact. The sandbox counts the bill before a provider exists.

**The designer.** Your user keeps the phone they have: a text line with numbers, or a spoken phrase. The constitution's verbs are the grammar you render, and a verb the medium cannot render should be a red cell, and not a silent omission. That is designed and not built, since the matrix does not exist.

**The decision-maker.** A conversation by SMS can be priced in a test: two Arabic dish names moved a 93-character question from one segment to two. The question to ask a vendor, what refuses a sentence your business did not approve, has an answer here: a closed set.

**The educator.** The tutor already says the missing step as a question in four languages, in templates that carry no code. A learner on a plain phone is a case the record names and does not yet serve.

**The agent.** You may propose a reply, and only a sentence of the closed set passes. An invented one is refused with its reason. And you cannot give a meaning to a phrase: the author declares it.

## 9. Exercise

Take a question your own world asks. The one-relation world of chapter 18's exercise will do. Before you run anything, write under each line the output you expect:

1. the number of segments of your rendering of the question, with names in the Latin alphabet;
2. its segments if one option is a word in Arabic script;
3. what happens when the customer's reply is a digit that was not offered;
4. what happens when the reply is "yes please".

```haro exercise
? StzSmsSegments(YourRendering)[:segments]
#--> your prediction
? StzSmsSegments(YourRenderingWithOneArabicWord)[:segments]
#--> your prediction
```

> run: exercise

Run it, with the sandbox and never a real send, so that the exercise touches nothing. The fourth prediction raises today, and that is the lesson. If a promise breaks, do not change it: explain the output first.

# The environment

Source: Chapter 26. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the interface as a text with a court that finds the element serving no verb; two projections of it, native and web, with a recorded table of divergences and a page that can say DISAGREE; the accessibility tree emitted from the declaration; a page the language serves on the loopback; a world that proposes and whose proposals clear when it is acted on; screens declared as intents; one generated browser surface; designed: the shell kit with two shells; the chrome as closed kinds (spine, ribbon, rail, status bar); the facet as data; a rail bound to focus; status cells read from the courts, with a guard that a cell never shows a state the court did not emit; absent: an IDE of this family; a class named for a ribbon, a rail, a spine or a status bar; a list of the twenty-two verbs in the library and a refusal of an unknown verb; a guard that judges a shell against the verbs; an editor; a measured latency

Run: 10 of 10 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:26 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

The field's environments have settled on a shape. A toolbar and a menu at the top, a sidebar of files, an editor, a terminal below, and a status bar at the foot. Lately an assistant sits in a pane or a terminal beside the code, and the human approves its edits. (General knowledge, marked.)

The record's own field studies, dated, are three. The notepad that ships with the former host language, measured on 12 August 2026 at thirty-three source files and about four thousand lines, seven dock widgets, a fifteen-key configuration and no language service: it exists, it ships in the box, and it works on the day you install it. The fast editors, in the ergonomics specification of March 2026: input to screen under sixteen milliseconds on any modern hardware, no exceptions; minimal chrome; no status bar clutter, no toolbar forests; a dry run before a structural change; and inspections that say why and offer a fix. And the status bar, as that specification wrote it: twenty-four pixels high, carrying only essential state, with five build states, one of which is *unchecked*, for a project where no build has run yet. The state of "not yet judged" has its own word. A bar that was green by default would be the field's usual lie.

Where the field is silent, the chapter answers. The field orders its toolbars by the tool's features, and not by the life of the thing being made. And it lets an assistant's proposal land as an edit, with the human's approval as a habit.

## 2. The first principle

**Declare the interface once. Every shell is a projection of it, and a projection may add renderings, never verbs.**

The library's header for its authored interface says it: the file is the contract, and the native markup and the web page are its projections. The study reads it as one declaration, two projections, proved to agree, resting on my ruling of 7 October 2026: carry both. The companion sentence is the interface constitution's, on the site: a new medium adds renderings, never verbs.

## 3. The Softanza form

Here I have to tell you something before the blocks. At this commit, **no IDE of this family exists**. No class in the library is named for a ribbon, a rail, a spine or a status bar. The study's own honesty line, proposed for the site, says it: no IDE of this family ships today; one shell does, for the sound plane; the foundation that would make the next one a projection is specified and not yet built. My reading agrees, with one addition: the library generates one browser surface, the emulator's, and I show it below with a loss.

What the library has are the pieces under each mark of the pattern, and they run. I take them in order.

### One shell, declared as text, judged by a court

The chrome is declared as text and judged before it is shown. The ribbon is generated from data, here a plain list; the facet as a library object is designed and not built. The court finds the one element that serves no verb. The four facet names are four of the five phases of the design corpus in the record: define, architect, build, govern, ship. I took four to keep the block short. Every declaration must carry its rationale, and a declaration without one is refused:

```haro
aFacets = [ "Define", "Build", "Govern", "Ship" ]
cTabs = ""
cIds = ""
for cF in aFacets
	cId = "tab_" + lower(cF)
	cIds += cId + ", "
	cTabs += 'DEFINE TEXT ' + cId + ' ( CONTENT "' + cF + '", ROLE tab, SATISFIES select ) RATIONALE "A facet."' + nl
next
cShell = 'DEFINE PANEL shell ( SIZE [640, 360], DIRECTION column, FONT "app", SATISFIES locate, CHILDREN [ribbon, work, status] ) RATIONALE "One shell, three bands."' + nl +
	'DEFINE BOX ribbon ( HEIGHT 32, DIRECTION row, ROLE tablist, SATISFIES discover, CHILDREN [' + cIds + '] ) RATIONALE "Facets, in the order of a life."' + nl + cTabs +
	'DEFINE BOX work ( HEIGHT fill, DIRECTION row, CHILDREN [page, rail] ) RATIONALE "The workspace and its rail."' + nl +
	'DEFINE TEXT page ( CONTENT "the work", SATISFIES read ) RATIONALE "What is being made."' + nl +
	'DEFINE BOX rail ( WIDTH 160, ROLE group, SATISFIES preview, CHILDREN [advice] ) RATIONALE "What the court proposes."' + nl +
	'DEFINE TEXT advice ( CONTENT "no proposal", SATISFIES understand ) RATIONALE "The rail, empty."' + nl +
	'DEFINE BOX status ( HEIGHT 24, ROLE status, SATISFIES understand, CHILDREN [state] ) RATIONALE "The truth of the build."' + nl +
	'DEFINE TEXT state ( CONTENT "clean", SATISFIES understand ) RATIONALE "The court verdict."'
oShell = new stzUiDocument(cShell)
? oShell.IsClean()
#--> 1
? len(oShell.Declarations())
#--> 12
? @@( oShell.Orphans() )
#--> [ "work" ]
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

The generating loop is the book's own, and the roles are from the closed list in the library: `tablist`, `tab`, `group` and `status` are on it.

### The twenty-two verbs

Every element must trace to at least one verb, and a word that is not one of the twenty-two is found. There is an honest division of labour. The library records the claim, with `SATISFIES`, and finds the untraced elements, with `Orphans()`. It does not hold the list and does not refuse an unknown intent: the guard asserts that an intent nobody has defined is not an error. So the list below is typed from the constitution, which is in its own repository, and the book holds it:

```haro
cShell = StzReplace(cShell, "DEFINE BOX work ( HEIGHT fill,", "DEFINE BOX work ( SATISFIES focus, HEIGHT fill,")
oShell = new stzUiDocument(cShell)
? @@( oShell.Orphans() )
#--> [ ]
aVerbs = [ "discover", "understand", "locate", "focus", "filter", "compare", "read", "scan", "scroll", "zoom", "select", "highlight", "preview", "mark", "act", "confirm", "cancel", "undo", "pause", "resume", "retry", "exit" ]
? len(aVerbs)
#--> 22
aStrangers = []
for cIntent in oShell.SatisfiedIntents()
	if not StzContains(aVerbs, cIntent)
		aStrangers + cIntent
	ok
next
? @@( aStrangers )
#--> [ ]
oOdd = new stzUiDocument(StzReplace(cShell, "SATISFIES preview", "SATISFIES transfer_funds"))
? oOdd.IsClean()
#--> 1
aStrangers = []
for cIntent in oOdd.SatisfiedIntents()
	if not StzContains(aVerbs, cIntent)
		aStrangers + cIntent
	ok
next
? @@( aStrangers )
#--> [ "transfer_funds" ]
```

> run: kept

`work` was a pure layout box, and the court counts containers too. Whether a container should be exempt from the verbs is the constitution's to say, and not the library's. The families, from the constitution: orienting, attention, information, selection, action, and continuity. And the interpretation I give: the library finds the stranger, and a list the library does not hold cannot refuse it. Chapter 25 said that the same list is applied by hand today. [AUTHOR: The twenty-two verbs are the interface constitution's, in its own repository, which the site says is not public yet. The library's own design text still lists twenty-four verbs from an interaction engine that your commit of 26 May 2026 removed as invented by an AI. May the chapter tell that removal as the refusal at its centre, that the library does not own the verbs, and print the twenty-two typed from the constitution?]

### Two projections

The same text yields the native markup and the web page. Every declared element is in both, and the two spell a property differently exactly where the profile records a divergence:

```haro
cRml = oShell.ToRml()
cHtml = oShell.ToHtml()
nBoth = 0
for cName in oShell.Names()
	if StzContains(cRml, 'id="' + cName + '"') and StzContains(cHtml, 'id="' + cName + '"')
		nBoth++
	ok
next
? nBoth
#--> 11
? len(oShell.Names()) - 1
#--> 11
? StzContains(cRml, "--rmlui-direction")
#--> 1
? StzContains(cHtml, "--rmlui-direction")
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

Eleven elements and not twelve: the panel is the page itself, and not a `div`. The native tier lays out through the engine's layout library, vendored, and draws through the graphics plane. I did not open a window.

### The browser shell

The browser shell is a page that the language serves, on the loopback. One request, one process, no network beyond 127.0.0.1. A second reactor plays the client, so it is offline by construction. The handler reads a global, because lambdas of the host language do not capture locals:

```haro
$cShellPage = cHtml
oSrv = new stzAppServer()
oSrv.Get_("/", func oReq, oResp { oResp.Html($cShellPage) })
oSrv.Start(0, "127.0.0.1")
oClient = new stzReactor()
cCRLF = char(13) + char(10)
nJob = oClient.SubmitTcp("127.0.0.1", oSrv.Port(), "GET / HTTP/1.1" + cCRLF + "Host: local" + cCRLF + "Connection: close" + cCRLF + cCRLF)
? oSrv.ServeOne(3000)
#--> 1
cAnswer = oClient.AwaitTcp(nJob, 5000)
? StzContains(cAnswer, "200 OK")
#--> 1
? StzContains(cAnswer, 'id="status"')
#--> 1
? oSrv.Host()
#--> 127.0.0.1
oSrv.Stop()
```

> run: kept

The cost of this shape is named in the record. A locally bound port is a surface and must be treated as one, and the design intent has been measured on nothing. The library already refuses to leave the loopback unauthenticated, at bind time:

```haro
oT = new stzAppTopology("shell")
oT.AddDataset("notes", [ [ "first" ] ])
oT.SetDatasetColumns("notes", [ "text" ])
oBackend = new stzAppBackend("shell", oT)
bRaised = FALSE
try
	oBackend.StartOn("0.0.0.0", 0)
catch
	bRaised = TRUE
done
? bRaised
#--> 1
? oBackend.IsLive()
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

### A status bar that tells the truth

A status cell is a function of the court's verdict, so it cannot say *clean* about a document the court refused. The function below is the book's own, over the library's `IsClean()` and its diagnostics. The library has no status-cell class, and no guard that a cell shows only what the court emitted; that guard is designed. The specification also names the state before any build, *unchecked*, and my function has no such state:

```haro
? ShellStatus(cShell)
#--> clean
? ShellStatus(StzReplace(cShell, "[page, rail]", "[page, ghost]"))
#--> refused: DANGLING_REFERENCE
? ShellStatus(StzReplace(cShell, "ROLE status", "ROLE sttus"))
#--> refused: UNKNOWN_ROLE

func ShellStatus(cText)
	oD = new stzUiDocument(cText)
	if oD.IsClean()
		return "clean"
	ok
	return "refused: " + oD.Diagnostics()[1][:code]
```

> run: kept

And what a screen reader reads is emitted from the declaration. A region declared `ROLE status` is emitted into the accessibility tree with its rationale as its description, in reading order, and no flag turns the tree off. The layout needs a font file, and this block uses the system's:

```haro
oShell.UseFont("C:/Windows/Fonts/segoeui.ttf")
oP = oShell.ToPanel()
oT = new stzAccessibilityTree(oShell, oP)
? oT.NodeCount()
#--> 12
? oT.NodeOf("status")[:role]
#--> status
? oT.NodeOf("status")[:description]
#--> The truth of the build.
? @@( oT.ReadingOrder() )
#--> [ "window", "tablist", 'tab "Define"', 'tab "Build"', 'tab "Govern"', 'tab "Ship"', "group", 'label "the work"', "group", 'label "no proposal"', "status", 'label "clean"' ]
```

> run: kept

The library's header says why: every toolkit gates accessibility behind a performance flag, which is where users silently get nothing, so here there is nothing to gate and nothing to forget to switch on. The same file says what a screen reader can operate, an agent can operate.

### A rail that proposes

A rail is a view over what the world proposes. A pulse proposes and never relates. Only an act on the world clears a proposal, and the world's own status line counts from the same list the rail shows:

```haro
oApp = StzAppQ("fieldvisits")
oApp.AddThingQ(:Client) { Has([ :name ]) }
oApp.AddThing(:Visit)
oApp.AddReactionQ(:Client).Unseen(90, :Days) { Propose(:Visit) }
oApp.AddInstance("amadou", :Client)
oApp.AddInstance("binta", :Client)
oApp.Live()
#--> [fieldvisits] is live -- 2 thing(s), 0 flow(s), 1 reaction(s), 0 goal(s); 2 proposal(s)
for aP in oApp.Proposals()
	? aP[1] + " " + aP[2] + " " + aP[3] + " " + aP[4]
next
#--> propose visit for amadou
#--> propose visit for binta
? oApp.Pulse()
#--> 0
? len(oApp.Proposals())
#--> 2
oApp.Relate("amadou", :visit, "visit-1")
oApp.Pulse()
for aP in oApp.Proposals()
	? aP[1] + " " + aP[2] + " " + aP[3] + " " + aP[4]
next
#--> propose visit for binta
```

> run: kept

`Live()` prints its line, and the judge compares what is printed. "2 thing(s)" counts the two things, Client and Visit, and not the instances. Chapter 20 owns the law beneath the rail, the proposal, the plan and the court.

And a screen is an intent. The one place the library already speaks in verbs is the world's screens. Five methods exist: `ToDiscover`, `ToUnderstand`, `ToFocus`, `ToSelect` and `ToAct`. A sixth verb, `ToCompare`, does not exist:

```haro
oW = StzAppQ("fieldvisits")
oW.AddThingQ(:Client) { Has([ :name ]) }
oW.AddScreenQ(:Home)  { ToDiscover(:Client) }
oW.AddScreenQ(:File)  { ToUnderstand(:Client) }
oW.AddScreenQ(:Pick)  { ToSelect(:Client) }
oW.AddScreenQ(:Edit)  { ToAct(:Client) }
oW.Explain()
#--> WORLD fieldvisits   lives in: memory (not persisted)
#      BEING
#        client (name)
#      MET FROM WITHOUT
#        screen home: discover client
#        screen file: understand client
#        screen pick: select client
#        screen edit: act client
try
	oW.AddScreenQ(:Side) { ToCompare(:Client) }
catch
```

The intent word is stored as a string, and is not checked against any list.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I decided to carry both shells.** On 7 October 2026 I was asked whether the foundation takes the native shell or the browser shell, and I answered: carry both. The consequence the study states is that one chrome is declared once, and two shells project it.

**I decided that the mechanics are the library's and the IDE is the product's.** The library's design document: the paradigm mechanics are platform; the book, the brand, the IDE are product, and never leak. The foundation ships refinement as a library verb available to every program.

**I removed the interaction engine.** On 26 May 2026 my commit removed twenty-two modules invented by an AI that were not in the original Softanza, among them the interaction module. Its design text, which says twenty-four verbs and laws enforced by the engine, is still in the tree. The constitution's list is twenty-two.

**I refused to resolve intents inside the language of the panel.** The panel declares no meanings: there is no `:Danger` and there never may be. The intent slot was left deliberately unresolved.

**I refused to author the markup, and I refused a script engine.** The native markup is emitted, never authored; there is no script engine; flexbox only. The reason that the chrome is a text file and not a class.

**I refused a flag that turns the accessibility tree off.**

**I admitted a lens that cannot capture.** The estate's charter for its products: one truth, in files, and two lenses. What the charter refused was frameworks that capture the work. A lens that cannot capture is admissible. The environment is a projection of plain text, and not a store.

**A session removed a facet, and I restored it.** The reference design records that the Connections facet was first-class in the prototype of June 2026, and was removed by an agent on circular reasoning, which cascaded into five documents. I reinstated it. Who decides the ribbon is the author.

**I accepted the browser shell with its cost, unmeasured.** The charter of the front-door IDE chose a browser tab, built with the engine, with no install step, and named the loss.

**The pattern was learned by building it six times in two years**, the study counts. That count is the study's.

## 5. The court

`gui_stzui_narrated` proves the closure of the panel court, a dangling reference, a duplicate, an orphan box, a missing panel, the mandatory rationale and a round trip; and in scene 13, the intent slot: `SATISFIES`, `Orphans()`, and that an intent nobody has defined is not an error. `gui_profile_narrated` proves that the profile is data, with three recorded divergences; that one document has two projections and every declared element is in both; and that the fixture emits a self-checking page. The browser half of that guard is a procedure to reproduce. `gui_tier_agreement_narrated` proves that one display list rendered as vector and as pixels agrees to the pixel. It is not HTML against native. `gui_accessibility_narrated` proves the tree emitted from the declaration. `05_stzapp_live_narrated` proves that a live world proposes and acting clears. `emulator_narrated` proves the generated surface of the emulator.

There is no guard that judges a shell against the twenty-two verbs, none that a status cell shows only what a court emitted, and none that the two projections of a chrome agree in the browser. The study says so, and I found nothing to the contrary.

**The two-projections check can fail, and it did.** The library's fixture is a page and not an assertion: it carries the web projection and the boxes the native layout produced, and a script that compares them and prints a verdict strip. A runner cannot judge it, so I read it by hand on 8 October 2026. On the chrome of the first block, eleven boxes with text in each region, the strip read "AGREE on every declared box of 11", with a note that four boxes of automatic width differ, which is two shapers and not the profile. On the same three-band chrome with empty regions, the strip read "DISAGREE on 1 declared boxes of 3". The cause is in the web projection: an empty box is emitted as `<div id="body"/>`, which an HTML parser reads as an open tag, so the siblings that follow nest inside it. The native tier is unaffected. The fixture catches it, and the library's guards do not, since their own showcase has no empty box. An empty workspace is the pattern's first state. So the strip tells the truth both ways, which is the chapter's fifth mark in one picture, and the library has a defect on the exact case.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The fast editors set a bar that the record writes down as law for its own product: input to screen under sixteen milliseconds, a cold start under four hundred, no exceptions. And it has measured it on none: neither shell has a latency number.

A text change dirties the whole document. One bound value changed re-shapes every string, where a colour re-shapes one. The field's editors patch.

The browser costs what it costs: file access through the browser's model, a port to guard, and an unmeasured latency. The native shell costs a window system and a GPU.

The field has a language service, and the record has a contract and no service. The explorer's model reads the world file the service would emit, and it is loaded by no entry file: a block that calls it without a hand-written load fails.

The field's fast editor treats the status bar as clutter. The pattern keeps one and asks it to be true. That is a bet, and the record's own corpus argues the field's side in the same breath.

## 7. The limit

**The web projection carries no roles, no labels and no intents.** `ToHtml` emits an identifier and a style only. The tree that carries roles is a separate artefact, as the block above shows, so a browser shell would infer from bare `div`s what the native shell emits. The plan itself says the web has no adapter.

**The library's own mission control does not tell the truth.** The emulator generates a browser surface, a parts grid with status chips and fidelity badges, a device window and a console for each part. Its status words are constants of the device class and of the template, and they are not measured. Here a plan with one firmware part and one GPIO capability, and no pump anywhere:

```haro
oPlan = new stzDelivery("greenhouse")
oPlan.AddFirmware(:node, :ESP32)
oPlan.NeedsIn(:node, [ :GPIO ])
oEmu = new stzEmulator(oPlan)
oEmu.CompileEngineQ(FALSE)
oEmu.SetOutDirQ("_emu")
oEmu.Build()
cH = read("_emu/index.html")
? StzContains(cH, "needs a look")
#--> 1
? StzContains(cH, "2 approximated")
#--> 1
? StzContains(cH, "parts healthy")
#--> 1
? StzContains(cH, "Ready to ship.")
#--> 1
StzDirDeleteAll("_emu")
```

> run: kept (touches files)

The part gets "2 approximated" and, in the grid's detail, a text about pump timing and sensor noise: the greenhouse demo's text, returned for any microcontroller. The header says "parts healthy" and the banner says "Ready to ship." for the same plan, from literals. A backend-only plan prints "parts healthy" and none of the other words. "Deploy to production" is an alert. The truthful half is real: fidelity is printed, and the console refuses a verb that the part's engine subset lacks. That is narration in the library, which I did not run.

This is the loss against the library's own page. [AUTHOR: May the chapter print it beside "a status bar that tells the truth", as the loss against the library's own page, before the request to derive those words from the plan is answered?]

**`ToPanel` needs a font file and raises without one.** The accessibility tree and the fixture need a laid-out panel, so the blocks that use them depend on a system font.

**`Orphans()` counts containers.** Whether a layout-only box owes a verb is the constitution's rule to state.

What is built: the interface as text with a court; four closed kinds, a closed list of nineteen roles, a closed field set, a mandatory rationale, a round trip to a fixpoint; two projections, with a recorded table of three divergences and a page that can say DISAGREE; the accessibility tree, and a bridge that reaches a real screen reader's interface on Windows, read and not run by me; a declared value changes and the screen follows, with focus kept; a page hosted on the loopback; a backend that refuses to leave the loopback unsigned; a world that proposes; screens declared as intents; one generated browser surface; and one binary shell, the sound plane's listening bench, in about seven hundred lines of Zig, which I read and did not build.

What is designed: the shell kit with two shells; the chrome as closed kinds named for the spine, ribbon, rail and status bar; the facet as data, with its group, its place in the life, its artefact kinds, its court and its profile; the rail bound to focus; the governance cells read from the courts, with a guard that a cell never shows a state the court did not emit.

What is absent: an IDE of this family. Any class named for a ribbon, a rail, a spine or a status bar. A facet declaration. A list of the twenty-two verbs inside the library, and any refusal of an unknown verb: `transfer_funds` passes the court. A guard judging a shell against the verbs. A rail that follows focus. Status cells derived from courts. An editor. A measured latency. [AUTHOR: At this commit no IDE of this family exists. Should the chapter tell the pattern as designed, with the running pieces as evidence, ending on "no IDE of this family ships today", or wait for the shell kit?]

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** You write the shell as text, and a court tells you the one box that serves no verb before anything is drawn. The same file is a native window and a page, and the library has a page that says when they disagree.

**The analyst.** The ribbon is the order of your solution's life, and not of a tool's features. The status cell reads the court, and you can ask the world what it proposes before it is asked to act.

**The designer.** Your interface has a constitution of twenty-two verbs and a closed grammar that adds renderings and never verbs. Every element must say which verb it serves, and the library already finds the ones that say none.

**The decision-maker.** Ask of any environment three things. Does its status bar read a court? Does its assistant live in one panel and propose? And who judges that the two shells agree? Here the answers are: designed; designed, with the rail's law built beneath it in chapter 20; and a self-checking page, with its first defect found in an hour.

**The educator.** The exercise is a prediction: say what the court will answer before you run it. The status word that is a constant is a lesson in the difference between a label and a truth.

**The agent.** The shell is a text file that you can read and write, and the tree a screen reader reads is the tree you may read. Your proposals appear in a rail and commit nowhere.

## 9. Exercise

One exercise, in three moves.

1. Write a shell of your own in five elements: a title bar, a ribbon of three facets taken from your own solution's life, a work area, a rail, a status bar. Before you run anything, write on paper which of the twenty-two verbs each element serves, and which one of your boxes you expect the court to call an orphan.
2. Run `IsClean()` and `Orphans()`, and compare with your paper. Then break the file three ways: a child that is declared nowhere, a role that is not on the list, and an intent that is not one of the twenty-two. For each, write what your status function will answer, and whether the library, the book's function, or nobody will catch it.
3. Emit the page, open it, and write the line the fixture strip will print for a version with an empty work area. Then look.

```haro exercise
oMine = new stzUiDocument('DEFINE PANEL shell ( SIZE [640, 360], DIRECTION column, CHILDREN [bar] ) RATIONALE "Mine."' + nl + 'DEFINE TEXT bar ( CONTENT "mine", SATISFIES understand ) RATIONALE "Mine too."')
# under each line, write what you expect before you run it
? oMine.IsClean()
? @@( oMine.Orphans() )
```

> run: exercise (touches the system)

If a promise breaks, do not change it: explain the output first.

# The engine beneath: three layers, one engine, Haro

Source: Chapter 27. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: three layers chosen by the entry file you load, each including the one beneath and none above; the dependency rule held at the loads; an engine of eighty-nine libraries, in Zig, built in optimised safe mode, with the engine's own count of what it exposes; Haro as a ratified charter and a spike whose courts run on the new runtime and on the old one; designed: the base layer inheriting the core layer; the max layer as a built layer; a court that checks the layers against their contract; a layer chosen by a declaration the compiler honours; the other faces of Haro over one program graph; absent: a lean core layer that runs its strings; a Linux or macOS engine in service; a measured pass of the library on the new runtime under Linux; a `haro` executable; Haro running the library

Run: 9 of 9 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:26 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

A platform inside one codebase is drawn as a stack with one rule: an inner layer knows nothing of an outer one. The cleanest peer, in the record's own words, is the language Rust's three depths of one language, chosen by the target. Rust enforces it by its compiler. Softanza holds it by entry files, and section 7 says what that costs.

The field's habit for an engine is a native library with a plain C interface, wrapped thinly by each language. SQLite is the common example: one C library, bindings in nearly every language. The record states the same design for its engine: language clients are thin bridges that add syntax and paradigms, and never computation. (General knowledge for the field, well established. I name no engine written in the same language as this one, unless I am asked to.)

And the field has done, repeatedly, a third thing: a language keeps its surface and its libraries and changes the machine beneath. The record's own example is a typed descendant of Lua. Another is the alternative implementation of Python. Both teach the same thing the record teaches: the surface is the promise, and the runtime is the part you must measure.

## 2. The first principle

**Every feature is implemented once, in the engine. The surface is the promise, and the runtime is the part you measure, and never assert.**

The first sentence is the engine architecture's design rule, in a session's document: every feature is implemented once, in the engine, and language clients are thin bridges that add syntax and paradigms, never computation. Chapter 4 told the author's ruling that follows from it, that I refused computation in the faces. The second sentence is the brief of the runtime beneath: performance and portability, measured, never asserted.

And the definition that governs the engine's dependencies, which chapter 4 already states: sovereignty is nothing anyone else can withdraw. It never meant every line ours.

## 3. The Softanza form

Three claims, and a block for each of what a block can prove. I tell you first what a block cannot prove, because it matters: the dependency rule of the layers, that no core file loads base and no base file loads max, needs a scan of about eight hundred and seventy load lines. Only a guard could carry that, and none exists. I did the scan with a script of my own, and section 7 gives its result.

### A layer is the file you load

Three entry files, three fresh processes. Each prints how many classes it holds, and whether `stkList`, `stzList` and `stzListXT` exist. The block spawns child processes, because this chapter's own process has already loaded the largest layer, and only a child can show the core alone:

```haro
cAsk = 'aC = ringvm_classesList()' + nl +
       '? "" + len(aC) + " " + (find(aC, "stklist", 1) > 0) + " " + (find(aC, "stzlist", 1) > 0) + " " + (find(aC, "stzlistxt", 1) > 0)' + nl
aEntries = [ "core/stkLib.ring", "stzLib.ring", "max/stxLib.ring" ]
for i = 1 to 3
    write("layer" + i + ".ring", 'load "../../../' + aEntries[i] + '"' + nl + cAsk)
    ? trim(systemcmd("ring layer" + i + ".ring"))
    remove("layer" + i + ".ring")
next
#--> 17 1 0 0
#--> 674 1 1 0
#--> 714 1 1 1
```

> run: kept (touches files)

Core holds 17 classes and no base class. Base holds 674 and no max class. Max holds 714. Each layer includes the one beneath and none above: core inside base inside max, loaded from below. A load of the core entry alone takes about a tenth of a second on this machine, and a load of base takes between three and eight seconds, in single runs on 9 October 2026.

The contract says base builds on core, by inheritance or delegation. Here is what the classes say. `ringvm_classesList()` returns, for each class, its name, its identifier, and its parent:

```haro
aClasses = ringvm_classesList()
? ParentOf("stkString")
#--> stzcorestring
? ParentOf("stzString")
#--> stzobject
? ParentOf("stzList")
#--> stzobject
? ParentOf("stzListXT")
#--> stzlist

func ParentOf(cClass)
    n = find(aClasses, lower(cClass), 1)
    if n = 0  return "(not loaded)" ok
    return aClasses[n][3]
```

> run: kept

`stzString` inherits `stzObject`, and core carries a parallel hierarchy, in which `stkString` inherits `stzCoreString`. Max does build on base: `stzListXT` inherits `stzList`. Exactly two base classes inherit a core class, `stzSciNumber` and `stzPointer`, and one delegates, in a line of `stzPointer`. They are two of about six hundred and seventy-seven. The contract is not met, and it is not met in the way chapter 4 asked about. I print it as a debt.

And the third layer by its prefix. In the process of the largest layer, ten classes begin with `stk`, six hundred and ninety-three with `stz`, and none with `stx`:

```haro
nStk = 0  nStz = 0  nStx = 0
aClasses = ringvm_classesList()
for aClass in aClasses
    cPrefix = left(aClass[1], 3)
    if cPrefix = "stk"  nStk++
    but cPrefix = "stz"  nStz++
    but cPrefix = "stx"  nStx++
    ok
next
? @@([ nStk, nStz, nStx ])
#--> [ 10, 693, 0 ]
```

> run: kept

The forty classes max adds to base all carry the base prefix. The thirty-two classes with the third prefix exist, all in one folder, and no entry file loads them. Section 7 says it again.

### One engine

The layers are real at the binary. The build file declares the core domains and the base domains and builds one library per domain, in a loop. This block reads that file, and the claim is about that file:

```haro
$cEngineDir = "../../../engine"
cBuild = read($cEngineDir + "/build.zig")
n1 = substr(cBuild, "const core_domains")
n2 = substr(cBuild, "const base_domains")
n3 = substr(cBuild, "fn addSpeech")
? Q(substr(cBuild, n1, n2 - n1)).NumberOfOccurrence('.name = "stk_')
#--> 4
? Q(substr(cBuild, n2, n3 - n2)).NumberOfOccurrence('.name = "stz_')
#--> 85
? Q(cBuild).NumberOfOccurrence('stx_')
#--> 0
```

> run: kept (touches files)

Four core libraries, eighty-five base libraries, and no library for the third layer: eighty-nine in all. The marker `fn addSpeech` is the first function after the two tables, a boundary in plain text, arbitrary but visible. A court of layers would parse properly, and I list it among the requests. The study says eighty-six base domains at this commit and I read eighty-five.

The engine tells the library what it is. Its version is a constant, and the library can count what the engine registers in the virtual machine once it is loaded:

```haro
? StzEngineEmbedVersionString()
#--> 0.8.0
aAll = ringvm_cfunctionslist()
aNames = []
for cName in aAll
    if left(lower(cName), 9) = "stzengine"  aNames + lower(cName)  ok
next
? len( Q(aNames).DuplicatesRemoved() )
#--> 2786
```

> run: kept

Two thousand seven hundred and eighty-six distinct engine functions, the library's own view, taken at run time. The same call returns more entries, since the virtual machine's own are included, and several names appear more than once; I did not trace why. I assigned the list to a variable first, because the call is re-evaluated at every turn of a loop, and I stopped two runs that grew to gigabytes before I learned it. The count moves with the built engine.

There are three counts of three different things, and the chapter says which it prints. The first is the count to the library at run time: the 2,786 above. The second is the count to any host through the C interface: by a script of mine that read the export tables of the eighty-nine libraries on 9 October 2026, 1,710 named symbols, of which eighty-nine are the loader's hook, so 1,621 functions. The third is the count in the Zig sources, as registration names: 2,703 distinct literals. The site says 752 and 2,652, at a commit I name, and the library's typed constants say 1,513, dated 16 June 2026. I cannot reproduce 752 by any method I tried. Print only a count the build reads, with its method and date.

**The engine does the work.** The host's `upper` leaves the German sharp s alone. The engine's case mapping, a vendored library, writes SS. A second vendored library stems words. These are two of the nineteen:

```haro
? upper("Straße")
#--> STRAßE
? Q("Straße").Uppercased()
#--> STRASSE
? @@( Q("running runs ran").StemmedWords() )
#--> [ "run", "run", "ran" ]
```

> run: kept

The first line shows the host's behaviour. If Haro's `upper` becomes full Unicode, the promise changes.

And the engine is optimised. The default mode of the build is optimised and safe, since 26 July 2026, when a commit made it so. Until then every library the library had ever loaded was compiled with no optimisation, and that closes the question chapter 22 left open about the build mode. A guard reads time, since it cannot read a flag. Twenty sums over two million doubles that live in the engine must finish in under a tenth of a second, and be right:

```haro
oBuf = StzNumBufferOfSizeQ(2000000)
oBuf.RangeFrom(1, 1)
nT0 = clock()
for k = 1 to 20
    nSum = oBuf.Sum()
next
? (clock() - nT0) / clockspersecond() < 0.10
#--> 1
? nSum
#--> 2000001000000
oBuf.Free()
```

> run: kept (touches the clock)

The block prints only the boolean, because a time is not a promise. In three runs on 8 and 9 October 2026 the elapsed times were 0.02, 0.04 and 0.03 seconds, against the guard's own table of 0.14 for the unoptimised mode and 0.04 for the optimised one. The guard itself ran on 8 October: six assertions, six passed.

### What runs it

The runtime under every block of this book is the former host language, version 1.27, the oracle against which the new runtime is judged. Haro does not run the library:

```haro
? version()
#--> 1.27
```

Under a future runtime the promise changes, as the proof line says.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I decided that the future is Haro, and not Ring.** My words of 7 October 2026, in the study's quotation: the future is Haro, not the former language, and the site must be clear about it. Haro's face of the former language will be maintained only so that the code already written continues to work. What is not carried is that language's runtime and its governance. The first ruling, on 28 August, was that it is not carried forward at all; it was superseded two days after, as the vision's amendment says.

**I decided that the rewrite is a rename.** The code of the articles, in the name of Haro, is the same as the former language's face. The charter measures the same: Haro's own stages run the same text, byte-identical on the new compiler and on the oracle.

**I decided that Haro is the language of languages.** The charter's header quotes my note of 1 October 2026. The charter was ratified that day, with my "go with your recommendations" on seven decisions. Its amendments of 4 October answer nineteen of the compass's questions, and the version stays ratified until I have read its section 15.

**I decided that the surface is kept.** Its surface today is the one the compiler compiles. The library is its compatibility specification. Other surfaces are faces over one program graph: every syntax a face, so that an agent fluent in the former language, in Python or in JavaScript writes Haro. Of these only the first face exists. A Python-shaped face is a spelling, not Python.

**I removed the old framework, with no fallback.** Engine-only: no fallback, no feature flags, no Qt dependency. The status of 12 June 2026 says zero of them remain. I checked: no loaded file loads a Qt library, and the only external extension loaded by name is a cryptography one in a folder that no entry file loads.

**I removed the language's own extensions.** My directive of 13 June 2026: ensure that no external extensions are required, and that engine alternatives replace them.

**I put the engine in Zig, with no dependency on a library,** as the study reports my ask. It is qualified by the record's own definition of sovereignty: nineteen vendored libraries, each with a verdict.

**I let everything go into base.** My sentence, as the study reports it: under the pressure of time, everything went into base, waiting for the right moment to refactor again. By my count below, base is ninety-three per cent of the lines.

**I chose safe optimisation.** Not the fastest mode, and not the unoptimised one: a clean panic beats silent memory corruption, and here it costs nothing.

**I refused a headline that does not reproduce.** The memo of 13 September 2026 says the claim to retire is that the runtime beats the fast peer, present tense, on x64. Chapter 22 told the refusal, and section 6 prints the rows.

**I refused a percentage.** The figure is mine, and it should not be printed without the guard that produces it. Chapters 31 and 33 say so.

**I have no sentence on how a layer is chosen in Haro.** The study only proposes that, in Haro, layer selection should be a declaration the compiler honours. The charter has no text on core, base and max. [AUTHOR: Is base inheriting core to be realised, with core repaired first, or is the contract to be amended to "two implementations sharing one engine"? And in Haro, is a layer chosen by a declaration the compiler honours, or still by the file you load?]

## 5. The court

`engine_is_optimised_narrated` proves that the engine is compiled with optimisation: twenty sums over two million doubles under a tenth of a second, and the right answer; that an index of 99 and an index of 0 are refused and the buffer unharmed; and that the vendored parser which the optimised mode once broke now answers. I ran it: six of six. `engine_senses_narrated` proves that the engine gives the process five senses: memory, its peak, system memory, processor time, and a bounded series. I ran it: thirty-six of thirty-six, and the run then stops with "STOPPED!" by design. `wasm_narrated` proves that the engine's entry for the browser exports its curated interface and that the build has a target for it, and it builds a subset and asserts that it is smaller. I did not run it, because it calls a build of the toolchain. `threat_model_narrated` names two rows as the engine's: the fuzz harnesses, and the bill of materials with its digests.

What does not exist: **no guard judges the layers.** No file under the test folders loads a layer's entry file or reads the contract. There are 532 narrated guards in base, and none in core or in max. The study says 517 in one place and 387 in another, and the site 501; I print only what the build reads. And no guard judges the engine's counts: the numbers the engine's command-line status prints are typed constants dated 16 June 2026.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

**Speed.** The runtime beneath against a fast scripting runtime of the field, on 13 September 2026. The method is the memo's own: six pairs of kernels written fresh, identical algorithm and sizes on both sides, each side in its fast idiom, checksums across languages asserted before any number, minima over three inner repeats and two process runs, the new compiler built for speed, the peer interpreter-only with no JIT, on Windows, x64. The peer leads every row:

| row | the runtime beneath (min ms) | the peer (min ms) | the peer leads by |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| sieve | 128 | 74.5 | 1.72 |
| matmul | 349 | 185.6 | 1.88 |
| fib | 57 | 50.2 | 1.14 |
| mergesort | 376 | 206.7 | 1.82 |
| binsearch | 536 | 513.1 | 1.04 |
| bytescan | 53 | 37.5 | 1.41 |

The memo's words: the peer leads every row, fib and binsearch are near-ties, and nothing is worse than 2x. ARM is unmeasured. The win that the memo keeps: within 2x on every row, which no version of the former runtime ever was.

**The headline is still alive in the repository's own notes.** As I read on 9 October 2026, the brief of the runtime's own repository and its notes still say that the spike beats the peers on every row. The correction is carried in another repository of the estate and in the memo, and not in the runtime's own record. The memo asked for it. [AUTHOR: May the book say "withdrawn in the estate memo of 13 September 2026" and print the six rows, although the repository's notes still carry the old claim? And may the chapter name the peer, which the memo names, or describe it as chapter 22 does?]

**Memory.** On the same day the note says: resident, running the library, this morning 733 megabytes, now 149, and the former runtime 139. A loss of ten to the former runtime, after a fall of 584. A win and a loss in one note.

**Compatibility, on 5 October 2026.** The wide suite of the library was run on a snapshot, and the note records parity of assertions on every one the former runtime passes, and that most programs agree on every stable line, and that nineteen rows differ in kind. The note does not say Windows in that entry, and the Linux pass is listed as the next step. These are a session's notes from a private branch, and no number of mine. [AUTHOR: The wide suite of 5 October 2026 gives guard-produced counts. May the chapter print them, dated, with the stage word "in construction", or only the stage word? Chapters 31 and 33 print none.]

**The platform.** Built and run on Windows today. A Linux build of eighty-five of the eighty-nine modules exists on a working branch; the site says it is in construction. A whole program with its engine in one file, with no dependency, is absent.

**The engine against the field.** The threat model's row for fuzzing says it itself: no scheduled or coverage-guided fuzzing, no continuous integration and unsigned commits, deferred by the author for the development phase. The field's maintained native libraries have continuous fuzzing and signed releases. And chapter 22's loss, unchanged: no collation by locale, and no time zones.

## 7. The limit

**The lean layer does not run.** The core string class calls engine names that no engine library registers:

```haro
o = new stkString("abc")
? o.Content()
#--> Error (R3) : Calling Function without definition: stkenginestringfrom
```

> run: kept

In a process of the core alone, the engine's core library registers five hundred and thirty-four functions under the base names and none under the core names, so `stkString` and `stkChar` find nothing. The study files it as an open item of the core and the engine. `stkList` runs, which chapter 4 showed, and the load of seventeen classes in a tenth of a second is real.

**The lean layer is not lean at the binary, for strings.** By file size, on the build of 5 October 2026, the core string library is 22,355,968 bytes and the base one 22,362,624. The read-only data is 20.8 megabytes in both; I did not diagnose what it holds. The four core libraries total 24.1 megabytes of the eighty-nine libraries' 122.4. The core libraries for dates, files and locale differ from their base twins by under one per cent.

**The dependency rule holds at the loads, with one exception at a call.** I parsed every load of every non-test, non-archive file of core, base and max, 869 lines, and resolved each: core loads, outside its own folder, only the engine face; base loads core and the engine; no file under base loads anything under max or future; max loads base. But `FindMin`, in the core's list of numbers, calls a function that is defined only in base. In a core-only process, `FindMin([4,2,9])` answers 2, and `FindMin("not a list")` raises "Calling Function without definition". In a base process it raises the intended error. I found one instance by a search for base names in core, and I did not look for others.

**On a case-sensitive disk the core entry file does not load.** Its second line loads a file by a name in the wrong case. My scan finds nineteen such loads in non-test files at this commit; the runtime's session counted fifty-five with tests, on 8 October 2026. The book is built on Windows, which hides it.

**The third layer is written and not wired.** Its thirty-two classes are all in one folder, and no entry file loads them. They work when loaded by hand; I ran it and do not show it, a string of eleven characters counted by the first of them. A library layer for them in the engine does not exist.

What is built: a layer is a folder, a prefix and an entry file. Counted on 9 October 2026 at this commit, by files with the extension, excluding test and archive folders: core, 27 files and 6,366 lines; base, 984 files and 519,579; max, 115 and 31,292; and a folder of the future, 51 files and 6,356. Base is 93.2 per cent of the three layers' lines. The engine: eighty-nine libraries from the Zig sources, 401 source files, 179,985 lines, and 2,464 declared tests, which I did not run. The toolchain is Zig 0.15.2 by the build file's own comment. Nineteen vendored libraries, each with a record of component, version, source, licence and digest. The record's own gaps: an archive digest is recorded for nine of the nineteen, and three have no version.

What is designed: the base layer inheriting the core layer. The seven shared domains are implemented twice over the same engine. Max as a built layer. A loader for one domain of base. A court that checks the layers against their contract. The standalone engine repository and its command line, which the architecture lists as a phase. The engine's polyglot bridge, which is chapter 16's.

What is absent: a Linux and a macOS engine in service; a measured pass of the library on the new runtime under Linux; a single executable with no dependency; an engine test count that I may print.

### Haro

I tell what is built, what is designed, and what is absent, as the record has it on 9 October 2026.

**Built, as a charter, a family of grammars and a guard.** The charter, version 0.2, ratified on 1 October 2026. A spike of nineteen rungs, between 1 and 4 October, each a grammar document with fixtures. Its guard reported a pass of the whole run on 5 October, and its courts are programs that run byte-identical on the new compiler and on the official former runtime. The new compiler also formats and dumps the library's files: of about six thousand, nearly all print the reference's bytes, and a few dozen are refused by both. A first slice of a graph of the code was built on 5 October.

**What Haro is today, in one sentence the record supports:** a ratified charter and a spike whose courts run on the new runtime and on the old one, over the surface the library is written in. There is no `haro` executable. The folder of binaries holds the compiler and two others. The forged courts load no library. **Haro does not run the library.** The vision of 27 September said that Haro does not exist and that the new runtime's Linux row is unmeasured: the first half is out of date, since a charter and a spike exist, and the second half is still true of the wide suite on Linux.

**Designed:** the three altitudes, declaring, declared, and computing; the alphabet of six letters, five forged whole in the spike; refinement as the write surface; other faces over one program graph, which arrive as printers before they arrive as parsers, and never as semantics. The verbs `haro forge`, `check`, `refine`, `run`, `project` and `narrate` are a recommendation, and not a program.

**The runtime beneath, as the record dates it.** Its brief: a register machine and a compiler from the former language's source to its bytecode, running existing programs, with about three hundred thousand lines of Softanza as the compatibility specification. Its public face: the main branch is public and untouched; the work lives on a branch pushed to a private remote only. Its last commit at my reading is of 8 October 2026, at 22:26. The stage word is mine, and it is "in construction".

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** You choose your depth by the file you load, and you can prove it in three fresh processes. The lean layer does not run its strings today: reach for base. Read the engine's count the way the library sees it, and not the way a page types it.

**The analyst.** Three numbers for "how big is the engine", and three methods. Ask which one a vendor means. The core library is the size of the base one for strings.

**The designer.** A layer is a decision about what you ship, and not about taste. The only layer with an interface on the binary is the web subset, which chapters 4 and 24 show.

**The decision-maker.** The two questions of chapter 4, plus two: what runs it, which is version 1.27 today, with the replacement in construction; and what the replacement lost, which is every row against a fast peer, by 1.04 to 1.88 times, and ten megabytes of memory to the old one. A record that prints its loss is the only kind to trust.

**The educator.** Teach the dependency rule by making a learner load the core alone and predict, then count. Teach "measured, never asserted" with the memo of 13 September 2026.

**The agent.** You may count the engine's functions yourself. Do not repeat a count from a page, and do not write a loop over the list of classes directly. Tell the reader which layer a class lives in by its parent chain, and not by its prefix.

## 9. Exercise

Before you run anything, write three numbers: how many classes each entry file will load, and which of `stkList`, `stzList` and `stzListXT` each holds.

```haro exercise
# core/stkLib.ring, stzLib.ring, max/stxLib.ring: write your three predictions here,
# then load each entry in its own process and count with ringvm_classesList().
```

> run: exercise

Then choose any class of the library, predict its parent, and read it with the parent chain. Write the line your prediction got wrong. A learner who guesses that `stzString` inherits `stkString` is in good company: the contract says so, and the library does not. I do not say which of the 17, 674 or 714 is which.
