# A restaurant in Lyon

Source: Chapter 28. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the constellation of worlds with its bonds and norms; the payments sandbox and the delivery gate that refuses a fake; the speed budget declared and judged; designed: the Lock as a rule of the library; the constellation written in the near-natural idiom the design describes; a speed expectation in the owner's own percentile; browser-side timing; absent: the restaurant's live payment adapter; a measurement of the budget on a real mobile network; the platform on the Softanza engine

Run: 5 of 5 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:26 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

Each lesson of this project has an ordinary home in the field, and the field keeps it outside the program.

A fixed scope for a cycle lives in a process. In Scrum the goal of a sprint does not change while it runs; in Shape Up, the time is fixed and the scope varies, and new ideas wait outside the cycle. Trying a service before paying for it lives in a vendor's test mode and in tools that stand in for an HTTP service. A performance budget lives in a continuous-integration check of a page's weight and speed, and the web's own measure of speed is a figure taken over real page loads, at the seventy-fifth percentile. The picture of a whole constellation of small applications lives in the super-app: one host that embeds a runtime and runs mini-applications inside it. (All of this is general knowledge, not a study of the record.)

All four are good practices, and in all four the program itself knows nothing of them.

## 2. The first principle

The project was a paid lesson, and the lesson is one sentence on the site: **deliver small, fast and useful, and let the foundations mature in parallel without ever blocking a delivery.** Its companion is this chapter's rule of evidence, taken from the same page: a lesson is listed only when a file of the library carries it.

## 3. The Softanza form

A restaurateur in Lyon owns a couscous restaurant, and with a few neighbours he wanted a platform for neighbourhood commerce. He is not a programmer, and the project's own notes say so. I am going to tell the project in order, with its dates, and then show what the library carries of each lesson.

It began with an offer, on 2 May 2024, for a mission of business analysis. What followed was two years, in the owner's own count, of research and reflection, and of what I called specification: founding analyses, a novel of narrations, a first product specified in full. It produced nothing he could use. In the middle of 2025 the project came close to ending, and a silence of six months followed. That is where I learned the rule I started this section with.

On 3 July 2026, two of us rehearsed the project: a scoping of his answers that I wrote with a session, and a campaign of twenty-two simulated tests, run by the sessions playing the owner on his first day. I say "simulated" every time, because the answers in that file are not his. The next day, 4 July, he answered a real scoping report in his own words. He reversed the order of the work. We had imagined a line that ran from the supplier to the restaurateur and ended with the customer. He turned it around: customer first, restaurateur next, supplier last. And he wrote that the speed of ordering was his first priority, non-negotiable. His great fear was friction at the first load, and the second fear, in the same report, was the calendar: none of the deadlines until then had been kept.

The project's answer to his fear is a document and a notebook. We called it the Lock. In July, a second scoping proposed it to him, clause by clause: a single amendment; every new idea goes into a notebook, read at the end of the cycle; one entry needs one written exit; a demonstration every two weeks and no date beyond the next cycle; and a definition of done. He accepted four clauses as written and adjusted the fifth by adding a criterion of his own: that other merchants and partners must be able to install the product alone. In the record I am the one who asked for the Lock first, as a rule on myself: that the change of course had to be the only change of course allowed. On 4 August 2026, the first amendment closed. Twelve decisions were sealed; three were asked again in plain words because he himself had flagged that he had not understood them, and the lesson written down is about how to ask: the moment a question speaks of milestones and phasing and cycles, the owner stops following, so the questions moved into his trade's language.

On 15 August the first demonstration was designed for him alone. The checklist is titled with his name and "alone": he opens the delivery on his own computer, scans one code with his phone and another with his tablet, places his own order from the phone, and watches it land on the tablet. If he gets stuck, a button prepares a message to paste into a messaging app. The criterion of success reads: he orders from his phone, and it lands on his tablet, without us. The notebook shows it happened: his remarks of that day were entered in it on the 17th. And on 19 August he spent a field day with two phones and photographs sent over a messaging app, measuring on a real phone what no one else had measured: a hotspot works with no card in it; the address of the gateway was stable across restarts; a phone is a Wi-Fi client or a hotspot, never both; a phone's system does not return by itself to a network with no internet. The project's notes then call him its co-architect. The record ends on 25 August 2026.

That is the story. Here is what the library carries of it.

**The Lock.** I have to be exact here, because the Forged page of the site holds a rule I made: a lesson is listed only when a file of the library carries it. No file carries the Lock. It was a pact and a notebook, kept in documents. What the library can do is give you the nearest declaration of it: a rule over the worlds of a cycle, that a world without a written admission is a finding. This block is a construction, not the restaurant's code:

```haro
oRule = new stzGraphRule("the-lock")
oRule.SetDomainQ("scope")
oRule.WhenQ("kind", "equals", "world")
oRule.WhenQ("admitted-by", "missing", "")
oRule.ThenViolationQ("a world entered the cycle with no written admission")
g = new stzGraph("cycle-1")
g.AddNode("customer-phone")
g.SetNodeProperty("customer-phone", "kind", "world")
g.SetNodeProperty("customer-phone", "admitted-by", "the opening scope")
g.AddRule(oRule)
? g.RulesAreSound()
#--> 1
g.AddNode("loyalty-club")
g.SetNodeProperty("loyalty-club", "kind", "world")
g.RulesReport()
#--> Rule report 'cycle-1': 1 finding(s), 1 error(s), 0 warning(s) -> UNSOUND (errors present)
#      [scope] 1 finding(s)
#        ERROR the-lock @ loyalty-club -- a world entered the cycle with no written admission
g.SetNodeProperty("loyalty-club", "admitted-by", "a notebook entry, and one exit written")
? g.RulesAreSound()
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

The idea of a loyalty club arrives, and the cycle is no longer sound. It becomes sound again when the idea carries its written admission, and the written exit that comes with it.

**Try payments without a subscription.** The project's rule was to test without committing: a payment institution's free sandbox, and the library's simulated services, and no subscription before a gate that the notes call the go-live gate. The library's side of it is a payments sandbox that is not agreeable. It behaves like a gateway: a decline is an answer, an outage is not, and a capture cannot be made twice. The amounts are integers in minor units, so 4200 is forty-two euros:

```haro
oPay = new stzPaymentsSandbox()
oPay.DeclineTokenQ("tok_chargeback")
? oPay.Authorize(4200, "tok_chargeback")[:status]
#--> declined
oPay.FailNextQ()
? oPay.Authorize(4200, "tok_visa")[:status]
#--> refused
aAuth = oPay.Authorize(4200, "tok_visa")
? aAuth[:status]
#--> authorized
oPay.Capture(aAuth[:id])
? oPay.Capture(aAuth[:id])[:why]
#--> this authorization was already captured
```

> run: kept

And the last door refuses to open while a fake is still bound, even for an entitled person. It opens when the binding is real. Here the live binding is an HTTP sandbox standing in for an adapter, as the guard does it: the registry holds only the name of the secret.

```haro
oReg = new stzServiceRegistry("shop")
oReg.BindSandbox(:payments, new stzPaymentsSandbox())
oDel = new stzDelivery("shop")
oDel.AddBackend("api", "linux")
oDel.UseServicesQ(oReg)
oDel.NeedsServiceInQ("api", [ :payments ])
oDel.DeployTo(StzDeploymentSiteQ("host1"), "api")
oDel.SetActor( HumanActor("owner") )
? oDel.Deploy(:Production).WasCommitted()
#--> 0
? oDel.Log().EntriesOfLevel(:error)[1][:message]
#--> REFUSED -- the external surface is not production-ready
? oDel.WhyServicesNotReady()
#--> sandbox-in-production: still bound to a SANDBOX in a production phase -- a fake must never ship
oDel.ServicesQ().BindLiveQ(:payments, new stzHttpSandbox(), "payments_key")
? oDel.ServicesAreProductionReady()
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

**The constellation.** When he reversed the order of the work, the project's architecture was redrawn as a constellation of five worlds on a shared ground: the customer's phone, the kitchen screen, the merchant's workshop, management, which is the first application kept and put to sleep, and the publisher's console. The shared ground is identity, the catalogue, orders, payments and the fleet. In the library, the last world in the order of work can be declared and not yet active; a call to it is refused with its reason, and it is revived when its turn comes:

```haro
oCon = new stzSuperApp("neighbourhood")
oCon.AddWorld("customer", StzAppQ("customer"))
oCon.AddWorld("kitchen", StzAppQ("kitchen"))
oCon.AddWorld("supplier", StzAppQ("supplier"))
oCon.Bond("customer", "kitchen", "send-order")
oCon.Bond("kitchen", "supplier", "order-produce")
oCon.GovDeclareRisk("send-order", 1)
oCon.GovDeclareRisk("order-produce", 2)
oCon.GovGrant("customer", "send-order")
oCon.GovGrant("kitchen", "order-produce")
oCon.GovSetAuthority("customer", :Delegated)
oCon.GovSetAuthority("kitchen", :Delegated)
oCon.Retire("supplier")
? oCon.CallAcross("customer", "kitchen", "send-order")
#--> 1
? oCon.CallAcross("kitchen", "supplier", "order-produce")
#--> 0
? oCon.Why()
#--> target world 'supplier' is not active.
oCon.Revive("supplier")
? oCon.CallAcross("kitchen", "supplier", "order-produce")
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

**The speed budget.** The owner's first priority was speed, so speed had to be a promise judged by something, not an aspiration. The budget has three numbers: the time to first display, the time to react to a tap, and the time for an order to reach the kitchen. The library declares them next to the code as expectations, judges them against what was measured, and a breach fails the same gate as a security finding. The bounds and the samples in this block are mine, to show the form; they are not the project's budget.

```haro
oMon = StzPerfMonitor("remote-control")
oMon.NewTimer("first.display.ms").Record(900)
oMon.NewTimer("tap.ms").Record(40)
oMon.NewTimer("order.to.kitchen.ms").Record(1800)
oSla = StzSla("speed-budget")
oSla.ExpectP95("first.display.ms").Under(1200)
oSla.ExpectP95("tap.ms").Under(80)
oSla.ExpectP95("order.to.kitchen.ms").Under(2500)
oSla.CheckAgainst(oMon)
oSla.Show()
#--> SLA speed-budget -- 3 expectation(s), last check: MET.
#      [MET] p95-first.display.ms-under-1200: measured 900 (must be <= 1200)
#      [MET] p95-tap.ms-under-80: measured 40 (must be <= 80)
#      [MET] p95-order.to.kitchen.ms-under-2500: measured 1800 (must be <= 2500)
oMon.MetricQ("order.to.kitchen.ms").Record(3100)
oSla.CheckAgainst(oMon)
oRep = new stzRuleReport("cycle-gate")
oRep.Ingest(oSla.Findings())
? oRep.IsSound()
#--> 0
? oSla.Findings()[1][:message]
#--> p95-order.to.kitchen.ms-under-2500: measured 3100 (must be <= 2500)
```

> run: kept

One more thing the project learned, and the library does not carry. The project's file of interface rules is forty-two pages of experience in a few lines: one clear action per screen; undo instead of confirm; a button that is refused is disabled in view, never hidden; and a test in which a simulated first day on an empty database found the gravest hole, that nothing could be created in production. Those rules were forged, in the project's own count, by eleven iterations and a campaign of twenty-two simulated tests with seventeen fixes. The library carries none of them as a check, so by the rule of this chapter they are the project's lesson and not the library's, and I tell them here without a block.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused the grand system in one piece.** After two years of rich specification, the project nearly ended. The estimate of 2025 stays the reference for the project's scope; it is never to be presented as today's calendar. What replaced it is the doctrine of this chapter: deliver small, fast and useful.

**I refused to throw the first application away.** When the owner reversed the order, the application already built was kept, dormant, until the first version of the new order was in production. The project's own words for it: nothing is thrown away.

**I refused silent drift.** The Lock exists because a project that has failed once drifts without anyone deciding to. A second change of course before the first version runs in production is refused, and so is an idea that enters the cycle without a written exit.

**I refused the agreeable fake.** A double that answers yes to everything produces a test that passes for the wrong reason. The sandbox declines when a gateway declines, refuses when a gateway is down, and refuses a second capture. And I refused a fixed fee before the go-live gate: no subscription to a payment institution before the owner's written go.

**I refused to credit the owner with what a simulation said.** The project carried a rule for years, that no action on its screens takes more than two gestures, and its documents and the site print it as part of the first amendment. Its earliest source is a line in the file of simulated answers of 3 July, written by me and a session. The project set it for itself, from its own simulation of his answers, and kept it. I tell it that way. What he did say, and what I take as his, is the priority on speed.

**I refused to say the library was born of his need.** The constellation design, the virtualization plan, the registry, the payments port and the performance judgment were all first committed after the reversal of 4 July, and all were asked for in July. The site says carefully that the project needed these things and that the library carries them. I will say no more than that. [AUTHOR: Did the owner's need give rise to these parts of the library, or were they already in your plan, and the project found them?]

## 5. The court

`06_stzsuperapp_narrated` proves the constellation: worlds joining, a call across worlds needing both worlds active, a declared bond and governance, and a retired world breaking its bonds and reviving. `service_delivery_narrated` proves that a production deployment by an entitled actor is refused while a fake is bound, with every reason logged, and that it opens once the binding is real. `payments_port_narrated` proves the sandbox's state machine: capture once, capture only an authorised charge, refund no more than captured, a decline is an answer and an outage is not. `judgment_narrated` proves an expectation declared in the operational vocabulary and judged against measured numbers, a breach as a finding that fails the gate, and an expectation on a number nobody measures as an error and never a silent pass. `graphrule_object_narrated` proves the rule as an object attached to a model, with the operators the Lock's construction uses.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in storage, in queries and in ecosystem. The library's own study of the performance field, written against the tools of early 2026, says it without softening: no time-series database, no query language for it, no dashboards, by design; export instead. What it claims against them is one thing: nothing in the field couples a budget to the codebase's own rule system.

The field leads in the browser. The restaurant's worlds run in the browsers of customers and the kitchen, and I found nothing in the library that measures a first display or a tap there. The library's monitor observes a server, and the SLA judges whatever numbers are recorded into a timer. The owner's measurement protocol, at the seventy-fifth percentile on a mid-range phone, cannot be declared exactly in the library's terms, which offer the fiftieth, the ninety-fifth and the ninety-ninth.

The field leads on real services. A double is not the service, and the plan says so: you still need at least one real run before production, and recorded responses drift. The real providers lead in the edge cases the sandbox does not model.

And the super-app hosts lead in installed base. The design names what the industry's super-app has, a store, a review, a native bridge, and replaces it; it does not claim users.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the constellation of worlds with its bonds and norm-gated crossings, retirement, revival, hot swap and nesting; the payments sandbox and the delivery gate; the speed budget declared and judged; the graph rules the Lock's construction uses.

What is designed: the Lock as a rule of the library, of which the record has no code; the near-natural idiom of the constellation, with the verbs the design names and the class does not have; the two-gesture rule and the other interface rules as checks; a seventy-fifth percentile expectation; browser-side timing.

What is absent: the restaurant's live payment adapter, which the library's charter calls a later task and the owner's decision; a measurement of the budget on a real mobile network, which the project's dashboard of 23 August postponed to the next cycle; and the state of the platform after 25 August 2026, where the record ends. Nothing in it says the first version reached production at the restaurant, passed the go-live gate, or took a real order from a real customer. [AUTHOR: What happened after 25 August 2026? And may the book print the owner's tightened speed thresholds and his measurement protocol, or only say that such a budget exists?]

And what runs today is ordinary. The worlds are single HTML files, one per world, with a vendored framework, no build step and no content network. The server is a Node program of a few hundred lines that uses five of Node's own modules, serves the worlds on the local network, and keeps one thing: a journal, chained by hashes, replayed at restart. The first management application has a phone wrapper around a web view. None of it runs on the Softanza engine. The library is the project's design reference and the origin, in the project's own account, of its service, delivery and performance planes. The constellation is, in the site's words, a design the library can run. The restaurant does not.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** You declare worlds and bonds and run a constellation in which a call to a world that is not yet active is refused with its reason. The restaurant's own code, however, is a web application.

**The analyst.** Rigour in numbers was the contract of the first application: a due date computed from the invoice date, a date required, a debt with no due date made visible.

**The designer.** Fifteen interface rules from one directive, the three-second test; and a refused button is disabled and visible, never hidden. [AUTHOR: Who held the phone in the eleven iterations, the owner or you? The project's files say the real user, and they say you; the sentence needs one answer.]

**The decision-maker.** The owner accepted the Lock in writing, tightened the speed budget himself, and kept the gate on fixed fees. The library's mirror is a deployment that an entitled person may commit and that is still refused over a fake.

**The educator.** The record supports only a small thing: the first application taught its user by doing, with guides whose words leave no doubt. There is no educator in this story, and I say so.

**The agent.** An agent can drive a production deployment and change nothing: the gate passes the services, nothing is committed, and the reason given is the actor and not the services. In the project, the agent is the session working beside me under the Lock, with a person deciding.

## 9. Exercise

Declare your own Lock for one cycle. Write the worlds of the cycle you are in, three of them, as a restaurant would: the customer's phone, the kitchen screen, and one more, each with the written reason it is in. Add a fourth, the idea you had this morning. Run the court. Change the declaration, not the court, until it says the cycle is sound.

```haro exercise
? YourGraph.RulesAreSound()
#--> 1
```

> run: exercise

The court will tell you the circumstance and the rule. It will not write the declaration. If you want a harder one, add a rule about the actions of your cycle, and find the action that costs a third gesture.


=====

# A bank in Niamey

Source: Chapter 29. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the organisation chart as a text a person can edit; the validators of the regional regulator and of segregation of duties; the seven regimes as one mechanism; a rule written as data; one report with one verdict; designed: the content that would make the other six regimes differ from the first; a rule file that judges, and not only loads; absent: the delivered product running on the library; a word from the bank's own people; an auditor's reading of a finding the library produced

Run: 6 of 6 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:27 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

A bank is judged on its structure as much as on its numbers. A regulator asks who reports to whom: whether the audit function is independent of the executives it audits, whether there is a board, whether a risk function exists, whether the person who operates a payment is the one who approves it. The field answers with documents: an organisation chart drawn in a slide, a policy written in a word processor, an audit that compares the two once a year.

The field also has a family of tools called governance, risk and compliance platforms, which keep registers of risks and controls and track findings through to closure. They record that a control exists and who owns it. They do not read the structure of the organisation and tell you that it is wrong. (General knowledge, not a study of the record.)

## 2. The first principle

**An organisation can be judged by its own chart, under a regulator's rules, by a program that reads the chart as data: the chart says what is, the rule says what must be, and the verdict names the rule and the place.**

## 3. The Softanza form

A commercial bank in Niamey, under the rules of the central bank of the West African monetary union, runs an organisation-assessment product I built. It is installed on the bank's own internal network, under licence, and the installation guide I delivered to the bank is the document that attests the reference. The product itself is a Python application: a small web server with a database. I say that first, because the lesson I am going to show you is in the library, and not inside that product.

The lesson is that an organisation chart is data a program can judge. It begins with a text that a person can edit, with no programming: positions, their titles, their departments, and who each one reports to. This is the chart of a small bank:

```haro
cChart = '
orgchart "bank"

positions
    board
        title: Board of Directors
        level: executive
        department: board
        reportsTo:

    ceo
        title: Chief Executive
        level: executive
        department: executive
        reportsTo: board

    risk
        title: Chief Risk Officer
        level: management
        department: risk
        reportsTo: board

    audit
        title: Internal Audit
        level: management
        department: audit
        reportsTo: ceo
'
oOrg = new stzOrgChart("bank")
oOrg.ImportStzOrg(cChart)
? oOrg.NodeCount()
#--> 4
? @@( oOrg.ValidateXT(:BCEAO)[:issues] )
#--> [ "BCEAO-002: Audit reports to non-board position" ]
? oOrg.IsValidXT(:BCEAO)
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

The regulator's rule reads the chart. Audit reports to the chief executive, and the verdict says so, with a rule code an auditor can look up. A chart is judged by more than one court at a time, each finding carries its own code, and the compliance report says compliant or not. Here a second rule, the segregation of duties, which says operations must not report to treasury:

```haro
oOrg.AddManagementPositionXT("treasury", "Treasury")
oOrg.SetPositionDepartment("treasury", "treasury")
oOrg.AddManagementPositionXT("ops", "Operations")
oOrg.SetPositionDepartment("ops", "operations")
oOrg.ReportsTo("treasury", "ceo")
oOrg.ReportsTo("ops", "treasury")
aV = oOrg.ValidateXT([ :BCEAO, :SOD ])
? aV[:validatorsFailed]
#--> 2
? aV[:results][1][:issues][1]
#--> BCEAO-002: Audit reports to non-board position
? aV[:results][2][:issues][1]
#--> SOD-001: Operations reports through Treasury
? oOrg.ComplianceReport()[:overallStatus]
#--> non-compliant
```

> run: kept

The library names seven regimes: the regional one, Basel III, SOX, PCI DSS, ISO 27001, GDPR and HIPAA. They share one mechanism. I will print what each holds, rather than write a sentence that says "Basel III is validated":

```haro
aBases = [ new stzBCEAORuleBase(), new stzBaselIIIRuleBase(), new stzSOXRuleBase(), new stzPCIDSSRuleBase(),
           new stzISO27001RuleBase(), new stzGDPRRuleBase(), new stzHIPAARuleBase() ]
for oBase in aBases
	? oBase.Name() + " : " + oBase.NumberOfRules()
next
#--> BCEAO : 4
#--> Basel III : 4
#--> SOX : 5
#--> PCI-DSS : 4
#--> ISO 27001 : 4
#--> GDPR : 4
#--> HIPAA : 4
```

> run: kept

Four rules every base carries: nobody reports to themselves, no reporting cycle, no orphan position, and a span of control. SOX adds one of its own, the separation of duties. The library's comment says it plainly: the regime classes differ by name today, and only one adds a rule of its own. I will come back to what that means.

A bank's own rule is data too. Take the rule that internal audit reports to the board. It is not code. It names the department it governs, the line it requires and the sentence it prints, and it is added to a regime's rule base. The report says which rule broke, and where:

```haro
oRule = new stzGraphRule("audit-reports-to-board")
oRule.SetDomainQ("orgchart")
oRule.WhenQ("department", "equals", "audit")
oRule.ThenQ("reportsTo", "equals", "board")
oRule.ThenViolationQ("internal audit must report to the board")
oBasel = new stzBaselIIIRuleBase()
oBasel.AddRule(oRule)
oRep = new stzRuleReport("bank")
oRep.Ingest(oOrg.CheckCompliance(oBasel))
oRep.Report()
#--> Rule report 'bank': 1 finding(s), 1 error(s), 0 warning(s) -> UNSOUND (errors present)
#      [orgchart] 1 finding(s)
#        ERROR audit-reports-to-board @ audit -- internal audit must report to the board
```

> run: kept

Then the verdict follows the chart. Two edits to two reporting lines, audit now to the board and operations to the chief executive, and the courts that said no say yes:

```haro
oOrg.ReportsTo("audit", "board")
oOrg.ReportsTo("ops", "ceo")
? oOrg.IsValidXT([ :BCEAO, :SOD ])
#--> 1
? oOrg.ComplianceReport()[:overallStatus]
#--> compliant
oRep = new stzRuleReport("bank")
oRep.Ingest(oOrg.CheckCompliance(oBasel))
oRep.Report()
#--> Rule report 'bank': 0 finding(s), 0 error(s), 0 warning(s) -> SOUND
? oRep.IsSound()
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

That is the move the narration of a virtual bank tells, from a non-compliant structure to a governed one, and the report is one gate over every court. And here is the limit of it, as a block. A court reads what the chart declares and no more. Leave the chief executive's department blank, and an audit function that reports to him passes; declare the department and the finding appears:

```haro
oOrg2 = new stzOrgChart("bank")
oOrg2.ImportStzOrg( substr(cChart, "department: executive", "department:") )
? oOrg2.IsValidXT(:BCEAO)
#--> 1
? oOrg2.ValidateXT(:Banking)[:status]
#--> pass
oOrg2.SetPositionDepartment("ceo", "executive")
? oOrg2.IsValidXT(:BCEAO)
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

The same block shows that the banking validator always passes: it is, in the library's own words, a placeholder for rules not written yet.

My first book, *Refinement-Oriented Programming*, tells the discipline of governing change through the story of a bank facing a regulator's letter. This chapter does not retell it. It tells what the library carries.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused to hide the structure in a database.** In the delivered product, an organisation chart is rows in two tables. In the library, it is a text that a person can read and edit, and the same text is the unit the court judges. A rule applied to a database row is a query; a rule applied to a text is something a person can read before it runs.

**I refused to claim more than the library holds.** The Forged page of the site uses the word "validator" for two different things. The regional regulator has a validator of three rules, and a rule base of four universal ones. Basel III has the rule base only, and so do the others. I printed the count, and the block above shows it.

**I refused to carry a regulator's text in a code I cannot source.** The codes BCEAO-001 to BCEAO-003 are the library's own; the record ties them to no article of the central bank's text. [AUTHOR: Which text of the central bank are BCEAO-001 to BCEAO-003 drawn from? The book should say, or say that they are the library's own.]

**I refused to tell what I do not know.** What the bank's own people declare, operate or say is not in the record. The installation guide is my document to the bank: installation steps, a licence file, a support contact. The Forged page keeps no figure and no person of a client. The record is silent on who at the bank declares the chart, how long the product has been installed, and how many people use it. I will invent none of it. [AUTHOR: Who at the bank declares the chart, the questionnaire or the rules, and in what sense is the delivered product "built with Softanza"? Chapter 1 says it is, and the record shows a Python application that calls nothing of the library.]

## 5. The court

`orgrule_narrated` proves the seven rule bases as one mechanism over the chart's graph, with the four universal rules and the separation of duties that SOX adds. `14_bceao_governance` proves the regional validator. `15_multiple_validators` proves the combined verdict, and `13_segregation_of_duties` the second court. `rulereport_narrated` proves the one report over every court. `36_import_from_stzorg` proves the text form of the chart.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in coverage. A governance and compliance platform holds a register of every regulation, every control and every finding, with workflows, owners and deadlines. The library holds one structural check of a regional regulator, one of segregation of duties, and a mechanism. For GDPR, HIPAA and the others, it holds the mechanism and the four universal rules, and nothing that is specific to them.

The field leads in actual use: a platform that a bank's auditors already know, and that a regulator has already seen.

Where I think this stands apart is that the chart is the data and the court is a rule over it. A chart is a text, a rule is data, a finding names the rule and the position, and the report is one verdict. The library's own comparison table for this feature is all wins, undated, with cost figures that cite nothing, and I will not print it.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the organisation chart as a model and as text; the validators of the regional regulator and of segregation of duties; the seven regimes as one mechanism with the SOX exemplar; a rule written as data; the one report with its verdict.

What is designed, or built in part:

- The content of the six other regimes. Each holds the same four universal rules.
- A rule file that judges. The library can load a file of rules in a `when` and `then` text, and the one in its test data loads two rules. After the load, on a chart with no board and an audit position under the chief executive, the report said sound. The format is admitted, and the rule is not enforced.
- A validator reads declared properties. It finds the board by the word "board" in a position title, and it needs the departments to be declared.
- The succession validator flags every filled position, by the library's own comment a known defect. The reorganisation simulation raises when it is asked to change a reporting line, also a known defect. The article that tells the simulation names a method that exists nowhere in the library.

What is absent:

- The delivered product running on the library. It is a Python application; a search of its code found no regional or Basel check and no call to the library.
- A word from the bank. The record is silent.
- A perceived verdict. No document says that an auditor or a supervisor read a finding that the library produced.
- A delivery date. The delivered files carry file-system times, and those are not a delivery date.

The web layer of the product was born in its own repository: its first commit is dated 4 February 2026, and the interface constitution's text was first committed on 8 February. The constitution's rules 117 to 124 were harvested from a banking-supervision product, in the record's words, and the record does not say that product is the bank's installation. I will not say that it is.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** The chart is a text, the rule is data, the court is a call. A finding names the rule and the position.

**The analyst.** You change a reporting line in a text and you re-run: the verdict follows the chart.

**The designer.** The report is one verdict over every court: sound or unsound, with each finding where it was found.

**The decision-maker.** Ask which of the regulations the library holds, and ask for the count. Seven regimes, one with real content of its own and one that adds a rule; the rest carry the universal four.

**The educator.** The narration of a virtual bank tells the move from a non-compliant structure to a governed one, and the book shows the move with calls that run.

**The agent.** You may read a chart and a report. A rule you add is data a person can read, and the court judges the chart with it.

## 9. Exercise

Draw the chart of an organisation you know as a text of positions, titles, departments and reporting lines. Before you run anything, write what you expect the regional rule, then segregation of duties, to say of it. Then write a rule of your own as data, one that governs one department, and say what the report will print.

Run it. Then leave one department blank and say which finding disappears, and why that is dangerous.

# An organisation in the field

Source: Chapter 30. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: a rule written as data on a model, changed and judged again; the governor that names a rule that governs nothing; a refusal that reaches a person with its context; authority set against risk; a decision kept with its authority at the time; offline as a capability a world must ask for; designed: the hub and its study; a rule language a local developer can learn; the study's promises that a rule is checked before it applies and can be undone; absent: the hub, built; a mechanism for work done offline, kept and sent later; a version history of a rule; a customer platform on the engine

Run: 7 of 7 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:27 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

The field has two practices that this project needed, and keeps them in separate places.

The first is the business rule that a business person can change. Rule engines and decision tables have existed for decades; the Decision Model and Notation, a standard of 2015, gives business analysts a table in which an approval ceiling is a number in a cell. The second is the application that keeps working with no network. Offline-first design is a practice of its own, with synchronising databases and, since an essay of 2019, a name for its philosophy, local-first software: the data is on your device, and the network is a convenience.

A third practice has come with the agents: a model proposes, and a person approves. In the field it is a button.

## 2. The first principle

The organisation's own study of the project says it in one sentence, and I take it as the principle of this chapter: **the agent proposes, and a person decides.**

## 3. The Softanza form

An organisation works in Niger, with a head office in Niamey and bases in the field. Its own specification preferred a low-code platform built on a large vendor's office suite. My offer of 5 August 2026 was the one retained, for a reason chapter 2 gave: ownership. It owns its vocabulary and its rules; it changes them without programming; it pays no licence per seat; and it can hand everything to a developer of its own country. We built the design study together, with its teams, from 22 September to 8 October 2026. I say "design study" and not "delivery" every time, because the hub is not built. The study's version 1.2 is submitted to the executive director for validation, and the record does not say it was validated.

Four things in that study the library carries. I will show them in the order the organisation would meet them, and then the one it asked for that the library does not carry.

**A rule is data.** An approval ceiling is a sentence with a number in it, attached to the model of the advances it governs. The model judges itself, and says how many subjects the rule looks at. The data below are invented for this chapter, four advances with an amount and a count of approvals; they are not the organisation's figures, and the numbers five hundred and two hundred are mine, not its thresholds.

```haro
oG = new stzGraph("advances")
oG.AddNodeXTT("a0", "A0", [ :amount = 120, :approvals = 1 ])
oG.AddNodeXTT("a1", "A1", [ :amount = 250, :approvals = 1 ])
oG.AddNodeXTT("a2", "A2", [ :amount = 600, :approvals = 1 ])
oG.AddNodeXTT("a3", "A3", [ :amount = 900, :approvals = 2 ])
oRule = new stzGraphRule("second-approval")
oRule.SetDomainQ("advances")
oRule.SetMessageQ("above the ceiling an advance needs two approvals")
oRule.WhenQ("amount", "greaterthan", 500)
oRule.ThenQ("approvals", "greaterthan", 1)
oRule.ThenViolationQ("two approvals needed")
oG.AddRule(oRule)
oG.RulesReport()
#--> Rule report 'advances': 1 finding(s), 1 error(s), 0 warning(s) -> UNSOUND (errors present)
#      [advances] 1 finding(s)
#        ERROR second-approval @ a2 -- two approvals needed
? len(oRule.SubjectsIn(oG))
#--> 2
```

> run: kept

The ceiling is changed, and the same model is judged again. A change of the rule is a change of its values, and the verdict and its scope follow:

```haro
oG.ClearAttachedRules()
oRule = new stzGraphRule("second-approval")
oRule.SetDomainQ("advances")
oRule.SetMessageQ("above the ceiling an advance needs two approvals")
oRule.WhenQ("amount", "greaterthan", 200)
oRule.ThenQ("approvals", "greaterthan", 1)
oRule.ThenViolationQ("two approvals needed")
oG.AddRule(oRule)
oG.RulesReport()
#--> Rule report 'advances': 2 finding(s), 2 error(s), 0 warning(s) -> UNSOUND (errors present)
#      [advances] 2 finding(s)
#        ERROR second-approval @ a1 -- two approvals needed
#        ERROR second-approval @ a2 -- two approvals needed
? len(oRule.SubjectsIn(oG))
#--> 3
```

> run: kept

I have to be honest about what this block shows. The "change" is a rule rebuilt in code with another number. That the organisation's own person changes it like filling in a form is the study's promise, and I did not find a file of the library that makes it so; section 7 says what does and does not exist.

What the library does carry is the answer to the worst thing that can go wrong when a non-programmer edits a rule: a rule that quietly governs nothing. Misspell a property, and the rule finds nothing, and the model reports that it is sound. A governor, which asks rules what they govern and not what they say, names the dead rule:

```haro
oTypo = new stzGraphRule("second-approval-typo")
oTypo.SetMessageQ("above the ceiling an advance needs two approvals")
oTypo.WhenQ("amont", "greaterthan", 200)
oTypo.ThenQ("approvals", "greaterthan", 1)
oTypo.ThenViolationQ("two approvals needed")
? len(oTypo.Check(oG))
#--> 0
oWatch = StzRuleGovernance("hub")
oWatch.AddRule(oTypo)
oWatch.AddCase("advances", oG)
aF = oWatch.CheckRules()
? aF[1][:rule]
#--> scope_empty
? aF[1][:message]
#--> governs nothing in 1 pictures -- a dead rule and a misspelt scope predicate both report as passing
oWatch2 = StzRuleGovernance("hub")
oWatch2.AddRule(oRule)
oWatch2.AddCase("advances", oG)
? len(oWatch2.CheckRules())
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

A dead rule and an all-clear produce identical output: silence. The court has to tell "nothing was wrong" from "nothing was looked at". The word "pictures" in the message is the governor's origin: it was built for the graph plane's drawings first, and I would say "cases".

**A person sees the refusal.** When an answer breaks a law the organisation declared, it is refused with its reason, and the refusal is kept as a checkpoint for a person to see, with its context. Chapter 17 showed the asking half of this machine. Here is the refusing half: a request needs one approval, the law says a request has one approver, and a second approver is refused:

```haro
oKB = new stzKnowledgeGraph("requests")
oKB.AddConversationQ("review").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireOne("request-7", "approved-by"))
? oKB.AskIn("review")
#--> What does 'request-7' have for 'approved-by'?  (why: 'request-7' needs 'approved-by')
oKB.ConstrainRelation("approved-by", :Unique)
oKB.KnowRelation("request-7", "approved-by", "approver-1")
aR = oKB.ReplyIn("review", "approver-2")
? aR[:refused][1][2]
#--> 'request-7' already bears 'approved-by' (to 'approver-1'); the relation is :Unique
aCp = oKB.ConversationQ("review").Checkpoints()
? len(aCp)
#--> 1
? aCp[1][:attempted]
#--> approver-2
? aCp[1][:why]
#--> 'request-7' already bears 'approved-by' (to 'approver-1'); the relation is :Unique
```

> run: kept

**Authority is not permission.** The assistant of this organisation may prepare a file. It may hold the permission to release an advance and still be refused, because its authority does not cover the act's risk. A person with the authority is allowed. The verdict says why, in either case:

```haro
oGov = new stzGovernance("hub")
oGov.DeclareRisk("prepare-file", 1)
oGov.DeclareRisk("release-advance", 3)
oGov.GrantPermission("assistant", "prepare-file")
oGov.GrantPermission("assistant", "release-advance")
oGov.SetAuthority("assistant", :Advisory)
oGov.GrantPermission("approver-1", "release-advance")
oGov.SetAuthority("approver-1", :Autonomous)
? oGov.MayProceed("assistant", "prepare-file")
#--> 1
? oGov.MayProceed("assistant", "release-advance")
#--> 0
? oGov.Why()
#--> refused: 'assistant' holds 'advisory' authority (level 1) but 'release-advance' is risk tier 3 (SHOULD does not cover it)
? oGov.MayProceed("approver-1", "release-advance")
#--> 1
? oGov.Why()
#--> allowed: permission held AND 'autonomous' authority (level 3) covers risk tier 3
```

> run: kept

The library knows an actor only by a name and an authority level. Whether an actor is a person or an assistant is what its level is declared to be, and binding a level to a signed-in person is the work of the authentication plane, which I have not read for this chapter.

**A decision keeps its authority at the time.** Each decision records its reason, its actor, the act, the authority and the risk of that moment. A new decision supersedes the old one without erasing it:

```haro
nAt = 1785700000000
oGov.RecordDecisionAt("c-1", "ceiling raised for the harvest season", "approver-1", "release-advance", nAt)
oGov.RecordDecisionAt("c-1", "ceiling restored, the season is over", "approver-1", "release-advance", nAt + 3600000)
? oGov.LineageOf("c-1")[:rationale]
#--> ceiling restored, the season is over
? len(oGov.LineageHistoryOf("c-1"))
#--> 2
? oGov.LineageHistoryOf("c-1")[1][:rationale]
#--> ceiling raised for the harvest season
? oGov.LineageOf("c-1")[:authorityAtTime]
#--> autonomous
```

> run: kept

The instant is a fixed number, so the clock is not read. This keeps the decision answerable. It is not a version store for the rule itself, and I will not pretend it is.

And the one the organisation asked for first. Its default option is to work offline: bases in the field with no network, entering what they do and sending it later. The library knows offline as a declared, governed capability of a world, asked for and granted or refused like the camera or payments. That is all it knows:

```haro
oApp = StzAppQ("field-visits")
oPlat = StzPlatformQ("field")
oPlat.SetWorld(oApp)
oRegime = new stzGovernance("field-regime")
oRegime.GrantPermission("field-visits", "use-offline")
oRegime.SetAuthority("field-visits", :Advisory)
oPlat.SetGovernedBy(oRegime)
? oPlat.Granted(:offline)
#--> 0
? oPlat.Why()
#--> capability 'offline' was never requested via AddCapabilityQ().
oPlat.AddCapabilityQ(:offline).SetPurposes([ :enter_without_network ])
? oPlat.Granted(:offline)
#--> 1
? oPlat.Why()
#--> allowed: permission held AND 'advisory' authority (level 1) covers risk tier 1
```

> run: kept

Read this block as a limit and not as a win. Nothing in it queues anything. The library does not keep entries on a device, send them when the network comes back, or settle two people's edits.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused a per-seat platform as the organisation's ground.** The specification preferred low-code on a vendor's suite. The retained offer proposes Softanza for one reason above the others: the organisation owns its vocabulary and its rules, which it can change without programming, and the whole of the work, programs, configuration and data, is its own, with a handover to a local developer. The vendor's suite stays where it was good, for the user accounts, the documents and the notifications. [AUTHOR: Who chose, and what was weighed against low-code? The record has the outcome and the gains, listed without a ranking, and not the deliberation.]

**I refused to let the assistant decide.** It proposes. Its authority is advisory; a person's covers the risk. The refusal names the level, the risk tier, and the sentence that says the authority does not cover the act.

**I refused a silent rule.** A rule that governs nothing is reported. A rule with no message is reported too. That is the price of letting a non-programmer touch a rule, and I did not wait for the first incident to pay it.

**I refused to call offline a lesson.** The Forged page says it in four words: a gap, not a lesson. A restaurant asked for the same. The study is clear that offline is the organisation's default, and the library has only a permission for it. [AUTHOR: The organisation's study says its offline part "is already in service", and the Forged page says "a gap". The record of a sister package of the estate, a browser-side one, holds a durable outbox on the device and an ordered, idempotent replay, and I did not run it. May the book mention it, as outside the library and not run?]

## 5. The court

`graphruleset_narrated` proves a rule with a comparison and a requirement turned into an implication. `gg_rule_governance` proves that a rule that governs nothing is reported. `wisecoding_narrated`, its third scene, proves a refusal that is governed and checkpointed. `governance_narrated` proves that can is not should: permission held and authority lacking is a refusal. `governance_lineage_narrated` proves the decision kept with its authority at the time. `01_platform_narrated` proves the capability seam: the camera granted, payments refused, a capability never asked for refused with an honest reason. The guard does not itself use the capability for offline; the block above does, with the tier the platform declares for it.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in business rules for business people. Decision tables are a standard; a person edits a cell, not a program. Here the change is a rule rebuilt in code, and the file form that would carry a rule as text loads rules and judges nothing: it reads a rule's name, its kind, its severity, its function and its message, and does not read the `when` and `then` lines of its own sample.

The field leads in offline. Local-first software is a practice with synchronising databases and a decade of experience. The library has a permission.

The field leads in people. A decision table is a thing a trained analyst already knows how to read. And the organisation's own specification, which preferred the vendor's suite, had good reasons: its users already have accounts there.

## 7. The limit

What is built: a rule as data, changed and judged again; the governor that names a dead rule; the checkpoint; authority against risk; the lineage; offline as a capability.

What is designed: the hub and its study, whose schedule is in the future; a rule language that a local developer can learn, whose sample in the study is written in a notation that is not the library's; and the study's promises about rules: that they are checked before they apply, that their effect is shown before it is applied, that they can be undone and that their history is kept. The library has the pieces, the governor, the refusal of an unknown rule type or a missing file, and the refusal of a duplicate name.

What is absent: a version history of a rule with an undo: the lineage records the decision, and the rule is rebound; I found no store of rule versions, and my search was not exhaustive. The hub, built. Offline as a mechanism: no class in the library queues, synchronises or settles a conflict. And a customer platform on the Softanza engine.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** A rule is an object with a comparison and a requirement, attached to a model, and a governor tells you when it governs nothing.

**The analyst.** You change a rule's values, not its program, and the model is judged again with the new scope.

**The designer.** A refusal keeps its context for the person who will read it.

**The decision-maker.** Authority is not permission: ask which level covers which risk, and read the lineage of a decision.

**The educator.** An organisation's rule can be taught as a sentence with a number in it.

**The agent.** You prepare. A person decides. And you will be refused, with the level and the risk tier named.

## 9. Exercise

Pick one rule of an organisation you know that has a number in it, a ceiling, a delay, a count of approvals. Write it as a rule with a condition and a requirement, on four invented cases, two that satisfy it and two that do not. Before you run anything, write the report you expect. Then misspell one property of the condition on purpose, and say what the model reports, and what the governor reports.

Run it. The difference between those two answers is the chapter.

# What the projects did not prove

Source: Chapter 31. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the three lessons the library carries, each with a guard (a fake never ships; a chart judged under a regulator's rules; authority against risk, with its lineage); refusals that show an absence instead of hiding it; designed: the customers' declarations written as Softanza's own; the five-row guarantee sheet; a runtime that is the language's own running the library; the declared machine on a board; absent: any customer platform on the engine; a word from the bank; the restaurant's use after 25 August 2026; the hub; an offline mechanism in the library; a live payments run against the hub's sandbox; a named person's verdict on any of it

Run: 5 of 5 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:27 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

The field has three habits for saying what a piece of work has proved.

The first is the reference: a logo on a home page, a customer story of two pages, the sentence "trusted by". It proves that someone paid or signed. It does not say what they ran, for how long, or what they said.

The second is the readiness scale. NASA's technology readiness levels, later an international standard, name how far a technology has travelled from a laboratory to the place it was built for. The highest level is a system proven in operation. It is a vocabulary of stage, and this book uses one of its own (built, designed, absent).

The third is the limits section. An empirical study must state its threats to validity; a machine-learning model ships with a card whose last part is its caveats; an artifact submitted to a research conference is badged by what someone else did with it. This is the form closest to this chapter, and the one most often skipped when the reader is a buyer.

What the three have in common is that the claim and its proof travel apart. A page says "used by", and the proof is a trust in the page. (General knowledge, not a study of the record.)

## 2. The first principle

The record gives two sentences, and they are one. The site says: "Nothing is a reference until a document proves it." And, of what the author remembers: what "no file proves is not listed."

**A project proves exactly what a document, a file, a run and a named person reach, and the page says where they stop.** [AUTHOR: Is this the principle in your words, or the site's two sentences as they stand?]

## 3. The Softanza form

This chapter is the other half of chapters 28 to 30. Those told what three projects taught the library. This one says, project by project, what they did not prove. I did not write it after the fact, to be modest. The site carries the same page, with the same heading, and it was written before any reader asked.

The shape of the answer is a table of three columns for each project: the claim, the document or file that proves it, and what is absent. I leave a cell empty when I cannot name what fills it. And I will not fill the third column from memory.

Before the tables, five small blocks. Each is a place where the library shows an absence by refusing, instead of passing silently. They are the chapter's sentence, written as code.

**A dependency declared and never bound is an error.** The surface of a solution is declared before anything is bound. A declared service that nothing backs is counted as a finding, not a pass, and asking for it raises where the mistake is. This is the first unproved thing the restaurant's lesson cannot hide: no live payments service is bound anywhere in the record.

```haro
oReg = new stzServiceRegistry("shop")
oReg.DeclareMany([ :mail, :payments ])
? @@( oReg.UnboundServices() )
#--> [ "mail", "payments" ]
? oReg.Findings()[1][:invariant]
#--> unbound-service
? oReg.IsSound()
#--> 0
try
	oReg.Service(:payments)
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> stzServiceRegistry(shop): nothing is bound for service 'payments'. Bind it (or BindLive it) before use.
```

> run: kept

**What a rehearsal can claim, and what only the target can.** The platform model gives three verdicts for the same operation. *Refused*: the target forbids it. *Native*: the host can really do it. *Rehearsed*: the target allows it and the host lacks it, so the run proves only the plan. Reading a pin on a development machine is rehearsed and never native. The word depends on the machine this book was run on: a Windows PC has no such pin, and a board that has one would print `native` for the same line.

```haro
oSol = new stzPlatformProfile("kiosk")
oSol.DevelopedOn(:Windows)
oSol.WithServer(:backend, :LinuxServer)
oSol.WithApp(:firmware, :ESP32)
oFw = oSol.App(:firmware).System()
? oFw.ReadPin(4)
#--> rehearsed
try
	oFw.Spawn("worker")
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> scope 'esp32' (espidf) forbids 'process' -- spawn 'worker'. This target has no such capability.
oFw.Show()
#--> Scope: esp32 (espidf)
#      [rehearsed] read pin 4
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

The library's own comment above this code says the last step, flashing a board, "remains a planned external action".

**The live payments adapter exists, and refuses to guess.** The code for the live hub exists. It has never been run against the hub's sandbox. Given no credentials, it refuses before any network call and says what is missing. The host in this block does not exist, and nothing reaches it:

```haro
oAd = new stzPispiHttpAdapter()
oAd.WithParticipant("shop")
oAd.WithBaseUrl("https://pispi.example")
oAd.AsLive()
? oAd.IsSandbox()
#--> 0
try
	oAd.Request("GET", "/comptes", [], [])
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> stzPispiHttpAdapter: no credentials. Give it the store and an actor: WithSecretsFrom(oStore, oActor).
? oAd.TokensFetched()
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

The library has a script that tries the live run. I ran it once, with none of its variables set, for this chapter. It printed the word UNPERCEIVED, named the four variables that are missing, and said that nothing about the live adapter is perceived until a named person has watched a payment land. It ended without failure. The run was postponed, by me, for a reason that is not the library's: it needs the organisation's involvement. A run that could not happen is reported as unperceived. It is not reported as a pass or a failure.

**A feature that waits for a named person.** In the sound plane, the library holds as data the verdict of a person on a thing only a person can judge. While the verdict is UNPERCEIVED, the verb that would claim the result refuses and says why. Where a person has said yes, the verdict carries the date and their words:

```haro
oVoice = StzSoundFormantVoiceQ()
oVoice.Sing("la la la", "c4 e4 g4")
? oVoice.Refusals()
#--> 1
? oVoice.LastError()
#--> Sing is not available YET: the formant voice has not been heard by the author (sound_mu5_demo.ring plays it). SAPI was measured and does not hold a pitch (measured 2026-09-26: +-6 st asked, +-2.5 st given, up to 407 cents off, 286-575 cents of wander within a syllable -- it speaks, it cannot hold a note). Until the author calls the formant voice singing, Vowel and VowelsQ are what this voice honestly does
aVerdicts = StzSoundSingingVerdict()
? aVerdicts[:formant][:verdict]
#--> UNPERCEIVED
? aVerdicts[:retuned][:verdict]
#--> SINGING
? aVerdicts[:retuned][:date]
#--> 2026-09-27
? aVerdicts[:retuned][:said]
#--> the retuned voice is somehow singing, open Sing()
```

> run: kept

This is the form of the line chapter 33 says the customers' runs do not yet have: a date, a person, and a sentence. The record also carries the person's name beside it, and I do not print it here. (SAPI is the operating system's speech interface.)

**A pre-flight that says "feasible" without touching a host.** The deployment's pre-flight refuses a host that is too small. For a host whose provider is a cloud, it says feasible and "provision on aws", without any account. A feasibility is a plan, not a verification. The design file says it plainly: what remains is the verification against a real host or account, gated by infrastructure. The figures below are illustrative.

```haro
oBrain = new stzDelivery("shop")
oBrain.AddBackend(:api, :LinuxServer)
oBrain.RequiresIn(:api, StzResourcesQ().SetMemoryQ(2048).SetComputeQ(2).SetStorageQ(20))
oSmall = new stzDeploymentSite("tiny")
oSmall.SetKindQ(:Server).SetCapacityQ(StzResourcesQ().SetMemoryQ(512).SetComputeQ(1).SetStorageQ(5))
oTiny = new stzDeployment(oBrain)
oTiny.SetTarget(:api, oSmall)
? oTiny.Feasible()
#--> 0
? oTiny.Feasibility()[1][5]
#--> SHORTFALL -- host too small
oCloud = new stzDeploymentSite("pool")
oCloud.SetKindQ(:Server).SetProviderQ(:aws)
oPool = new stzDeployment(oBrain)
oPool.SetTarget(:api, oCloud)
? oPool.Feasible()
#--> 1
? oPool.Feasibility()[1][5]
#--> provision on aws
```

> run: kept

### What the study of the record did not measure

The study applies its own perception law to itself, which is why I trust the paragraph.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused the author's memory as evidence.** The site lists nothing that I remember and no file proves. The same rule bound the bank: I know the product, because I built it, and what the bank's own people did with it is not in a file.

**I refused to call the library the origin of what it only uses as an example.** The forged page says "example" where the library holds a project as its example and not as its origin. And I will not repeat a sentence in the study of 12.29 that says a lesson was "born of the client's need". The registry and the SLA were first committed after the reversal of 4 July, and the order of the commits makes "born of" unproved. What I can say is "needed" and "carries".

**I refused two wordings of my own.** In chapters 1, 2 and 33 I wrote that the bank "runs" a product "built with" Softanza, and that the restaurant "runs" a platform. The record shows a Python application that does not call the library, built beside it by the same person, and for the restaurant a first delivery and a field day, then silence after 25 August. The honest wording is *built by me, under the discipline the library now carries*, and *delivered and field-tested*. [AUTHOR: May I correct chapters 1, 2 and 33 to those wordings? Chapter 1 is your text, and I have changed none of it. And shall the block under "a date, a person, and a sentence" print the person's name too, as the record's form of "named"? That is yours to say.]

**I refused a stage word the repository does not carry.** The site's estate page says it: never called available before it is. A piece of work that reaches a person through a sense is perceived by a person, and until then it is unperceived, which is a state and not a failure.

**I refused to print a percentage.** I have said aloud how far the bridge runtime has come. The study ruled that the figure should not be printed without the guard that produces it. I agree.

## 5. The court

`service_registry_narrated` proves that a declared service nobody backs is an error finding, that an unbound service raises, and that a sandbox in production is a violation. `full_scope_model_narrated`, scenes 5 to 8, proves the three verdicts: refused, rehearsed, native. `payments_live_adapter_narrated` proves the adapter against a twin served over HTTP, an adapter without credentials that refuses to guess, and the scenario that says stage (b) is unperceived. It starts real server processes, and the book does not run it; it only counts its assertions. `sound_mu5_narrated` proves, in its fifth scene, that while the verdict is unperceived `Sing` returns nothing and says why. `deployment_narrated` proves that an undersized host is not feasible, and that an assistant rehearses and commits nothing; it writes a scratch folder beside itself, so the book does not run it either. `orgrule_narrated` and `governance_narrated` carry the other two lessons.

The court of this chapter's subject is not a guard. It is a line of the record, of the form the estate's law prescribes: a date, a person, and what they said. The library holds one such line in its data, in the sound plane. The estate holds one in its record, and chapter 33 tells it.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in production. Ordinary stacks run real workloads for real users, including, on the site's own page, the three platforms of this part.

The field leads in references at scale. The site prints two references and a design study, and quotes no number of countries and no number of developers. A buyer reading a logo wall sees more.

The field leads in live infrastructure. Provisioning tools drive real providers every day. The library's deployment stops short of a real host, and the fifth block shows where.

The field leads in hardware in the field. The declared machine boots and judges itself in an emulator, and the vision says no customer runs on it. Its guarantee text says four of four promised, three kept in the emulator; the fourth, surviving a cut, waits for a watchdog on a real board, and the board is on order.

The field leads in speed. The runtime Softanza measured itself against was faster on every row, by a factor between one and two, and both benchmarks were run on the former runtime.

The field leads in pictures judged by people. Forty-one pictures and text sections of the gallery were judged by a model: twenty right, nine wrong, twelve uncertain. The record says that no human has looked at them.

Where I think the record stands apart is that its limit is a page, with a heading, written before any reader asked. Every stage has a word. A feature can be built and still refuse until a named person has spoken. And a live run that could not happen is reported unperceived, never a failure and never a pass.

## 7. The limit

This whole chapter is a limit. Here is its sum in the stage words.

What is built: the three lessons that are proved. *A fake never ships*: the registry and the delivery gate. *An organisation is judged by its own chart under a regulator's rules*: the organisation chart and its validators. *Authority is not permission, and a decision keeps its authority at the time*: the governance object. Each has a file of the library that carries it, a guard that resolves, and a block already kept in a neighbouring chapter. The limit of each is in the tables: a sandbox is bound and no live payment adapter is; the bank's product does not call the chart validator; no hub exists and offline is a gap.

What is designed: the customers' declarations written as Softanza's own; the five-row guarantee sheet; the runtime that is the language's own, running the library; the declared machine on a board.

What is absent: any customer platform on the engine; a word from the bank; the restaurant's use after 25 August; the hub; an offline mechanism in the library; a live payments run against the hub's sandbox; a named person's verdict on any of it.

The sum of every absent cell is the one line chapter 33 says is still owed: *the solution ran on Softanza's own engine, and this is what the user said.* [AUTHOR: The site says the customers' declarations are being written. I found no declaration file in the three projects' repositories. Do they live in a repository I did not search, or are they still to be written? The chapter's word for them, designed or absent, depends on it.]

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Your demo is a rehearsal until a target has run it. Ask the scope whether the line was native, rehearsed or refused, and write the same word beside your own claims.

**The analyst.** When you cite a project, write three columns: the claim, the document, and what is absent. Do not fill the third from memory.

**The designer.** A feature that no person has seen or heard ships shut, and says so. The stage is a state of the design and not an apology.

**The decision-maker.** Read the stage word before the testimonial. "Built" is a statement about a guard. "Runs at a customer" is a statement about a document, and you can ask for it in writing, with a name beside it.

**The educator.** Teach the difference between a project that taught the library and a project that runs on it. The first is proved here, and the second is the line still owed. Unperceived is a state, not a failure.

**The agent.** You may read the tables and quote any cell. You may not turn an absent cell into a present one. If you do, the next reader of the record will find your sentence and the missing file side by side.

Declare the surface of one project of yours: a mail service, a payments service, and one more of your own. Before you run anything, write the line you expect under each of three questions: which services are unbound; whether the registry is sound; which are unbound once the mail service has a sandbox bound.

```haro exercise
oReg = new stzServiceRegistry("my-project")
oReg.DeclareMany([ :mail, :payments, :your_service ])
? @@( oReg.UnboundServices() )
#--> what you expect
? oReg.IsSound()
#--> what you expect

? @@( oReg.UnboundServices() )
#--> what you expect
```
