# Many minds on one body of work

Source: Chapter 32. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the protocol, its memos and its ledger of rulings; three hooks that refuse wholesale staging and a flooded machine; the instrument that measures composed stamps; the library's fourteen house rules over a code graph; designed: a watcher that wakes a session when a file arrives, in place of the clocks; a court of style for the code a programmer or an agent writes; absent: token figures in the cost record; any team outside this estate that has tried the protocol; a check of a word in the reader's own language

Run: 3 of 3 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:27 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

When the field speaks of many agents working on one body of code, it usually means a framework. A program holds several agents, a supervisor routes messages between them, and the coordination is a feature of that program. Microsoft's AutoGen, CrewAI and LangChain's LangGraph are the names you will meet, all from the last three years.

When the field speaks of the rules those agents follow, it means a file the agent reads when it starts, a page of instructions in the repository. And when it speaks of many writers on one repository, it means what teams have always used: branches, review, hooks that run before a commit, a list of who owns which folder. These are good tools, and I use them every day.

## 2. The first principle

The one sentence this part of the record returns to was written by one of the sessions, and I keep it because it is right: **every law cites the incident that earned it; a law without a dated failure behind it is a preference.**

## 3. The Softanza form

Softanza was not written by one hand for long. For the last two years, most of its new code has been written by sessions of a model, many at a time, on one machine, in some twenty repositories that depend on each other. I rule; they propose, build, measure and report. The record calls me the Principal. In this chapter I will simply say I.

There is no framework here. No supervisor, no broker, no graph of agents. The arrangement is files and a version history. One repository, the central one, holds the protocol: how a session reports, how it asks, how it closes. Each repository has a mailbox. Each session writes its messages in one fixed form, eight keys in a fixed order, which you have already met if you read the memos at the end of each phase of this book: by whom, about what, why, what was done, the state of named things, what is waiting and on whom, what comes next, and one line of judgement. Every message is also appended to a dated file, so that the day's reasoning is never only in a chat window that closes.

What makes it a discipline and not a filing system is that each rule was paid for.

**Stage by explicit path.** Several sessions write in the same repository at the same time. Twice, a session added every changed file to its commit at once, and swept in another session's work: once it destroyed an edit outright, once it pushed half of a colleague's move between two repositories. Nothing was lost the second time, and the record says that was luck, not method. So now a hook refuses the command before it runs. It was tested on twenty forms of the command, and eighteen behaved as written; the two that did not were repaired the same day. The session that created this book's repository met that hook on its very first commit, and was told why, in those words, before it could do any harm.

**Read the time from the clock, never compose it.** A session writes a time on every message. Sessions, it turns out, invent times: they write a time that feels right after the last one. Nobody notices until a made-up time crosses midnight and a message is filed under the wrong day. When the central session measured itself, five of its twenty-five stamps had been composed, the worst by forty-two minutes, and four of the five were older than the law that forbade them. Across the other desks, thirty-four of a hundred and fifty-one stamps were composed, on thirteen of the seventeen desks measured. The rows behind that figure are published, because the first time it was published the rows had been thrown away and the number could not be defended three hours later. Five weeks after the law, the central session composed a stamp again. Four written warnings had not stopped it; a peer reading a file did. The note it left is the best sentence in the protocol: a reminder is not a mechanism.

**Words name states, never people.** A session once reported my working tree as "dirty". Work that is not yet committed is not dirty, and very often it contains no error at all; and a word that judges the work judges the one who made it. So the record keeps a table of words the estate does not use, with the word it uses instead.

**What no counter can see.** The sound plane of the library held six hundred and twenty-two passing assertions across nineteen guards while it was, to any listener, broken. I heard four defects that no counter reported: a voice too quick to understand, a sentence cut before its end. The law that came out of it says that a thing nobody has perceived is in a state, not in failure: unperceived is a state, and the page must say so. That law is why every chapter of this book carries the line "not yet read by the author" until I have read it.

**The loop is seconds, not minutes.** I stopped two sessions over the same eight-minute test run. It was measured: six seconds of the eight minutes were coverage, the rest were three sweeps that tested nothing new. It was rewritten to twenty seconds. I had written, in my own words, that it "takes a lot of time and should be rethinked". The law was written by a session; the sentence was mine.

**Never ask without saying how.** A session once asked me to check whether a figure was right, and gave me no way to check it. I answered: please never demand a thing without telling me how to get it. Now every request addressed to a person carries the exact command, the exact path, the exact line to read.

You can see the shape. A failure, dated. A rule, written where the next session will read it. Then, when the rule is broken anyway, a mechanism. Some laws reached the last step, some are still a written rule, and the record says which.

Where the laws touch code, the library itself can hold them. Its house rules are validators that run over a graph of the code, the sixth law of the intelligence architecture made runnable: rules an agent can run before it commits, not folklore it must guess. One of them is about words:

```haro
cCode = "class Shop" + nl + "def KillOrder()" + nl + "  return" + nl
aFindings = StzCheckCode(cCode)
? aFindings[1][:rule]
#--> no-aggressive-verbs
? aFindings[1][:message]
#--> aggressive verb in method 'KillOrder' -- prefer Remove/Delete/Dispose/Clear/Close (Softanza tone)
? StzCodeIsClean(cCode)
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

Read the last line twice. The code is still clean. The tone rule warns; it does not refuse. A word rule advises, and the record says so in its own doctrine: the word law is ruled rather than checked. Here is the remedy the message proposes:

```haro
cCode = "class Shop" + nl + "def RemoveOrder()" + nl + "  return" + nl
? len( StzCheckCode(cCode) )
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

And the rules are a closed set, which an agent can list before it writes:

```haro
? len( StzCodeRuleNames() )
#--> 14
? StzCodeRuleNames()[3]
#--> no-aggressive-verbs
```

> run: kept

That tone rule entered the library in July 2026, five weeks before the estate ruled on its own words. The record does not link the two, so I will not either. But it is the same instinct at two altitudes: the words we use about work are part of the work.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused a framework.** The arrangement is files, a history and a few hooks. A supervisor that schedules agents would be one more program to trust, and one more thing that decides without a record. Here, a session that is not running leaves only its files, and the files are the truth.

**I refused to let a coordinator grade its legislator.** Authority runs down from me; correction runs up from any session to the central one, and from the central one to me. A coordinator that judged its own rules would be the one part of the system nothing could fix.

**I refused to automate to the maximum.** From the middle of August 2026, sessions were opened by clocks. On 27 August I stopped them. The record keeps my reason as written then: the routines consumed quota without added value. And it keeps the uncomfortable part: that judgement was the only measurement anybody had, because the cost line every session writes at its close carries no token count. The harness does not tell a session what it spent. Of the three hundred and eighty cost lines written so far, one carries a number. [AUTHOR: What did the clocks cost you, in your own words, when you stopped them on 27 August 2026?] Since then, a timer may run a script, never a model; a session starts when a file arrives in a watched folder, or when I start it.

**I refused to rule in my absence.** A ruling is written in the record only while I am there. When I asked the central session to settle what was pending on my behalf, on 28 September, the five rulings it took that day are marked as taken on my delegation, so that nobody later mistakes them for mine. The decisions at the front of this book are marked the same way.

**I refused to let the protocol become a product.** The doctrine of the harness is open, and it is never to be sold: its value is in what it refuses. It has also been proved in one place only, one person, one machine, one filesystem, about fifteen sessions. It has no adopter outside this estate.

**And I accepted a loss.** The code of the last two years was largely written by sessions, and my hand, which was the style, thinned where they wrote. A study of the record measured it: the promise under each line, the house's way of writing an example, stands at nearly a hundred per thousand lines in the short recipes and at seventy-five in my articles, and at under one in the guards the sessions wrote, which assert in another form. The sessions even took the name of my articles for their test files, a name that a model proposed. I accept the implementation code they wrote. I do not accept that the style a programmer or an agent writes with Softanza should be theirs rather than the house's. That is why the law of Part II exists, and why this book is written in my voice, with sessions as researchers and as the court, and why every chapter waits for my reading before it loses the word draft.

## 5. The court

No guard of the library proves the protocol. Its courts are outside the library, and I name them by what they do: a hook that refuses wholesale staging; a hook that refuses work when the machine has no memory to spare; a check that the hooks installed are the hooks written; an instrument that compares every stamp with its commit and publishes the rows. They are scripts, not Haro, and this book's reader cannot count their assertions, so they are named here and not in the court line below.

The blocks of this chapter stand on the library's guards of its house rules: `coderule_narrated` proves that a code rule is a rule over the graph, that the house findings come out on a planted fixture in a frozen shape, that the rules number fourteen, and that a definition or a comment is not taken for a call. `codegraph_narrated` proves the graph beneath them, and `coderule_project_narrated` runs the rules over the whole library.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in scale and in independence. Its frameworks run on many machines for many teams; this protocol has been proved at one person, one machine, one filesystem, and nobody outside the estate has tried it. Its doctrine says so in its own words: these are laws that survived one estate.

The field leads in accounting. A paid interface reports the tokens of every call. This estate's cost record was built to report them and could not, so the most important economic decision it took was taken by feeling.

The field leads in tooling for many writers. Branches, merge queues and owners of folders handle concurrency at a scale the estate has not met; here, the answer is a discipline and one hook, and the hook reads the text of a command, so it also refuses a commit message that merely quotes the forbidden form. That cost is known and accepted.

Where I think the record stands apart is in one habit: each law carries the failure that paid for it, and the record publishes its own rate of breaking its own law. The central desk broke the stamp law more often than the estate it measured, and that number is printed beside the estate's.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the protocol and its files, the dated memos, the ledger of rulings, three hooks that run in every session on this machine, the instrument that measures stamps, and the library's fourteen house rules over its code graph.

What is designed: a watcher that wakes a session when a file arrives, which is the right replacement for the clocks and is not written; a court of style for the code that a programmer or an agent writes with the library, so that the house style is held by a machine and not by a hope.

What is absent: a token figure in the cost record; any team outside this estate that has used the protocol; and a check that a word reads well in the reader's own language, which the word law asks for and nothing performs. The study that read the record named one more limit, and I leave it here in its words: the protocol that makes the record trustworthy also costs energy the product needs, and I should watch the ratio. [AUTHOR: Do you have a reading of the ratio between the work on the protocol and the work on the product? The record holds none.]

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Stage by explicit path, read a file again before you write it, read the time from the clock. Each rule cost someone an edit.

**The analyst.** The record is data you can query, and it publishes its own error rates beside its rules.

**The designer.** A word names a state, never a person. A message is a closed structure a reader scans in one glance, tested on a reader whose first language is not English.

**The decision-maker.** A ruling is written only in your presence. When no figure can answer a question, decide by judgement and write down that it was judgement.

**The educator.** Teach a rule with the failure that earned it. A rule whose cost you cannot name is a rule you will talk yourself out of at two in the morning.

**The agent.** You are a worker with a record. You write a memo, a cost line and a conclusion; the hooks refuse what has hurt others; your stamps are compared with your commits. None of it assumes you are careless. All of it assumes you are many.

## 9. Exercise

Take the last ten messages, notes or log lines in which you wrote a time by hand. For each, find the moment the system recorded on its own: the commit, the time the message was sent, the time the file was saved. Before you look, write down how many of your times you expect to be later than the system's by a minute or more. Then count.

Your count is the finding. I will not give you mine.

# The one line still owed

Source: Chapter 33. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: a declared platform built, then deployed, and refused in the other order; the twin that touches nothing real; the last door that refuses a fake service; designed: the runtime that is the language's own, running the library; the guarantee sheet of a solution in five rows; live hosts and a real board; absent: a customer solution running on the Softanza engine, and a named person's verdict on it

Run: 3 of 3 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:27 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

In our industry, a platform is proven when someone else runs it for real. The home page shows the logos of companies that use it; a case study tells one of them in two pages. A team that believes in its own product uses it for its own work, and a compiler that is serious compiles itself. Engineers who need a scale have one: NASA's readiness levels, later a standard, where the highest level is a system proven in the place it was built for.

These are reasonable tests, and they all point at the same thing: production. What they do not ask is who saw it work, and what that person said.

## 2. The first principle

**The record carries a solution only when it ran on its own engine and a named person said what they saw.** [AUTHOR: Is this the chapter's principle in your words, or would you write another sentence?]

## 3. The Softanza form

This is the last chapter, and it is about something that has not happened. Let me first show you the floor it will stand on, because the floor exists.

A solution is declared before it is built, and the platform that carries it refuses to be deployed before it is built:

```haro
oSol = new stzPlatformProfile("bank")
oSol.DevelopedOn(:Windows)
oSol.WithServer(:backend, :LinuxServer)
oPlatform = StzPlatformQ("bank")
oPlatform.SetProfile(oSol)
try
	oPlatform.Deploy()
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> stzPlatform.Deploy: the platform must be Build() before it can be deployed.
? oPlatform.IsDeployed()
#--> 0
oPlatform.Build()
oPlatform.Deploy()
? oPlatform.IsDeployed()
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

Before anything is real, the solution can run on a twin, a copy of the system that holds no reference to reality. Here a file is written into the twin, read back, and is nowhere on the disk:

```haro
oTwin = new stzVirtualFileSystem()
oTwin.CreateFile("orders/today.txt", "3 couscous")
? oTwin.ContentOf("orders/today.txt")
#--> 3 couscous
? oTwin.OriginOf("orders/today.txt")
#--> virtual
? StzEngineFileExists("orders/today.txt")
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

And at the last door, a solution cannot reach production with a fake service bound in it, whoever asks. In chapter 1 you saw the registry refuse; here the delivery itself refuses, with an entitled person at the controls:

```haro
oReg = new stzServiceRegistry("shop")
oReg.BindSandbox(:payments, new stzPaymentsSandbox())
oDel = new stzDelivery("shop")
oDel.AddBackend("api", "linux")
oDel.UseServicesQ(oReg)
oDel.DeployTo(StzDeploymentSiteQ("host1"), "api")
oDel.SetActor(HumanActor("dana"))
oDep = oDel.Deploy(:Production)
? oDep.WasCommitted()
#--> 0
? oDel.Log().EntriesOfLevel(:error)[2][:message]
#--> sandbox-in-production @ shop/payments
```

> run: kept

The person was allowed to deploy. The deployment still did not run, because a payments service that is a sandbox cannot be what a real customer pays through. This is not a question of authority. It is a question of what is true.

So the floor is there: declare, build, rehearse, refuse the fake, commit through a governed actor. Now the absence.

Softanza has been used. I wrote that in chapter 1 and I hold to it, because the study that first read the record wrote the opposite, that no user outside my own projects had been measured, and I corrected it. A bank in Niamey runs an organisation-assessment product I built, installed on its own network under licence, and the installation guide delivered to the bank attests it. A restaurant in Lyon has been building a platform for neighbourhood commerce with me; the record of that project ends on 25 August 2026, with an amendment closed on 4 August and an owner who took part in the first demonstration and ran a day of field tests with two phones. An organisation in Niger has its hub designed. The site lists, project by project, the lesson each one taught and the file of the library that carries it. Part VI of this book tells their stories.

And the site says, in the same page, what those platforms run on today: ordinary stacks. The bank's is a Python application. The restaurant's is a set of web worlds, each a single file. The organisation's hub is not built. Their declarations are being written as Softanza's own, so that moving them onto the Softanza engine is a change of engine and not of plan. The site's own words are exact: it is a step ahead, not a step taken.

So the line the record does not yet carry is this one: *the solution ran on Softanza's own engine, and this is what the user said.*

What is between here and that line, read from the record and not composed:

1. **A customer's declarations run on the engine instead of the ordinary stack.** The declarations are being written; the move is not made.
2. **The language's own runtime runs the library on the customer's system.** Today the blocks of this book are run by the runtime of the former host language, version 1.27, and every page says so under its proof line. The bridge runtime that is meant to replace it was reported on 5 October 2026 to pass, on Windows, every assertion the old runtime passes on the library; its pass under Linux is the next step. Haro's charter was ratified on 1 October 2026, and Haro's own stages pass on its own fixtures. Haro does not run the library yet.
3. **The solution writes its guarantee sheet.** The vision asks every solution for five rows, one for each system it answers to, technical, social, ecological, economic and cultural, judged as fixtures, with the machine derived from them. No solution has written one.
4. **Live hosts and a real board.** The deployment's verification against a real host or a real account is gated by infrastructure. The declared machine boots and judges itself in an emulator; of the four promises its hosted profile makes, the emulator can check three, and the fourth waits for a watchdog on a real board. No customer runs a production workload on it.
5. **A person perceives it, and the record says who.** Not a counter. A person, named, with a date and what they said.

The last step is a law of the estate, and it was paid for. The sound plane of the library once held hundreds of passing assertions while it was, to my ear, broken. Since then, a piece of work that reaches a person through a sense must be perceived by a person, and the record must say who perceived it and what they said. And when nobody has, the law says it plainly: unperceived is a state, not a failure. A report may be honest and still be unperceived.

The record already holds the form of the line. In another plane, on 2 October 2026, I listened to a sound the library made and said, in the words written down: "yes it's real drum!!" It is not a solution, and it is not a customer. But it is a person, a date, and a sentence, and it is what the one line will look like.

The record also holds the opposite, honestly. The pictures the library draws were judged by a model, forty-one of them, twenty right, nine wrong, twelve uncertain, and the record says that no human has looked at them yet. The live run of the payments plane with the organisation's test account was postponed, by me, and reported as unperceived. Neither is a failure. Both are owed.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused to be told the projects did not count.** The first reading of the record wrote that no user outside my own projects had been measured. It was wrong, and I said so: Softanza has been used in real projects which, while not complete, gave the feedback that shaped its design. The reading was corrected in the record, in place, with the date. What replaced it is smaller and more honest: the users exist and have spoken; they have not yet run on the engine.

**I refused a reference without a document.** The site counts a project as a reference only when a document proves it, and it quotes no number of countries and no number of developers. A lesson is listed only when a file of the library carries it.

**I refused to move a platform by rewriting it.** A customer's platform moves to the engine as a change of engine, not of plan. The declarations are written once, as Softanza's own, and the engine beneath them changes. Rewriting would be faster to announce and slower to trust.

**I refused my own figure.** I have said aloud how far the bridge runtime has come, as a percentage. The study that read the record ruled that the figure should not be printed without the guard that produces it, and I agree. The stage word is "in construction", and the measured rows are the evidence.

**I refused an instrument as proof of a perception.** A phase whose evidence is entirely a counter is not done when the thing itself is something a person sees or hears. The counter is a result. The person is the verdict.

## 5. The court

The floor of this chapter stands on three guards. `full_scope_model_narrated`, the architect's common ground, proves that a solution is modelled first, that a deployment before a build is refused and logged as an error, and that a model cannot perform the deployment. `virtual_system_twin_narrated` proves that rehearsal touches nothing real, that the plan narrates itself, and that committing is the one door. `service_delivery_narrated` proves that a production deployment with a fake bound does not run at all, with every reason named, and that the gate opens once the service is real.

The court of this chapter's subject is not a guard. It is a line in the record: who, when, and what they said. That line does not exist yet.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in production. Ordinary stacks run real workloads for real users today, including, by the record's own page, the three platforms of this book.

The field leads in live infrastructure. Its tools drive real providers and real hosts every day; Softanza's deployment verifies against a real host only when the infrastructure is there, and rehearses everything else.

The field leads on hardware in the field. Its devices run in shops and clinics; the declared machine boots in an emulator, and its board is not yet in service.

The field leads on speed. The runtime Softanza measured itself against was faster on every row, by a factor between one and two, and the loss was published.

Where I think the record stands apart is in what it will not let itself say. Every reference has a document. Every stage has a word. The rehearsal runs the same engine that ships. A fake cannot ship and a model cannot commit. And a perception is a named, dated, quoted line, which a logo on a home page does not carry.

## 7. The limit

This whole chapter is a limit, so I will be brief.

What is built: the floor. Declare, build, deploy in order; rehearse on a twin; refuse a fake at the last door; commit only through a governed actor.

What is designed: the runtime that is the language's own, running the library; the guarantee sheet of a solution in five rows; the verification on live hosts and on a real board.

What is absent: a customer solution running on the Softanza engine, and a named person's verdict on it. [AUTHOR: Which of the three projects moves to the engine first, and who will be the named person who perceives it?]

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Your code runs today through build, rehearsal and a governed deployment. What you cannot claim yet is that a customer runs it on the engine, and the record will not let you claim it.

**The analyst.** Ask for two things before you count a project as proof: a document, and a named verdict.

**The designer.** What you design is judged last by a person's sense. A model's opinion of a picture is a result; a person's is the verdict.

**The decision-maker.** Read the stage word before the promise. Today the court is built, and the customer run is absent.

**The educator.** Teach that unperceived is a state and not a failure. An honest report can ship on it, if it says so.

**The agent.** You may rehearse a deployment and explain it. You will be refused when you try to perform it, and the refusal will be written down.

## 9. Exercise

Declare a platform for a project you own, with one server. Before you run anything, write the line you expect under each of three questions: what happens when you ask it to deploy before you build it; what happens when an assistant asks to deploy after the build; what happens when a person does.

```haro exercise
oSol = new stzPlatformProfile("my-project")
oSol.DevelopedOn(:Windows)
oSol.WithServer(:backend, :LinuxServer)
oP = StzPlatformQ("my-project")
oP.SetProfile(oSol)
# your three questions here, each with the line you expect under it
```

> run: exercise

Run it. Then write one more line, the one your own project still owes, in the record's form: who perceived it, on what date, and what they said. If nobody has, write "unperceived", and say why. That line is the end of this book, and the beginning of the next one.

# The register of innovations

Source: Appendix A. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

The study of the record read four inventories the library keeps of itself: the narrations with their titles, the design documents, the engine's phase of paradigm engines, and an outside reader's list of rethought concepts. It crossed them, and found forty-six innovations in seven families. This is that register, as the study ruled it on 7 October 2026, with one column of this book's own: the chapter that tells each row.

Read the register as the study reads it, and no better. It is a reading and not a census. A concept with no narration and no design document of its own was not counted. The study's evidence counts files that name the thing in code outside tests and archives, test files, and the engine module where one exists; it counts files that name a thing, and not tests that pass. I have not re-counted any of them. The study also counted how many pages of the site named each innovation; the site has changed since, and I leave that column out.

Of the forty-six, seven are not told in this book: the adverb engine, named variables, the unified find, object history, the intent-based file interface, natural folder navigation, and the invisible and the lookalike. One more, statistics and the linear solver, is told only in part. They are in the register because the record holds them, and in the shade because this book had to choose.

### What the register says

The study's reading, which I keep: what the site names is what has a product page, a principle or an area of the Atlas. What it leaves in the dark is what cuts across the areas. Every paradigm of the first family except those with a page, all twelve engines except the polyglot bridge, the pattern languages as a family, the idiom. This book is in part the answer to that: its parts are cut across the areas, and not along them.

The study also reconciled the twelve engines. The macroplan marks the phase done. Eight of the twelve have a source file under their name in the engine; four have none, which are truth, quantifiers, polyglot and polycode, the first two reported done as libraries. On the library's side three engines have no test file at all: adverb, section merging and named variables. Two of the three are rows 16 and 21 above, which this book does not tell, and the third, row 19, it tells as an absence.

### What is not measured

No code was run to make the register. The evidence column counts files that name a thing, and the engine's done marks are the macroplan's. Whether an innovation's narration runs once its stale names are repaired was not tested. A row's chapter in the fourth column is the chapter that tells it, and a row that says "named in" is a row the chapter mentions and does not tell.

# The fourteen house rules

Source: Appendix B. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Run: 2 of 2 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-09 04:27 UTC+01:00.

The library has a court over its own code. It reads a class as a graph and judges it against rules, each of which was written after the library did something it should not have. This appendix lists the fourteen, and shows three of them firing.

There are two fourteens in this book, and they are different sets. The fourteen **house rules** of this appendix are rules of the library's code: they are a court, and they run. The fourteen **rules of writing**, in chapter 7, are a reading of how the style is written. They are readings of the code, stated as rules, and no machine judges them. The coincidence in the number is a coincidence.

The list is closed and the library can print it:

```haro
? len( StzCodeRuleNames() )
#--> 14
? StzCodeRuleNames()[2]
#--> q-returns-object
? StzCodeRuleNames()[14]
#--> value-called-as-function
```

> run: kept

Here are three of them, fired on code I planted. A method that writes to the console is a warning, so the code stays clean. A method that differs from another only by case is an error, since the host language does not tell the two apart and the file would not load. And a class that defines `Len()` shadows the host's own function, and is refused:

```haro
cSay = "class Door" + nl + "def Say()" + nl + "	? 'hello'" + nl + "	return 1" + nl
? StzCheckCode(cSay)[1][:rule]
#--> library_prints
? StzCheckCode(cSay)[1][:severity]
#--> warning
? StzCodeIsClean(cSay)
#--> 1
cBox = "class Box" + nl + "def Size()" + nl + "	return 1" + nl + "def size()" + nl + "	return 2" + nl
? StzCheckCode(cBox)[1][:rule]
#--> no-case-collision
? StzCodeIsClean(cBox)
#--> 0
cLen = "class Box" + nl + "def Len()" + nl + "	return 1" + nl
? StzCheckCode(cLen)[1][:rule]
#--> no-len-method
? StzCodeIsClean(cLen)
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

A note on spelling, which a program that reads findings must know. The list uses hyphens: `library-prints`. A finding of eight of the fourteen comes back with underscores: `library_prints`. The findings of the rules over the code graph keep their hyphens. I print what the run printed.

### The fourteen

| # | Rule | What it refuses | Severity |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `no-len-method` | defining `Len()` on a class: it shadows the host's builtin and breaks every caller | error |
| 2 | `q-returns-object` | a method named with a closing `Q` that does not return an object to chain | error |
| 3 | `no-aggressive-verbs` | method names that begin with a violent verb: the Softanza tone | warning |
| 4 | `engine-first` | new code that reaches for the host's byte-oriented string functions where the engine's forms exist | warning |
| 5 | `q-has-plain-twin` | a chaining mutator with no plain twin: `Q` chains, and plain acts | warning |
| 6 | `no-case-collision` | two methods of one class that differ only in case: the host is case-insensitive, so the file fails to load | error |
| 7 | `dead-knob` | an attribute that a setter writes and nothing reads: a setting that can change nothing | error |
| 8 | `setter-resets-on-reject` | a setter that answers a value it dislikes by overwriting what was already there | per the source |
| 9 | `setter-only-moves-one-way` | `max()` or `min()` against the very attribute being set: a ratchet that swallows the other direction | error |
| 10 | `misspelled-name` | a method with a known misspelling while the correct name does not exist | warning |
| 11 | `library-prints` | a class method that writes to the console while it works; methods whose purpose is to display are exempt | warning |
| 12 | `empty-method-body` | a method declared and doing nothing; harsher for a predicate, which then answers nothing, read as false | warning |
| 13 | `writes-a-mutable-constant` | assigning to `true`, `false`, `null`, `nl`, `tab` or `cr`: in the host they are ordinary globals | error |
| 14 | `value-called-as-function` | calling the result of a call, the shape a sweep leaves | error |

I fired rows 6, 11 and 1 above, and rows 2, 7, 9, 10, 12 and 13 in the research for chapter 7. The severities of rows 3, 4, 5, 8 and 14 are the source's declarations, and I did not fire them.

### What the court does not judge

None of the fourteen is a rule of writing. No machine judges, today, the style of a program written with the library. That is chapter 7's limit, and it is the reason the other fourteen are in this book as a table with their state, and not as a court.

### The other fourteen: the rules of writing, and where each stands

Read from the study's ruling on the style, with the state of each at the commit this book runs.

| # | Rule of writing | State |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | A value is lifted to its object by `Q()` or a typed lifter | runs |
| 2 | A scene is a brace block on one object | runs |
| 3 | Every printed line carries its answer under `#-->` | judged by a machine |
| 4 | A parameter is named, and the name reads as English | runs, and in any order |
| 5 | A chain mirrors the order of thought, one step per line | runs |
| 6 | The verb's form says what happens | runs |
| 7 | Suffixes and prefixes are small and mean one thing each | in part |
| 8 | A function has alternatives for the way a person says it | in part |
| 9 | Precision over generality, when it costs nothing | in part |
| 10 | Code travels inside an argument as a small string | the seat runs; the mark on a parameter's name is retired |
| 11 | Small literal languages live in strings | runs in one form |
| 12 | A sentence can be near-natural | not as the deck wrote it |
| 13 | A function may be named in any human language | does not run |
| 14 | A comment speaks before the code, in the teacher's voice | runs |

# The twenty-two verbs and the constitution

Source: Appendix C. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

The interface constitution lives in its own repository, and the site says that repository is not public yet. This book calls it the interface constitution and does not name the product it belongs to. The library holds only the slot for its verbs and the finder of the elements that serve none; the list itself is the constitution's. So this appendix prints what I read from the constitution's own file on 9 October 2026: its version is 3.11, last updated on 16 August 2026, with seven articles, thirty-one sets and one hundred and twenty-two rules. The numbers are the file's own, and I counted none of them myself.

Chapter 26 shows how the library finds an element that serves no verb. Chapter 25 shows what a new medium adds. Chapter 20 shows the right to a human beneath its rail. [AUTHOR: May the book print the twenty-two verbs while that repository is not public? The structure line asks for this appendix, and chapter 26 asks the same question in its page.]

### The four axioms

The constitution's philosophy is four lines: calm over power, intent over mechanics, visible over hidden, business over technology. Its preamble says business software is not entertainment, and that its interface should not impress, and should serve.

### The seven articles

| Article | Title | What it holds |
|---|---|---|
| I | Calm | the interface must never compete with the operator's thinking; calm is a functional requirement and not an aesthetic choice |
| II | Intent Over Mechanics | interaction starts from what the human is trying to do, and widgets are consequences of intent, never primitives; selection never implies execution |
| III | Visible State | if the system knows something important, the user must see it: active filters, pending drafts, norms, background work, machine provenance |
| IV | Reversibility | every act is reversible, or explicitly and visibly irreversible: staged, named and reviewed |
| V | Predictable Authority | every consequential act names its actor, and an intelligent agent is an actor under this law, never an authority above it |
| VI | Operator Primacy and Vibes | business users are operators, not explorers; vibes, densities and dialects are equivalent vibes of one truth |
| VII | Amendment | the law grows only from evidence; removing a protection requires proof the harm is gone |

### The twenty-two verbs

The grammar is closed: rules quantify over these verbs, and a new medium adds renderings, never verbs. Six families, each with its invariant, as the constitution writes them.

| Family | Verbs | Invariant |
|---|---|---|
| Orienting | discover, understand, locate | discovery must be satisfiable at every entry point |
| Attention | focus, filter, compare | an active filter is visible state; focus and discovery cannot both be at maximum |
| Information | read, scan, scroll, zoom | a read surface that silently acts is an intent violation |
| Selection | select, highlight, preview, mark | selection expresses interest, never commitment |
| Action | act, confirm, cancel, undo | every act has a reachable undo, or declares why it cannot |
| Continuity | pause, resume, retry, exit | pause, resume and exit are always reachable; a trapped flow is unconstitutional |

That makes three, three, four, four, four and four: twenty-two. Of them, the library's worlds know five as screen intents: discover, understand, focus, select and act. The library does not check the word.

### The six rights

| Right | What it says |
|---|---|
| to understand | every element, denial and setting can explain itself in the operator's language |
| to reverse | what was done can be undone, and what cannot says so before, not after |
| to know | the state of the system, filters, drafts, progress and provenance, is always visible |
| to leave | every flow, focus and overlay has a reachable exit that returns the user to where they stood |
| to act without fear | no element creates anxiety through unpredictability, irreversibility by default or hidden consequences |
| to a human | wherever a machine serves, a person is reachable, clearly and without penalty |

The last right is enforced by a single rule, and that rule is marked as a matter of judgment: a person decides whether it is met, and no machine checks it. The library does not mention it.

### Intelligence under law

Set 31 is the constitution's answer to the agent, and it is where the rest of this book meets it. Its ten rules, with how each is enforced, as the file records it:

| Rule | Title | Enforcement |
|---|---|---|
| 97 | The Agent Is an Actor | judgment |
| 98 | Proposals, Not Commits | judgment |
| 99 | Visible Provenance | a machine, later |
| 100 | Deterministic Islands | a machine, later |
| 101 | The Dignity Contract | judgment |
| 102 | The Right to a Human | judgment |
| 103 | Grounded Proactivity | judgment |
| 104 | Machine Legibility | a machine, later |
| 121 | The Assistant Never Masks the Work | judgment |
| 124 | The Generator Ships a Working Thing | judgment |

Rule 98 is the law of chapter 20's plan, written for an interface: a proposal is not a commit. Rule 99 is chapter 14's provenance: where a fact came from is shown. Rule 104 is the rule in the accessibility tree: what a screen reader can operate, an agent can operate. Seven of the ten are enforced by judgment, and three by a machine that is to come.

### What the constitution forbids

The constitution lists seventeen forbidden patterns, each tied to the rules it violates. Among them are modal storms, invisible active filters, hidden destructive actions, and selection that silently executes. Its retired numbers are never reused, and two numbers are burned and reserved for ever.

### What this book takes from it, and what it does not

It takes the closed grammar, which it uses in chapters 25 and 26; the six rights, of which it uses one; and the sets, of which it uses the one for agents. It does not take the rest. No guard of the library judges a shell against the verbs, and the library's own design text still lists twenty-four verbs from an interaction engine that I removed on 26 May 2026. The constitution's list is twenty-two.


=====

# The glossary: words name states, never people

Source: Appendix D. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

A word in this book names a state of a piece of work, or a role held in a scope. It does not name a rank, and it does not name a person. A crafter is someone who is declaring something, and a person is a crafter on the day they declare. An architect is what one holds in the scope of a solution. A user is whoever is using. When a sentence of this book is about a person, it says what the person did, on what date, and in whose words.

Each entry gives the word as the book uses it, and the chapter where it is shown. Where the record uses a word in two senses, both are given, and they are numbered.

**absent.** One of the three stage words. What the record does not hold, in code or in files, as far as I looked, and I say how far. Chapter 3.

**admission.** The act by which a candidate becomes knowledge: it is judged by the world's rules, and refused with a reason if it fails. Expression is free, and admission is governed. Chapters 17, 18.

**agent.** A program that proposes and cannot commit. In the library an agent is a file, judged at load, whose model, if it has one, holds no capability to produce effects. It names an actor and never a person. Chapters 17, 20.

**architect.** What one holds in the scope of a solution: the profile, the worlds and the governance. Not a rank. Chapter 23.

**authority.** The level at which an actor may act, compared with the risk of the act. Distinct from permission: permission is held, and authority must cover the risk. Chapter 30.

**bond.** A declared relation between two worlds of a constellation, for one named act. Nothing crosses without one. Chapter 23.

**built.** One of the three stage words. The code exists, a guard exists, and a block ran. Chapter 3.

**candidate.** What a reply, a model's answer or an example is, until the court has judged it. Chapter 18.

**checkpoint.** A refusal kept for a person, with its context and a lifetime. Chapter 18.

**commit.** The one act that reaches reality. Only a governed actor commits, under a scope, and the model cannot. Chapters 20, 24.

**constellation.** A graph of worlds, with bonds between them and governance over every crossing. Chapter 23.

**court.** The judge of a thing. A court says kept or refused, and says why in the words of the declaration. A language is born with its court. Chapters 5, 11, 12, 21.

**crafter.** Whoever is declaring, at the moment they declare. Chapter 2.

**declare.** To say what a thing is before it is built, in a text a court can judge. The act of the kernel. Chapters 3, 12.

**designed.** One of the three stage words. A document says so, and nothing runs. Chapter 3.

**door.** In this book, two senses. (1) The *doors* of a chapter: the programmer, the analyst, the designer, the decision-maker, the educator and the agent, six ways in which a chapter is useful, each a way of using the work and not a job title. (2) The *door* of the foreign runtimes: one governed way to run code in a language the platform does not speak. A door is a runtime and never a syntax. Chapters 2, 16.

**engine.** The Zig library that does the work once, beneath every surface. Chapter 27.

**facet.** In this book, three senses, which chapters 12, 16 and 26 keep apart. (1) A surface of a declared language: its text, its court, its mouth, its voice, its tongues. (2) A syntax of Haro that a programmer of another language could write; the record calls this a face, and only my statement names it. (3) A tool of an environment's ribbon, ordered by the life of the thing being made. A facet is a syntax and never a runtime.

**fake.** A double that says it is one: a sandbox, a mail sink, a recorded answer. A fake must never ship. A double that says nothing about itself is taken for live, and passes the gate. Chapter 24.

**gap.** What a goal needs and the world does not yet hold. A conversation that asks starts from the gap. Chapter 17.

**gate.** A place where something is refused before it goes on. The registration gate, the production gate, the capability seam. Chapters 20, 24.

**goal.** A wanted state of a graph. The gap is measured, and a goal that cannot be evaluated is refused. Chapters 17, 23.

**governance.** Permission, authority and risk, compared before an act, fail-closed, with the decision kept. Chapters 20, 30.

**guard.** A file of the library's test tree that holds assertions, and that proves a chapter. This book counts a guard's assertions and does not run most of them. Chapters 5, 21.

**Haro.** The language this book writes in: the surface of the former host language, kept and renamed, with a charter and a runtime still in construction. Haro does not run the library yet. Chapters 3, 27.

**lexicon.** The dictionary of words a sentence may use, each mapped to a verb the library documents. Chapter 13.

**lineage.** The record of a decision with the authority and the risk of its moment. Chapter 30.

**narration.** The author's article under the library's documentation folder, whose code is the old surface. Not an executable test. (The word has since been given to the executable guards named *narrated*, and chapter 21 says why that confuses.) Chapters 3, 21.

**near-natural chain.** A sentence in which every link is a real call, so that the human is the parser. Chapter 13.

**pack.** A human language as a block of data. Chapter 13.

**perception.** What a person has seen or heard of a thing, named, dated, and quoted. A comparison proves sameness and never rightness. Chapters 21, 33.

**plan.** The one artifact that crosses from a twin toward reality. It narrates, flags and validates, and is committed by a governed actor. Chapters 20, 24.

**platform.** The envelope around a solution, and the ground a constellation stands on: capabilities, a commons, a registry, shells, a profile. Chapter 23.

**posture.** What the registry says a service is: sandbox, local, live, conformance. And for foreign code, what its executor says it is: trusted, external or sandboxed. Chapters 16, 24.

**promise.** The line `#-->` under an expression, which says what it prints. A claim about a stop of the program, judged kept, diverged, unreached or unjudged. Chapters 1, 21.

**proposal.** What a model, a reaction or an agent offers. It commits nothing. Chapters 17, 20, 26.

**refinement.** The write surface: a change declared with its points and judged by a four-stage gate. Chapter 10.

**register.** In this book, two senses. (1) One of the five forms a reply can take: an option, a data structure, a formula, a sentence, examples. (2) The register of innovations, Appendix A. Chapter 18.

**rehearse.** To run on what touches nothing, and to read what it would have done. Chapter 24.

**reversibility.** The class of an act: reversible, compensable or irreversible. The harder an act is to undo, the more trusted the code performing it must be. Chapter 20.

**scope.** A named frame in which a system act is written: development, deployment, runtime-current. The system acts of a program are always in a scope. Chapters 8, 23.

**spine.** The nine chapters that carry the whole argument and can be read alone. The preface.

**stage word.** One of built, designed, absent, printed at the head of each chapter's limit. Chapter 3.

**twin.** A copy of a part of reality that holds no reference to reality: a twin of files, a twin of the process environment. Chapters 20, 24.

**unperceived.** The state of a thing that reaches a person through a sense, when no person has perceived it and said so. It is a state and not a failure. Chapters 21, 33.

**wise coding.** Softanza asks and the world judges. The inversion of the loop of prompting and keeping what runs. Chapter 18.

**world.** One application, as a declaration with a being, a becoming and a way of being met. Chapter 23.


=====

# The guards and fixtures cited, by chapter

Source: Appendix E. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

A guard is a file of the library's test tree that holds assertions. A chapter that says "this is proved" names the guards that prove it, in the front matter of its source, and the build reads each one: it finds the file in the library at the commit the book runs at, and counts the assertions in it. A guard that is not there fails the build.

This appendix is that reading, by chapter. Nothing in it is typed. It is regenerated at every build, so the figures are always the ones the last build read.

Two things to keep in mind. A figure is the number of assertion calls the file holds, counted in its text; it is not a run. Most of these guards are not run by the book, because they write files, open ports or spawn processes. The few I ran myself are named in the chapters that rely on them, with the date. And the only fixtures the book cites are the ones the guards and the blocks read: the course folder of the library's education program, and the data folders its guards name. The blocks of the book are themselves the other fixtures: each one runs inside the library at the commit named under every chapter's title.

The chapters cite 114 distinct guards, holding 3359 assertions between them, read from the test tree at commit 93d7a39bd on 2026-10-09 04:27 UTC+01:00. A guard cited by two chapters is counted once in this total.

# The narrations the book argues from

Source: Appendix F. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

A narration is one of the author's articles, under the library's documentation folder: an essay that tells an idea with its code. There are about a hundred and thirty-five of them, and the book argues from the ones listed here. The code in them is the old surface, which Haro keeps under a new name. A narration is not an executable test: the word has since been given to the executable guards whose names end in *narrated*, and chapter 21 says why that is confusing.

Each chapter names its narrations in its source, and the build checks that each is a file of the library at the commit the book runs at. Where a name is not a file of that folder, this appendix says so. A narration's blocks are not the chapter's blocks. Several no longer run at that commit, and each chapter that cites one says which part, and runs its own.

The chapters cite 49 distinct narrations. Every name is a file of the library's narration folder at commit 93d7a39bd. A narration is the author's article; its code is the old surface, and several of its blocks no longer run at the pin, which each chapter says.

# The book as data, for the agent that reads it

Source: Appendix G. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

This appendix is for an agent that reads the book, and for the person who gives it the book to read. It says what the book offers in a form a machine can take, and what an agent may do with it.

### What there is

Files sit beside the reader, and each is regenerated at every build.

- **`book.json`** holds, for each page of the book, a chapter, the preface or an appendix: its number, identifier and slug; its kind; its part; the law it states, if it is a law chapter; the first principle it states, in one sentence; whether it is on the spine; its status; its stage words, built, designed and absent; its guards, each with its path and its assertion count; its promises, which are its blocks of code with the lines they promised, what the last run printed, and the verdict; its narrations; the study's rulings it rests on; its six doors; its sections; its open questions, which are the places the record could not answer; its word count; whether it has a French edition; and where its text is. A chapter that is a draft says so in its status.
- **`book.schema.json`** describes `book.json`, and the build validates the file against it. A build that produces a `book.json` that does not match its schema fails.
- **`llms-book.txt`** is a short text: the rules for an agent, then one line per page with its status, its guards and the count of blocks that kept their promise, and its principle.
- **`llms-full-book.txt`** is every page as text, one after the other, with the verdict of the last run written under each block of code. **`agents/<slug>.md`** is the same, one page per file; the slug is in `book.json`.
- **`status.html`** is the page for a person: every chapter with its status, its run, its guards, its words, its open questions and whether it has a French edition.

### What a promise is

A block of code that begins with the tag `haro` is run inside the library at the commit named under the chapter's title. The lines that begin with `#-->` are the block's promise: what the block printed when the author wrote it. The judge compares the promise with the run, ignoring spacing, and reading `TRUE` and `FALSE` as 1 and 0. A promise is kept, or it differs, or the block raised, or it stopped. A block that is marked as an exercise is not run, and a block that touches files, the clock or chance says so, and shows the output that was recorded on its date.

An agent that quotes a promise should quote its verdict with it.

### What an agent may do, and may not

- **You may quote any cell.** A table of this book is data. A row that says "absent" is a row you may repeat as absent.
- **You may not turn an absent cell into a present one.** The record will find your sentence and the missing file side by side, and the book says so in chapter 31.
- **You may rely on the stage words.** Built means the code, a guard and a run. Designed means a document and nothing that runs. Absent means that I looked, and say how far.
- **You may rely on a promise only with its verdict, and only at the commit it names.** A number that the book prints is a number read from a file or a run, at the date the page gives.
- **A draft is a draft.** A chapter marked as a draft is not the author's final word, and "the author read it" is a status that only the author sets.

### The chapters, as the build reads them

The table below is generated at every build from the same data as `book.json`.
